# CEO Plan: BRIO Wooden Track Circuit Designer
Generated by /plan-ceo-review on 2026-10-06
Branch: (none — greenfield, no repo yet) | Mode: SCOPE EXPANSION
Repo: none yet
Approach selected: C (Vario tolerance engine + 2D authoring + 3D viewer + train sim)

## Problem Statement

The user's original request was a feature list: "extensive BRIO track database,
graphical drag-and-drop editor, multiple levels, bridges." Premise challenge
reframed it as an outcome:

> A wooden-railway builder goes from a pile of track on the floor to a working
> train on a multi-level circuit, **first try**, with no dismantling and no
> "it doesn't reach."

The real competitor is not other software. It is the specific, familiar
frustration of a layout that almost fits.

The target user is the **large-layout / multi-level builder** (the
woodenrailway.info audience who currently resorts to SketchUp), not the
20-piece casual set owner. The user's explicit callout of multi-level and
bridges pointed at this segment, which is also the unserved one.

### Metric that matters
Not "layouts designed." **Layouts built successfully on the first try.**

## Landscape

| Tool | What it does | Gap |
|---|---|---|
| TrackGenius (trackgenius.app) | Inventory-constrained auto-generation for BRIO-compatible sets, 20+ layouts from your pieces | No editor. Generation only. |
| woodenrailway.info | Authoritative enthusiast reference: full track guide w/ part numbers, Track Math, Track Distance Solver | Reference site, not a tool |
| SketchUp | What serious builders actually use today | Zero track knowledge, no validation |
| AnyRail / SCARM / XTrackCAD | Mature model-railway CAD | Rigid geometry engines; none ships a BRIO library |

**The gap: nobody has shipped a BRIO-aware drag-and-drop editor.**

## THE LOAD-BEARING INSIGHT (Eureka)

Real BRIO geometry, researched:

```
STRAIGHTS   A2 = 54mm   A1 = 108mm   A = 144mm   D = 216mm
            relations:  A1 = 2 x A2,  D = 2 x A1,  2 x D = 3 x A
            (A3 = 72mm, retired)
CURVES      E:  radius 182mm @ 45 deg   (part #33342, most common piece)
            E1: radius  90mm @ 45 deg   (part #33337)
SWITCHES    Q five-point: 45 deg outer, 22.5 deg INNER  (#33462)
            T / X: three or four 90 deg directions @ ~88mm radius
ASCENDING   N / N1: 216mm run, rises 64mm  (#33332)
            -> BRIO's own ascending track reaches 0 / 64 / 128 / 192mm,
               but height is stored CONTINUOUS (heightMm) per 12A; the
               snap set is DERIVED from available ascending/riser parts,
               so other brands' rises and printed risers also fit.
BRIDGE      spans 144 (A) / 216 (D) / 324 (A1+D) / 360 (A+D) mm
BUFFERS     R / S: 40mm      RAMPS  U / V: 54mm
```

45-degree curves mean every diagonal run is a multiple of sqrt(2).

**CORRECTED 2026-10-06 (eng review, finding OR-1).** This section originally read
"Exact closure of any non-trivial BRIO loop is mathematically impossible." That is
false and is corrected here as a factual error; it changes no approved decision
(see D17, answer B).

The correct statement: every reachable pose lies in the ring **Z[sqrt2]**, i.e.
values of the form `a + b*sqrt2` with integer `a, b`. The E curve displaces its
endpoint by exactly `(91*sqrt2, 182 - 91*sqrt2)`; straights at 45-degree headings
contribute integer multiples of `sqrt2`. Because `a + b*sqrt2 = 0` with integer
`a, b` forces `a = b = 0` (otherwise sqrt2 would be rational), closure requires the
integer part and the sqrt2 part to cancel **independently**. That is a decidable
Diophantine condition, not an impossibility: most layouts miss, and when they miss
they miss by a quantized amount. Verified numerically — 8 x E closes to 4.5e-14 mm
and the 4E+D+4E+D stadium to 7.8e-14 mm, both floating-point zero. The D14 fixture
set is therefore evidence for this corrected statement, not a lucky coincidence.

What survives unchanged: the Vario System is real, layouts that miss by a few
millimetres do work in practice, and "will this derail?" remains the question no
existing tool answers. What does NOT follow from the corrected premise is the
original argument for a continuous numerical solver — see open risks OR-1 and OR-2.

BRIO knows this. Their answer is the **Vario System**: deliberate slop
engineered into every joint so pieces "wiggle around in the joints to make the
track fit together at slight angles and even pull it slightly apart."

Their own guidance: *"For most layouts, getting 'close' is good enough.
Distances of a few millimeters are not significant in a straight run."*
And the failure mode: *"if you push the Vario System too hard, you can create
sharp turns at joints that derail trains."*

### Therefore

A rigid-geometry CAD engine — the approach all three incumbents use — is
**structurally wrong for BRIO**. It will reject layouts that work beautifully on
a real floor, and accept layouts that derail real trains.

The correct engine models a layout as a **tolerance-constrained system with a
per-joint slop budget**. Closure is not a boolean; it is a residual error
distributed across joints. **"Will this derail?" is the real question, and no
existing tool can answer it.**

This single insight is the product.

## Vision

### 10x Check

Approach C is a *design tool*. The 10x version is not a better design tool — it
owns the whole loop from *pile of wood on the floor* to *train running*.

Today that loop is: dump the box, guess, build, discover it doesn't reach,
dismantle a third of it, guess again, give up on the bridge, settle for an oval.
A design tool only touches the middle. The 10x product touches all of it: it
knows what you own, designs within that, proves the design runs, and hands you
an assembly sequence your kid can execute.

**The design is the cheap part. Knowing your inventory and producing a buildable
sequence is the expensive part, and it is where all the frustration lives.**

### Platonic Ideal — what the user feels

Sunday morning. Track all over the living room floor. You pick up the tablet; it
already knows every piece you own because you scanned the boxes when they
arrived. You sketch a figure-eight with a bridge over the lower loop. The app
quietly slides one piece 3mm and says: *"This closes. One tight joint on the
north curve — seat that one firmly."*

You press Build. It walks you through 34 steps, one picture each. Your kid takes
over at step 12 and finishes alone. The train runs. **First try.** No swearing,
no leftover pieces, no "it doesn't reach."

### 12-month ideal

```
  CURRENT STATE                THIS PLAN                    12-MONTH IDEAL
  SketchUp + trial/error  -->  BRIO-aware editor that   -->  The tool every wooden-railway
  No tool knows BRIO           knows real geometry           builder opens first. Point a
  Rigid CAD rejects valid      and tolerance, so it          phone at your floor, it reads
  layouts; no tool can         answers "will this             your pieces, you design in
  predict a derailment         actually work?"                3D, the train drives it, and
                                                             it prints a build order.
```

## Implementation Alternatives Considered

```
APPROACH A: Rigid Snap Editor                        Completeness 5/10  REJECTED
  Pieces snap at exact connector positions, rotations quantized to 22.5 deg,
  levels to 64mm. Closure is a boolean.
  Effort: human ~1 week / CC ~2-3 hours
  REJECTED BECAUSE: structurally wrong for BRIO. Contradicts the Vario System.
  Dead-end foundation — adding tolerance later is a full rewrite.

APPROACH B: Vario Tolerance Engine (2D + discrete levels)   Completeness 9/10
  Per-joint slop budget, Gauss-Newton closure solve, joint stress heatmap.
  Effort: human ~4-6 weeks / CC ~1-2 days
  NOT CHOSEN: correct, but C's train sim is executable verification of exactly
  this solver, and C's 3D meshes are procedurally generated from the same
  parametric data, so C is far cheaper than it looks.

APPROACH C: B + 3D Viewer + Train Simulation         Completeness 10/10  SELECTED
  Effort: human ~2-3 months / CC ~4-7 days
  SELECTED BECAUSE (corrected 2026-10-06, eng/CEO finding N1):
  multi-level and bridge layouts are far more legible in 3D than in any flat
  projection of stacked levels, and procedural mesh generation from the
  parametric part data means 3D is NOT 40 hand-modeled assets. D19A changed the
  solver but not the part parametrics, so the procedural-mesh argument still holds.
  STRUCK: "the train sim proves the solver (completed circuit = layout provably
  works)". That was the originally stated first reason and it is FALSE (OR-6).
  The sim traverses the topology graph, which proves topology and not geometry,
  and it consumes the evaluateJoint verdicts it would supposedly validate, which
  is circular. Train wheelbase, bogie pivot and coupler swing are unmodelled and
  open question 3 is still open. Approach C remains selected on the two surviving
  reasons; no implementer should read a completed circuit as verified geometry.
```

## Architecture (as selected)

```
  parametric part DB  (multi-brand, see E5)
          |
          v
  layout = graph of piece instances joined at connectors
          |
  each JOINT carries a slop budget:
     +/- axial pull-apart     +/- angular deflection
          |
          v
  cycle basis (E-V+1 per component)
          |
          +--> STAGE 1  exact residual r_k per cycle, in Z[zeta16]
          |             integer arithmetic, no float, no solve
          |
          +--> STAGE 2  one Chebyshev LP (yalps):
          |             min t  s.t. |u_i|/budget_i <= t,  J_k*u = -r_k
          |             -> t* = worst joint's normalised usage
          |             -> primal u = per-joint usage (E4, 7A, 13A)
          |             -> infeasible binding rows = suspect joints (2A)
          |
          +--> per-joint verdict via evaluateJoint (7A):
          |       PASS   ratio < 0.8        GREEN  + circle glyph
          |       TIGHT  0.8 <= ratio <= 1  AMBER  + triangle glyph
          |       FAIL   ratio > 1          RED    + cross glyph
          |       UNKNOWN solve failed      GREY   + question glyph
          |
          +--> heightMm continuous; snap set derived from parts (12A)
          +--> under-bridge clearance vs train height
          +--> loop invariant: total rise == total fall
          |
          v
  3D viewer (meshes GENERATED: straights = extrusion,
             curves = sweep along 45 deg arc)
          |
          v
  train drives the topology graph
     completes circuit = TRAVERSABLE (topology, grade, clearance)
                         NOT proof of geometry -- see OR-6 / N1
     stalls / derails   = locates the offending joint
```

Key reuse decisions (do not hand-roll):
- 2D canvas + drag: react-konva or PixiJS
- 3D: Three.js + react-three-fiber
- Closure engine (eng review D19A, superseding D5/D6): TWO STAGES, no nonlinear solve.
  1. Nominal residual, EXACT. Poses are cyclotomic integers in `Z[zeta16]`
     (rotation by 22.5 deg = multiply by zeta16; straights are integers). Each
     cycle's closure residual is an exact algebraic constant; "closes exactly" is
     an integer test. No floating point, no convergence, no NaN at this stage.
  2. Slop distribution, ONE Chebyshev LP via `yalps` (MIT, pure TS, 240 KB):
     minimise t s.t. |u_i|/budget_i <= t and J_k*u = -r_k per cycle.
     3c equality rows (c = E-V+1, typically 3-15) over ~2J bounded variables.
     t* <= 1 means it works; t* IS the worst joint's usage; infeasible binding
     rows name the joints at fault.
  Rejected: Cassowary/kiwi.js (cannot express the rotations); `ml-levenberg-marquardt`
  (scalar curve-fitting API); `@salusoft89/planegcs` (LGPL-2.0-or-later, and an
  equality solver minimises constraint error, not slop usage — see OR-2).
  Adapter owns: `u = u+ - u-` split for signed slop; 2A's SolveResult union and
  finiteness assert, because yalps returns NaN on "infeasible" and +-Infinity on
  "unbounded"; pinned `maxPivots`/`checkCycles` with "cycled" as a hard failure.
- Part data source: woodenrailway.info track guide (near-complete with part
  numbers). Cross-check dimensions against Thingiverse/MakerWorld
  BRIO-compatible printable models.

## Scope Decisions

| # | Proposal | Effort (human / CC) | Decision | Reasoning |
|---|----------|--------|----------|-----------|
| — | Approach C: Vario engine + 2D authoring + 3D viewer + train sim | ~2-3 mo / ~4-7 d | ACCEPTED | Only architecture correct for BRIO; train sim is executable verification of the solver |
| E1 | Inventory system + inventory-constrained design + buy-list w/ part numbers | ~1 wk / ~3-4 h | ACCEPTED | Difference between a drawing tool and a build tool. Absorbs TrackGenius's entire value prop as one feature. |
| E2 | Nudge-to-fit automatic gap resolution | ~1-2 wk / ~4-6 h | ACCEPTED | Highest-leverage feature on the Vario engine; reuses the residual the solver already computes. On sqrt(2) geometry, near-miss is the NORMAL case. |
| E3 | Assembly order / build mode w/ per-step 3D render | ~1-2 wk / ~4-6 h | ACCEPTED | The moment "works first try" is kept or broken. Per-step render nearly free given C's meshes. Makes the build kid-executable. |
| E4 | Derailment replay + solver inspector | ~3-4 d / ~1-2 h | ACCEPTED | This is the instrument you calibrate and debug the solver with. Completeness on the sim, not a new feature. |
| E5 | Full multi-brand part DB (BRIO, IKEA Lillabo, Hape, Thomas, Melissa & Doug) + connector mating matrix | ~3-4 wk / ~1-2 d | ACCEPTED | Only a tolerance engine can model a MARGINAL cross-brand joint. Schema is the one genuinely expensive retrofit in the plan. |
| E6 | Full hosted layout library: backend, accounts, publish/fork, inventory filter, moderation | ~4-6 wk / ~1-2 d + ops | ACCEPTED | Inventory-filtered discovery is the strongest repeat-visit mechanic available. |

### Reviewer concern carried forward on E5 (user decided to proceed with full scope)

Slop budgets and connector dimensions are **physical constants** requiring
measurement against real track. Full multi-brand data is gated on owning and
measuring five brands — a procurement problem, not a coding one.

**Agreed mitigation (in scope):** the part DB carries a
`calibrationStatus: measured | published | estimated` field per brand, and the
UI must visibly downgrade confidence on any joint whose tolerance is not
`measured`. Uncalibrated numbers must never be presented as confident advice,
because the failure mode is telling a user a joint is safe when forcing it
will break their piece.

### Reviewer concern carried forward on E6 (user decided to proceed with full scope)

E6 converts a purely client-side tool into an operated service: hosting,
migrations, backups, uptime, accounts, email, and moderation of user-submitted
content. **The users are parents and layouts may carry a child's name**, which
makes child-privacy (COPPA / GDPR) a real duty rather than a checkbox.

**Agreed mitigation (in scope):** the layout document format must still be
portable, versioned and self-describing (`schemaVersion`, parts + brands + part
codes, placements, joint slop assignments, level assignments, solver residual at
save time, inventory snapshot). File export/import and share-by-URL ship
alongside the hosted library so the tool degrades gracefully if the backend is
unavailable, and no layout is ever trapped server-side.

## NOT in scope (available on request, not yet decided)

These were identified as delight opportunities but not presented as individual
decisions. None were rejected — they were deprioritized below the top six.

- **E7 Kid mode** — big targets, no menus, drive-the-train only, layout locked. (S)
- **E8 Table / room bounds** — declare your table or rug, get warned on overhang. (S)
- **E9 Photo-based inventory** — CV counts scattered track by type from a photo. (L, speculative)
- **E10 Symmetry / mirror tools + auto-complete-the-loop** — pick two open ends, app fills the gap from inventory. (M)

## Genuinely out of scope
- Non-wooden track systems (plastic, Lego, model railway scales)
- Train/locomotive physics beyond traversal (couplings, momentum, weight)
- Marketplace / commerce beyond outbound buy-list links

## Open questions for implementation (from Temporal Interrogation)
See the eng review. The load-bearing unresolved items are:
1. Slop budget numeric values — require physical measurement before the solver
   gives advice. What is the bootstrap value and how is it flagged?
2. Solver non-convergence fallback behavior — what does the UI show?
3. Train height / clearance source of truth — per-brand train dimension data.
4. Switch state in the sim — does the train pick a branch, or does the user set it?
5. Whether multiple disconnected components in one layout are legal.

## Prior learning applied
**`layout-follows-differentiator`** (confidence 6/10, 2026-08-07) — "when a
product differentiates on constraint data rather than appearance, the layout
must structurally carry that data." This product differentiates on *geometric
constraint data* (does it close, will it derail, does the train clear the
bridge). A pretty track canvas that does not surface tolerance state would
repeat exactly the mistake that learning records. The joint stress heatmap is
not decoration; it is the differentiator made visible.

# REVIEW FINDINGS (11-section deep review, 2026-10-06)

All 14 findings resolved by explicit user decision. No unresolved decisions.

| # | Section | Finding | Decision |
|---|---------|---------|----------|
| 1 | Architecture | Switches create multiple cycles sharing joints; independent per-cycle solving yields contradictory joint assignments and approves unbuildable layouts | **1A** Global coupled solve: one least-squares over all joints, every independent cycle (E−V+1 per component) as a simultaneous constraint |
| 2 | Errors | CRITICAL GAP — `SolverNumericError` unrescued. NaN comparisons are always false, so a failed solve renders every joint GREEN | **2A** `SolveResult` discriminated union + finiteness assert at engine boundary. NaN cannot cross into UI. Failure is a first-class GREY state; grey ≠ green |
| 3 | Security | Layout documents from library/shared URLs are untrusted input fed to a numerical solver with no resource ceiling. Tab-kill DoS, unbounded hang, XSS via title, prototype pollution | **3A** Parse-boundary hardening (byte/piece/depth caps, strict schema rejecting unknown keys, prototype-safe parse, escaped+capped text) + solver in a Web Worker with a wall-clock budget and forced terminate |
| 4 | Security | Child PII: users are parents, layout titles naturally contain a child's name. Publishing exposes it. COPPA/GDPR-K attach if minors hold accounts | **4A** Privacy by design: email-only accounts, no name/DOB/age/location, public handles only, publish-time name-pattern detection with a one-tap rename, no third-party analytics on library pages, hard delete, audit log. (Not legal advice — obtain counsel before launch) |
| 5 | Data/UX | Undefined behavior when a saved or imported layout needs pieces the user no longer owns | **5A** Layout immutable, never mutated on open. Diff saved inventory snapshot vs live, show concrete shortfall with part codes, offer buy list / rebuild-with-what-I-own (via E2) / just-view |
| 6 | Data/UX | Undo/redo entirely absent from the plan | **6A** Document snapshots only, never solver output (derived state goes stale). One entry per completed action, E2 suggestion = one entry, bounded depth, coalescing |
| 7 | Code quality | "Can a train pass this joint?" asked by 5 consumers (solver, heatmap, sim, build mode, E2 ranking). Independent implementations drift and the app contradicts itself | **7A** One shared pure `evaluateJoint()` returning a verdict with measured value, budget, ratio, reasons and calibrationStatus. All five render it; none re-derives |
| 8 | Tests | The solver's actual claim is physical. No internal test tier can verify it. This is the one cost AI does not compress | **8A** Three tiers: (1) unit+property incl. determinism, order-independence, never-non-finite; (2) fuzz/chaos; (3) PHYSICAL GOLDEN CORPUS of real built-and-measured layouts, which is also the calibration source |
| 9 | Performance | 1A made the solve global, so every drag triggers a full re-solve. Also a UX defect: live slop redistribution makes the whole layout visibly squirm during a drag | **9A** Rigid forward-kinematics preview during drag (no solve). On release: warm-started, component-scoped, Worker-hosted full solve. Layout settles once |
| 10 | Observability | Client-side app + 4A's minimal-collection posture = zero visibility into real solver failures, and no way to learn which brand's calibration is wrong | **10A** Opt-in content-free telemetry (outcome, iterations, residual, piece/cycle/component counts, brandMix, levelsUsed, partDbVersion, appVersion, solveMs — never geometry, titles, parts or user id) + explicit send-this-layout with full payload preview |
| 11 | Deployment | 8A's ongoing recalibration retroactively changes every saved layout's verdict. Green becomes red with no user action, contradicting layouts they already built successfully | **11A** Layouts record `solvedAgainstPartDb`. Re-derive against current data, but surface an explicit diff naming what changed and which joints moved. Add an "it worked fine when I built it" control that feeds 8A's corpus |
| 12 | Trajectory | The 64mm height quantum is a path-dependent assumption. Books, blocks, bridge supports, other brands' rises and printed risers all violate it. Reversibility 1 | **12A** Store `heightMm` as continuous. Derive the snap set from available ascending/riser parts. UI still presents tidy named levels |
| 13 | Design | Joint verdicts encoded in colour alone. ~8% of men cannot read the engine's primary output, and E3's printable build sheet is blank in greyscale | **13A** Multi-channel: colour + glyph shape (circle/triangle/cross/question) + numeric ratio on demand, plus dotted outline for uncalibrated tolerances (E5 calibrationStatus made visible) |
| 14 | TODO | Community custom-part registry not recorded anywhere | **14A** Added to TODOS.md as P2 |

## Technical debt created
- **Calibration provenance** — commit the golden corpus RAW MEASUREMENTS, not just fitted constants. Otherwise "why is the budget 1.8 degrees?" is unanswerable in 12 months and nobody will dare change it.
- Historical calibration changelog, maintained for 11A, indefinitely
- Moderation operational load (E6), ongoing and unbounded
- Five separate per-brand tolerance models to keep honest

## Dream state delta
This plan lands roughly 70% of the 12-month ideal. Present: BRIO-accurate multi-brand database, tolerance-aware engine, 2D authoring, 3D view, train simulation as proof, inventory constraint, assembly order, shareable library. Absent: point-a-phone-at-your-floor inventory capture (E9), AR overlay, and the community part registry (14A, P2). None of the absent items require architectural change — they are consumers of the same parametric part DB and layout document.

## What already exists (reuse, do not rebuild)
| Sub-problem | Source |
|---|---|
| BRIO part data + part numbers | woodenrailway.info track guide — transcribe, cross-check against printable-model dimensions |
| Track math / closure problem statement | woodenrailway.info Track Math (the sqrt-2 and Vario System framing) |
| 2D canvas + drag | react-konva or PixiJS |
| 3D | Three.js + react-three-fiber |
| Cross-brand dimension deltas | published measurements: Thomas 42mm vs 40mm standard; IKEA plug 6.25mm vs BRIO nest 6.4mm; IKEA small curves = half arc angle at same radius |
| Inventory-constrained generation (prior art to beat, not copy) | TrackGenius |
| NOT reusable | Cassowary/kiwi.js — the problem is nonlinear in rotation, so linear constraint solvers do not apply |

## Implementation Tasks

42 tasks synthesized from this review's findings. Full machine-readable list in
`tasks-ceo-review-20261006-173003.jsonl`. 29 P1 (block ship), 12 P2 (same
branch), 1 P3 (follow-up).

Critical path: T1 (part schema) -> T3 (topology + cycle basis) -> T4 (coupled
solver + SolveResult) -> T5 (FK init) -> T6 (evaluateJoint) -> T7 (Worker) ->
T16 (physical calibration corpus). Everything else consumes that spine.

T16 is the long pole and does NOT compress with AI assistance: it requires
physically building and measuring real layouts. Start it in parallel with T1,
not after T15.

## Solver state machine

```
                      place / move / delete / undo
                                  |
                                  v
                            [ DIRTY ]
                                  |
                      drag active?  --yes--> [ PREVIEW ]  rigid FK, no solve
                                  |              |         (9A: layout still)
                                 no              | release
                                  |              |
                                  +<-------------+
                                  v
                     [ STAGE 1: exact residual ]  integer, cannot fail
                                  |
                            [ STAGE 2: LP ]  in Worker (3A hostile-input bound)
                                  |
      +---------------+-----------+-----------+---------------+
      |               |           |           |               |
  optimal         infeasible   unbounded   cycled        timedout
  AND t* <= 1         |           |           |               |
      |               |           |           |               |
      v               v           v           v               v
 [ SOLVED ]   [ UNSOLVED:    [ UNSOLVED:  [ UNSOLVED:   [ UNSOLVED:
              WONT_FIT +      MODEL_ERR    CYCLED ]      BUDGET ]
              binding rows    NaN/Inf ->
              = suspect ]     finiteness assert (2A)
      |               |                                       |
      |          grey verdict, residual + suspect joints      |
      |          E4 inspector available, E2 fix offered       |
      |               |                                       |
      |               +------- NEVER renders green -----------+
      v                               (2A invariant)
 per-joint verdicts via evaluateJoint (7A)
      |
      +-- all PASS ------> sim allowed, build mode allowed
      +-- any TIGHT -----> sim allowed, build warns at that step
      +-- any FAIL ------> sim derails there, build blocked w/ reason
      |
   budget exceeded at any point -> worker.terminate() -> [ UNSOLVED: BUDGET ]
```

# ENG REVIEW (/plan-eng-review, 2026-10-06)

Target: this file (`ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md`), selected at the
Scope gate as option B. Report file: this file.

## Scope Challenge — scope record

Feature answers: D3 → A, phase E5's four non-BRIO brands behind a BRIO-validated
solver (answer: D3 selection "A) Validate on BRIO, then add brands"). Grounds for
reopening an approved choice: concrete new technical risk, not the cost argument
already overruled — calibrating five brands against an unvalidated solver
confounds solver error with per-brand calibration error, leaving a wrong verdict
unattributable among six simultaneous unknowns.

Structure: Smaller arrangement (answer: D4 selection "Smaller arrangement").

Accepted scope:
- All six CEO expansions (E1-E6) and all fourteen CEO findings (1A-13A, 14A)
  remain in scope, unchanged.
- E5 part schema, five-brand enumeration and cross-brand connector mating matrix
  land now. BRIO part data and physical calibration complete first; the solver is
  validated against the BRIO corpus before IKEA Lillabo, Hape, Thomas and
  Melissa & Doug data and calibration are added. Brands read
  `calibrationStatus: estimated` until measured, surfaced via 13A's dotted outline.
- Package arrangement is two packages, not four:
  - `engine/` — pure, no React/DOM/network. `src/parts`, `src/topology`,
    `src/solver`, `src/joints`, `src/sim`, `src/nudge`, `src/format`,
    `src/worker`, `test/`, `data/corpus/`. Absorbs the former `format/` package,
    because engine already owns `solvedAgainstPartDb` and the inventory snapshot
    that live inside the document, and both consumers already depend on engine.
  - `app/` — UI plus colocated server routes. `src/editor`, `src/viewer3d`,
    `src/build`, `src/inventory`, `src/history`, `src/render`, `src/diagnostics`,
    `src/onboarding`, `src/publish`, `src/telemetry`, `src/layout`, `src/api`.
    Absorbs the former `server/` package, giving one shared layout-document type
    with no client/server version skew — which the offline-capable requirement
    depends on. Extracting `src/api` to its own package later is reversibility 4.

Scope Challenge result: scope accepted as-is. (A smaller arrangement preserving
the same feature set is not a scope reduction; D3 is a sequencing change within
E5's approved scope, not a feature cut.) MODE: FULL_REVIEW.

Pending remedies: R1 solver library choice, R2 stale quantized-height assertions
in this plan body, R3 distribution and CI path, R4 frontend framework choice.

### R1: Nonlinear solver — own it or adopt planegcs
Finding: [P2] (confidence: 9/10) ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md:185 — reviewer: plan-eng-review Scope Challenge A (search check)
Plan baseline: line 185, "- Closure solver: custom ~250 LOC Gauss-Newton. NOT Cassowary/kiwi.js — the problem is nonlinear (rotations), so linear constraint solvers do not apply." The Cassowary rejection is correct. No nonlinear library was evaluated.
Runtime evidence: no code exists. Library survey (WebSearch, Aside unavailable): `ml-levenberg-marquardt` v5 exposes `levenbergMarquardt(data, parameterizedFunction, options)` with data as {x,y} arrays — a scalar curve-fitting API, dense only, cannot accept a residual vector over joint variables; not applicable. `@salusoft89/planegcs` is FreeCAD's production 2D geometric constraint solver compiled to WASM with full TypeScript annotations, supporting points/lines/circles/arcs/ellipses and DogLeg/Levenberg-Marquardt/BFGS/SQP backends; it is directly on-domain and was never considered.

| R1 size estimate | ~250 LOC (understated) | ~600-900 LOC incl. Jacobian, damping, convergence, bounds | ~250-400 LOC adapter + WASM dependency | both, throwaway |
| 1A global coupled solve over cycle basis | approved (CEO finding 1) | preserved, native | preserved, expressed as GCS constraints | preserved |

| R3 distribution/CI, R4 framework | pending | pending | pending | pending |

Question D5:
D5 — Build the closure solver or adopt FreeCAD's WASM solver?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; plan line 185 proposes a custom ~250 LOC Gauss-Newton solver and never evaluated a nonlinear library.
ELI10: the solver is the one genuinely hard new component, so it is worth checking whether someone already built it. Two candidates exist. One is a curve-fitting library whose interface only fits a simple function to data points, so it cannot express this problem at all. The other is the real contender: FreeCAD's own 2D constraint solver, compiled to run in a browser, with ten-plus years of production hardening on exactly this kind of geometry. The catch is that it solves constraints that must hold exactly, whereas this product's whole model is that each joint may bend up to a budget and you need to know how much of that budget got used. That per-joint usage number is what the heatmap, the derailment replay and the fix suggester all read.
Stakes if we pick wrong: adopting a solver that cannot report per-joint budget usage means rebuilding that reporting yourself anyway, on top of a dependency you now also have to fight. Hand-rolling when a proven solver would have done means owning numerical code that can diverge in ways FreeCAD fixed years ago.
Recommendation: A because the approved E4 diagnostics and the bounded slop budget are precisely the two things a sketch solver does not expose, and both are already-approved contracts rather than nice-to-haves.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10, C=8/10.
Net: planegcs genuinely lost on contract fit, not on reluctance to use a dependency.
Header: D5 Solver
Options:
A) Own the solver (recommended)
Hand-rolled bounded Levenberg-Marquardt: analytic Jacobian, trust-region damping, explicit convergence criteria, sparse cycle structure. Resized honestly to ~600-900 LOC, not 250. Native per-joint slop usage and residual history for E4.
B) Adopt @salusoft89/planegcs
Map joints to FreeCAD GCS entities and constraints; WASM solver with DogLeg/LM/BFGS/SQP. Proven numerical core, but per-joint budget usage and iteration traces must be reconstructed outside it.
C) Bounded spike on both
Implement both against the BRIO golden corpus, compare convergence and diagnostics, then commit. Costs a throwaway implementation; the choice is internal to engine/ and reversibility 4 either way.

State: approved
Actual answer: B) Adopt @salusoft89/planegcs (D5 answer; reviewer recommended A, user selected B)
State: SUPERSEDED by D19A (exact lattice + YALPS LP). Retained as the audit trail of the D5 decision; the planegcs adoption is no longer in effect.
Accepted scope: adopt `@salusoft89/planegcs` as the closure solver core. Map joints to FreeCAD GCS entities and constraints; the WASM solver (DogLeg/LM/BFGS/SQP) performs the numerical solve. Plan line 185 is amended from "custom ~250 LOC Gauss-Newton" to the planegcs adapter. All approved contracts are preserved: 1A global coupled solve over the full cycle basis is expressed as simultaneous GCS constraints; 2A's SolveResult union and finiteness assertion wrap the WASM return path so non-finite values still cannot reach the UI.
Consequence carried forward (reviewer concern, user decided): planegcs solves equality constraints and returns a solution, not a trace. Two approved contracts therefore need their source named explicitly rather than inherited from the solver — E4's per-joint slop usage and residual/iteration history, and the bounded slop budget itself. That is an independent implementation-policy choice, separated as R5 and resolved at D6 below; it was not bundled into this adoption answer.

### R5: How the slop budget is enforced and reported on top of planegcs
Finding: [P1] (confidence: 9/10) ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md:185 (as amended by D5) — reviewer: plan-eng-review Scope Challenge C, dependent on the D5 adoption answer
Plan baseline: D5 answer B approved planegcs as the solver core. Two approved contracts depend on information the GCS does not return: E4 ("per-joint slop usage + residual/iteration history", CEO finding E4) and the Vario slop budget itself (the per-joint bound that 7A's `evaluateJoint` reads and 13A renders).
Runtime evidence: no code exists. From the planegcs feature survey: it solves Point/Line/Circle/Arc/Ellipse geometry under FreeCAD's constraint set via DogLeg/LM/BFGS/SQP, and supports non-driving and temporary constraints plus reference sketch parameters. Equality-constraint solving with a returned solution is confirmed; per-constraint strain reporting and inequality bounds are not features it advertises. Unverified whether its WASM surface exposes per-iteration residuals — marked unknown.
Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current value | A) Slack parameters in the sketch | B) Post-hoc deviation measurement | C) Weighted constraints plus post-hoc |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R5 budget enforcement | undefined after D5 | each joint's deviation is an explicit GCS parameter; bound checked by solve-check-reconstrain loop | not enforced in the solve; measured after | biased in the solve by per-constraint weight proportional to budget; bound checked after |
| R5 usage reporting for E4 | undefined after D5 | read solved slack values directly; usage = \|slack\| / budget | compute each joint's deviation from nominal in adapter code | compute in adapter code, same as B |
| Vario intent: spread residual so every joint stays inside budget | the heart of the product (plan lines 78-81) | preserved, enforced | NOT preserved — solver may blow one joint while others sit unused | approximated, not guaranteed |
| 7A evaluateJoint contract | approved (CEO finding 7) | preserved, fed by slack values | preserved, fed by measured deviation | preserved, fed by measured deviation |
| 2A SolveResult + finiteness assert | approved (CEO finding 2) | preserved in adapter | preserved in adapter | preserved in adapter |
| E2 nudge-to-fit ranking by cleanliness | approved (CEO finding E2) | ranks on enforced budget usage | ranks on observed usage only | ranks on observed usage only |
| Residual/iteration history for E4 inspector | unknown whether planegcs exposes it | adapter records its own outer-loop iterations | single solve, no outer loop to record | adapter records weight-adjustment passes |
| Added adapter complexity | — | highest: outer loop plus slack modeling | lowest | middle |
| R3 distribution/CI, R4 framework | pending | pending | pending | pending |

Question D6:
D6 — With planegcs chosen, how is the per-joint slop budget enforced and reported?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; D5 adopted planegcs, which solves exact constraints and returns a solution rather than a strain report.
ELI10: the product's core claim is that it spreads the unavoidable misfit across many joints so no single joint bends past what BRIO's wiggle room allows, and then tells you how much each joint used. FreeCAD's solver does not work in those terms. It finds a geometry that satisfies constraints exactly, and it does not report how hard any individual joint was pushed. So you have to decide where that budget logic lives now. The cheapest option measures each joint after the fact, but then nothing ever steered the solver toward a balanced answer, so it can happily blow one joint past its limit while five others sit unused. The thorough option makes each joint's give an explicit quantity the solver works with, so the budget is actually enforced.
Stakes if we pick wrong: post-hoc measurement alone means the app can report a red joint on a layout that a budget-aware solve would have found a perfectly green arrangement for, so the product would reject buildable layouts — exactly the failure the whole tolerance engine exists to avoid.
Recommendation: A because enforcing the budget inside the solve is the Vario model itself, and it is also the only option that yields E4's usage numbers as solver output rather than as a reconstruction.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=4/10, C=7/10.
Net: planegcs supplies the numerics; this decides whether it also supplies the product's actual guarantee.
Header: D6 Budget
Options:
A) Slack parameters in the sketch (recommended)
Model each joint's axial and angular give as explicit GCS parameters constrained to the nominal pose. Read solved slack values directly for usage. Enforce bounds with a solve-check-reconstrain outer loop the adapter owns, which also supplies E4's iteration history.
B) Post-hoc deviation measurement
Let planegcs solve connection constraints, then measure each joint's deviation from nominal in adapter code and compare against budget. Simplest adapter. The solve itself is budget-blind, so a balanced arrangement is never sought.
C) Weighted constraints plus post-hoc
Use planegcs per-constraint weighting to bias error distribution proportionally to each joint's budget, then measure deviation afterward. Approximates balance without guaranteeing it; relies on weighting behavior that needs verifying against the WASM build.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Slack parameters in the sketch (D6 answer)
State: SUPERSEDED by D19A. GCS sketch-parameter slack is replaced by LP variables with native bounds; the mechanism no longer exists.
Accepted scope: each joint's axial and angular give becomes an explicit GCS parameter constrained to the nominal pose. Solved slack values are read directly as per-joint usage, feeding 7A's `evaluateJoint` and 13A's rendering. Bounds are enforced by a solve-check-reconstrain outer loop owned by the engine adapter, not by planegcs. That outer loop is also the source of E4's iteration history, replacing the per-iteration residual trace the WASM surface was not confirmed to expose. The Vario guarantee — residual spread so every joint stays inside budget — is therefore enforced in the solve rather than observed after it.

Question D7:
D7 — Which application framework for the app package?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; the plan names canvas and 3D libraries but no framework, and D4 requires one that colocates server routes.
ELI10: the plan picked the drawing libraries but never picked the thing that holds the app together and serves the API. Two decisions made since then narrow it. The solver is now a WebAssembly module that has to run on a background thread, so how easily the build tool handles WebAssembly and workers genuinely matters. And the editor itself is a canvas, so server-side rendering buys it nothing, though the public layout library is exactly the kind of page you would want search engines to index. A third path is to not decide yet, because the engine package is pure TypeScript with no framework dependency at all and a large share of the work lives there.
Stakes if we pick wrong: fighting a framework over WebAssembly and worker bundling costs days on the one component the product cannot ship without, and picking the heavier option taxes every editor module for a rendering benefit a canvas cannot use.
Recommendation: A because the WebAssembly solver in a worker is the hard dependency and Vite handles that natively, while the library's indexing need can be met later without rework.
Completeness: A=9/10, B=8/10, C=6/10.
Net: Optimize the build for the WebAssembly solver, not for rendering a canvas on a server.
Header: D7 Framework
Options:
A) Vite SPA + Hono API (recommended)
Vite plus React for the client, Hono route handlers at `app/src/api`, one deploy. First-class `?worker` and WASM imports for the planegcs solver. No SSR tax on a canvas editor; library routes can be prerendered or given SSR later.
B) Next.js App Router
Next.js with route handlers at `app/src/api`. SSR and metadata built in, so E6's public library pages are indexable from day one. Costs more configuration for WASM in a Worker, and every editor module becomes a client component.
C) Defer, build engine/ first
Decide nothing now. `engine/` is pure TypeScript with no framework dependency, so the solver, topology, joints, sim, nudge, format and both test tiers can all be built and validated first. Leaves R3 fully pending.

State: approved

## Section 1 — Architecture review

### R6: Where the planegcs outer loop and layout-to-GCS mapping live
Finding: [P1] (confidence: 9/10) ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md:188-196 (as amended by D5) and :284 — reviewer: plan-eng-review Section 1
Plan baseline: CEO finding 3A at line 284 requires the solver to run "in a Web Worker with a wall-clock budget and forced terminate". D6 added a solve-check-reconstrain outer loop owned by the engine adapter. D7 fixed Vite, whose `?worker` and WASM imports host the module. Nothing states which side of the postMessage boundary the mapping and the loop sit on.
Runtime evidence: no code exists. planegcs is a WASM module, so it must be instantiated inside whichever thread calls it. Structural consequence, not a measurement: an outer loop driven from the main thread serializes the full GCS sketch once per iteration, and a wall-clock budget enforced by `worker.terminate()` can only bound work that is actually inside the Worker.
Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current value | A) Whole pipeline in the Worker | B) Map on main thread, solve in Worker | C) Persistent GCS session in Worker |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R6 mapping location | unspecified | inside Worker, from the layout document | main thread builds the sketch | inside Worker, incrementally updated |
| R6 outer loop location | unspecified after D6 | inside Worker | main thread, re-posting each iteration | inside Worker, across calls |
| postMessage round trips per solve | unspecified | 1 | one per outer-loop iteration | 1, plus incremental updates |
| 3A wall-clock budget enforceable by terminate | approved (CEO finding 3) | yes, the whole loop is inside | partially; main thread must own the deadline itself | yes |
| 9A warm start (approved CEO finding 9) | approved | Worker caches the previous solution keyed by layout hash | main thread passes the previous solution each time | native, session carries state |
| T14 determinism property (same document, same verdict) | accepted test scope | holds: Worker is a thin host over pure functions | holds | AT RISK: session state makes the result depend on call history |
| engine/ purity (no DOM, no network) | approved (D4) | holds: loop is pure given the sketch | holds | holds, but the session is stateful |
| 2A finiteness assert at the engine boundary | approved (CEO finding 2) | one boundary to guard | one boundary per round trip | one boundary |

Question D9:
D9 — Which side of the Worker boundary owns the solve loop?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; 3A puts the solver in a Worker, D6 added an outer loop, and nothing says where the loop runs.
ELI10: the solver now runs on a background thread so the interface stays responsive and a runaway solve can be killed. D6 also means solving is not one call but a short loop: solve, check whether any joint used more than its allowance, adjust, solve again. The question is whether that loop runs on the background thread or on the main one. If it runs on the main thread, the entire geometry description gets copied across the thread boundary on every pass, and the timeout that is supposed to kill a runaway solve can no longer bound the loop as a whole, because only part of it is on the thread you can kill. A third option keeps a live solver session on the background thread, which is faster but makes results depend on what was solved before, and you have a test that specifically requires the same layout to always produce the same verdict.
Stakes if we pick wrong: a loop split across the thread boundary makes the wall-clock kill switch unable to bound the work it exists to bound, which is the mechanism protecting against the hostile-layout freeze from finding 3.
Recommendation: A because it is the only arrangement where the kill switch bounds the entire solve, and it keeps the determinism test intact.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10, C=5/10.
Net: Put the whole loop where the timeout can actually reach it.
Header: D9 Worker
Options:
A) Whole pipeline in the Worker (recommended)
Worker receives the layout document, builds the GCS sketch, runs the full solve-check-reconstrain loop, returns one SolveResult. One round trip per solve. `worker.terminate()` bounds everything. 9A's warm start comes from the Worker caching its previous solution keyed by a layout hash.
B) Map on main thread, solve in Worker
Main thread builds the GCS sketch and drives the loop, posting each iteration to the Worker. Serializes the full sketch per iteration and leaves the main thread owning the deadline, so terminate no longer bounds the whole solve.
C) Persistent GCS session in Worker
Worker keeps the sketch alive between calls and applies incremental constraint updates. Fastest warm starts, but the solver becomes stateful, so results can depend on call history and the determinism property test is at risk.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Whole pipeline in the Worker (D9 answer)
State: PARTIALLY SUPERSEDED by D19A. The solve-check-reconstrain outer loop and the warm-start cache are gone. The Worker is retained solely as 3A's hostile-input bound.
Accepted scope: the Worker receives the layout document, instantiates planegcs, builds the GCS sketch including D6's slack parameters, runs the entire solve-check-reconstrain loop, and returns one `SolveResult`. One postMessage round trip per solve. 3A's wall-clock budget is enforced by `worker.terminate()` and now bounds the complete solve rather than a fragment of it. 9A's warm start is served by the Worker caching its previous solution keyed by a layout hash, which keeps the cache internal and leaves T14's determinism property intact. 2A's finiteness assertion guards the single Worker return boundary.

Finding: [P1] (confidence: 8/10) ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md:201 and :282 — reviewer: plan-eng-review Section 1

| 4A audit log over moderate actions | approved (CEO finding 4) | preserved | preserved | preserved |
| Added infrastructure | — | none; the queue is a database query of pending rows | a scheduled-invocation dependency on the host | a separate runtime, a queue library and its own deploy |
| Unreviewed content exposure window | — | zero; nothing goes live before approval | up to one cron interval for auto-approved items | depends on worker latency |
| Time to publish, from the user's side | — | delayed until a human approves | fast for items passing automated checks | fast |

Question D10:
D10 — How does moderation actually run?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; E6 accepted a moderation queue, but D4 and D8 left no long-running process to drain one.
ELI10: you approved that user-submitted layouts get moderated. Since then two structural decisions removed the thing that would do the moderating: the backend is now a set of request-handlers that only run when someone calls them, so there is no background process sitting there working through a queue. The good news is that the simplest fix is also the safest. Instead of a queue that gets drained, a published layout can simply sit in a pending state and not be publicly visible until you approve it, with an admin page listing what is waiting. That needs no extra infrastructure at all, and nothing can ever slip out unreviewed. The cost is that publishing is not instant for the user.
Stakes if we pick wrong: an automated or scheduled path means user content can become publicly visible before any human sees it, which in a product whose users are parents posting layouts that may name a child is the wrong direction to err.
Recommendation: A because it needs no infrastructure, cannot expose unreviewed content, and matches launch volume; it also pairs with 4A's publish-time rename prompt, which already catches the common case before submission.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=8/10.
Net: The accepted requirement is that nothing publishes unreviewed, not that a queue exists.
Header: D10 Moderation
Options:
A) Pending-state gate, human-drained (recommended)
Publishing puts a layout in `pending`; it is not publicly visible. An admin route lists pending items and you approve or reject with a reason. The queue is a database query, not a process. Zero added infrastructure and no unreviewed exposure.
B) Scheduled drain via cron route
A scheduled invocation hits a route handler that runs automated checks (length caps, blocklist) and auto-approves what passes, flagging the rest for human review. Faster for users, but auto-approved content goes live without a human seeing it.
C) Dedicated worker process
Extract the API into a long-running service with a real queue library. Correct at volume you do not have, adds a second runtime and deploy, and reverses D4's accepted two-package arrangement.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Pending-state gate, human-drained (D10 answer)
Accepted scope: publishing sets a layout to `pending`, which is excluded from every public query and from E6's inventory-filtered browse. An authenticated admin route lists pending items and records approve or reject with a reason, written to 4A's audit log. The moderation queue is a database query over pending rows, not a process, so no scheduled invocation, queue library or second runtime is introduced and D4's two-package arrangement is preserved. No user-submitted content is ever publicly visible before a human approves it. Rejection surfaces its reason to the author, per CEO finding 4A's requirement that `ModerationRejectedError` explain rather than just refuse.

Question D11:
D11 — What has to be recorded so a verdict never changes silently?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; 11A records the part-data version, but D5 and D6 moved the maths into a third-party solver plus first-party adapter code.
ELI10: you already decided that if a saved layout's verdict changes, the app must say what changed and why, rather than quietly flipping a joint from green to red. That decision assumed the only thing that could change was the measured track data. Since then the maths moved into an external solver library plus your own wrapper code that decides how much give each joint is allowed and when to stop iterating. Either of those changing can move a verdict just as much as a measurement correction can, and neither is currently recorded anywhere in the saved layout. Your own rollback switch for the solver also has no version number to refer to.
Stakes if we pick wrong: a solver upgrade or an adapter tweak silently flips verdicts on every saved layout, which is the precise failure 11A exists to prevent, and it would be indistinguishable from the app simply being unreliable.
Recommendation: A because the adapter is first-party code you will change far more often than the part data, so omitting it leaves the largest source of verdict drift untracked.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=4/10.
Net: 11A's promise covers the data; the maths now needs the same treatment.
Header: D11 Versioning
Options:
A) Full engine fingerprint (recommended)
Record `partDbVersion`, `planegcsVersion` and `adapterVersion` in the layout document. 11A's diff fires when any of the three changes and names which one. planegcs is pinned exactly so upgrades are deliberate, which also gives T38's `solver_version` flag a real version to roll back to.
B) Add solverVersion only
Record the planegcs version alongside the part-DB version. Catches dependency upgrades but not changes to your own slack modelling or outer loop, which is the code most likely to change during calibration.
C) Leave 11A as written
Keep `solvedAgainstPartDb` as the only recorded version. Solver and adapter changes move verdicts with no explanation, and T38's `solver_version` flag references a version nothing records.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Full engine fingerprint (D11 answer)
State: AMENDED by D19A. The field `planegcsVersion` is renamed `solverVersion`; the three-field fingerprint is otherwise unchanged and still in effect.
Accepted scope: the layout document records `partDbVersion`, `planegcsVersion` and `adapterVersion`. 11A's open-time diff fires when any of the three differs from current and names which one changed, alongside the existing human-readable calibration changelog. `@salusoft89/planegcs` is pinned to an exact version with no caret range, so upgrades are deliberate rather than incidental, and T38's `solver_version` rollback flag now references a recorded value.

## Section 2 — Code quality review

Verified planegcs API surface, read from the package's own type declarations
(`npm pack @salusoft89/planegcs` 1.3.0, extracted and inspected):

```
dist/planegcs_dist/enums.d.ts:33
  SolveStatus = { Success: 0, Converged: 1, Failed: 2, SuccessfulSolutionInvalid: 3 }
  Algorithm   = { BFGS: 0, LevenbergMarquardt: 1, DogLeg: 2 }
  DebugMode   = { NoDebug: 0, Minimal: 1, IterationLevel: 2 }

dist/sketch/gcs_wrapper.d.ts
  solve(algorithm?: Algorithm): SolveStatus
  apply_solution(): void                      // separate from solve: inspect before applying
  set_convergence_threshold(n) / get_convergence_threshold()
  set_max_iterations(n)        / get_max_iterations()
  get_gcs_conflicting_constraints(): string[]
  get_gcs_redundant_constraints(): string[]
  get_gcs_partially_redundant_constraints(): string[]
  has_gcs_conflicting_constraints(): boolean    (+ redundant, partially_redundant)
  push_sketch_param(name, value, fixed?) / get_sketch_param_value(name)
  get_sketch_param_values(): Map<string, number>
  debug_mode accessor (NoDebug | Minimal | IterationLevel)

dist/planegcs_dist/gcs_system.d.ts:38+
  every add_constraint_* takes ( ..., tagId: number, driving: boolean, scale: number )
```

Three consequences of that surface:

1. **D6A is confirmed feasible, not assumed.** `push_sketch_param(name, value, fixed?)`
   plus `get_sketch_param_value(name)` is precisely the named-slack-parameter
   mechanism D6 relies on. Per-constraint `scale` and `driving` flags also exist,
   so D6's option C was a real capability rather than a guess.
2. **A Section 4 finding dissolves.** `set_max_iterations()` and
   `set_convergence_threshold()` are built in, so the outer loop needs no
   hand-rolled iteration cap and there is no decision to make. No finding.
3. **`get_gcs_conflicting_constraints(): string[]` returns constraint tags.**
   Tagging each joint's constraints by joint id yields 2A's `suspectJoints` and
   E4's zoom-to-offending-joint for free, with no extra inference.

Prior learning applied: nan-renders-as-pass (confidence 9/10, from 2026-10-06).

### R9: Solver status taxonomy, and SuccessfulSolutionInvalid as a silent-failure hazard
Finding: [P1] (confidence: 10/10) ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md:280 — reviewer: plan-eng-review Section 2
Plan baseline: CEO finding 2A at line 280 reads "`SolveResult` discriminated union + finiteness assert at engine boundary. NaN cannot cross into UI. Failure is a first-class GREY state; grey ≠ green". The CEO review's 25-class error map names `SolverSingularJacobianError`, `SolverDivergedError` and `SolverMaxIterationsError` — concepts belonging to the hand-rolled solver that D5 replaced. Those rows no longer describe anything the chosen solver reports.
Runtime evidence: verified by reading `@salusoft89/planegcs@1.3.0` type declarations, quoted above. `SolveStatus` has four values, and `SuccessfulSolutionInvalid: 3` is the hazard: its name reads as success while meaning the solution is invalid. An adapter testing `status < 2`, or any truthiness or name-based check, accepts an invalid solution and renders it green. This is the same failure shape as finding 2's NaN case, in a new disguise, and it is matched by the prior learning nan-renders-as-pass. Separately, `has_gcs_conflicting_constraints()` can be true on an otherwise converged solve, so status alone is not a sufficient success test.
Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current value | A) Strict success predicate | B) Status 3 usable with a warning | C) Map status only |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R9 success condition | undefined; error map names solver classes that no longer exist | `Success(0)` or `Converged(1)` AND no conflicting constraints AND every slack within budget | `Success`, `Converged`, or `SuccessfulSolutionInvalid` with a warning banner | status in {0,1} only; redundancy predicates ignored |
| `SuccessfulSolutionInvalid: 3` | unhandled | hard failure, renders GREY | renders as a usable layout carrying a warning | hard failure |
| Conflicting-constraint check | unhandled | required: `has_gcs_conflicting_constraints()` must be false | checked, surfaced as warning | not checked, so a converged-but-conflicting solve passes |
| 2A grey-is-not-green invariant | approved (CEO finding 2) | fully preserved | WEAKENED: an invalid solution renders as geometry | preserved for status, not for conflicts |
| Suspect joints for 2A and E4 | approved | `get_gcs_conflicting_constraints()` tags map to joint ids | same | unavailable |
| Error-map rows to rewrite | 3 stale classes | replaced by the real four-value taxonomy plus the three redundancy predicates | same | partial |

Question D12:
D12 — What counts as a successful solve?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; the solver is now planegcs, whose status enum includes a value named "successful" that means the answer is invalid.
ELI10: the chosen solver reports one of four outcomes. Two mean it worked, one means it failed, and the fourth is called SuccessfulSolutionInvalid, which sounds like success but means the numbers it produced cannot be trusted. Any check written as "status is less than failed" or anything reading the name would wave that straight through and draw the layout as if it were fine. That is exactly the bug you already fixed once, where a failed calculation showed up as a perfect result, just wearing a different costume. There is a second trap too: the solver can converge and still report that some of your constraints conflict with each other, so the status on its own is not enough to call it good.
Stakes if we pick wrong: treating the invalid-solution status as usable puts authoritative-looking track geometry on screen that the solver itself does not vouch for, which re-opens the exact silent-failure hole finding 2 was created to close.
Recommendation: A because it is the only option that keeps 2A's grey-is-not-green invariant intact, and it uses redundancy predicates the library already provides at no cost.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=5/10, C=7/10.
Net: The library hands you an honest failure signal; the only question is whether the adapter respects it.
Header: D12 Solve status
Options:
A) Strict success predicate (recommended)
A solve succeeds only when status is `Success` or `Converged`, AND `has_gcs_conflicting_constraints()` is false, AND every slack parameter sits within its joint's budget. Anything else returns `ok:false` and renders grey. `SuccessfulSolutionInvalid` is a hard failure. Conflicting constraint tags become 2A's suspect joints.
B) Status 3 usable with a warning
Accept `SuccessfulSolutionInvalid` as a usable result carrying a visible warning, on the grounds that an approximate layout is more helpful than none. Conflicts surface as warnings too. Geometry the solver does not vouch for still gets drawn.
C) Map status only
Treat status in {Success, Converged} as success and ignore the conflicting and redundant predicates. Catches the misleading status name but lets a converged-yet-conflicting solve pass as clean, and gives up free suspect-joint information.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Strict success predicate (D12 answer)
State: SUPERSEDED by D19A. The predicate is rewritten over the yalps status set plus `t* <= 1`. The DISCIPLINE survives intact: NaN on "infeasible" and +-Infinity on "unbounded" mean the strict predicate and finiteness assert still apply.
Accepted scope: a solve is successful only when all three hold — `SolveStatus` is `Success(0)` or `Converged(1)`; `has_gcs_conflicting_constraints()` returns false; and every slack parameter read via `get_sketch_param_value` sits within its joint's budget. Any other combination returns `ok:false` and renders grey, never green. `SuccessfulSolutionInvalid(3)` is a hard failure despite its name. `get_gcs_conflicting_constraints()` tags map to joint ids and populate 2A's `suspectJoints` and E4's zoom-to-joint. The CEO error map's `SolverSingularJacobianError`, `SolverDivergedError` and `SolverMaxIterationsError` rows are replaced by the real four-value taxonomy plus the three redundancy predicates; `apply_solution()` is called only after the predicate passes, so an invalid solution is never written into the layout.

### R10: Test framework
Finding: [P2] (confidence: 10/10) no test infrastructure exists — reviewer: plan-eng-review Section 3, Test Framework Detection
Plan baseline: the plan names no test framework anywhere. Accepted test scope is substantial: T14 property tests, T15 fuzz harness, T16 the physical golden corpus, and T39 runs the corpus as a CI gate on GitHub Actions per D8.
Runtime evidence: detection output — no `package.json`, no `jest.config.*`/`vitest.config.*`/`playwright.config.*`, `TESTFILES: 0`. D7 fixed Vite as the build tool, and D5/D9 require loading the planegcs WASM module inside a Worker.
Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current value | A) Vitest | B) node:test + tsx | C) Jest |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R10 framework | none | Vitest | built-in `node:test` | Jest |
| planegcs WASM loading in tests | unspecified | reuses Vite's resolver and WASM handling from D7, so tests load it exactly as the app does | separate loading path to solve and maintain | separate transform config; historically awkward for ESM plus WASM |
| Worker testing (3A, D9) | unspecified | Vite's worker handling available in tests | manual | manual |
| T14 property tests | accepted scope | supported, plus fast-check integrates cleanly | supported | supported |
| T15 fuzz harness | accepted scope | supported, seedable | supported | supported |
| T39 CI gate on GitHub Actions | approved (D8) | single command, machine-readable reporters | single command | single command |
| Added dependencies | — | one, already aligned with the chosen bundler | none | one, plus a transform chain |

Question D13:
D13 — Which test framework?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; no test infrastructure exists and D7 fixed Vite as the build tool.
ELI10: there is no test setup at all yet, and a lot of the accepted work is tests. The deciding factor is narrow but real. The solver is a WebAssembly module that runs on a background thread, and tests have to load it the same way the application does, or you end up maintaining two different ways of loading the same thing and the tests stop resembling reality. One option shares its configuration with the build tool you already chose, so that loading just works. The others mean solving WebAssembly and worker loading a second time, separately, for tests only.
Stakes if we pick wrong: a second loading path for the WebAssembly solver means tests can pass against a differently-configured module than the one users run, which is the worst possible place for a divergence.
Recommendation: A because it inherits D7's Vite resolver, so the solver loads identically in tests and in the app, with one dependency and no second transform chain.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=6/10.
Net: Pick the runner that already knows how to load your WebAssembly solver.
Header: D13 Test runner
Options:
A) Vitest (recommended)
Shares Vite's config, resolver and WASM/Worker handling from D7, so `engine/` tests load planegcs exactly as the app does. Native ESM, seedable fuzzing, integrates with fast-check for T14's property tests, and one command for T39's CI gate.
B) node:test + tsx
No extra test dependency at all, using Node's built-in runner. Means solving WASM and Worker loading separately for tests, so the test harness and the app can drift apart on the one component that must not drift.
C) Jest
Mature and widely known, but needs its own transform configuration and has historically been awkward with ESM plus WebAssembly, which is exactly this project's combination.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Vitest (D13 answer)
Accepted scope: Vitest is the test runner for both packages, sharing D7's Vite config, resolver and WASM/Worker handling so `engine/` tests load planegcs identically to the app. fast-check supplies T14's property generators; T15's fuzz harness uses a logged seed for reproducibility; T39's GitHub Actions gate runs one Vitest command with a machine-readable reporter.

### R11: No tier isolates a solver mapping error from a calibration error
Finding: [P1] (confidence: 9/10) ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md:286 — reviewer: plan-eng-review Section 3
Plan baseline: CEO finding 8A at line 286 reads "Three tiers: (1) unit+property incl. determinism, order-independence, never-non-finite; (2) fuzz/chaos; (3) PHYSICAL GOLDEN CORPUS of real built-and-measured layouts, which is also the calibration source". D5 and D6 introduced a layout-to-GCS mapping that did not exist when 8A was written.
Runtime evidence: no code exists. Structural gap, reasoned from the tier definitions rather than measured: a joint wired to the wrong GCS constraint produces a solver that solves the wrong problem correctly. Tier 1 cannot see it — a wrong mapping is still deterministic, still order-independent, still finite, and still yields the right cycle count. Tier 2 cannot see it — output is still well-formed, still terminates, still non-NaN. Tier 3 would see it, but slowly, at physical-measurement cost, and inseparably from calibration error. That is the same confounding of two unknowns that D3 accepted brand phasing specifically to avoid.
Known-exact BRIO closures available as fixtures, from the verified geometry at plan lines 47-51:

```
8 x E (r=182, 45 deg)               = 360 deg closed circle    residual 0, slop 0
4 x E + 2 x D + 4 x E + 2 x D       = stadium oval             residual 0, slop 0
                                      (two 180 deg arcs, equal parallel straights)
2 x A2 == 1 x A1,  2 x A1 == 1 x D,  2 x D == 3 x A  (plan line 48)
                                    = identical endpoint built three ways
1 x E, open chain                   = endpoint exactly
                                      (182*sin45, 182*(1-cos45)) from start
                                      pure forward kinematics, no solve at all
```

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current value | A) Add a fourth analytic tier | B) Fold cases into the property tier | C) Rely on the physical corpus |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R11 mapping-error detection | none of the three tiers detects it | dedicated tier asserting residual 0 and slop usage 0 on known-exact layouts | same assertions, mixed into tier 1 | detected only via physical measurement |
| Diagnostic clarity on failure | — | unambiguous: "the GCS mapping is wrong" | ambiguous: a failing property-suite case could be a law violation or a mapping bug | ambiguous: mapping error or calibration error |
| Cost to build and run | — | free: all answers computable analytically, runs in milliseconds | free | physical measurement, the one cost that does not compress |
| 8A tiers 1-3 | approved (CEO finding 8) | unchanged, tier inserted before tier 3 | tier 1 grows, 2 and 3 unchanged | unchanged |
| T39 CI gate (approved D8) | corpus gate | analytic tier gates ahead of the corpus, so mapping breaks fail fast | gated with tier 1 | corpus only |
| Consistency with D3's reasoning | — | same principle: isolate one unknown at a time | partial | contradicts it |

Question D14:
D14 — Add a fourth test tier for known-exact layouts?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; D5/D6 added a layout-to-solver mapping that 8A's three test tiers predate and cannot check.
ELI10: some BRIO layouts close perfectly by pure geometry. Eight large curves make exactly a full circle. Two half-circles joined by equal straights make an exact oval. The short straights add up to the long ones in fixed ratios. For all of those, the correct answer is known in advance: zero gap, and not one joint needing to bend at all. That makes them a free and instant check on whether your code wired the track up to the solver correctly. Without them, a wiring mistake produces a solver that confidently solves the wrong problem, and none of the three planned test layers would notice, because the wrong answer is still consistent, still terminates and still looks like a number. The only layer that would catch it is physically building track, which is slow and would leave you unable to tell a wiring bug from a measurement error.
Stakes if we pick wrong: a mapping bug that survives into calibration means you are tuning measured tolerance numbers to compensate for miswired geometry, which bakes the bug into the data and makes both nearly impossible to unpick later.
Recommendation: A because it is free, instant, and the only tier whose failure points unambiguously at the mapping, which is the same isolate-one-unknown reasoning you accepted at D3.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=3/10.
Net: Known answers are the cheapest test you will ever get, and this project has several lying around.
Header: D14 Analytic tier
Options:
A) Add a fourth analytic tier (recommended)
A named tier of known-exact layouts (8 x E circle, stadium oval, straight-length equivalences, single-curve forward kinematics) asserting zero residual and zero slop usage. Runs in milliseconds and gates ahead of the physical corpus in CI, so a mapping break fails fast and unambiguously.
B) Fold cases into the property tier
Add the same analytic cases to T14's property suite. Same assertions and same cost, but a failure no longer distinguishes a violated invariant from a miswired mapping, so the diagnostic value is diluted.
C) Rely on the physical corpus
Keep 8A's three tiers as written and let T16's measured layouts catch mapping errors. Slowest and most expensive detection, and it confounds mapping error with calibration error.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Add a fourth analytic tier (D14 answer)
State: AMENDED by D19A. The tier stands and now passes EXACTLY: integer residual zero and `t* = 0`, with no float tolerance needed on the residual (this was OR-5).
Accepted scope: a named analytic tier of known-exact layouts asserting residual 0 and slop usage 0 on every joint — the 8 x E full circle, the 4E+2D+4E+2D stadium oval, the A2/A1/D/A straight-length equivalences built three ways to the same endpoint, and single-curve forward kinematics to (182*sin45, 182*(1-cos45)). 8A's three tiers are unchanged; this inserts between tier 2 and tier 3 and gates ahead of the physical corpus in T39's GitHub Actions workflow, so a GCS mapping break fails in milliseconds and points unambiguously at the mapping rather than at calibration.

### R12: Emscripten heap growth across a long editing session
Finding: [P2] (confidence: 6/10 — medium confidence, verify this is actually an issue) ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md:188-196 — reviewer: plan-eng-review Section 4
Plan baseline: D9 put the whole solve pipeline in one long-lived Worker, with a warm-start cache keyed by layout hash. D5 made the solver an Emscripten-compiled WASM module.
Runtime evidence: UNVERIFIED — no code exists and no measurement was taken. The concern is a known property of Emscripten modules rather than an observed behaviour here: their linear memory grows to the high-water mark of a session and is not returned to the OS, so a single long-lived module instance that once solved a very large layout keeps that footprint for the rest of the session. Tablet is the primary device for build mode per the CEO review's Section 11, which is where memory pressure would bite first. Whether the footprint is material at realistic layout sizes is not known and is measurable during T16.
Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current value | A) Measure during T16, decide then | B) Recreate the Worker per solve | C) Reuse unconditionally |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R12 Worker lifecycle | one long-lived Worker (D9) | unchanged for now; heap high-water recorded alongside corpus runs | fresh Worker and WASM instance every solve | unchanged, permanently |
| D9 warm-start cache (approved) | approved | preserved | DESTROYED: a fresh instance has no cache | preserved |
| WASM instantiation cost per solve | once per session | once per session | paid every solve | once per session |
| Memory ceiling in a long session | unbounded at high-water | measured, then bounded if needed | bounded | unbounded at high-water |
| 10A telemetry | approved | add peak heap to the existing content-free payload | same | unchanged |
| What stays pending | — | the lifecycle remedy itself stays pending until measured | nothing pending | nothing pending |

Question D15:
D15 — How to handle possible WebAssembly memory growth in long sessions?
Project/branch/task: BRIO track designer; D9 keeps one long-lived solver thread for the whole session.
ELI10: the solver is compiled WebAssembly, and that kind of module grows its memory to the largest size it ever needed and then keeps it for as long as it lives. Since D9 keeps one solver thread alive for the whole session, solving one very large layout once could leave the app holding that memory for the rest of the session. Tablets are the primary device for the build walkthrough, and that is where memory pressure shows up first. I want to be straight with you: I have not measured this and no code exists, so this is a known property of the technology rather than an observed problem here, and it may well be irrelevant at the layout sizes people actually build. The cheap move is to record peak memory while you are already running the measurement corpus, and only act if the number is bad.
Stakes if we pick wrong: throwing away the solver thread on every solve would pay the module startup cost repeatedly and destroy the warm-start cache D9 relies on, which is a real, certain cost to avoid a problem that may not exist.
Recommendation: A because the concern is unverified and the measurement is nearly free once T16's corpus runs exist, whereas acting now would trade a certain cost for a speculative benefit.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10, C=4/10.
Net: Measure it while you are already measuring things, then decide with a number in hand.
Header: D15 WASM memory
Options:
A) Measure during T16, decide then (recommended)
Keep D9's long-lived Worker. Record peak WASM heap alongside the golden-corpus runs and add it to 10A's content-free telemetry payload. The lifecycle remedy stays explicitly pending until there is a measurement; nothing else changes.
B) Recreate the Worker per solve
Spin up a fresh Worker and WASM instance for every solve. Bounds memory firmly, but pays module instantiation on every solve and destroys D9's warm-start cache, which 9A's solve-on-release behaviour depends on.
C) Reuse unconditionally
Accept a long-lived instance with no measurement and no ceiling. Simplest, and may well be fine, but leaves a plausible tablet memory problem unexamined with no signal that would reveal it.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Measure during T16, decide then (D15 answer)
State: DISSOLVED by D19A. There is no WASM module in the engine, so there is no Emscripten heap to measure. No task remains.
Accepted scope: D9's long-lived Worker and warm-start cache are unchanged. Peak WASM heap is recorded alongside the golden-corpus runs in T16 and added to 10A's content-free telemetry payload, which already carries `pieceCount` and `solveMs` so the heap figure is interpretable against layout size. The Worker-lifecycle remedy itself remains explicitly pending until a measurement exists; this answer approves instrumentation only, not a lifecycle change.
