## Open risks identified (dispositions revised at D17A / D19A — see table below)

Each is a real defect or gap, not a hypothetical. These were first recorded as
accepted risks under D17 answer B; after the user revised D17 to A and D19A
replaced the engine, most are RESOLVED. See the disposition table after the solver
rework section for the current status of each. OR-6 is the one that remains an
accepted risk, and it is flagged as a critical gap in the Failure modes registry.

**OR-1 — Premise corrected, engine rationale now unsupported.** Lines 60-61 were
false; corrected above. The corrected premise (a Z[sqrt2] lattice condition) does
not imply a continuous numerical solver is needed. The nominal residual of a fixed
topology is an exact algebraic constant computable in integer pairs. The approved
engine remains planegcs by decision.

**OR-2 — CRITICAL GAP. D6A does not deliver the Vario guarantee it claims.** With
slack parameters free, the equality system is underdetermined: a manifold of slack
assignments satisfies closure with zero constraint error. FreeCAD's DogLeg/LM
minimises *constraint error*, which is already zero everywhere on that manifold, so
nothing in the solve minimises or fairly spreads slop usage. The solve lands
wherever its initial guess and internals carry it. Plan line 526's claim that the
guarantee is "enforced in the solve rather than observed after it" is not supported
by the chosen mechanism. D6's own stated objection to its option B — that a solver
"can happily blow one joint past its limit while five others sit unused" — applies
to the approved option A as well.

**OR-3 — D6's outer loop is a broken active-set method.** Clamping an over-budget
joint and re-solving, with no bound-release step, can terminate at a non-optimal
point and can clamp its way into reporting conflict on a layout that is feasible.
That is the "rejects buildable layouts" failure the tolerance engine exists to
avoid. Mitigated only partially by D12A, which renders such a case grey rather than
falsely green.

**OR-4 — D9's warm start conflicts with T14's determinism property.** A cached
previous solution changes the starting point of a nonlinear solve on an
underdetermined manifold, which changes which solution is reached, which changes
per-joint usage and therefore the verdict. D9's accepted scope asserts determinism
holds because the cache is Worker-internal; internality is irrelevant to the
argument. 9A and T14 are in direct conflict. T14 must be written to exercise cache
state explicitly, or it will not detect this.

**OR-5 — D14's "slop usage 0" assertion will fail for the wrong reason.** Per OR-2
the solver has no reason to prefer the all-zeros slack assignment on the 8 x E
circle. The tier whose purpose is to fail unambiguously on mapping errors will fail
on correct mappings, and the likely response is to loosen the assertion until it
passes, which destroys the tier. Residual needs a float tolerance too; zero is
unachievable in floating point (measured 4.5e-14).

**OR-6 — The train sim does not prove the solver.** T20 traverses the topology
graph, which proves topology, not geometry, and it consumes the verdicts it is
supposed to validate — circular. Derailment also depends on train wheelbase, bogie
pivot and coupler swing, none of which are modelled; "train height / clearance
source of truth" remains open question 3. Approach C was selected over the
feature-identical Approach B specifically on the grounds that the sim proves the
solver (line 145-149). That argument does not hold.

**OR-7 — CRITICAL GAP. T16 fits and gates on the same corpus.** 8A makes the
corpus the calibration source and D8/T39 makes it a CI gate and production canary.
Constants fitted to the corpus will always pass a gate run against that corpus, so
the gate detects code regressions only and carries zero evidential weight for
calibration error — the thing it was built to protect. A declared split is needed:
calibration layouts versus held-out validation layouts, gating on validation only.
Compounding: n=10 to fit a constant presented at sub-millimetre confidence, and
slop is not a constant — it varies with wood wear, plug cycles, humidity,
manufacturing era, and whether a joint is loaded in tension or compression. No
decision exists on measurement instrument, repeatability or repeat count.

**OR-8 — The 42-task list diverged from the decision ledger.** Verified directly
against `tasks-ceo-review-20261006-173003.jsonl`: T4 still reads "Global coupled
Gauss-Newton solver"; there are zero occurrences of planegcs, slack or analytic
across all 42 tasks; and seven tasks point at packages D4 abolished (T8, T9 at
`format/`; T25, T26, T34, T35, T42 at `server/`). **Superseded by this review's own
Implementation Tasks section and its JSONL artifact, which use the D4 arrangement
and reflect every decision D3 through D17.** The CEO-review JSONL is retained as a
historical record and must not be used as the work list.

**OR-9 — Licensing and IP entirely unaddressed.** FreeCAD's PlaneGCS is LGPL and
`@salusoft89/planegcs` compiles it to WASM for pinning into a static bundle, which
carries attribution and relinking obligations. The part database is a wholesale
transcription of woodenrailway.info's curated measurement tables, plausibly a
protected compilation. BRIO is a live trademark and the product is named after it.
The plan has a COPPA/GDPR posture and zero lines on any of this. Proposed as a TODO
at D18.

**OR-10 — The north-star metric is uninstrumented.** Line 29 names "layouts built
successfully on the first try" as the metric that matters. 10A's telemetry carries
solver internals only (outcome, iterations, residual, counts, brandMix, versions,
solveMs). The only outcome signal anywhere is 11A's "it worked fine when I built
it" control, which feeds the calibration corpus rather than a metric. Nothing
measures the one number the plan says matters.

## Solver rework (D17 revised to A)

### R14: Replacement engine formulation
Finding: [P1] (confidence: 10/10) ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md:188-199 and open risks OR-1 through OR-5 — reviewer: plan-eng-review, solver rework
Plan baseline: D5 approved `@salusoft89/planegcs`; D6 approved free slack parameters with a solve-check-reconstrain outer loop; D9 placed that loop in the Worker; D12 defined success over `SolveStatus`; D14 asserted residual 0 and slop 0 on analytic fixtures. OR-2 established that this chain does not deliver the fair-spread guarantee, OR-3 that the outer loop is a broken active-set method, OR-4 that the warm start voids determinism, OR-5 that D14's assertion fails for the wrong reason.
Runtime evidence: two probes, both first-party reads of package declarations, plus one numerical verification.
- Lattice claim verified by computation: E curve displacement is exactly `(91*sqrt2, 182 - 91*sqrt2)`; 8 x E residual 4.457714e-14 mm; stadium 4E+D+4E+D residual 7.765363e-14 mm. Exact closures exist; residual of a fixed topology is an exact algebraic constant.
- Rotations by 22.5 degrees (the Q five-point switch, #33462) leave `Z[sqrt2]`. The closed exact ring for all BRIO headings is the cyclotomic ring `Z[zeta16]` (`zeta16 = e^(i*pi/8)`), where rotation by 22.5 degrees is multiplication by `zeta16` and every straight length is an integer. Closure is then an exact integer test, not a float comparison. The 45-degree-only subset degenerates to the simpler `Z[sqrt2]` case.
- `yalps@0.6.4` declarations read directly: `solve(model, options): Solution`; `SolutionStatus = "optimal" | "infeasible" | "unbounded" | "timedout" | "cycled"`; `Constraint = { equal?, min?, max? }` so equality rows and variable bounds are both native; helpers `equalTo`, `greaterEq`, `lessEq`, `inRange`; options `timeout`, `maxIterations`, `maxPivots`, `checkCycles`. Pure TypeScript, no WASM. Caveats confirmed from the same declarations: `"infeasible"` sets `result` to `NaN` and `"unbounded"` sets it to `+-Infinity`; this lineage takes non-negative variables so signed slop needs a `u = u+ - u-` split; `"cycled"` is a reachable status and `maxPivots` is a fallback, so determinism requires pinned options and must be proven by test rather than assumed.

The formulation: compute each cycle's nominal closure residual exactly in `Z[zeta16]`. Then solve one Chebyshev (minimax) LP — minimise `t` subject to `|u_i| / budget_i <= t` for every joint slop variable and `J_k * u = -r_k` for every independent cycle. With `c = E - V + 1` cycles that is `3c` equality rows (typically 3 to 15) over roughly `2J` bounded variables. `t* <= 1` means the layout works; `t*` is the worst joint's normalised usage; the primal solution is the per-joint usage vector; an `"infeasible"` status with its binding rows names the joints at fault. Angular slop makes `J_k` mildly pose-dependent, so wrap it in 2-3 sequential LP passes with a trust region.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current value (approved) | A) Exact lattice + YALPS LP | B) Exact lattice + hand-rolled simplex | C) Keep planegcs as approved |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Nominal residual | float solve inside planegcs | exact integer test in `Z[zeta16]` | exact integer test in `Z[zeta16]` | float solve |
| Slop distribution | free slack params, D6 | Chebyshev LP, minimax objective | Chebyshev LP, minimax objective | free slack params |
| OR-2 fair-spread guarantee | NOT delivered | delivered as the LP objective itself | delivered | NOT delivered |
| OR-3 broken active set | present | eliminated: no clamping loop, LP handles bounds natively | eliminated | present |
| OR-4 determinism vs warm start | in conflict | no warm start needed; LP is microseconds. Determinism requires pinned `maxPivots`/`checkCycles` and is proven by T14 | full control of pivot rule (Bland's) | in conflict |
| OR-5 D14 analytic tier | fails for wrong reason | passes exactly: integer residual 0, `t* = 0` | passes exactly | fails for wrong reason |
| Per-joint usage (E4, 7A, 13A) | reconstructed | primal solution, direct | primal solution, direct | reconstructed |
| Suspect joints (2A) | GCS conflicting tags | infeasible binding rows | binding rows | GCS conflicting tags |
| NaN hazard | `SuccessfulSolutionInvalid` | `"infeasible"` returns NaN — same 2A predicate discipline required | hand-rolled, you define the contract | `SuccessfulSolutionInvalid` |
| D6 slack mechanism | approved | SUPERSEDED — LP variables replace GCS sketch params | SUPERSEDED | unchanged |
| D9 Worker + outer loop | approved | outer loop GONE; Worker retained only as 3A's hostile-input bound | same | unchanged |
| D12 success predicate | `SolveStatus` based | rewritten over YALPS status + `t* <= 1` | rewritten over your own status | unchanged |
| D11 version fields | partDb + planegcs + adapter | partDb + solver + adapter (`planegcsVersion` becomes `solverVersion`) | partDb + adapter only | unchanged |

Question D19:
D19 — Which replacement engine formulation?
Header: D19 Engine
Options:
A) Exact lattice + YALPS LP (recommended)
Compute cycle residuals exactly as cyclotomic integers, then solve one Chebyshev minimax LP with `yalps` (pure TypeScript, native equality rows and variable bounds). Delivers the fair-spread guarantee as the objective, per-joint usage as the primal, and binding joints on infeasibility. Supersedes D6, D9's outer loop, D12 and D15; dissolves OR-2 through OR-5.
B) Exact lattice + hand-rolled minimax simplex
Same exact-residual foundation, but implement the simplex yourself (~250-350 extra LOC) for complete control of pivoting and provable determinism under degeneracy. No third-party solver at all. More numerically delicate code to own for a 15-row problem.
C) Keep planegcs as approved
Revert to D17 answer B. Keep the approved chain and carry OR-2 through OR-5 as accepted risks into implementation.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Exact lattice + YALPS LP (D19 answer)
Accepted scope:

**Exact nominal geometry.** Every piece pose is an exact cyclotomic integer in
`Z[zeta16]` (`zeta16 = e^(i*pi/8)`): rotation by 22.5 degrees is multiplication by
`zeta16`, and every straight length (54, 108, 144, 216) is an integer. Each
independent cycle's nominal closure residual is therefore an exact algebraic
constant, and "does this close exactly" is an integer equality test with no
floating point and no convergence. The 45-degree-only subset degenerates to
`Z[sqrt2]`.

**Slop distribution as one Chebyshev LP.** For a topology with `c = E - V + 1`
independent cycles and `J` joints each carrying axial and angular slop:
minimise `t` subject to `|u_i| / budget_i <= t` for all `i`, and `J_k * u = -r_k`
for each cycle `k`. That is `3c` equality rows (typically 3-15) over roughly `2J`
bounded variables. Solved with `yalps` (MIT, pure TypeScript, 240 KB unpacked,
native `equal`/`min`/`max` constraints). `t* <= 1` means the layout works; `t*` is
the worst joint's normalised usage. Angular slop makes `J_k` mildly pose-dependent,
so wrap in 2-3 sequential LP passes with a trust region.

**Required adapter discipline, from the verified yalps surface.** Signed slop
splits as `u = u+ - u-` because this solver lineage takes non-negative variables.
`"infeasible"` returns `result: NaN` and `"unbounded"` returns `+-Infinity`, so 2A's
strict predicate and finiteness assertion apply unchanged to this library — the
NaN hazard moved, it did not disappear. `maxPivots` and `checkCycles` are pinned in
a single shared options object so the solve is reproducible, and `"cycled"` is
treated as a hard failure.

**What this supersedes.** D6's GCS sketch-parameter slack mechanism is superseded by
LP variables. D9's solve-check-reconstrain outer loop is superseded by the LP's
native bound handling; the Worker is retained solely as 3A's hostile-input bound,
not for responsiveness, and the warm-start cache is dropped. D12's predicate is
rewritten over yalps status plus `t* <= 1`. D15 dissolves entirely — no WASM in the
engine. D11's `planegcsVersion` field is renamed `solverVersion`; the three-field
fingerprint (`partDbVersion`, `solverVersion`, `adapterVersion`) is unchanged in
substance. D14's analytic tier now passes exactly: integer residual zero and
`t* = 0`, with no float tolerance needed on the residual.

**What is unchanged.** All product scope E1-E6. Decisions D3 (brand phasing),
D4 (two packages), D7 (Vite + Hono), D8 (GitHub Actions, static host, Node runtime,
Postgres), D10 (pending-state moderation), D13 (Vitest — rationale is now simply
Vite alignment rather than WASM loading). CEO findings 1A, 2A, 5A, 7A, 10A, 11A,
13A and all of E1-E6 stand as written.

**Also accepted under D17A** (its option text committed to fixing findings 7, 8 and
10): OR-7's corpus split — T16's measured layouts are partitioned into a declared
calibration set and a held-out validation set, with T39's CI gate and production
canary running against validation only, so the gate carries real evidential weight.
OR-8's remedy is this review's Implementation Tasks section and its JSONL, which
supersede the CEO-review task list. OR-10's remedy is a build-outcome signal added
to 10A's content-free payload, so the north-star metric is measured rather than
merely stated.

## NOT in scope

- **E7 Kid mode, E8 table bounds, E9 photo inventory, E10 symmetry and auto-complete** — surfaced in the CEO review, never promoted to decisions, recorded in `brio-TODOS.md`.
- **Non-wooden track systems, train physics beyond traversal, commerce beyond outbound buy-list links** — out of scope since the CEO review.
- **Four of five brands' part data and calibration** — deferred by D3 until the solver is validated against BRIO. The schema and mating matrix land now; only the data waits.
- **A dedicated backend runtime and queue library** — D10 made moderation a pending-state database query, so no second runtime is needed. Extraction stays available at reversibility 4.
- **Server-side rendering for library pages** — D7 chose a Vite SPA. Prerendering or SSR for public layout routes can be added later without rework.
- **Hand-rolled simplex** — D19 answer A chose `yalps`; writing the LP solver was option B and was not taken.
- **The woodenrailway data-compilation and product-naming questions** — D20 recorded them as a pre-launch TODO; they need counsel, not engineering.

| `solver/lp` | `infeasible` (returns NaN) | Y (T8 predicate) | T19, T21 | "Won't fit" + suspect joints | Y (T37) |

| `solver/lp` | `timedout` | Y (hard fail) | T21 | GREY + budget reason | Y |
| `solver/sequential` | trust-region fails to settle in 3 passes | Y (return last with reason) | T20 | GREY "couldn't settle" | Y |
| `worker` | wall-clock budget exceeded on hostile input | Y (`terminate()`) | T21 | GREY + budget reason | Y |
| `format/parse` | oversized / deep-nested / unknown-key / prototype-polluting document | Y (caps + strict schema) | T21 | "This file isn't a layout" | Y |
| `format/layoutDoc` | fingerprint mismatch on open | Y (T30 diff) | T20 | named change + which joints moved | Y |
| `parts/schema` | part data load failure | Y (hard fail at boot) | T20 | "Can't load track data" | Y |
| `levels/validate` | rise does not equal fall | Y (reject) | T20 | placement refused | n/a |
| `sim/train` | grade exceeded / clearance violated / joint FAIL | Y (stop + replay) | T19, T32 | replay at the joint with numbers | Y |
| `build/order` | dependency cycle in assembly order | Y (fallback to level order) | T20 | "Build order approximate" | Y |
| `inventory` | saved layout needs pieces no longer owned | Y (T29) | T29 | shortfall + rebuild offer | n/a |
| `api/publish` | double submit | Y (T43 idempotency key) | T43 | one layout | Y |
| `api/library` | backend unavailable | Y (full offline mode) | T42 | "Library offline, local features work" | Y |
| `api/moderation` | content rejected | Y (reason surfaced) | T34 | reason, not just refusal | Y (audit log) |
| **`sim/train` as solver proof** | **the sim validates topology, not geometry, and consumes the verdicts it would validate** | **N** | **N** | **nothing — appears to be verification** | **N** |

**CRITICAL GAP: 1.** The last row is OR-6, accepted as risk. The train simulator
completing a circuit reads as proof that the layout works, but it only proves the
graph is traversable; it consumes `evaluateJoint` verdicts rather than independently
checking geometry, and train wheelbase, bogie pivot and coupler swing are not
modelled at all. Approach C was selected over the feature-identical Approach B
partly on this argument. No test can close this because the gap is in what the
feature means, not in whether it works. Open question 3 (train dimension source of
truth) remains open and is its prerequisite.

All other failure modes have a rescue, a test and a visible user outcome.

## Worktree parallelization strategy

| Step | Modules touched | Depends on |
|---|---|---|
| S1 part schema + BRIO data | `engine/src/parts`, `engine/data` | — |
| S2 exact geometry | `engine/src/geom` | S1 |
| S3 topology + cycle basis | `engine/src/topology` | S2 |
| S4 LP engine | `engine/src/solver`, `engine/src/joints` | S3 |
| S5 document format + parse | `engine/src/format` | S1 |
| S6 worker bound | `engine/src/worker` | S4 |
| S7 downstream engine features | `engine/src/sim`, `engine/src/nudge`, `engine/src/build`, `engine/src/levels` | S4 |
| S8 test tiers | `engine/test` | S4 |
| S9 physical corpus | `engine/data/corpus` | S1 only |
| S10 editor and render | `app/src/editor`, `app/src/render`, `app/src/history` | S4, S5 |
| S11 3D and build mode | `app/src/viewer3d`, `app/src/build`, `app/src/diagnostics` | S7, S10 |
| S12 inventory and layout flows | `app/src/inventory`, `app/src/layout`, `app/src/onboarding` | S5 |
| S13 API and publishing | `app/src/api`, `app/src/publish`, `app/src/flags` | S5 |
| S14 telemetry | `app/src/telemetry` | S4 |
| S15 CI and docs | `.github`, `docs` | S8, S9 |

**Parallel lanes:**
- `Lane A: S1 -> S2 -> S3 -> S4 -> S6 (shared engine/src/solver)` — the critical path.
- `Lane B: S9 (independent, engine/data/corpus)` — **launch immediately alongside S1.** This is the physical calibration corpus; it does not compress with AI and it gates every verdict downstream. Starting it after S4 is the single worst sequencing choice available.
- `Lane C: S5 (independent, engine/src/format)` — needs S1 only.
- `Lane D: S13 (independent, app/src/api)` — needs S5 only; disjoint from all engine work.
- `Lane E: S7 -> S11 (shared app/src)` — after S4.
- `Lane F: S8 (engine/test)` — after S4, runs alongside S7.
- `Lane G: S10 -> S12 (shared app/src)` — after S4 and S5.

**Execution order:** Launch A + B + C together. When S1 lands, D starts. When S4
lands, merge and launch E + F + G. S15 last.

**Conflict flags:** S4, S6, S7 and S8 all touch `engine/src/solver` or its tests —
sequence them within Lane A/E/F rather than running them concurrently. S10, S11 and
S12 all write under `app/src` — stagger or coordinate. S9 touches nothing any other
lane touches and is safe to run in a separate worktree for the whole project.

Both carry `calibrationStatus: estimated` until measured, and 13A's dotted-outline channel surfaces that uncertainty wherever a clearance or grade verdict is shown. ### N5: Verdict line composition across two independent axes (Section 1)
Finding: [P1] (confidence: 9/10) T25 "Always-visible verdict line with an unambiguous GREY state for solve-failed" and N3's accepted checker — reviewer: plan-ceo-review re-review, Section 1
Current: T25 was specified against a single verdict axis, joint tolerance from `evaluateJoint`. N3's accepted scope adds route traversability as a second, independent axis. No rule exists for the mixed cases.
Evidence: the two axes are genuinely independent. All joints can read PASS while under-bridge clearance fails (a tall vehicle under a low bridge on a geometrically perfect layout), and a route can be fully traversable while one joint reads FAIL (a tight joint on a simple oval). The plan's verdict strings ("This works", "Works — 2 tight joints", "Won't close — 8mm short", grey) cover one axis only. Mishandling this reintroduces the false-assurance failure N1 just corrected: "This works" shown on a layout whose bridge a train cannot pass under is a stronger false claim than the struck rationale ever made.
Comparison grid:

| Commitment | Source / approval | Current | A) Worst-axis wins, name the axis | B) Two separate indicators | C) Joint axis only in the line |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| verdict line content | T25 approved, one axis | undefined for mixed cases | single line showing the worse of the two, always naming which axis | one line per axis, both always visible | joint verdict only; traversability elsewhere |
| all-green joints + clearance fail | undefined | undefined | "Won't run — bridge clearance 38mm, needs 44mm" | joints "OK" / route "Clearance fails" | **"This works"** ← false assurance |
| red joint + traversable route | undefined | undefined | "Won't fit — 1 joint over budget" | joints "1 over budget" / route "OK" | "Won't fit — 1 joint over budget" |
| solve failed (2A grey) | 2A approved | grey | grey, both axes UNKNOWN (checker cannot run unsolved) | grey on both | grey |
| N1 no-false-assurance intent | N1 accepted | n/a | preserved | preserved | VIOLATED |
| information hierarchy (Section 11) | CEO review Section 11: one unmistakable status | one line | one line, preserved | two competing lines | one line |
| 13A multi-channel rendering | 13A approved | per-joint glyphs | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |
| Effort | — | — | S, ~3h human / ~20min CC | S, ~4h human / ~30min CC | S, zero extra work |

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Worst-axis wins, name the axis (D5 answer)
Accepted scope: the verdict line reports whichever of the two axes is worse and always names which one, so the user knows whether the fix is nudging a joint or raising a bridge. Joint-tolerance failures read as before ("Won't fit — 1 joint over budget"); traversability failures name the measured value and the requirement ("Won't run — bridge clearance 38mm, needs 44mm"). A solve failure keeps 2A's grey with BOTH axes reported unknown, because the checker cannot run on an unsolved layout. The single-status information hierarchy from the CEO review's Section 11 is preserved; 13A's per-joint multi-channel rendering is unchanged.

- **Two separate status indicators** — D5 option B, rejected in favour of one line naming the worse axis.
- **Everything else in the plan** — not re-reviewed. This was a narrow decision on two features.

### What already exists
No new dependency. The checker is a pure function over the existing topology graph and solved placements. N4's two scalars join the existing part-DB schema, reusing `calibrationStatus` and 13A's dotted-outline channel rather than inventing a confidence mechanism. T22's corpus already measures physical constants, so the scalars ride along at negligible marginal cost.

### Dream state delta
The 12-month ideal reads "you design in 3D, the train drives it, and it prints a build order." All three survive this review. What changed is that the train driving it no longer claims to prove anything, and the three checks it does perform are now testable without the interface.

| `sim/check.grade()` | segment grade over N4 limit | Y, verdict | Y | named value vs limit | Y |

| animation | layout edited mid-run | Y (T40) | Y | run stops | n/a |
| animation | checker reports not traversable | Y | Y | animation refuses to start | n/a |

**0 critical gaps from this review.** Every row has a rescue, a test and a visible
outcome. OR-6, previously the plan's single critical gap, is resolved: the sim no
longer claims verification anywhere in the document (N1) or in the interface (N6),
and what it genuinely checks is extracted and testable (N3).

The previously flagged critical gap `sim/train as solver proof` is **CLOSED**. Its
row in the eng-review registry is superseded by the eight rows above.

# DESIGN REVIEW (/plan-design-review, 2026-10-06)

First design review on this plan. Target: this file. Mode: all 7 passes (D1 → A).
Mockups UNAVAILABLE: the gstack designer has no OpenAI API key, so this review is
text-only and nothing visual has been produced or seen.
Outside design voices: SKIPPED by user (D2 → B); Codex not installed and the
native subagent had already exceeded its bound twice this session.
Surface classifier for the hard rules: **OPERATE** (App UI — an editor and a set
of tools), with the printable build sheet as a **READ** surface. No marketing or
landing surface is in scope.

## Pass 1 — Information Architecture: 5/10 → 8/10

Initial 5/10: the editor had a stated hierarchy (canvas dominant, verdict line
second, palette third, everything else behind deliberate affordances). Six other
accepted surfaces had none.

**Decision D3 → B: specify build mode and library browse only.** Inventory, buy
list, diagnostics and onboarding are deliberately left to implementation. Re-rated
8/10 — the two highest-risk surfaces are now decided; four remain unspecified, so
not a 10.

### Build mode hierarchy (tablet, on the floor, arm's length)

Design constraint: used kneeling, tablet flat on the floor, roughly 60cm away,
often with a child doing the tapping.

```
 ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │                                              │
 │     [1] THE NEXT PIECE, IN PLACE             │  dominant
 │     3D render of the layout so far with      │  ~70% of the frame
 │     the next piece highlighted where it      │
 │     goes. You must be able to recognise       │
 │     which physical piece to pick up.         │
 │                                              │
 ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │  [2] Large curve E  (#33342)                 │  legible at 60cm
 ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │  [3] Step 12 of 34        [4] tap to advance │  reassurance / action
 └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
      whole screen is the advance target
```

1. The next piece rendered in place, dominant. Recognition beats description.
2. Its name and BRIO part code, sized to read at 60cm.
3. Step position, small — this is reassurance, not instruction.
4. Advance: the entire screen is the tap target, because a child will be tapping.

Deliberately absent: navigation chrome, menus, and the verdict line. The layout was
verified before build mode opened; re-stating it here would compete with the only
thing that matters, which is which piece comes next.

Tight-joint warnings (13A TIGHT) appear inline at the step where that joint is
seated, never as a persistent banner.

**Printed sheet (READ surface):** same order, but the render becomes a line
drawing so it survives greyscale, and 13A's glyph shapes carry the tight-joint
warning since colour is unavailable on paper.

### Library browse hierarchy (desktop)

**Prior learning applied: layout-follows-differentiator (confidence 6/10, from
2026-08-07)** — when a product differentiates on constraint data rather than
appearance, a card grid structurally cannot carry it, because half-width rows
truncate exactly the differentiating fields.

This surface's differentiating field is *can I build this with the track I own*,
which is a variable-length string ("buildable" / "needs 2 more large curves E").
A card grid truncates it. Cards are therefore rejected here on structural grounds,
not taste.

```
 ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │ [2] ☑ Buildable with my inventory    sort: ▾ pieces           │  primary control
 ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │ [1] ▦  Figure-eight with bridge    34 pieces   ✓ buildable    │  dense rows
 │     ▦  Double oval + siding        52 pieces   needs 2× E     │  one row per
 │     ▦  Spiral climb                71 pieces   needs 1× N,    │  layout
 │                                                 3× A2         │
 └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
      [3] secondary filters below the fold
```

1. A dense list, one row per layout, carrying a small thumbnail, the name, the
   piece count, and the buildability verdict with its full shortfall text
   untruncated.
2. The "buildable with my inventory" filter as the primary control, not buried in
   a filter drawer. It is the question the user actually arrives with.
3. Secondary sorts and filters below.

**Prior learning applied: colour-as-data-not-identity (confidence 6/10, from
2026-08-07)** — spend colour only where it changes a user decision. On this
surface the buildability verdict is the only field that changes a decision, so it
is the only place colour is spent. Thumbnails stay neutral.

## Pass 2 — Interaction State Coverage: 7/10 → 10/10

Initial 7/10: a real state table existed from the CEO review (7 features x five
states), but it was stale by six flows added afterwards, and 2A/D5A introduced
verdict states it did not represent.

Five of the six missing rows were transcription of already-approved behaviour and
were recorded without a separate question. One cell was genuinely undecided and
one more was created by resolving it.

```
 FLOW                      | LOADING    | EMPTY            | ERROR               | SUCCESS          | PARTIAL
 --------------------------|------------|------------------|---------------------|------------------|------------------
 Verdict line (2A, D5A)    | solving    | no pieces placed | GREY, both axes     | worse axis named  | TIGHT joints
                           |            |                  | reported unknown    |                  | named
 Shortfall on open (5A)    | —          | —                | —                   | 3 paths offered   | some parts short
 Calibration diff (11A)    | —          | —                | —                   | change narrated   | some joints moved
 Solver inspector (E4)     | —          | no failure yet   | shows the failure   | numbers shown     | —
 Buy list (phone)          | —          | nothing short    | offline             | part codes shown  | partial basket
 Publish -> moderation     | submitting | —                | rejected w/ reason  | shown as          | —
   author view (D4 -> B)   |            |                  |                     | PUBLISHED         |
 Shared link, recipient    | —          | —                | —                   | layout renders    | —
   view (D5 -> A)          |            |                  | (not 404)           | once approved     |
 Pending link, recipient   | —          | —                | explains it is      | —                 | —
   view (D5 -> A)          |            |                  | awaiting review +   |                  |
                           |            |                  | link to browse      |                  |
```

**Decision D4 → B (author view): optimistic.** On publish the author sees their
layout as published. It is not publicly visible until approved.

Accepted residual risk, user decided with the tradeoff stated: the author is not
proactively told a review queue exists, so they may share the link believing it is
live. D5 reduces the damage by making the recipient's page explain the queue
rather than fail, which is also how the author discovers it when testing their own
link. Not mitigated further by choice: the share action is deliberately not gated
or warned, because gating it would contradict D4's accepted "show as published".

**Decision D5 → A (recipient view):** opening a shared link to a still-pending
layout returns a real page explaining the layout is awaiting review, with a link
to browse approved layouts. Never a bare 404. Does not alter the author's view.

Re-rated 10/10: every flow now specifies what each user sees in every applicable
state, for both the author and the recipient.

## Pass 3 — User Journey & Emotional Arc: 6/10

The plan carries an unusually strong happy-path arc already (the platonic ideal:
Sunday morning, sketch a figure-eight, the app nudges one piece 3mm, press Build,
the kid takes over at step 12, the train runs first try). Recorded below as the
required storyboard; this is transcription of an accepted requirement, not a new
design choice.

```
 STEP | USER DOES                  | USER FEELS           | PLAN SPECIFIES?
 -----|----------------------------|----------------------|----------------------------
 1    | Opens the app, floor       | mild dread: this     | YES — active empty state,
      | covered in track           | usually ends badly   | "which sets do you own?"
 2    | Adds the sets they own     | small relief: it     | YES — add by set, palette
      |                            | knows my pieces      | shows owned counts
 3    | Sketches a figure-eight    | tentative: will this | YES — rigid preview during
      | with a bridge              | even be possible     | drag, no squirming (9A)
 4    | Releases the last piece    | the held breath      | YES — layout settles once;
      |                            |                      | motion UNSPECIFIED (Pass 4)
 5    | Reads the verdict          | relief or deflation  | YES — worse axis named,
      |                            |                      | grey never reads as green
 6    | Presses Build              | commitment           | YES — build mode, tablet
 7    | Follows steps 1-11         | rhythm, competence   | YES — step hierarchy (D3)
 8    | Hands the tablet over      | pride, a little      | PARTIAL — no handover
      | at step 12                 | nervous              | affordance specified
 9    | Train completes the route  | earned satisfaction  | YES — names the three
      |                            |                      | checks (N6), not "works"
```

Time horizons: 5 seconds (visceral) is the empty state and the canvas; 5 minutes
(behavioural) is drag, settle, verdict, nudge; 5 years (reflective) is whether
this became the thing you open every time the track comes out.

It is a 6 because **every arc in the plan is a success arc.** This product's
defining emotional moment is being told no, and no disappointment arc exists
anywhere.

**Decision D6 → A: storyboard all three failure arcs.** Re-rated 9/10 — the
handover affordance at step 8 of the happy arc remains unspecified, so not a 10.

### Failure arc 1 — "Won't fit" after ten minutes of designing

```
 STEP | USER DOES              | USER FEELS            | WHAT SUPPORTS IT
 -----|------------------------|-----------------------|---------------------------
 1    | releases the last      | held breath           | layout settles once (9A)
      | piece of a big layout  |                       |
 2    | reads "Won't fit —     | deflation, a flicker  | TONE RULE below
      | 1 joint over budget"   | of "I did it wrong"   |
 3    | sees the joint named   | orientation           | 13A glyph + the number
      | and highlighted        |                       | on that joint
 4    | taps the offered fix   | relief, agency        | E2 ranked suggestions
```

**Tone rule.** A near-miss is the NORMAL case on this geometry, not user error.
45-degree curves make diagonal runs irrational, so almost every interesting layout
lands a few millimetres off. The copy must say that, because it is both true and
exculpating. Lead with proximity, then the domain fact, then the fix:
*"8mm short. That's normal with 45-degree curves — here's how to close it."*
Never phrase a near-miss as a mistake, and never use failure language ("invalid",
"error", "cannot") for a layout that is one piece swap from working.

### Failure arc 2 — the calibration contradiction (sharpest)

```
 STEP | USER DOES              | USER FEELS            | WHAT SUPPORTS IT
 -----|------------------------|-----------------------|---------------------------
 1    | opens a layout they    | fond, expecting it    | layout opens unmutated (5A)
      | built months ago       | to be as they left it |
 2    | 11A diff fires: two    | BETRAYED. "It worked  | TONE RULE below
      | joints now read amber  | when I built it."     |
 3    | reads what changed     | partially mollified   | names which of partDb /
      |                        |                       | solver / adapter changed
 4    | taps "It worked fine   | heard, not dismissed  | feeds 8A's corpus
      | when I built it"       |                       |
```

**Tone rule — the user is a witness, not mistaken.** Their layout physically
worked; they watched it. That observation outranks our model, because reality does.
The copy must therefore:
- Lead with what changed on OUR side, never with what is wrong with their layout.
  *"We measured real track and tightened the large-curve tolerance from 2.5 to 1.8
  degrees. Two joints that read green now read amber."*
- Present amber as reduced confidence in OUR number, not in their layout.
- Never say "your layout may not work" about a layout the user has already built.
- Frame the "it worked fine when I built it" control as them correcting US:
  *"Noted. That goes into the calibration set."* Not as us tolerating a complaint.

This is the single highest-stakes piece of copy in the product. Getting it wrong
teaches users the engine is unreliable at the exact moment it became more accurate.

### Failure arc 3 — moderation rejection

```
 STEP | USER DOES              | USER FEELS            | WHAT SUPPORTS IT
 -----|------------------------|-----------------------|---------------------------
 1    | publishes; sees it as  | pride, done           | D4 → B optimistic view
      | published              |                       |
 2    | later: rejected        | embarrassed, exposed  | TONE RULE below
      | (personal name in      | — more so if it is a  |
      | the title)             | child's name          |
 3    | reads the reason       | understands, not      | 4A requires reason,
      |                        | judged                | never bare refusal
 4    | one-tap rename and     | resolved quickly      | reuses 4A's rename
      | resubmit               |                       | suggestion
```

**Tone rule.** The rejection is about the TITLE, never about the person or the
layout. *"We don't publish layouts with personal names in the title. Your layout
is fine — rename it and it goes straight back in the queue."*

And own the failure: 4A's name-prompt fires BEFORE publishing specifically to
prevent this. A name-based rejection therefore means our prompt missed, which is
our miss and not the user's. The copy should carry no implication that they did
something wrong.

## Pass 4 — AI Slop Risk: 6/10 → 8/10

Classifier: **OPERATE** (App UI). Printable build sheet: **READ**. No marketing
surface in scope, so Landing Page rules do not apply.

Initial 6/10, capped below 8 by unresolved hard rejection #7 (App UI made of
stacked cards instead of layout) on inventory, buy list and diagnostics.

**Hard rejections after review:** #1 clear (canvas is first; library resolved to
dense rows at D3). #2-#6 not applicable, no marketing surface. **#7 RESOLVED by
D7.**

**Litmus after review:** 1 brand unmistakable NO (no design system, nothing drawn
— see Pass 5); 2 one visual anchor YES; 3 scannable by status alone YES;
4 one job per area YES; 5 cards necessary YES (D7); 6 motion improves hierarchy
YES (D8); 7 premium without decorative shadows PARTIAL (D7 confines shadows to
interactive cards; no depth spec yet).

Re-rated 8/10. The remaining NO on litmus 1 is real and belongs to Pass 5.

**Decision D7 → A: one global component rule.**
A card is used only when the card IS the interaction — something clicked, dragged
or selected as a unit. Never for grouping, never for decoration, never as a way to
fence off a block of text. Applies to all seven surfaces, including the four with
no hierarchy spec, and to any surface added later. Shadows therefore appear only
on interactive cards, which also answers most of litmus 7.

**Decision D8 → A: propagating settle (the one authored motion moment).**
On release, motion radiates outward from the released piece along the topology, so
the user watches their change propagate through the joints it actually affected.
Roughly 250ms, exponential ease-out, starting from the already-visible rigid
preview rather than from nothing. A hard total-duration cap keeps long chains from
dragging. The verdict resolves after the settle completes, so no content hides
behind animation timing. This is the product's only authored motion moment: motion
carries the constraint data rather than decorating, which is the same principle as
`layout-follows-differentiator` applied to time instead of space.

**Decision D9 → A: toy warmth crossed with instrument precision.**
The stated direction (warm beech, off-white, primary accents, Scandinavian
restraint) is exactly what anyone would guess from the category, which is the
calibration warning. It is not a generic default — it is the real aesthetic of the
actual subject — but it supplies no differentiation on its own.

The second axis is that this product is a measuring instrument. Everything it says
is millimetres, tolerances, ratios and part codes. So:
- Warm paper ground and the wooden track's own colour supply the warmth.
- The chrome reads as a drafting tool: tabular figures wherever numbers appear, a
  technical mono for measurements and part codes, hairline measurement rules,
  drafting-grade restraint.
- The tension between children's toy and precision instrument IS the identity.

This also satisfies `colour-as-data-not-identity` (6/10, 2026-08-07): the warmth
comes from the track, which is data; the instrument chrome stays neutral; colour
is spent only on verdicts, where it changes a decision.

**Universal-rule exposures still open, carried to Pass 5:** no CSS variables, no
typeface chosen (live exposure to `system-ui` as display voice), body size and
contrast unspecified, placeholder-as-label unaddressed on inventory forms.

## Pass 5 — Design System Alignment: 2/10 → 4/10

No DESIGN.md exists for this product. The `designs/design-system-20260807/DESIGN.md`
in this slug bucket belongs to the unrelated giving-app and is not reusable.

Initial 2/10: semantic verdict roles (PASS/TIGHT/FAIL/UNKNOWN) and their glyph
pairing were real; every other token class was absent — no typeface, type scale,
colour values, spacing scale, radius, or depth values.

**Decision D10 → A: run `/design-consultation` next.** The gap is owned and
routed, and D9's accepted direction (toy warmth crossed with instrument precision)
is the brief it needs. Re-rated 4/10 — motion is now specified (D8), the direction
is set, and the gap has an owner, but the plan itself still carries no tokens, so
the score stays low. It is a 10 only once a DESIGN.md exists.

**Carry into the consultation** (product-specific requirements it must be told,
not generic preferences):
- **Tabular figures are mandatory.** This product renders columns of measurements
  everywhere (38mm, 44mm, ratios, part counts, step numbers). Proportional figures
  make those columns jitter as values change.
- A technical mono for measurements and part codes, per D9.
- Colour values for exactly four verdict states, and nowhere else by default.
- A contrast floor: body text at least 16px and at least 4.5:1.
- Focus-ring, selection, caret and scrollbar theming — these ship with browser
  defaults that belong to no design system.
- Depth: D7 already confines shadows to interactive cards; the consultation sets
  the offset and blur, and must not produce a zero-offset halo.
- A touch-target minimum value (44px floor) for the tablet surfaces.

## Pass 6 — Responsive & Accessibility: 5/10 → 9/10

Initial 5/10: accessibility intent was unusually strong (13A multi-channel verdict
encoding, greyscale print requirement, touch targets and keyboard nav flagged), and
accessibility implementation was almost entirely absent, with one structural hole.

**Decision D11 → A: full navigable text view of the canvas.**
A keyboard-navigable alternative rendering of the same joint data the glyphs draw:
move joint by joint, each announcing its part, measured value, allowance, ratio and
verdict, plus the route-level result from the checker. ARIA landmarks across all
seven surfaces.

This is close to a rendering choice rather than a feature build, because
`evaluateJoint` already produces every fact needed — the glyph layer and the text
layer are two renderings of one dataset. It also gives sighted keyboard users a
path through the joints, which T44 wanted anyway.

**Decision D12 → B: primary device plus one real fallback per surface.**

```
 SURFACE      | PRIMARY  | DESIGNED FALLBACK | EXPLICITLY REFUSED
 -------------|----------|-------------------|--------------------------------
 Editor       | desktop  | tablet            | phone — clear message, not a
              |          |                   | cramped layout. Dragging 54mm
              |          |                   | pieces at 375px should not ship.
 Build mode   | tablet   | phone             | —
 Library      | desktop  | phone             | —
 Buy list     | phone    | desktop           | —
 Inventory    | desktop  | (per D3, left to implementation)
 Diagnostics  | desktop  | (per D3, left to implementation)
 Onboarding   | desktop  | (per D3, left to implementation)
```

An explicit refusal with a clear message is a specified state, not a gap. Design
effort concentrates on the four pairings people will actually reach for.

Re-rated 9/10. Not a 10 because the concrete values — contrast floor, focus-ring
treatment, tabular figures, the 44px touch target — are routed to D10's
consultation and do not exist yet.

## Pass 7 — Unresolved Design Decisions (unscored register)

```
 DECISION NEEDED                          | DISPOSITION
 -----------------------------------------|------------------------------------------
 Hierarchy for inventory, buy list,       | DEFERRED by choice (D3 → B). Implementer
 diagnostics, onboarding                  | picks; D7's card rule prevents the
                                          | generic-looking failure mode.
 Design tokens: typeface, colour values,  | ROUTED (D10 → A) to /design-consultation
 spacing, radius, contrast, focus rings   | with D9's brief and Pass 5's carry-list.
 Navigation across seven surfaces         | RESOLVED (D13 → B), below.
 Build-mode back-step                     | UNRESOLVED — see TODO below.
 Product name                             | BLOCKED on D20's counsel question, not on
                                          | design. Keeps litmus 1 at NO until settled.
```

**Decision D13 → B: layout-as-home, full-screen modes.**

The user is always in a layout. One small persistent control opens inventory,
library and build, each as a full-screen mode with an unambiguous way back to the
layout. No persistent sidebar and no toolbar strip.

This follows from two decisions already made rather than from taste: the canvas is
stated as dominant, and build mode's hierarchy (D3) deliberately excludes all
chrome so the next piece can fill the frame. Permanent navigation furniture
contradicts both. It also keeps the product clear of the generic-tool-chrome
failure the plan flagged as its main AI-slop risk, while still satisfying the App
UI rule that navigation must exist as an organising element.

```
            ┌──────────────────────────────┐
            │         THE LAYOUT           │  ← home; always here
            │   canvas · verdict · palette │
            └──────────────┬───────────────┘
                     one small control
          ┌────────────┬────┴─────┬────────────┐

     inventory     library     build        buy list
     full-screen   full-screen full-screen  full-screen
          └────────────┴──────────┴────────────┘
                   unambiguous way back

  Build mode is the one mode that is deliberately a dead end while active:
  nothing but the next piece, one exit. You are on a floor, not navigating.
```

## 0A. Premise Challenge

**Real problem.** A BRIO layout either closes or it does not, and you find out
on the floor after the pieces are down. The pain is a child watching a parent
take a loop apart. No existing tool answers "will this work" because every tool
models wooden track as rigid CAD, which is the wrong physics: BRIO's Vario
System is deliberate give.

**Does the plan solve the pain directly?** Yes, and the engine now proves the
mechanism: exact closure in Z[zeta16] plus a Chebyshev minimax LP over bounded
per-joint slop. That is the real answer to the real question.

**But the built product does not yet solve it,** for three reasons, in order of
severity:

1. **The verdicts are quantitatively wrong.** `axialMm: 0.8` against a published
   2-3mm. The engine fails layouts that work in the hands. A tool that is
   confidently wrong about the one question it exists to answer is worse than no
   tool, because the user has no way to know which verdicts to distrust.
2. **The editor is not the editor the brief asked for.** Click-to-append a
   single chain is not drag-and-drop, and a single chain cannot express a
   switch, which means it cannot express most real layouts.
3. **Nothing survives the tab closing.** No save, no load.

**Do-nothing cost.** The engine spine is the hard, novel, already-finished part.
Doing nothing wastes a proven mechanism behind a surface that cannot use it.

**Proxy risk found.** Four of the accepted expansions (library, publish,
moderation, inventory) serve distribution, not the pain. Distributing a tool
whose numbers are 3x wrong distributes the error.

| Persistence | - | T12 layout document. New, small |

No rebuilds proposed. Every item below either reuses the engine or adds beside it.

**Correction recorded against prior work:** `engine/src/parts/schema.ts:5` states
the schema "carries connector geometry per part". It does not; there are no
plug/nest fields. T1 specified them and the MVP omitted them while the comment
claimed otherwise. The comment is wrong, not merely incomplete.

## currentDecision (W1)

Commitment comparison:

```text
Commitment                      | Source/approval or pending        | Current                  | A                                    | B                          | C
Connector plug/nest fields      | T1 approved, never implemented    | absent                   | added                                | added                      | added
Axial budget value              | pending                           | 0.8mm guess              | (nest-plug)/2 = 1.75-2.75mm          | 0.8mm until calipers       | 1.75-2.75mm derived
Angular budget value            | pending                           | 1.5deg guess             | end-gap / gauge width, ~3deg         | 1.5deg until calipers      | ~3deg derived
calibrationStatus shown         | E5 approved the field             | estimated                | published                            | estimated                  | published
Dotted-ring UI channel          | 13A/T26 approved, shipped         | on every joint           | off (published is not estimated)     | on every joint             | off
T22 physical corpus role        | T22 approved as calibration       | fit budgets, blocking    | held-out validation, non-blocking    | fit budgets, blocking      | validation AND launch blocker
Launch claim gate               | pending                           | none stated              | none added                           | none added                 | corpus must pass before launch
Derivation + citation recorded  | pending                           | none                     | required in data file                | none                       | required in data file
Other W rows                    | pending                           | unchanged                | unchanged                            | unchanged                  | unchanged
```

Question:
D1 — W1: Where do the slop budgets come from, now that published connector geometry exists?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main, 0 commits; engine and UI built, every slop budget still an estimated guess.
ELI10: BRIO track is built loose on purpose. Each joint can be pulled apart a bit and twisted a bit, and that wiggle is exactly what lets an eight-curve circle close when the maths says the diagonals are irrational. The whole product is one sum: add up how much wiggle a layout needs, and check no single joint is asked for more than it has. Right now the engine thinks each joint gives 0.8mm, but the published figures say a real joint stretches 2 to 3mm. So the engine is failing layouts that work fine on the floor. The question is whether to compute the real number today from connector dimensions that are already published, or wait until someone measures physical track with calipers.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Pick wrong toward waiting and the tool keeps telling people their working layout will not fit, which is the single failure that destroys trust in it; pick wrong toward deriving and a mistyped published dimension becomes a confident verdict nobody will question.
Recommendation: A because the derivation independently reproduces the published 2-3mm figure from a different number, which is stronger evidence than either source alone and far stronger than the current guess.
Completeness: A=9/10, B=6/10, C=10/10
Pros / cons:
Header: Calibration
A) Derive from connector geometry now (recommended)
Add T1's missing plug/nest fields, compute axial as (nest-plug)/2 and angular from end gap over gauge width, mark both `published`, record the derivation and the woodenrailway citation beside the numbers, and keep T22 as a held-out validation set rather than a prerequisite. Effort S (human ~half day / CC ~20 min), risk low. Reuses SlopBudget, CalibrationStatus and the dotted-ring channel already shipped. Verification: the derived axial must land inside the published 2-3mm band and the derived angular inside "a few degrees", so two independent sources agree rather than one guess standing alone. Completeness 9/10. ✅ every verdict stops being roughly 3x wrong today, for arithmetic rather than calipers. ✅ takes the physical corpus off the critical path, which was this project's long pole. ❌ derived is not measured, so a wrong published dimension propagates silently into every verdict wearing a confident `published` label.
B) Keep measure-first; budgets stay estimated until calipers
Change nothing about calibration. Leave 0.8mm and 1.5deg as `estimated`, keep the dotted ring on every joint, and keep T22 as the approved mechanism that must run before any confident verdict. Effort S, zero implementation work, risk medium. Completeness 6/10: correct about provenance, silent about the fact that the current numbers are known to contradict a published source. ✅ never shows a `published` label the project has not earned, which is the honest posture the plan already committed to. ✅ no new code and no new failure surface, and the UI already downgrades confidence visibly on every joint. ❌ knowingly keeps numbers that contradict a published source for however long the corpus takes, so most FAIL verdicts in the meantime are probably false.
C) Derive now AND make the corpus a hard pre-launch gate
Everything in A, plus a recorded launch gate: no public launch until T22's held-out validation set passes against the derived budgets. Effort S implementation plus a standing launch blocker, risk low. Completeness 10/10. ✅ fixes the numbers today and still refuses to ship confidence that has never met real track. ✅ turns derived-versus-measured into a release criterion instead of a code comment. ❌ adds a launch blocker to a project with zero commits, which is governance for a problem you do not have yet.
Net: A fixes the numbers now and unblocks the long pole; B protects provenance by keeping known-wrong numbers; C is A plus a gate you cannot need yet.


## W1 resolved

Approved: option A, D1 answer of 2026-10-07. Status approved; scope exactly as
recorded in the ledger row. T22 is no longer a prerequisite for confident
verdicts; it becomes a held-out validation set. No pre-launch gate was added.

Implementation owner must prove: the derived axial budget lands inside the
published 2-3mm band and the derived angular budget inside "a few degrees", both
computed from the part record rather than hard-coded, and the dotted-ring
channel switches off only for parts whose status is actually `published`.

Question:
D6 — W6: Nudge-to-fit — defer or keep?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer, SCOPE REDUCTION; T17 is in scope, E10 was never decided.
ELI10: When a layout does not close, this feature does not just say no. It searches for the smallest change that would make it work: swap this 216mm straight for a 144mm, move that curve one position, and now it closes. It is the most magical feature in the plan and it is the hardest, because it is a discrete search over the exact lattice rather than one linear program.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Defer it and a FAIL verdict is a dead end the user has to solve themselves; keep it and the hardest algorithm in the plan gets built on top of budgets that were only corrected today.
Recommendation: A (defer) because T11, which names the joints that are binding, already turns a FAIL into actionable information, and nudge-to-fit is the expensive upgrade on top of that rather than the thing that makes failure survivable.
Note: options differ in kind, not coverage — no completeness score.
Pros / cons:
Net: T11 makes failure legible for little cost; nudge-to-fit makes it self-solving for a lot.
Header: Nudge-to-fit
A) Defer to TODOS.md (recommended)
Moves T17 to TODOS.md and keeps T11's binding-joint reporting in core as part of K1, so a failing layout still names which joints are the problem. E10 stays undecided and out of scope. Effort saved: human ~2 weeks / CC ~1 day. ✅ keeps the part that makes failure actionable at a fraction of the cost, since T11 reads the LP's own infeasibility certificate. ✅ a discrete lattice search wants correct slop budgets and a multi-cycle solver underneath it, and both land in the core first. ❌ a FAIL verdict stays a problem the user has to solve with their own hands, which is the moment the tool is least helpful and most needed.
B) Keep in scope
Build the discrete lattice search for piece substitution and slop redistribution alongside the core. Effort: human ~2 weeks / CC ~1 day. ✅ turning "this does not work" into "swap this one piece" is the single most delightful thing the engine could do with the maths it already has. ✅ the exact lattice makes the search space enumerable rather than approximate, so this is genuinely tractable here in a way it is not in float CAD. ❌ the hardest algorithm in the plan would be built against budgets corrected today and a solver that still cannot handle switches.

## Scope after reduction (all seven W rows resolved, 2026-10-07)

Every deferral was approved. Nothing was cut, nothing was deleted, and every
deferred item keeps its full context in TODOS.md.

### IN SCOPE — the minimum viable core

| Core | Capability | Backlog ids | Implementation owner must prove |
|---|---|---|---|
| K1 | Correct, cited slop budgets derived from connector geometry | W1, T1 remainder, T11, T46 | Derived axial lands inside the published 2-3mm band and derived angular inside "a few degrees"; both computed from the part record, never hard-coded; provenance and derivation recorded beside the numbers; the dotted-ring channel switches off only where status is genuinely `published` |
| K2 | Free placement, arbitrary topology, multi-cycle coupled solve, drag-and-drop | T5, T24, T39 guard, G10/G11 remainder | Cycle basis is E-V+1 per connected component with one gauge-fixed anchor each; a switch produces two cycles sharing joints and the shared joint's slop is spent once, not twice; verdicts stay independent of which edge the basis cuts |
| K3 | Levels and bridges, with the headless traversal checker | T15, T18, C1, C2 | Rise equals fall around every closed cycle; grade never exceeds maxGrade; under-bridge clearance uses the vehicle height; assembly order places elevated before span and under-bridge before bridge |
| K4 | Complete BRIO part database | T2 | Every part carries plug/nest geometry, gauge width and a calibration status; switches carry their centreline shift; the six current parts grow to the full BRIO catalogue |
| K5 | Save and load, versioned layout document | T12, T30 | A layout written by one engine version opens in the next with the fingerprint diff naming what changed; the parse boundary rejects unknown keys |
| K6 | Tests covering K1-K5 | T19, T20, C5 | Analytic fixtures stay exact; order-independence holds under rotation of the piece order; no result is ever non-finite; traversability asserted on the 8xE circle |
| K7 | Undo/redo over layout-document snapshots, riding with K2 | T27 | One entry per completed action; solver output is never snapshotted; undo restores the document and the verdict recomputes from it rather than being restored |
| K8 | Planar overlap at equal height, reported through C3's traversability axis | W9 | Overlap tolerance is stated and defended, not guessed tight; cost is bounded by a spatial grid rather than all-pairs; a placed crossing piece permits overlap at its own footprint once K4 lands; the one-level figure-eight is a test fixture that must FAIL without a crossing piece and PASS with one |

Design system remains routed to /design-consultation (G9, D10 -> A) and is not
engineering work in this review. Accessibility basics already shipped in the
four-channel joint glyphs.

### NOT in scope (deferred, with reason)

| Deferred | Ids | Reason recorded at deferral |
|---|---|---|
| Inventory, manifests, constrained palette, buy list | T28, T29, T38 | Answers "can I build this with what I own", which only matters once "will this work" is answered correctly. D2. |
| Backend, library, publish, moderation, auth, server inventory filter | T34, T35, T36, T42, T43, G3, G4, G5 | Publishing a tool whose verdicts are wrong distributes the error. K5's local save covers not losing work. D3. |
| 3D viewer, build mode, printable sheet, step persistence, build hierarchy, swipe-back | T31, T33, T41, G1, G2 | Build mode is downstream of an editor that cannot yet place a switch or a second level. Sequencing, not rejection. Reverses N2. D4. |
| Animated train playback and its completion copy | T16 anim, N6, G6 tone | The checker carries the correctness value; the animation carries the reassurance. Reverses N3 on the animation only. D5. |
| Nudge-to-fit discrete lattice search | T17 | T11 already turns FAIL into actionable information for a fraction of the cost. D6. |
| Auto-complete-the-loop | E10 | Never decided; remains undecided. |
| Fuzz harness, CI with corpus gate, feature flags, telemetry, runbooks, worker hostile-input bound | T14, T21, T23, T37, T45, T47 | Operational apparatus of a live service, for a project with zero commits and zero users. D7. |
| Physical calibration corpus | T22 | Demoted by W1 from prerequisite to held-out validation, then deferred. The engine has still never met real track. D7 and W1. |
| Community custom-part registry | T49 | Already P2/P3; depends on a calibration pipeline that is now deferred. |
| Kid mode, table bounds, photo inventory, symmetry/mirror | E7, E8, E9, E10 | Never decided; remain in TODOS.md. |

### Accepted residual risks of this reduction

1. **No automated regression gate.** The 21 tests run only when someone
   remembers. A solver regression ships silently until the next manual run.
2. **The engine has never met real track.** W1 replaced a guess with a
   derivation from two agreeing published sources, which is better evidence but
   is still not measurement. A wrong published dimension now propagates with a
   `published` label on it.
3. **Single-player only.** No library, so no compounding value from other
   people's layouts for as long as the core takes.
4. **FAIL is a dead end.** T11 names the binding joints but does not propose a fix.

Question:
D8 — W8: Undo/redo — core, or deferred? (A gap this review left, not a deliberate cut.)
Project/branch/task: brio-designer, SCOPE REDUCTION; T27 was approved in the eng review and this review's K1-K6 core does not mention it.
ELI10: There is no undo. Today that barely matters, because the only edit is adding a piece to the end and there is a "Remove last" button. K2 changes that: once you can drag any piece anywhere, the entire value of dragging is that experiments are cheap, and an experiment is only cheap if you can take it back. T27 already specified the right design, snapshotting the layout document and never the solver's output, so nothing needs designing. I simply failed to place it in either the keep list or a defer block, and I would rather you decide than have me assume.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Put it in core and K2 costs a little more; leave it out and the drag-and-drop editor ships with no way to take back a mistake, which is the thing people try first and the thing that makes them stop trusting the canvas.
Recommendation: A because undo is not a feature of a drag editor, it is a precondition for one, and T27 already settled how it should work.
Note: options differ in kind, not coverage — no completeness score.
Pros / cons:
Net: Either undo rides along with drag-and-drop, or the drag editor ships without a way back.
Header: Undo/redo
A) Put it in core, riding with K2 (recommended)
Undo/redo over layout-document snapshots, one entry per completed action, never snapshotting solver output, with the standard keyboard shortcuts. Rides with the K2 drag work because that is where the layout document starts mutating in arbitrary ways. Effort S (human ~1 day / CC ~30 min), risk low. ✅ drag-and-drop without undo punishes exactly the experimentation it exists to invite, so this protects K2's whole point. ✅ T27 already fixed the design, including the trap of snapshotting solver output instead of the document, so there is no open question to resolve. ❌ adds state management to the editor at the same moment topology and drag are both changing, which is the riskiest file in the core.
B) Defer to TODOS.md
Ship the drag editor with the existing "Remove last" button as the only recovery, and revisit undo later. Effort S, zero implementation work. ✅ keeps the K2 change as small as possible while topology and the graph walker are both in flux. ✅ a layout is cheap to rebuild at MVP sizes, and save/load in K5 gives a crude checkpoint in the meantime. ❌ "Remove last" is meaningless once pieces can be dragged anywhere, so the editor would effectively have no undo at all, not merely a weak one.

Question:
D9 — W9: Should the engine refuse a layout that passes through itself?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer, SCOPE REDUCTION; found by Section 6's hostile-QA test against accepted core K3.
ELI10: Lay out a figure-eight on the floor with ordinary curves. The two loops cross in the middle. The maths says it closes perfectly and every joint is comfortable, so the engine says it works. You cannot build it: at the crossing point two pieces of wood would have to be in the same place. BRIO sells a crossing piece exactly for this, and a layout that needs one but does not have one is impossible. The engine already does the harder three-dimensional version of this check for track passing under a bridge; it just never checks the flat case, where two pieces at the same height want the same square centimetre.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Leave it out and the product's one promise, "will this work", is false for a whole class of layouts including the first interesting shape a child draws; add it badly and the tool starts refusing layouts that are actually fine, which is a worse failure than the one being fixed.
Recommendation: A because the tool exists to answer one question truthfully, and today it answers that question wrongly for any layout that crosses itself.
Note: options differ in kind, not coverage — no completeness score.
Pros / cons:
Net: A fixes a false PASS at the cost of a possible false FAIL; B keeps the hole open and visible; C is A with a narrower trigger and more ways to be subtly wrong.
Header: Self-crossing
A) Add planar overlap to K3 (recommended)
Detect pieces whose footprints overlap at the same height and report it through the traversability axis C3 already composes, so no new verdict surface is needed. Bound the cost with a spatial grid rather than comparing every pair. Effort M (human ~3 days / CC ~1 hour), risk medium. ✅ closes a false PASS on the product's core promise, for the first shape most people try after a circle. ✅ reuses C3's existing second axis and the vertical clearance check it generalises, so it adds no new concept to the UI. ❌ footprint overlap needs a tolerance of its own, and too tight a tolerance will refuse layouts that are physically fine, turning a false PASS into a false FAIL.
B) Defer to TODOS.md
Record the hole and ship the core without it. Effort S, zero implementation work. ✅ keeps K3 to the three checks C1 actually specified, which are already unbuilt. ✅ the hole is narrow at MVP scale, since a six-part catalogue cannot easily build a crossing layout anyway. ❌ the engine knowingly returns a confident PASS for layouts that cannot be built, which is exactly the failure that makes a correctness tool worthless.
C) Check overlap only where a crossing part is absent
Same detection as A, but only flag an overlap when the layout does not already place a crossing piece there, treating a crossing part as explicit permission to overlap. Effort M (human ~3 days / CC ~1.5 hours), risk medium. ✅ models the real rule, since overlapping IS legal exactly where a crossing piece sits. ✅ avoids refusing the legitimate figure-eight that a builder solved correctly with the right part. ❌ depends on crossing-part geometry that K4 has not yet added, so it cannot be built or tested until the catalogue lands, and it has more ways to be subtly wrong than A.

### Factual correction to the reviewer

Finding G cites plan line 1733 ("Section 6: No issues found") as evidence the
figure-eight PASS was asserted and never run. That line belongs to the EARLIER
eng review's Section 6, not this review's. This review's Section 6 is where the
case surfaced. The substantive criticism, that it was asserted rather than
executed, was correct and has now been resolved by execution.

### Executed evidence for finding G

```
8xE left  (the circle)    -> ok=true closesExactly=true worstRatio=0.0000   correct
16xE left (twice round)   -> ok=true closesExactly=true worstRatio=0.0000   FALSE PASS
24xE left (3x round)      -> ok=true closesExactly=true worstRatio=0.0000   FALSE PASS
8L then 8R (retrace)      -> ok=true closesExactly=true worstRatio=0.0000   FALSE PASS
4L 4R 4L 4R               -> ok=true closesExactly=false worstRatio=30.36   correctly FAIL
```

Three physically impossible layouts render "This works. Closes exactly, no give
needed at any joint." K8's fixture set is therefore specified concretely as
these three, not as an unnamed "figure-eight". All three are caught by
non-adjacent footprint overlap, which raises K8's value above what W9 argued.

None on substance. Two narrowings are recorded above (C2's impact is
undemonstrated; the Section 6 citation was a conflation). A5, E1 and I2 are
accepted as open risks with owners rather than as established defects, because
each needs a measurement or a proof nobody has made.

Question:
D10 — W1-R: Reopen W1. The derivation does not hold. What replaces it?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer; the outside voice falsified W1's justification, and I verified every claim against the source.
ELI10: I told you the joint budgets were about 3x too tight and that I could compute the right ones from published connector dimensions. Three things were wrong with that. First, the field means one-sided give, while the published "2 to 3mm" almost certainly means total, so the real error is nearer 1.5x than 3x. Second, I said two independent sources agreed; they are the same page. Third, your own plan already contains a different plug-and-nest pair, 6.25 against 6.4mm, which by my formula gives 0.075mm instead of 1.75mm, and nobody knows which pair governs pulling a joint apart. Separately, I was going to switch off the dotted ring that marks an unmeasured number, which would have given a figure off a wiki the same visual confidence as one off a caliper. The budgets probably should still go up. What cannot stand is calling them derived and published.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Keep calling it published and the tool looks confident exactly where it is weakest, and the worst failure mode in this plan is a confident wrong yes; revert too far and you knowingly keep numbers that contradict the only published figures you have.
Recommendation: A because the one number that actually decides verdicts is the angular budget, the vaguest figure in evidence, and an afternoon with real track settles it, the datum question and the one-sided-versus-total question all at once, before any of K1-K8 is built on top of a guess.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=5/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A buys ground truth with one afternoon; B keeps building on an improved guess; C returns to a worse guess for the sake of provenance purity.
Header: Reopen W1
A) Withdraw the claim, raise the budgets provisionally, measure before building (recommended)
Set the budgets to the one-sided reading of the published figures (about 1.0-1.5mm axial, about 3 degrees angular), labelled `estimated` with the dotted ring restored, and state the convention in the field. Withdraw "derived" and "published" from the plan. Add the plank-width field, take the joint budget from the mating PAIR rather than one side, and record polarity as K4 scope. Then spend one afternoon on real track before K1-K8 work starts: build the 72mm-gap fixture `4E+D+4E+A` and see whether it seats, measure a joint's pull-apart and bend, and check whether the ascending track's 216mm is the run or the plank. Effort S plus one afternoon (human ~half day / CC ~20 min for the code). ✅ settles the one-sided question, the datum question and the angular number, which is the figure that actually dominates every verdict because the lever arms are ~300mm against ~1mm axial budgets. ✅ restores the honesty channel the UI already implements, so no number wears more confidence than it earned. ❌ blocks the core on a physical errand that needs track, floor space and an afternoon you may not have this week.
B) Withdraw the claim, raise the budgets, proceed without measuring
Everything in A except the floor test: budgets go to the one-sided reading, labelled `estimated` with the ring on, the derivation claim is withdrawn, the pair-cardinality and plank-width fixes land, and the measurement stays deferred as T22. Effort S (human ~half day / CC ~20 min). Completeness 7/10: fixes every false claim and still builds on an unvalidated oracle. ✅ unblocks K1-K8 immediately while removing every overstatement from the plan. ✅ the budget direction is still an improvement on 0.8mm, and `estimated` plus the dotted ring tells the truth about what it is. ❌ weeks of editor work land on numbers nobody has checked, and the angular budget that decides most verdicts stays the vaguest figure in the whole plan.
C) Revert the budgets to 0.8mm / 1.5 degrees until measured
Undo D1's magnitude change entirely, keep `estimated` and the ring, and treat the published figures as unconfirmed. Effort S, nearly zero work. Completeness 5/10. ✅ the strictest provenance posture: no number moves without a measurement behind it. ✅ errs conservative, so the tool says no too often rather than yes wrongly, which is the safer direction for a correctness tool. ❌ knowingly keeps numbers that contradict the only published evidence available, so the tool stays wrong in a direction you have already identified.
