Question:
D11 — W10: The solver does not deliver the optimum the plan says it delivers.
Project/branch/task: brio-designer; found by the outside voice, confirmed at `refine.ts:119-134`.
ELI10: The engine's selling point is that it does not merely find SOME way to spread the slack around, it finds the FAIREST way, so that if it says a layout fails, no arrangement would have worked. That is what the linear program's answer, t-star, means. But the code throws t-star away and instead adds up the slack it allocated across up to eight successive passes, then measures the worst joint in that total. Adding up eight separately-fair steps does not give you one fair whole. So the number shown is one workable arrangement, not the best one, and it can look worse than the true best. The error leans toward saying no when the answer is yes, which is the safer direction, but the plan claims something the code does not do.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Leave it and the product's central mathematical claim is false, which is the kind of thing that collapses credibility all at once if anyone checks; chase the true optimum and you are signing up for a research problem, because the pose-dependence makes the honest version nonlinear.
Recommendation: B because the error direction is conservative rather than permissive, the honest restatement costs nothing, and a provably optimal nonlinear solve is a different project from shipping a track designer.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=8/10, C=3/10
Pros / cons:
Net: B tells the truth cheaply and keeps the conservative bias; A chases an optimum that the pose-dependence makes genuinely hard; C ships a false claim.
Header: Minimax claim
B) Restate the guarantee to match the code, and fix the two real defects (recommended)
Say what the solver actually delivers: a feasible slop configuration and its worst-joint usage, found by sequential relinearization, which may be suboptimal and therefore errs toward reporting failure. Reopen OR-2, whose RESOLVED status rests on the optimality claim. Gate `remainingMm` so a solve that never closed the gap cannot return ok:true. Populate `suspectJoints` from the joints whose ratio equals the worst, and delete the "infeasibility certificate" story from the plan, since this LP has no upper bound on t and therefore never returns infeasible for an over-budget layout. Effort S (human ~1 day / CC ~30 min), risk low. ✅ removes a false central claim and two real defects for a fraction of A's cost. ✅ the conservative direction is the right one to have if you must have a bias, and disclosing it lets the UI say "no arrangement I could find works" rather than overclaiming. ❌ gives up the clean "provably fair" story that made the Chebyshev formulation attractive in the first place, and that story was part of why this approach was chosen.
A) Deliver a true minimax
Re-solve once over the final accumulated configuration so the reported t is the optimum of the problem actually being answered, bound the residual nonlinearity, and prove the gap. Includes B's two defect fixes. Effort L (human ~2 weeks / CC ~1 day), risk high. ✅ makes the plan's existing claim true, so OR-2 stays resolved and the fairness guarantee is real. ✅ a genuine optimum means a FAIL verdict is final, which is exactly what makes nudge-to-fit worth building later. ❌ the sensitivities are pose-dependent, so the honest problem is nonlinear and a provable optimum may not be reachable with an LP at all, which is how the first engine died.
C) Leave the code and the claim as they are
Change nothing. Effort none. Completeness 3/10. ✅ no work, and the practical behaviour is conservative rather than dangerous. ❌ the plan asserts an optimum the code discards, OR-2 is recorded resolved on that false basis, and a non-converged solve can still render "This works".

Question:
D12 — W11: Can one joint spend all of its sideways give AND all of its twist?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer; found by the outside voice, confirmed at `refine.ts:124-131`.
ELI10: Each joint has two kinds of give: it can be pulled apart a little, and it can be bent a little. The code grades a joint by whichever of the two is more used, and ignores the other. So a joint using every bit of its pull AND every bit of its bend still scores as exactly at its limit rather than over it. In real wood those two freedoms come from the same gap around the same peg, so spending both is spending the same allowance twice. Being generous here means the tool says yes to layouts that will not seat, and a wrong yes is the failure this product can least afford.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Stay with the current rule and up to half the budget can be double-spent, producing confident yeses on layouts that do not fit; go too strict and you refuse layouts that are fine, which is annoying but visible and recoverable.
Recommendation: A because it is the conservative direction on the failure that matters most, and it is the only one of the three that is still a linear program, so yalps and the whole Chebyshev formulation survive unchanged.
Completeness: A=9/10, B=4/10, C=10/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A is conservative and still an LP; B keeps a known 2x optimism; C is the most faithful model and the only one that breaks the solver.
Header: Joint budget
A) Require pull and bend together to stay within budget (recommended)
Constrain axial usage over its limit plus angular usage over its limit to at most t, per joint. With the existing u-plus/u-minus split this stays linear, so yalps, the minimax objective and the whole formulation are untouched: it is one extra row per joint. Effort S (human ~1 day / CC ~20 min), risk low. ✅ removes a known up-to-2x optimism on exactly the failure mode the plan ranks worst, a confident wrong yes. ✅ costs one linear row per joint and keeps the LP, so nothing about the solver choice has to be revisited. ❌ slightly pessimistic where the two freedoms genuinely are independent, so some buildable layouts will read TIGHT that would otherwise read PASS.
B) Keep grading by whichever is worse
Change nothing. Effort none. Completeness 4/10. ✅ no work, and no risk of newly refusing layouts that currently pass. ✅ if the two freedoms turn out to be physically independent, this is the correct model and A would be needlessly strict. ❌ a joint can double-spend one physical allowance, and W1's own reasoning, that both channels come from one clearance, is the argument that they are not independent.
C) Use a circular budget
Treat the two freedoms as orthogonal axes and require the combined magnitude within one, which is the most physically faithful of the three. Effort L (human ~1 week / CC ~3 hours), risk high. Completeness 10/10. ✅ the most accurate model of a peg moving in a round hole, which is literally the geometry. ✅ only about 1.4x strict at worst, against A's 2x, so it refuses fewer good layouts. ❌ a quadratic constraint is not a linear program, so yalps cannot solve it and the solver would have to be replaced, which is precisely the dependency D19A removed.


## Scope and sequencing after the outside voice (2026-10-07)

Twelve decisions total: W1 (superseded by W1-R), W2-W9, W1-R, W10, W11.

### Corrected core, with the prove-notes the outside voice forced

| Core | Capability | Implementation owner must prove |
|---|---|---|
| **K0** | **Commit the working tree** | 1,820 lines and 21 passing tests exist at zero commits; step one of the core is a schema migration across every part record. There is no revert target until this is done |
| **K0b** | **One afternoon of physical measurement** (W1-R) | Build `4E+D+4E+A` (the 72mm-gap fixture) and record whether it seats; measure one joint's pull-apart and bend; settle whether `lengthMm` is end-face to end-face (A5) and whether the ascending track's 216mm is the run or the plank (E1). These four answers gate every budget number |
| K1 | Correct, honestly-labelled tolerances | Budgets one-sided and stated as such; status `estimated` until K0b, with the dotted ring on; budget taken from the mating PAIR, not `placements[j-1]` (F1); plank-width field added and read (gaugeWidthMm is gauge and currently read by nothing); provenance and derivation recorded beside every number (T46) |
| K1b | Coupled joint budget (W11) | axial/axMax + angular/angMax <= t per joint, linear in the u+/u- split; a joint can no longer spend both allowances in full |
| K1c | Honest solver guarantee (W10) | Reported ratio described as a feasible configuration, not an optimum; OR-2 reopened; remainingMm gated before ok:true; suspectJoints populated from ratio == t*; the infeasibility-certificate story deleted |
| K2 | Free placement, arbitrary topology, multi-cycle solve, drag-and-drop, undo (K7) | `JointSensitivity` becomes per-(joint, cycle) with a signed traversal orientation: `residual.ts` carries ONE lever to ONE cut today (B1). `walkFloat` "swings everything downstream", which has no meaning on a graph, so a spanning-tree walk with per-cut residual re-measurement is NEW CODE, not extended rows (B2). The vertex identity rule for E-V+1 must be defined, since merging coincident nodes changes the count (B4). The cut-independence plateau (1.9e-3 at C=1) must be bounded as C grows, because the TIGHT threshold at 0.8 means a small spread can flip what the user reads (B3). Open layouts must still report overlap, grade and clearance instead of a blanket grey OPEN_CHAIN (F2) |
| K3 | Levels and bridges, traversal checker | Moving `heightMm` to `Placement` is necessary but NOT sufficient: `Pose` is `Cyc` plus an integer heading, strictly planar, so the residual needs a vertical axis before elevation means anything (D4). A ramp joint bends ~16.5 degrees in the VERTICAL plane and the slop model has no vertical channel (E2). Rise equals fall round every cycle; grade within maxGrade; clearance from vehicle height, whose scalars remain `estimated` so the composed verdict must carry the WEAKEST calibration of its inputs |
| K4 | Full BRIO catalogue, **now before K8** | Every part carries plug/nest, polarity (male/female ends: two males cannot join, which is why adapters exist), plank width, footprint outline and slop; switches carry their centreline shift. Twelve parts covering the common sets unblocks K2's and K8's tests; "extensive" is brief-literalism and 50+ hand-transcribed records gate everything behind them |
| K5 | Save/load, versioned document | Must land AFTER K3's schema work, or every saved file encodes the broken shape and the fix owes a migration; parse boundary caps bytes, piece count and nesting and rejects unknown keys (T13) |
| K6 | Tests | The three executed false-PASS fixtures below are regression tests; both async completion orders; the error boundary |
| K7 | Undo/redo | Rides with K2, per D8 |
| K8 | Planar overlap, **now after K4** | Needs footprint geometry that does not exist yet and a crossing piece to permit legal overlap, both of which are K4. Fixtures are the three executed cases, not an unnamed "figure-eight" |

### Sequencing, corrected

```
  K0 commit  ──►  K0b measure  ──►  K1 + K1b + K1c  ──►  K3 schema (height + vertical axis)
                                                              │
                                                              ▼
                                            K4 catalogue  ──►  K2 + K7  ──►  K8  ──►  K5  ──►  K6 throughout
```

Three inversions the outside voice caught in my own sequencing:
1. **K8 was scheduled before K4** while its own acceptance criterion requires K4's crossing piece. I rejected option C at D9 *because* it depended on K4, then attached C's dependency to A as a prove-note. Corrected: K8 after K4.
2. **K2's switch criterion needs K4's switch part.** Corrected: K4 before K2.
3. **K0b was absent entirely.** W1-R puts measurement first.

## currentDecision (T32 — remaining TODO proposal)

**What:** Failure replay and solver inspector. Freeze the layout at the moment of
failure, zoom to the suspect joint, and report measured-versus-budget per joint.

**Why:** Section 8 found that debuggability three weeks after a report is zero:
no logs, no persisted state. W10 now populates `suspectJoints`, so the data this
inspector needs will exist for the first time.

**Pros:** Turns "it said my loop fails and it doesn't" into something
reproducible. The hardest bugs in this engine are numerical and invisible, and
both bugs found so far (the missing angular closure row, the greedy-versus-
minimax reporting) were found by reading code rather than by observing behaviour.

**Cons:** A developer-facing tool for a product with no users and one developer.
K5's save/load already makes a failing layout transmissible, which covers most
of the practical need at a fraction of the cost.

**Context:** T32 from the eng review. It appears in no W block and no K row, so
this review left it unplaced. W10's `suspectJoints` population and K1's
per-joint usage report are its inputs and both are now core. Someone picking
this up should know the inspector is mostly a view over data that will already
exist, not new computation.

**Effort:** human M / CC S (human ~4 days / CC ~2 hours)
**Priority:** P2
**Depends on / blocked by:** W10's suspectJoints population (core), K1's
per-joint usage report (core), K5 save/load for reproducing a reported layout (core)

```text
Commitment              | Source/approval or pending | Current    | A (TODOS.md) | B (skip) | C (keep in plan)
Failure replay + zoom   | T32 approved, unplaced      | ambiguous  | TODOS.md     | rejected | core
suspectJoints data      | W10 approved                | core       | core         | core     | core
K5 layout file as repro | K5 approved                 | core       | core         | core     | core
Other rows              | resolved                    | unchanged  | unchanged    | unchanged| unchanged
```

Question:
D13 — T32: Failure replay and solver inspector — where does it go?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer; the one eng-review task this review never placed.
ELI10: A debugging view: when the engine says a layout fails, freeze it, zoom to the joint that is the problem, and show what was asked of that joint against what it had. The data behind it only started existing with today's decisions. The question is whether it belongs in the core you build now, in the backlog, or nowhere.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Put it in core and it competes with the editor for the same weeks; skip it and the next invisible numerical bug gets found by reading code again rather than by looking at behaviour.
Recommendation: A because saving a layout already makes a failure reproducible, which is the part that actually matters, and the inspector is the comfortable version of that rather than the necessary one.
Note: options differ in kind, not coverage — no completeness score.
Pros / cons:
Net: A keeps it alive and out of the way; B denies the one tool that would have caught today's bugs by observation; C spends core weeks on a developer view.
Header: Solver inspector
A) Add to TODOS.md (recommended)
Record it as P2 with the context above, depending on W10, K1 and K5, all of which are core. Effort S now, zero implementation. ✅ K5's layout file already makes a reported failure reproducible, which is the load-bearing half of debuggability. ✅ keeps the item and its dependencies written down, so it is cheap to pick up once the inputs exist. ❌ the next numerical bug will again be found by reading code, which is how both bugs so far were found and it is slow.
B) Skip — not valuable enough
Reject it outright and remove it from the backlog. Effort none. ✅ one less item in a backlog that just absorbed six deferred blocks. ❌ throws away the only proposed tool aimed at this engine's characteristic failure, which is a wrong number that looks plausible, and both bugs found so far were exactly that.
C) Keep it in the current plan as core work
Build the inspector alongside K1-K8. Effort M (human ~4 days / CC ~2 hours). ✅ the engine's bugs are invisible by nature and this is the only thing that would surface them by observation rather than by audit. ✅ its inputs are all core now, so it is a view rather than new computation. ❌ spends core weeks on a single-developer tool while the editor the brief actually asked for is still unbuilt.

## What already exists (reuse, do not rebuild)

| Existing | Reused by | Status |
|---|---|---|
| `geom/cyclotomic.ts` exact Z[zeta16] arithmetic | everything | **Reuse unchanged.** 17 analytic tests pass against it |
| `geom/pose.ts` exact forward kinematics | K2, K3 | Reuse, but **must gain a vertical axis** for K3; it is strictly planar today |
| `solver/residual.ts` exact residual + sensitivities | K2 | Reuse the exactness; **the sensitivity type must become per-(joint, cycle)** |
| `solver/lp.ts` Chebyshev minimax assembly | K2, K1b | Reuse. Multi-cycle is 3C rows with one shared t; W11 adds one row per joint |
| `solver/refine.ts` sequential relinearization | K2 | Reuse the loop; **its downstream-swing assumption does not survive a graph** |
| `solver/result.ts` discriminated union + finiteness assert | all | Reuse. Needs the remainingMm gate and suspectJoints population |
| `joints/evaluate.ts` the ONE shared predicate | all five consumers | Reuse. `VerdictLine` must stop re-implementing its thresholds |
| `geom/fk.ts` shared float walker | renderer + solver | Reuse the sharing; the walker itself becomes a spanning-tree walk |
| `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx` four-channel joint glyphs | K2 | **Reuse. This is the accessibility work this project did right** |
| `app/src/VerdictLine.tsx` grey-is-not-green | K3, C3 | Reuse the discipline; add the second axis and the weakest-calibration rule |
| `engine/test/render.test.ts` SVG arc centre proof | K2 | Reuse. Pins arc direction under the y-flip |
| 21 passing tests, clean typecheck, clean build | K6 | Reuse as the baseline K0 must commit |

Nothing is rebuilt from scratch. The two genuinely new modules are the topology
graph (K2) and the overlap detector (K8).

## Dream state delta

```
  12-MONTH IDEAL     The tool every wooden-track builder opens before buying or building.
                     Trusted because its numbers were measured, not guessed.

  AFTER THIS PLAN    An editor that matches the brief (extensive database, drag-and-drop,
                     levels, bridges) over an engine whose verdicts are honest about
                     their own confidence, validated against one afternoon of real track.
                     Single-player. No library, no 3D, no build mode, no simulator.

  DELTA REMAINING    Distribution (the whole W3 block), the physical surfaces (W4),
                     the full calibration corpus (W7), and the magic (W6).
                     All deferred, none cut, all in TODOS.md with context.
```

This review moved the project toward the ideal on the trust axis only, and
deliberately away from it on the distribution axis. That is the trade it exists
to make: the 12-month ideal needs trust first, because distributing a tool whose
verdicts are wrong distributes the error.

product whose engine was built in one session and whose surface was built in
the next: the engine's exact core is sound and almost everything around it is
unguarded. None of these is a regression; all were present and unnoticed before
this review, and three were latent false PASSes now proven by execution.

## Scope Challenge

### A. Assessment

| Sub-problem | Existing solution | Disposition |
|---|---|---|

| Single joint verdict predicate | `engine/src/joints/evaluate.ts` | Reuse; `VerdictLine` must stop duplicating its thresholds |
| Arc-direction proof under the y-flip | `engine/test/render.test.ts` | Reuse as-is |
| Cycle basis | **nothing** - but none needed: BFS plus a parent-pointer walk | Build, ~30 lines, no dependency |
| Planar overlap | **nothing** - AABB all-pairs on static geometry | Build, no dependency |
| Error boundary | React built-in (native platform feature) | Use the platform, no dependency |

**Minimum changes:** 13 existing files plus 4 new ones. Deferrable without
blocking the core: V29 (perf measurement, follows the code it measures) and
V30-V34 (all P2).

**Complexity check:** 18 files, 5 new modules as planned. Gate tripped.

**Search check** (Aside unavailable; host WebSearch used, read-only):

- **[Layer 1]** A cycle basis is the set of fundamental cycles of a spanning
  forest: dimension `E - V + CC`, one cycle per non-tree edge, each found by
  walking tree parents between that edge's endpoints. Standard, settled, and
  small enough that a graph library would cost more than it saves.
  Sources: en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycle_basis, en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanning_tree
- **[Layer 2]** Spatial hash grids are the game-development default past roughly
  a thousand entities. Source: buildnewgames.com/broad-phase-collision-detection/
- **[Layer 3]** That threshold does not transfer. Game broadphase runs ~60 times
  per second over moving bodies; this overlap test runs once per edit over static
  geometry. 500 pieces is about 125,000 AABB comparisons, once. The grid K8's
  prove-note mandates is premature.

**TODOS cross-reference:** nothing in `brio-TODOS.md` blocks this core. The six
deferred blocks all depend on it rather than the reverse. Nothing there fits this
work without expanding scope. One new TODO candidate arises from S1.

**Completeness check:** full edge and error coverage is the goal; the gaps found
below are cheap in CC time and are not proposed as shortcuts.

**Distribution check:** the only artifact is a static Vite build. No CI, no
publish channel, no update path, all deferred at W7 and recorded in NOT in scope.
Target platform is desktop browsers; the editor explicitly refuses under 620px.

## Decision ledger

### R15: Cost bound for the planar overlap check
Finding: S1, [P2], confidence 9/10, plan K8 prove-note (W9 accepted scope at D9), native reviewer.
Plan baseline: "Bound cost with a spatial grid rather than all-pairs" - approved at D9, answer "Add planar overlap to K3" (2026-10-07).
Runtime evidence: unknown. No overlap code exists yet and no layout size has been measured. `engine/src/parts/schema.ts` carries no footprint or width field, so even the AABB inputs are absent today.
Comparison grid:

```text
Commitment                     | Source/approval            | Current                  | A (measure first)                 | B (keep the grid mandate)
Overlap detection exists       | D9 approved                | approved, unbuilt        | unchanged                         | unchanged
Reported through C3 axis       | D9 approved                | approved, unbuilt        | unchanged                         | unchanged
Crossing piece permits overlap | D9 carried prove-note      | approved, unbuilt        | unchanged                         | unchanged
Cost-bounding mechanism        | D9: spatial grid           | spatial grid mandated    | AABB all-pairs + bbox reject      | spatial grid
Grid added later               | pending                    | n/a                      | only on a measured failure, TODO  | n/a, built up front
Footprint/width schema field   | K4 scope                   | absent                   | unchanged (K4)                    | unchanged (K4)
Measurement obligation         | V29 covers solver only     | overlap unmeasured       | overlap added to V29's scope      | none
K0-K8 features                 | approved                   | unchanged                | unchanged                         | unchanged
Other pending remedies         | Sections 1-4               | pending                  | pending                           | pending
```

Question D2:
D2 — R1: Should the overlap check start with a spatial grid, or earn one?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; K8's accepted prove-note mandates a spatial grid for the planar overlap check.
ELI10: The overlap check compares track pieces to see whether any two occupy the same space. The simplest version compares every piece with every other, which grows as the square of the piece count. The plan says to avoid that by sorting pieces into grid cells first. That is the standard trick in games, where collision runs sixty times a second over moving objects. Here it runs once, when you change the layout, over pieces that are sitting still. For a 500-piece layout the simple version is about 125,000 cheap comparisons, once, which is nothing.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Build the grid now and you add a second spatial index, with its own cell-size tuning and its own bugs, to solve a cost nobody has measured; skip it and a genuinely huge layout could stutter on edit until the grid arrives.
Recommendation: A because the engineering preference is to avoid premature complexity, and the grid is cheap to add behind a measurement but not free to carry.
Completeness: A=9/10, B=7/10
Pros / cons:
A) Start with AABB all-pairs, earn the grid (recommended)
Compare axis-aligned bounding boxes for every pair, with an early reject on the layout's own bounds, and add overlap timing to V29's measurement scope so a real number decides the rest. If a measured layout is slow, add the grid then, and record that as a TODO rather than losing it. Effort S (human ~1 day / CC ~30 min), risk low, maintenance low: one function, no index to keep in sync. ✅ removes a tuned spatial index from the first version of a check that has never run, while keeping the trigger for adding it explicit and measured. ✅ an AABB pass over static geometry is simple enough to verify by reading, which matters for a check whose false positives would refuse buildable layouts. ❌ a 5,000-piece layout does roughly 12.5 million comparisons per edit, and if anyone builds one before V29 runs they feel it before the measurement exists.
B) Keep the spatial grid mandate
Build the grid up front as K8's prove-note already requires. Effort M (human ~3 days / CC ~1 hour), risk medium, maintenance higher: cell size needs choosing, and the index must be rebuilt or updated as pieces move. Completeness 7/10 on this decision: it bounds cost but leaves the cell size unjustified and still has no measurement behind it. ✅ large layouts stay responsive from the first version, with no later rework on the hot path. ✅ honours the prove-note exactly as approved at D9, so no approved scope changes. ❌ adds a tuned spatial index, and a wrong cell size degrades to all-pairs anyway while costing the complexity, all to solve a cost nobody has measured.
Net: A trades a theoretical worst case for a simpler check and an explicit measurement trigger; B pays real complexity now against an unmeasured risk.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Start with AABB all-pairs, earn the grid - D2 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: planar overlap uses AABB all-pairs with an early reject on the layout bounds; no spatial index in the first version; overlap timing added to V29's measurement scope; a grid is added only on a measured failure and is recorded as a TODO so it is not lost. Supersedes the "bound cost with a spatial grid" clause of W9's accepted scope (D9); every other part of that scope, including reporting through C3's axis and letting a placed crossing piece permit overlap, is unchanged.
History: D9 (2026-10-07) approved W9 with the clause "bound cost with a spatial grid rather than all-pairs". Reopened at R15 on contradictory evidence: the grid threshold comes from per-frame game broadphase over moving bodies, while this check runs once per edit over static geometry, and no measurement existed.

## Section 1 — Architecture review

Findings, most severe first:

`[P1] (confidence: 9/10)` plan:771 - layout document version fields name
`planegcsVersion`, a dependency D19A removed; nothing records solver semantics or
the slop convention, which are what now change verdicts. See R16.

`[P1] (confidence: 9/10)` plan:2685, `engine/src/solver/result.ts:60` - a tree
layout has `E - V + CC = 0`, so the LP is vacuously optimal at `t* = 0` and
`worstRatio` is 0. V21 makes open layouts reportable, so the joint axis would
render a confident PASS for a question with no constraints in it. Prior learning
applied: nan-renders-as-pass. **Disposition: required mechanics of V21's approved
scope.** The joint axis must render NOT APPLICABLE, visually distinct from PASS
and from UNKNOWN. Task, no question.

`[P1] (confidence: 9/10)` plan:344 (9A) against plan:2685 - the approved drag
contract ("rigid forward-kinematics preview during drag, no solve; on release a
warm-started, component-scoped, Worker-hosted full solve") names a Worker that
D19A dissolved and T14 deferred, and K2's prove-note carries no performance
contract. `app/src/App.tsx:58` solves synchronously in `useMemo` on every state
change. See R17.

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` W11 accepted scope - the coupled budget changes `t`
from the max over two normalised channels to the max over their sum, so a

- `topology.ts` feeds both the pose walker and the cycle basis, and the only

### R16: What the layout document records so a verdict is never silently reinterpreted
Finding: [P1] (confidence: 9/10) plan:771 - reviewer: plan-eng-review Section 1.
Plan baseline: R8 (eng review 2026-10-06) approved recording `partDbVersion`, `planegcsVersion` and `adapterVersion`, with 11A's diff firing when any changes and naming which one.
Runtime evidence: no layout document exists; K5 is unbuilt. `@salusoft89/planegcs` is absent from `package.json` (D19A removed it), so `planegcsVersion` has no value to record. Verified: `engine/src/parts/schema.ts` has no document type at all.
Comparison grid:

```text
Commitment                      | Source/approval        | Current                 | A (semantic fingerprint)        | B (minimal rename)        | C (keep R8's shape)
partDbVersion recorded          | R8 approved            | approved, unbuilt       | unchanged                       | unchanged                 | unchanged
planegcsVersion recorded        | R8 approved            | names a removed dep     | REMOVED                         | REMOVED                   | retained, always null
adapterVersion recorded         | R8 approved            | approved, unbuilt       | unchanged                       | unchanged                 | unchanged
solverVersion recorded          | pending                | absent                  | added                           | added                     | absent
Slop convention recorded        | pending (W1-R)         | absent                  | added (one-sided vs total)      | absent                    | absent
Feasible-set shape recorded     | pending (W11)          | absent                  | added (max vs sum)              | absent                    | absent
T30 diff names what changed     | T30 approved           | approved, unbuilt       | names the semantic field        | names solverVersion only  | names a field that cannot change
Opening an older layout         | 11A approved           | approved, unbuilt       | verdict recomputed, diff shown  | diff shown, convention silent | diff never fires
K0-K8 features                  | approved               | unchanged               | unchanged                       | unchanged                 | unchanged
Other pending remedies          | Sections 2-4           | pending                 | pending                         | pending                   | pending
```

Question D3:
D3 — R16: What does a saved layout record so its verdict cannot be silently reinterpreted?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; K5 saves layouts, and the fields R8 approved name a dependency D19A removed.
ELI10: When you save a layout and open it later, the tool has to know whether the answer it gave you then still means the same thing now. The old plan said to stamp the file with three version numbers, but one of them refers to a solver that was thrown out, and none of them covers the two things that actually changed the answer today: the solver now reports a different kind of number, and the joint tolerances are about to switch from a one-sided to a possibly-total convention. So a file could reopen, show "nothing has changed", and quietly give a different verdict.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Stamp too little and an old file reopens with a silently different verdict, which is the exact failure this product cannot afford; stamp too much and the file format carries fields that need maintaining forever.
Recommendation: A because the fields that decide whether a verdict still means the same thing are the semantics, not the package versions, and file formats are expensive to change after files exist in the wild.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=3/10
Pros / cons:
A) Record the semantics, not just the packages (recommended)
Drop `planegcsVersion`. Record `partDbVersion`, `adapterVersion`, `solverVersion`, plus two semantic fields: the slop convention (one-sided or total) and the feasible-set shape (max or sum). T30's diff names whichever changed and recomputes the verdict rather than trusting the stored one. Effort S (human ~1 day / CC ~30 min), risk low, maintenance low: five scalars written once per save. ✅ the diff fires on exactly the changes that alter a verdict, which the package versions do not capture: W10 and W11 both changed meaning without touching a dependency. ✅ settles the format before any file exists, and a persisted format is the most expensive thing in this plan to change later. ❌ two of the five fields describe internal engine semantics, so the file format is coupled to engine design decisions and will need a migration note if either concept is ever restructured.
B) Minimal rename: swap planegcsVersion for solverVersion
Record `partDbVersion`, `adapterVersion` and `solverVersion`. No semantic fields. Effort S (human ~2h / CC ~10 min), risk medium. Completeness 7/10: fixes the dead field and still cannot distinguish a convention change from no change. ✅ smallest honest fix, and bumping `solverVersion` on any semantic change is a discipline rather than a schema. ✅ keeps the document format to three plain version scalars with no engine concepts in it. ❌ relies entirely on remembering to bump `solverVersion` when the convention changes, and the convention change is landing in the same week as a dozen other edits.
C) Keep R8's shape unchanged
Record all three original fields including `planegcsVersion`, which will always be null or absent. Effort none. Completeness 3/10. ✅ no approved decision is reopened and R8 stands exactly as written. ❌ stamps every file with a dead field while the diff that is supposed to protect verdicts can never fire on the changes that matter.
Net: A fixes the format before files exist; B fixes the dead field and trusts discipline; C preserves a decision that D19A already voided.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Record the semantics, not just the packages - D3 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: the layout document records `partDbVersion`, `adapterVersion`, `solverVersion`, the slop convention (one-sided or total) and the feasible-set shape (max or sum). `planegcsVersion` is removed. T30's diff names whichever field changed and the verdict is RECOMPUTED on open rather than trusting the stored one. Supersedes R8's field list (eng review 2026-10-06) only on the field set; R8's requirement that the diff fire and name what changed is retained.
History: R8 (2026-10-06) approved `partDbVersion` + `planegcsVersion` + `adapterVersion`. Reopened at R16 because D19A removed planegcs, so one field can never change, while W10's restated solver semantics and W1-R's convention change both alter verdicts with no field to record them.

### R17: Who owns the drag and solve performance contract
Finding: [P1] (confidence: 9/10) plan:344 against plan:2685 and `app/src/App.tsx:58` - reviewer: plan-eng-review Section 1 and Section 4.
Plan baseline: 9A approved "Rigid forward-kinematics preview during drag (no solve). On release: warm-started, component-scoped, Worker-hosted full solve. Layout settles once". D19A dissolved the Worker; T14's worker wall-clock bound was deferred at W7. K2's accepted prove-note states no performance contract.
Runtime evidence: `app/src/App.tsx:58` calls `solveLayout(layout)` inside `useMemo`, synchronously, on every state change, with no debounce and no revision guard. Measured scale: unknown. The solver has never run on more than 16 pieces; the probe in this session used at most 24.
Comparison grid:

```text
Commitment                     | Source/approval         | Current                    | A (rigid preview, solve on release) | B (async + revision stamping) | C (keep synchronous)
Rigid FK preview during drag   | 9A approved             | no drag exists yet         | built                               | built                         | built
Solve during drag              | 9A: none                | solves every state change  | none                                | none during drag              | every state change
Solve trigger                  | pending                 | every state change         | on release only                     | on release, off main thread   | every state change
Worker hosting                 | 9A said Worker; D19A dissolved it | none            | none, main thread on release        | none, async on main thread    | none
Stale-result guard             | pending (Section 4)     | absent, solve is sync      | not needed, solve is sync           | REQUIRED: revision stamping   | not needed
Settles once                   | 9A approved             | n/a                         | preserved                          | preserved                     | violated: squirms per edit
V29 measurement                | V29 approved            | solver only                | solve-on-release latency added      | same                          | same
K0-K8 features                 | approved                | unchanged                  | unchanged                           | unchanged                     | unchanged
Other pending remedies         | Sections 2-4            | pending                    | pending                             | pending                       | pending
```

Question D4:
D4 — R17: What triggers a solve once pieces can be dragged?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; 9A's approved drag contract names a Worker that D19A removed, and K2's prove-note has no performance contract.
ELI10: Right now the tool re-checks the whole layout every single time anything changes, which is fine with six pieces. Once you can drag a piece around, that means re-solving continuously while your finger moves, and because the solver redistributes slack across every joint, the entire layout would visibly squirm as you drag. The plan already decided the right answer, a cheap rigid preview while dragging and one real solve when you let go, but it assigned that solve to a background Worker that a later decision removed. So nobody owns it now.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Leave it solving continuously and dragging one curve makes the whole layout wobble, which looks broken and is slow; go asynchronous and you inherit the stale-result bug where an older answer paints over a newer one.
Recommendation: A because it is what 9A already approved minus the dissolved Worker, it keeps the solve synchronous so there is no ordering hazard to guard, and the measurement in V29 will say whether asynchrony is ever needed.
Completeness: A=9/10, B=10/10, C=2/10
Pros / cons:
A) Rigid preview during drag, one solve on release, on the main thread (recommended)
Drag moves pieces by exact forward kinematics with no solve, so the layout does not squirm. On release, run one solve, warm-started and scoped to the affected component. Add solve-on-release latency to V29's measurement scope. No Worker, no async, no stale-result guard needed because the solve stays synchronous. Effort M (human ~3 days / CC ~1 hour), risk low. ✅ restores 9A's approved behaviour, "layout settles once", with the dissolved Worker simply dropped rather than replaced. ✅ staying synchronous means the late-result-overwrites-newer bug cannot occur at all, so one whole class of defect needs no guard and no test. ❌ a large layout blocks the main thread for the duration of one solve on release, and nobody knows how long that is until V29 runs.
B) Rigid preview during drag, asynchronous solve with revision stamping
As A, but the on-release solve runs asynchronously, and every result carries the layout revision it was computed from so a stale result is discarded. Effort L (human ~1 week / CC ~2 hours), risk medium. Completeness 10/10: handles the large-layout case and the ordering hazard it introduces. ✅ the interface never blocks, however large the layout, which is the only option that holds up if 5,000-piece layouts are real. ✅ revision stamping is the correct guard and is independently useful once anything else becomes async. ❌ introduces the stale-result ordering hazard on purpose, before any measurement says the blocking version is too slow, and both completion orders then need testing.
C) Keep solving on every state change
Change nothing about the trigger. Effort none. Completeness 2/10. ✅ no work, and it is correct today at six pieces. ❌ violates 9A's approved "settles once" by making the whole layout redistribute slack continuously during a drag, which reads as the tool being broken.
Net: A is 9A minus the Worker and has no ordering hazard; B is strictly more capable and buys a known bug class; C contradicts an approved behaviour.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Rigid preview, one solve on release, main thread - D4 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: during a drag, pieces move by exact forward kinematics with NO solve, so the layout does not squirm. On release, exactly one solve runs, warm-started and scoped to the affected component, synchronously on the main thread. No Worker. Solve-on-release latency is added to V29's measurement scope. CONSEQUENCE: because the solve stays synchronous, the stale-result ordering hazard cannot occur, so task V25 (revision stamping) and its two-completion-order regression test are RETIRED, not deferred. Restores 9A's approved "layout settles once" with the Worker clause dropped.
History: 9A (CEO review, plan:344) approved a rigid preview plus a Worker-hosted solve on release. D19A dissolved the Worker and W7 deferred T14's worker bound, leaving the contract unowned; K2's prove-note stated none. Reopened at R17 on that gap.

## Outside Voice (eng review, 2026-10-07)

**Provider: native Claude subagent (`Plan` type), completed.** Codex preflight
returned `CODEX_MODE: not_installed`, so **outside coverage is unavailable** for
this review too. A native fallback is a finding source, never outside coverage,
and no cross-model agreement is claimed. 18 findings, every load-bearing one
verified by me against source or reproduced by execution before acceptance.

### Verified by execution (the measurements that change the plan)

Reproduced against the committed tree (1b1b939), k copies of the stadium plus an
`A2`, a 54mm gap spread over many joints - physically trivial to build:

```
n= 161    101ms  OK    worst=8.8e-2 passes=8 remain=9.4e-7
n= 241   2622ms  OK    worst=5.9e-2 passes=8 remain=1.2e-6
n= 301    470ms  OK    worst=4.7e-2 passes=8 remain=1.4e-6
n= 321   2323ms  FAIL  CYCLED  solver cycled; treated as a hard failure
n= 341   2479ms  FAIL  CYCLED  solver cycled; treated as a hard failure
n= 401    988ms  OK    worst=3.5e-2 passes=8 remain=6.9e-7
n= 641   4629ms  OK    worst=2.2e-2 passes=8 remain=2.9e-6
```

Gauge sweep over every cut point of the same physical loop:

```
8xE circle (the fixture)   n=  8  spread 0.00e+0  VERDICTS = PASS
stadium (the fixture)      n= 10  spread 0.00e+0  VERDICTS = PASS
2x stadium + A1            n= 21  spread 8.84e-2  VERDICTS = FAIL
3x stadium + A1            n= 31  spread 4.67e-2  VERDICTS = TIGHT
4x stadium + A1 + A2       n= 42  spread 6.44e-2  VERDICTS = FAIL / TIGHT   <-- flips
5x stadium + A1            n= 51  spread 1.74e-2  VERDICTS = PASS
```

**At 42 pieces the product tells the same user "Won't fit" or "Works, but tight"
depending on which piece happens to be listed first.** The spread is 34x the
1.9e-3 "plateau" documented at `refine.ts:21-29`, which is a property of the
11-piece fixture and nothing more.

### Root cause, verified in the constraint matrix

`residual.ts:69` computes `lever = [-(ey - py), ex - px]`. For the seam joint
`pose` IS `end`, so its lever is `[0, 0]`. `lp.ts:99-104` nevertheless assigns
that joint `closeTheta: +1` / `-1`. **The cut joint therefore has full angular
authority at zero positional cost: it is structurally privileged, and which
joint is the cut is precisely the gauge choice.** This is a formulation flaw in
the constraint matrix, not a convergence limit, so no number of refinement
passes can fix it. It also predicts the spread grows with one privileged joint
per cut, i.e. linearly in C - which makes B3/V20's framing ("bound the plateau
as C grows") an investigation into a known defect rather than an open question.

### R18: What happens now that the single-cycle solver is measurably broken
Finding: outside voice findings 1, 2, 2b and 3, all [P0], confidence 10/10, reproduced by execution against commit 1b1b939 - `engine/src/solver/lp.ts:99-104` with `engine/src/solver/residual.ts:69`, `engine/src/solver/refine.ts:41,89-96`, `engine/src/solver/lp.ts:41-47`.
Plan baseline: the accepted core K0-K8 (CEO review 2026-10-07) builds arbitrary topology, a multi-cycle coupled minimax solve, a vertical axis, overlap geometry, a document format and undo on top of the existing single-cycle engine. R17 (D4) approved a synchronous main-thread solve on release and rated it "risk low". Section 3 of this review carried gauge independence forward as a preserved contract needing no new decision.
Runtime evidence: MEASURED, not inferred. `CYCLED` hard failure on buildable 321- and 341-piece layouts whose worst joint uses 3-5% of its budget, reproduced three times, non-monotone in n. 4.6 seconds synchronous at 641 pieces. `MAX_PASSES=8` saturated from 161 pieces down to 21, `CONVERGED_MM=1e-7` never reached. Gauge sweep over every cut point: spread 8.8e-2 at n=21, 6.4e-2 at n=42 with the verdict FLIPPING between FAIL and TIGHT. Root cause confirmed in the constraint matrix: the seam joint's lever is `[0,0]` while `lp.ts` still gives it `closeTheta: +/-1`, so the cut joint has full angular authority at zero positional cost. A formulation flaw, not a convergence limit.

Question D5:
D5 — R18: The single-cycle solver measurably fails. What happens to K2-K8?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; the outside voice reproduced four P0 defects in the committed engine, and I verified every one.
ELI10: The plan was to build free placement, switches, levels and bridges on top of the existing solver. I ran that solver at realistic sizes for the first time. On a 321-piece layout that a child could build, it gives up and shows a red failure. At 641 pieces it freezes for four and a half seconds. And at 42 pieces it tells you "won't fit" or "works, but tight" depending purely on which piece happens to be first in the list, because the joint where the loop is cut is allowed to bend for free. That last one is a mistake in the equations, not a tuning problem, so running it longer cannot fix it. Building multi-cycle support on this means adding more cycles to a solver that does not work on one.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Build on it and every new feature inherits a verdict that flips on bookkeeping, which destroys the one thing the product sells; cut too hard and you defer drag-and-drop, levels and bridges, three of the four things you originally asked for, on the strength of a bug that is probably a week's work.
Recommendation: A because the engine repair is the identical first step under A and B, and committing now to cutting three of your four named asks would be deciding on the basis of a defect that is about to be fixed.
Completeness: A=9/10, B=10/10, C=2/10
Pros / cons:
A) Repair the engine first, then re-decide the core (recommended)
Insert a new prerequisite, K0c, before any of K1-K8: remove the privileged cut joint and prove gauge independence by sweeping EVERY cut point across four sizes and asserting both the 0.8 and 1.0 thresholds; diagnose and fix the `CYCLED` instability and sweep to ~700 pieces; resolve the pass budget either with D19A's trust region or by reporting non-convergence honestly; fix `remainingMm` to report after the final correction; stop the finiteness test skipping failures; and build the scale harness FIRST so "Scale: unknown" can never be written again with the repo in hand. K4 and K5 stay in scope. Add open-chain gap reporting, which is nearly free. R15, R16 and R17 are SUSPENDED pending re-decision, because all three were approved against a solver that does not work. Effort: engine repair M-L (human ~1-2 weeks / CC ~1 day), then the core is re-decided with measurements in hand. ✅ fixes a verdict that currently flips on an arbitrary bookkeeping choice, which is the single defect most fatal to this product's only promise. ✅ keeps every one of your four named asks alive rather than cutting three of them on the strength of a bug, and the first step is identical to B so nothing is lost by choosing it. ❌ leaves the shape of the product undecided for another round, so there is no shipping date and no commitment to a smaller scope yet.
B) Repair the engine and commit now to the chain-first product
Everything in A's repair, plus the reviewer's smaller product: keep the CHAIN model, land K4's catalogue and K5's save/load, make open-chain gap reporting ("your two ends are 37mm and 22.5 degrees apart") the headline feature, and defer free placement, switches, multi-cycle, overlap and elevation to v2. Effort: repair M-L plus catalogue and save/load, with the hard half of the plan removed. Completeness 10/10 as a decision: it resolves both the engine and the product shape. ✅ ships a correct, useful tool soonest, and the exact closure answer it would ship is the part that already works. ✅ removes every one of the hard engine problems from the critical path, including the multi-cycle solve that the measurements say is premature. ❌ defers drag-and-drop, multiple levels and bridges, which is three of the four things you asked for, and does it before the repair tells you whether multi-cycle was ever the problem.
C) Proceed with K0-K8 as planned, defects as tasks
Keep the full accepted core and record the four P0 defects as P1 tasks alongside the other 34. Effort: as planned. Completeness 2/10. ✅ no approved decision is reopened and the plan's momentum is preserved. ❌ builds arbitrary topology, a coupled multi-cycle solve, a vertical axis and overlap geometry on a solver that hard-fails at 321 pieces and flips verdicts at 42, so every new feature inherits a broken foundation and the defects get harder to isolate with each one.
Net: A and B share an identical first step; A keeps your four asks alive while B buys a shipping path by cutting three of them; C builds on a measured failure.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Repair the engine first, then re-decide the core - D5 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: a new prerequisite K0c lands before any of K1-K8. It (a) removes the privileged cut joint and proves gauge independence by sweeping EVERY cut point across at least four sizes while asserting both the 0.8 and 1.0 thresholds; (b) diagnoses and fixes the `CYCLED` instability, swept to about 700 pieces with no hard failure on a buildable layout; (c) resolves the pass budget either with D19A's trust region or by reporting non-convergence honestly; (d) fixes `remainingMm` to report after the final correction and gates it; (e) stops the finiteness test skipping failure states; (f) builds the scale harness FIRST. K4 (catalogue) and K5 (save/load) stay in scope. Open-chain gap reporting is added, reusing the gap and heading mismatch `residual.ts:48-56` already computes and `result.ts:60-66` currently discards. R15, R16 and R17 are SUSPENDED pending re-decision because all three were approved against a solver that does not work. The following are explicitly DEFERRED to that later decision: the overlap narrowphase primitive, ownership of the snap/vertex-identity epsilon, the fingerprint mechanism (enumerated fields versus one semantics hash), whether `feasible.ts` stays one module, and the K7/K5 ordering. D1's forest-sharing half stands; its `feasible.ts` half is deferred with the rest.
History: the accepted core K0-K8 (CEO review 2026-10-07) assumed the existing single-cycle engine was a sound base. Reopened at R18 on measured evidence: `CYCLED` on buildable 321- and 341-piece layouts, 4.6s synchronous at 641, pass budget saturated from 21 pieces, and the verdict flipping FAIL/TIGHT on the cut point at 42 pieces with the cause confirmed as a privileged cut joint in the constraint matrix.

## Worktree parallelization strategy

Sequential implementation, with one genuinely independent lane.

| Step | Modules touched | Depends on |
|---|---|---|
| K0c-1 scale harness | `engine/test/` | - |
| K0c-2 privileged cut joint | `engine/src/solver/` (lp, residual, refine) | K0c-1 |
| K0c-3 CYCLED + pass budget | `engine/src/solver/` (lp, refine) | K0c-2 (same files, must sequence) |
| K0c-4 test hardening | `engine/test/` | K0c-2, K0c-3 |
| K4 catalogue | `engine/data/`, `engine/src/parts/` | K0b measurement |
| K5 save/load | `engine/src/` (new doc module), `app/src/` | K4 |
| Open-chain gap | `engine/src/solver/result.ts`, `app/src/VerdictLine.tsx` | K0c-2 |

**Parallel lanes:** `Lane A: K0c-1 -> K0c-2 -> K0c-3 -> K0c-4 (all shared solver files)` /
`Lane B: K4 (independent of the solver, needs only K0b)`.
**Execution order:** launch A and B together; merge both; then K5 and the
open-chain gap, which depend on A and B respectively.
**Conflict flags:** K0c-2 and K0c-3 both edit `lp.ts` and `refine.ts` and must
not run in parallel. The open-chain gap touches `result.ts`, which K0c-3 may
also touch; sequence it after A completes.

## Unresolved decisions that may bite you later

1. **R15 overlap cost** - suspended by R18. The AABB-versus-grid answer stands only if the narrowphase question below is settled first.
2. **R16 layout fingerprint** - suspended by R18. Enumerated fields were already incomplete at birth (missing the overlap tolerance and the snap epsilon); one semantics hash is the likely replacement. **Blocks H9.**
3. **R17 solve trigger** - suspended by R18. Its "warm-started and component-scoped" has no implementation path, and it specifies no verdict state during a drag.
4. **The overlap narrowphase primitive** - never decided. AABB alone cannot answer K8's own acceptance criterion.
5. **Ownership of the snap / vertex-identity epsilon** - never decided, and it is the most verdict-determining constant in K2.
6. **Whether `feasible.ts` stays one module** - D1's forest half stands; this half is deferred on the schedule argument.
7. **K7 and K5 ordering** - undo consumes the document type, so K5's type must precede K7 even though K5's file I/O need not.
8. **E1, the ascending-track datum** - if 216mm is the plank and not the run, the run is 206.34mm, irrational, and the exact lattice does not survive K3. No branch of this plan covers that answer. K0b settles it.

### What this review actually changed

It began as a routine architecture pass and ended by reopening the engine. Five
decisions were asked and answered. Three of them (R15, R16, R17) were then
**suspended by the fifth**, because the Outside Voice measured the committed
solver and found it returns a hard `CYCLED` failure on buildable 321-piece
layouts, blocks for 4.6 seconds at 641, saturates its refinement budget from 21
pieces, and **flips a user-visible verdict between FAIL and TIGHT on the
arbitrary cut point at 42 pieces**. I reproduced every one of those numbers.

The cause is in the constraint matrix, not in convergence: the seam joint gets
`closeTheta: +/-1` with a `[0,0]` lever, so the cut joint bends for free. No
amount of refinement fixes that.

Three errors of mine were caught and corrected: Section 3 carried gauge
independence forward as a *preserved contract* when it was already false; the
performance finding about `mulZetaPow` named a hot path `refine.ts` never
imports; and "early reject on the layout bounds" was vacuous as written. A
fourth, the task-artifact paths D1 invalidated, is task H12.

Mode: SCOPE REDUCTION; approved decisions: none at mode selection.
Mode provenance: actual question answer (four-mode menu), QUESTION_TUNING false,
auto_decided false. Recommended under 0E step 2 rule 1: counting the suspended
K2/K3/K7/K8 back in exceeds fifteen changed files, and their disposition is the
question this review exists to answer.

no remote). Report file: this plan.

## Landscape correction (the plan's premise was too strong)

The original CEO review's landscape asserted that no existing tool answers "will
this layout work". Two things found today contradict or narrow that:

- **TrackGenius** (trackgenius.app) generates "diverse, valid" BRIO-compatible
  layouts from your piece inventory, 20+ ideas at a time. It occupies the
  inventory-aware generation space that W2 deferred.
- **mattsouth/woodentrack** (github.com) is open-source wooden-track prior art
  that nobody on this project has looked at.

Three-layer synthesis:
- **[Layer 1]** The established approaches are draw-it-yourself (SketchUp,
  woodenrailway's guides) and pre-made layout books. Neither models joint tolerance.
- **[Layer 2]** TrackGenius does generation-from-inventory and calls the output
  valid; TrackPlanner.app serves model railways; woodentrack exists as code.
- **[Layer 3]** **Generation by construction dodges the hard question.** A
  generator that only emits layouts which close exactly never needs a tolerance
  model, and correspondingly cannot tell a user whether THEIR design works or
  which joint is at fault. That validation niche still appears unoccupied, but it
  is narrower than claimed. The sting: if the chain-first product's headline
  becomes "your ends are 37mm apart", that is a weaker version of what a
  generator already approximates by only producing layouts that close.

## 0A. Premise challenge

The problem is unchanged. What changed is the evidence. The engine's exact half
works and is genuinely novel; the tolerance half, which is what makes this a
product rather than a geometry demo, measurably does not: it flips verdicts on
piece order at 42 pieces and hard-fails at 321. The plan solves the real pain
only if the tolerance half is repaired, so the repair is not a scope question.

Do-nothing cost is higher than this morning: two commits exist and the next
planned step was to build on the broken half.

## 0B. Existing code leverage

| Sub-problem | Reuse | Rebuild |
|---|---|---|
| Exact arithmetic and FK | `cyclotomic.ts`, `pose.ts` | none |
| Joint verdict | `evaluate.ts` | none |
| Arc direction under the y-flip | `render.test.ts` | none |
| Open-chain gap | `residual.ts:48-56` already computes it | only the surfacing; `result.ts:60-66` discards it |
| Closure constraint rows | - | `lp.ts`: the defect is in the matrix |
| Refinement loop | - | `refine.ts`: pass budget and stale `remainingMm` |
| Wooden-track geometry generally | **unexamined: mattsouth/woodentrack** | - |

## 0C. Dream state mapping

```
  CURRENT STATE                    THIS REVIEW DECIDES          12-MONTH IDEAL
  Exact half works.          --->  what gets built AFTER   --->  The tool builders
  Tolerance half flips             the repair: full editor        open before they
  verdicts, hard-fails.            or a correct chain tool.       buy or build.
  2 commits, 0 users.              The repair happens either way.
```

Question:
D4 — X1a: What track topologies should v1 support?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; K2 was suspended by R18, and X2's layer decision needs a track under a bridge.
ELI10: Today a layout is one connected run of track, optionally joined into a loop. Your bridge example needs something underneath the bridge, which is either the same loop crossing over itself, or a second separate loop. Supporting several separate loops turns out to be cheap: because no two separate loops share a joint, the maths stays exactly what the engine already does, just run once per loop. What is expensive is switches, where the track branches and one joint belongs to two loops at once, so all the loops have to be solved together. That coupled solve is the thing the measurements say to be careful about.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Pick one chain and your bridge only works as a loop crossing over itself, never as a separate track underneath; pick full switches and you commit to the coupled solve on an engine whose single-loop repair has not been proven yet.
Recommendation: B because it gives your layer requirement the separate-track-under-a-bridge case for almost no solver work, while leaving the coupled multi-cycle solve, which is the measured risk, out of v1.
Completeness: A=5/10, B=8/10, C=10/10
Pros / cons:
Net: B buys the topology your layer decision actually needs without buying the coupled solve that the measurements warn about.
Header: Topology
A) One chain, as today
Keep a layout as a single connected run, optionally closed. A bridge is expressible only as one loop crossing over itself. Effort S, zero implementation work. Completeness 5/10: cannot express the separate track under a bridge your layer example describes. ✅ smallest possible v1, and every existing test and fixture applies unchanged. ✅ leaves the solver surface identical to the one K0c repairs, so the repair is proven against exactly what ships. ❌ your own bridge example reads as a separate track underneath, and this cannot represent that, so the layer feature lands half-expressed.
B) Multiple independent components, no switches (recommended)
A layout becomes a list of chains, each optionally closed, with no joint shared between them. Because nothing is shared, the constraint system is block-diagonal and each component solves independently with the existing single-cycle machinery, run once per component. The walker iterates components; the cycle count is one per closed component. Switches stay out. Effort S-M (human ~3 days / CC ~45 min), risk low. ✅ expresses the separate-track-under-a-bridge case your layer decision needs, which option A cannot. ✅ needs no coupled solve, no per-(joint,cycle) sensitivities and no spanning forest, so it inherits K0c's repair directly rather than extending it. ❌ implementation owner must prove the block-diagonal claim: that no construction without switches can share a joint across components, and that a shared endpoint is either one component or rejected.
C) Full switches and the coupled multi-cycle solve
All of K2's topology: arbitrary graphs, switches, joints on several cycles, per-(joint,cycle) sensitivities and a spanning-forest walker. Effort L (human ~3-4 weeks / CC ~4 hours), risk high. Completeness 10/10 on topology. ✅ switches are what real large layouts are made of, and the exact lattice makes them genuinely tractable here. ✅ one formulation pass instead of two if it is going to happen eventually anyway. ❌ commits the coupled solve to an engine that currently hard-fails on one cycle at 321 pieces and flips verdicts at 42, which is the specific mistake R18 was raised to avoid.

### X1a resolved

**Approved: option B.** D4 answer of 2026-10-07. A layout is a list of chains,
each optionally closed, with no joint shared between components.

Why this is cheap: with no switches, no joint can belong to two components, so
the constraint system is **block-diagonal**. Each component carries its own
closure rows and its own slop variables, and nothing couples them. The solve is
the existing single-cycle machinery run once per component. No coupled
multi-cycle solve, no per-(joint, cycle) sensitivities, no spanning forest.

Implementation owner must prove:
- The block-diagonal claim: that no construction reachable without switches can
  share a joint across components.
- The disposition of a shared endpoint: two components whose ends coincide are
  either merged into one component or rejected, and which one is a decision.
- That `walkFloat`, `computeResidual` and `bounds` iterate components rather than
  assuming one chain.

**Consequence for X1b:** append-only editing now needs a way to START a new
component, since a layout can hold several. That is a small addition either way
and does not change X1b's shape.
