Question:
D5 — X1b: Append-only editing, or drag-and-drop?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; "a graphical editor that allows the user to drag and drop" was a named ask in the original brief.
ELI10: Right now you build a layout by clicking pieces to add them to the end, and the only way back is removing the last one. Drag-and-drop means picking a piece up and putting it where you want, including moving pieces already placed. It was one of the four things you originally asked for. It is separable from the topology question: dragging can build a simple chain, and clicking can build several separate loops. It does drag three other things along with it: undo becomes necessary, dropped pieces need an overlap guard, and keyboard equivalents are needed so the editor is not mouse-only.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Stay append-only and the editor is not the one you asked for, and repositioning a mistake means deleting back to it; add drag and v1 carries the interaction work, the undo it requires and the accessibility obligation that comes with it.
Recommendation: A because every other item in v1 is correctness work on a measured defect, and drag pulls in undo, an overlap guard and keyboard parity with it, which is a second project rather than a feature.
Completeness: A=4/10, B=9/10
Pros / cons:
Net: Drag is a named ask and a coherent chunk of work; append-only keeps v1 entirely about making the verdict true.
A) Keep append-only for v1 (recommended)
Click a palette piece to append; remove the last. No repositioning. Drag-and-drop, its overlap guard, its undo requirement and its keyboard parity all move to TODOS.md together, since they are one body of work. Effort S, zero implementation work. Completeness 4/10 as an editor. ✅ keeps every hour of v1 on the thing that is measurably broken, which is the verdict rather than the interaction. ✅ avoids pulling undo, the drop-overlap guard and keyboard parity into v1 as mandatory companions. ❌ does not deliver drag-and-drop, which you named in the original brief, and building anything beyond a few dozen pieces by appending is genuinely tedious.
B) Add drag-and-drop to v1
Pieces drag from the palette and reposition after placement, with a rigid preview during the drag and one solve on release. Brings undo (X3), the drop-overlap guard and keyboard equivalents with it as requirements. Effort L (human ~2 weeks / CC ~2 hours), risk medium. Completeness 9/10. ✅ delivers a named brief ask and makes layouts of real size actually buildable in the tool. ✅ the rigid-preview-then-settle behaviour is already designed (9A) and the arrangement questions are already analysed. ❌ it is the largest remaining app-side work item and it makes undo, overlap-on-drop and keyboard parity mandatory rather than optional, so v1 grows by four things rather than one.
Header: Input method

### X1b resolved

**Approved: option A, append-only for v1.** D5 answer of 2026-10-07.

Accepted scope: click a palette piece to append it; remove the last piece; and a
new action to start another component, which X1a's multi-component decision
requires. No repositioning of placed pieces.

Deferred together, because they are one body of work rather than four features:
drag-and-drop, the drop-overlap guard (T39), undo/redo (X3's subject) and
keyboard parity with ARIA (G10/G11).

**Accepted shortcut, recorded.** This option is Completeness 4/10 as an editor
and is a durable scope cut, so it is logged as decision `cebd0e6d-567b-4811-9f0d-62c6f4d10b87`
with its ceiling and upgrade trigger. **Upgrade trigger:** the first time a layout
exceeds roughly 40 pieces in practice, or any edit richer than append is needed
(repositioning or rotating a placed piece), the whole deferred body lands
together. When the append-only editor is next touched in code, the cut corner
carries the marker `gstack-shortcut(dec-cebd0e6d-567b-4811-9f0d-62c6f4d10b87): append-only editor, no repositioning; upgrade when a layout needs an edit richer than append`.

**This does not deliver the original brief's drag-and-drop ask.** Recorded
plainly so the gap is visible rather than implied.

## currentDecision (X2)

**Disposition settled by user instruction (2026-10-07):** multi-layer tracks are
IN scope, capped at three layers (L0, L1, L2), with independent routing on each
layer and explicit transitions between them, modelled on PCB layouts. The
defer-or-keep question is therefore withdrawn. What remains open is the model.

### The PCB mapping, as the design vocabulary

| PCB concept | This product |
|---|---|
| Layer | Track level 0, 1, 2 (capped by instruction) |
| Stackup | The physical height of each layer, continuous and measured |
| Via | A ramp piece. BRIO ramps rise one step, so only ADJACENT layers connect; there are no blind vias |
| Short (same-layer overlap) | Two pieces on one layer in one place: must FAIL |
| Clearance (inter-layer) | A bridge over track: legal when vertical clearance clears the vehicle |
| Design Rule Check | The verdict engine itself |

Two consequences worth recording:
1. **Rise equals fall becomes exact integer arithmetic.** Layer transitions
   around a closed cycle must sum to zero: an integer check in the same spirit
   as the Z[zeta16] closure test, not a float height comparison.
2. **The user's bridge example is expressible as a CHAIN.** A loop that ramps up,
   crosses over itself and comes back down is a single self-crossing chain with
   layer transitions. So this requirement does not depend on X1's free placement,
   and it makes X4's overlap check strictly more necessary: same-layer overlap is
   a short, cross-layer overlap is the feature.

Commitment comparison:

```text
Commitment                     | Source/approval            | Current                  | A (layer index + stackup)        | B (continuous height only)     | C (integer levels only)
Multi-layer tracks             | USER INSTRUCTION           | suspended                | in scope                         | in scope                       | in scope
Layer cap of 3                 | USER INSTRUCTION           | none                     | enforced: 0, 1, 2                | not expressible as a cap       | enforced: 0, 1, 2
12A continuous heightMm        | 12A approved               | continuous, no index     | PRESERVED as per-layer stackup   | PRESERVED unchanged            | REVERSED
Topology vs physics separated  | pending                    | conflated                | separated                        | conflated (height is both)     | conflated (index is both)
Same-layer overlap = FAIL      | X4, pending                | unchecked                | decidable from the layer index   | needs a height-equality epsilon| decidable from the index
Cross-layer overlap legal      | USER INSTRUCTION           | unchecked                | legal if clearance holds         | legal if clearance holds       | legal, clearance unmodellable |
Rise equals fall               | C1 approved                | not built                | EXACT integer sum over a cycle   | float comparison with epsilon  | exact integer sum
Book / printed riser heights   | 12A rationale              | representable            | representable in the stackup     | representable                  | NOT representable
Vertical slop channel          | E2, open risk              | absent                   | still required                   | still required                 | still required
E1 ascending-track datum       | open risk, K0b             | unverified               | still measured at K0b            | still measured at K0b          | still measured at K0b
Other X rows                   | pending                    | suspended                | pending                          | pending                        | pending
```

Question:
D3 — X2: How should layers be modelled, now that they are in scope?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; the user added capped 3-layer PCB-style routing, which conflicts with accepted decision 12A.
ELI10: You want three track levels with their own routing and explicit ramps between them, like copper layers on a circuit board. The engine currently has a single continuous height number per piece, and an earlier decision deliberately refused to use integer level numbers, because a BRIO ramp rises 64mm but a track propped on a book rises whatever the book is. Both views are useful for different reasons: the level NUMBER is what decides whether two tracks are allowed to cross, and the actual HEIGHT is what decides whether a train fits underneath. The question is whether to carry both, or collapse to one.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Collapse to height alone and "are these on the same layer" becomes a floating-point comparison with an epsilon, which is exactly the class of bug that already flips verdicts in this engine; collapse to integers alone and a track on a book or a printed riser cannot be represented at all, reversing a decision made for good reasons.
Recommendation: A because the two questions are genuinely different (crossing legality is topological, clearance is physical), and separating them turns rise-equals-fall into exact integer arithmetic instead of another epsilon comparison.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10, C=4/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A carries both concepts because they answer different questions; B makes layer identity a float comparison; C cannot represent a book.
Header: Layer model
A) Discrete layer index plus a per-layer height stackup (recommended)
Every placement carries a layer index of 0, 1 or 2, enforced by the cap. Separately, the layout carries a stackup giving each layer's physical height, continuous and measured. Crossing legality and rise-equals-fall use the index, so both are exact integer questions. Clearance uses the stackup. 12A's reasoning is preserved: a book riser and a BRIO ramp can both be layer 1 at different heights. Effort M (human ~1 week / CC ~1 hour), risk low. ✅ makes "same layer" an integer identity rather than a float comparison, which removes an epsilon from the one part of the engine that already flips verdicts on small spreads. ✅ turns rise-equals-fall into an exact integer sum over a cycle, in the same spirit as the closure test, and keeps 12A's book-and-riser case representable. ❌ carries two related concepts that must stay consistent, so a placement whose layer index disagrees with its stackup height is a new invalid state needing its own check.
B) Continuous height only, as 12A specified
Keep a single continuous height per placement and derive everything from it. Layers become a presentation concept. Effort S (human ~3 days / CC ~30 min), risk medium. Completeness 6/10: expresses the geometry but not the cap or the topology cleanly. ✅ no change to an accepted decision, and one source of truth for vertical position. ✅ arbitrary riser heights work naturally, including ones between layers. ❌ "are these two pieces on the same layer" becomes a float comparison with a tolerance, and that tolerance then silently decides whether an overlap is a short or a legal bridge, which is the epsilon-decides-the-verdict pattern this engine is already suffering from.
C) Integer levels only, reversing 12A
Replace continuous height with the layer index alone, with a fixed height per layer. Effort S (human ~2 days / CC ~20 min), risk medium. Completeness 4/10. ✅ simplest possible model, and the cap falls out for free. ✅ every vertical question becomes integer arithmetic with no epsilon anywhere. ❌ reverses 12A and makes a track on a book, a printed riser, or another brand's ramp unrepresentable, which was the exact scenario 12A was written to protect.

### X2 resolved

**Approved: option A, simplified.** D3 answer of 2026-10-07, with the user's
instruction "keep it simple and allow one uniform height, determined by the Brio
Ramp Up piece".

Accepted scope:
- Every placement carries a **discrete layer index** in {0, 1, 2}. The cap is enforced.
- The layout carries a **single scalar step height**, sourced from the BRIO
  ascending track's rise (recorded as 64mm at `brio.ts:11`, unmeasured).
- Layer height = index x step. So L0 = 0, L1 = 64, L2 = 128 at the recorded rise.
- **Crossing legality and rise-equals-fall are exact integer questions** on the
  index: layer transitions around a closed cycle must sum to zero. No epsilon.
- **Clearance** uses the step height, the track plank thickness and the vehicle height.
- Same-layer overlap is a short and must FAIL. Cross-layer overlap is legal when
  clearance holds. This is X4's subject and is strengthened by this decision.
- A ramp connects **adjacent layers only**; L0 to L2 needs two ramps.

**Relationship to 12A, stated plainly.** 12A made `heightMm` continuous and
rejected an integer level index, specifically so that a track on a book, a
printed riser or another brand's ramp stayed representable. A single uniform
step **gives up that case for v1**: a 40mm book is not layer 1. This is a
deliberate simplification chosen by the user, not an oversight. The structure
chosen keeps the upgrade cheap: because the index and the stackup are separate,
a later per-layer stackup needs no change to any placement. **Upgrade trigger:**
the first time a layout needs a riser height that is not a whole number of ramp
steps, the scalar step becomes a per-layer table.

### Consequence found while recording this: the clearance margin is 8mm

```
  layer step (ramp rise)        64.0 mm   source: brio.ts:11, UNMEASURED
  track plank thickness         12.0 mm   source: woodenrailway, NOT IN THE SCHEMA
  air under a layer-1 bridge    52.0 mm
  vehicle height                44.0 mm   source: VEHICLE.heightMm, ESTIMATED
  clearance margin               8.0 mm   <-- decided by three unmeasured numbers
  two layers of separation     116.0 mm air, margin 72.0 mm
```

Two findings:
1. **Track plank thickness is absent from the schema.** `grep` across
   `engine/` finds no thickness field and no 12mm constant. It is now
   load-bearing for every clearance verdict. New task.
2. **The commonest bridge case passes or fails on 8mm**, decided by the ramp
   rise, the plank thickness and the vehicle height. One is estimated, one comes
   from a web page, and one is not in the data model. Two layers of separation is
   comfortable. This makes K0b's measurement load-bearing for layers, not only
   for closure, and the measurement sheet does not currently ask for plank
   thickness or vehicle height.

**Also resolved by this decision:** E1's open risk is narrowed. The ascending
track's 216mm datum question concerns the HORIZONTAL run; the RISE of 64mm is a
separate number and is what the layer model depends on. So a bad E1 answer
threatens lattice exactness for ramps but does not threaten the layer model.

## currentDecision (X3) — resolved without a separate question

**No new answer was needed.** D5 (X1b) already disposed of this row. Its accepted
scope reads: "Deferred together, because they are one body of work rather than
four features: drag-and-drop, the drop-overlap guard (T39), undo/redo (X3's
subject) and keyboard parity with ARIA (G10/G11)."

Disposition: **deferred to v2**, returning with drag-and-drop. Citing D5 rather
than asking again, because asking would re-litigate a choice the user already
made in the same session.

Why it holds on its own merits: with append-only editing the shipped remove-last
button is the undo for the only edit that exists, and the eng review's finding 12
showed undo's real dependency is K5's document type rather than file I/O, so
deferring it alongside the document decision avoids snapshotting a format that is
still unsettled.

T27's design is recorded and not reopened, including the trap it identified of
snapshotting solver output instead of the layout document.

Question:
D5 — X4: Planar overlap — keep in v1, or defer to v2?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; K8 was suspended by R18, but the bug it fixes is in CHAIN layouts, which v1 ships.
ELI10: This checks whether two pieces of track try to occupy the same patch of floor. I assumed it was only needed once you could place pieces freely, so it got suspended with the rest. That was wrong, and I can show it: I ran three ordinary CHAIN layouts today and all three returned "This works. Closes exactly, no give needed at any joint." Sixteen large curves, which goes round the same circle twice. Twenty-four, which goes round three times. And eight left curves followed by eight right curves, which retraces the loop backwards on top of itself. None can be built. All three are reachable with the append-only editor v1 ships.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Defer it and v1 ships a tool that confidently blesses layouts nobody can build, using the exact phrase "This works"; keep it and v1 carries the one piece of computational geometry in the plan, including a narrowphase test that is not yet designed.
Recommendation: A because the false PASS is in the chain model v1 actually ships, proven by execution, and a correctness tool that says "This works" about an impossible layout has no remaining value proposition.
Note: options differ in kind, not coverage — no completeness score.
Pros / cons:
Net: This is the one suspended item whose bug is already live in v1's own editor, so deferring it ships a known false PASS.
A) Keep in v1, after the repair and after K4 (recommended)
Overlap detection returns to accepted scope, scheduled after K0c and after K4 (which supplies the footprint geometry it needs). The three executed layouts become regression fixtures that MUST fail. The narrowphase primitive is still undecided and is carried as an open choice: AABB is a reject filter only, and an exact arc-and-segment test is the likely answer. Effort: human ~1 week / CC ~1 hour including the narrowphase. ✅ closes a false PASS that I reproduced today in the exact editor v1 ships, where the tool says "This works. Closes exactly" about a layout that needs two pieces in one place. ✅ the fixtures are already written down as concrete layouts, so the acceptance criterion is unambiguous rather than a judgement call. ❌ brings the plan's only computational geometry into v1, and its narrowphase is undesigned, so this is the item most likely to be underestimated.
B) Defer to TODOS.md for v2
Moves overlap to TODOS.md with the three executed fixtures recorded, and v1 ships without a self-intersection check. Effort saved: human ~1 week / CC ~1 hour. ✅ keeps v1 to the repair plus catalogue plus save/load, with no new geometry subsystem at all. ✅ the three layouts are degenerate cases that a user building deliberately is unlikely to produce by accident. ❌ v1 would knowingly print "This works. Closes exactly, no give needed at any joint." for a layout that traverses the same circle twice, which is the precise failure a correctness tool cannot survive.
Header: Overlap check
### X4 resolved

**Approved: option A, keep in v1.** D6 answer of 2026-10-07. Scheduled after K0c
and after K4.

Rules, now sharpened by X2's layer decision:
- **Same layer, same place = a short.** Must FAIL. Decidable from the integer
  layer index, with no epsilon.
- **Different layers, same place = a bridge.** Legal when clearance holds. This
  is the mechanism that makes the layer feature mean anything: without it,
  cross-layer bridging is indistinguishable from same-layer collision.
- A placed crossing piece permits same-layer overlap at its own footprint, once
  K4 supplies that part.

Regression fixtures, reproduced by execution on 2026-10-07 and currently all
returning "This works. Closes exactly, no give needed at any joint.":

```
  16xE left   closed, 720 deg, residual exactly 0   MUST FAIL (traverses the circle twice)
  24xE left   closed, 1080 deg, residual exactly 0  MUST FAIL (three times)
  8L then 8R  closed, net 0 turning, residual 0     MUST FAIL (retraces itself)
  8xE left    the genuine circle                    MUST PASS
```

**Still open and carried as unresolved:** the narrowphase primitive. R15 chose
AABB all-pairs over a spatial grid and that cost reasoning stands, but AABB is a
reject filter, not a decision primitive: a 45-degree curve's bounding box is
1.46x its true footprint at best orientation and 3.8x at 45 degrees, and BRIO
headings are multiples of 22.5 degrees, so the inflated cases are the common
ones. An exact arc-and-segment test is the likely answer. This is not decided.

Previously deferred and unchanged: inventory, the backend, 3D and build mode,
the train animation, nudge-to-fit, launch ops and the full physical corpus.

## Section 1 — Architecture review (X-series scope)

Findings, most severe first:

`[P1] (confidence: 9/10)` C1 "rise equals fall" against X1a's accepted scope -
**the invariant applies only to CLOSED components.** An open component may
legitimately start at L0 and end at L2: a dead-end ramp to a plateau is a real
layout. C1 was written when a layout was one closed loop. Correct statement:
layer transitions sum to zero around each closed cycle, and are unconstrained on
open components. **Disposition: required mechanics of X1a plus X2. Task, no
question.**

`[P1] (confidence: 9/10)` X1a accepted scope with `result.ts:60` - **no
aggregation rule exists for the verdict across components, and the modal layout
is now mixed.** An open component has no closure rows, so its joint axis is not
applicable; a closed one yields a real ratio. What the single headline says when
one component passes and another is open is undecided. See Z1.

`[P2] (confidence: 8/10)` X2 accepted scope - **the uniform step is a single
point of failure for the whole layer feature.** Clearance is
`step - plank >= vehicle`, and the step is global, so one scalar decides whether
every bridge in every layout is legal. At the recorded numbers the margin is 8mm;
a vehicle measured at 50mm rather than 44mm makes every layer-1 bridge illegal,
and under the 3-layer cap bridging would only work L0 to L2. K0b decides whether
the headline feature works at all. **Disposition: open risk with owner; K0b must
measure the ramp rise, the plank thickness and the vehicle height.**

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` X2 accepted scope, `engine/src/parts/schema.ts` -
**the ramp is the only via and it is reversible, so the layer delta depends on
traversal direction.** BRIO's ascending track is one part used both ways. On an
undirected chain, which end is up belongs to the placement, not the part, so the
schema needs an explicit per-placement direction or explicit from/to layers.
Without it the same placement means both +1 and -1. **Disposition: required
mechanics of X2. Task, no question.**

`[P2] (confidence: 8/10)` X2's own recorded con - **the layer index and the
stackup can disagree, a new invalid state.** Cheap guard: derive height from the
index and never store a height on a placement. **Disposition: task.**

Scaling: k components give k independent solves, so the measured per-solve cost
multiplies by k rather than compounding. Overlap is all-pairs across components
and is the one whole-layout cost. Single points of failure: the step scalar, and
the module that owns component iteration. Rollback: `git revert` works now that
two commits exist.

### Z1: What the verdict says when components disagree
Finding: Section 1, [P1], confidence 9/10, X1a accepted scope with `engine/src/solver/result.ts:60` - reviewer: plan-ceo-review Section 1.
Plan baseline: C3 (CEO re-review) approved "the verdict line reports the worse of two independent axes (joint tolerance, route traversability)". It assumed ONE component. The eng review's V21 approved that open layouts report overlap, grade and clearance instead of a blanket grey, also for one layout.
Runtime evidence: `result.ts:60` returns `OPEN_CHAIN` for the whole layout today and there is no component concept in the code at all. With X1a, a layout is a list of chains and nothing aggregates their verdicts.
Comparison grid:

```text
Commitment                      | Source/approval       | Current            | A (worst, named)             | B (per-component list)        | C (worst, unnamed)
Single headline verdict         | C3 approved           | one layout, one line| kept, names the component    | replaced by a list            | kept, anonymous
Open component joint axis       | V21, eng review       | blanket OPEN_CHAIN | NOT APPLICABLE, not PASS     | NOT APPLICABLE per component  | NOT APPLICABLE
Closed component joint axis     | C3 approved           | one ratio          | real ratio, worst wins       | real ratio each               | real ratio, worst wins
Which component is at fault     | pending               | n/a                | NAMED in the headline        | visible in the list           | NOT shown
Whole-layout feasibility        | X4 approved           | n/a                | reported separately          | reported separately           | reported separately
Scales to many components       | pending               | n/a                | one line, always             | list grows with k             | one line, always
K0c, K4, K5, X1a-X4             | approved              | unchanged          | unchanged                    | unchanged                     | unchanged
```

Question D7:
D7 — Z1: With several separate tracks in one layout, what does the single verdict line say?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; X1a made a layout a list of independent tracks, and the verdict line was designed for one.
ELI10: You can now have more than one separate loop in a layout, for instance a loop on the ground and another that climbs over it. Each gets its own answer, because they are solved independently. But the screen has one headline line that is supposed to tell you whether the layout works. If one loop is fine and another is still unfinished, or one is fine and another will not close, the line has to say something useful. The options are to report the worst one and name which it is, to list every track separately, or to report the worst without saying which.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Report the worst without naming it and the user sees "won't fit" with no idea which of three loops is the problem; list everything and the screen's one unmistakable answer becomes a table that grows as layouts grow.
Recommendation: A because naming the component costs one clause and is the difference between a verdict and a riddle, and the product's whole claim is that it tells you WHERE the problem is.
Completeness: A=9/10, B=10/10, C=4/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A keeps one unmistakable line and makes it actionable; B is more complete and gives up the single answer; C keeps the line and strips what makes it useful.
Header: Verdict rule
A) Worst component wins, and the line names it (recommended)
One headline line reporting the worst component's verdict, with the component named ("track 2 won't fit, 18mm short"). An open component reports NOT APPLICABLE on the joint axis, visually distinct from both PASS and UNKNOWN, and never counts as the worst. Whole-layout feasibility (overlap and clearance) is reported on its own axis as X4 and C3 already specify. Effort S (human ~1 day / CC ~30 min), risk low. ✅ preserves the one-unmistakable-answer design the whole screen is built around, while telling the user which of several tracks to look at. ✅ an open component reading NOT APPLICABLE rather than PASS closes the vacuous-feasibility hole the eng review found, now that mixed layouts are the normal case. ❌ a layout where two components are both bad shows only the worse one, so fixing it is iterative rather than all at once.
B) List every component's verdict
Replace the headline with a per-component list, one row each, plus the whole-layout feasibility line. Effort S-M (human ~2 days / CC ~30 min), risk low. Completeness 10/10. ✅ shows every problem at once, so a layout with three bad tracks is fixed in one pass rather than three. ✅ scales honestly: nothing is hidden behind an aggregate. ❌ gives up the single unmistakable verdict that the entire screen hierarchy was designed around, and the list grows without bound as layouts grow.
C) Worst component wins, unnamed
One line reporting the worst verdict with no component identified. Effort S, near-zero work. Completeness 4/10. ✅ smallest change: the existing line keeps working with no new concept on screen. ✅ no decision needed about how to name or number components. ❌ tells the user a layout fails without saying which of several tracks is at fault, which is precisely the information this product exists to provide.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Worst component wins, and the line names it - D7 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: one headline line reporting the worst component's verdict with the component NAMED; an open component reports NOT APPLICABLE on the joint axis, visually distinct from PASS and from UNKNOWN, and never counts as the worst; whole-layout feasibility (overlap and clearance) is reported on its own axis per C3 and X4.
History: C3 approved "the worse of two independent axes" when a layout was one component. X1a made a layout a list of components, so the rule needed extending rather than reopening.

## Section 2 — Error & Rescue Map (capability rows, strategy-only)

```
  CAPABILITY (v1)         | WHAT CAN GO WRONG                      | FAILURE CLASS
  ------------------------|----------------------------------------|------------------
  X2 layer index          | index outside {0,1,2}                  | invalid input
                          | layer changes on a non-ramp piece      | impossible geometry
                          | ramp direction ambiguous (+1 or -1)     | wrong layer, silent
                          | index disagrees with derived height     | inconsistent state
  X2 stackup              | step scalar absent or zero              | all bridges legal or none
  X2 rise = fall          | closed component unbalanced             | FAIL (exact integer)
                          | OPEN component unbalanced               | LEGAL, must not FAIL
  X1a components          | two components share an endpoint        | merge or reject, UNDECIDED
                          | one component's solve fails             | must not be hidden by Z1
  X4 overlap              | same-layer overlap missed               | false PASS
                          | AABB false positive                     | false FAIL
  K5 save/load            | old file has no layer or stackup field  | version drift (R16 suspended)
  K0c repair              | gauge sweep still spreads               | verdict flips on piece order
  UI                      | any throw during render                 | blank page
  ------------------------|----------------------------------------|------------------

  FAILURE CLASS            | RESCUED? | RESCUE ACTION                       | USER SEES
  -------------------------|----------|-------------------------------------|------------------
  invalid input            | N <- GAP | -                                   | unknown
  impossible geometry      | N <- GAP | -                                   | unknown
  wrong layer, silent      | N <- GAP | -                                   | nothing. CRITICAL
  inconsistent state       | N <- GAP | derive, never store                 | nothing. CRITICAL
  all bridges legal/none   | N <- GAP | -                                   | every bridge wrong
  FAIL (exact integer)     | Y        | integer sum over the cycle          | named FAIL
  LEGAL open imbalance     | N <- GAP | must NOT be treated as an error     | false FAIL
  false PASS (overlap)     | N <- GAP | X4 closes it; narrowphase undecided | "This works"
  false FAIL (AABB)        | N <- GAP | narrowphase undecided               | wrong FAIL
  version drift            | N <- GAP | R16 suspended, blocks K5            | stale verdict
  verdict flips            | N <- GAP | K0c's gauge sweep is the rescue      | contradictory verdicts
  blank page               | **Y, by accident** | see below                 | grey UNKNOWN
  -------------------------|----------|-------------------------------------|------------------
```

**One accidental win worth recording.** The eng review's finding 6 was that R17's
move of the solve into a mouse-up handler would make a React error boundary inert,
because boundaries catch render and lifecycle errors but not event-handler errors.
**X1b's append-only decision keeps the solve inside `useMemo` during render**, so a
boundary does cover it. The decision was taken for other reasons; this is a
side benefit, and it reverses if drag-and-drop returns.

**Decision gate:** no new choice. Every GAP above is rescue behaviour for approved
capabilities and becomes a task, except the component-shared-endpoint disposition
(X1a's own prove-note) and the overlap narrowphase, both already carried as
unresolved.

## Section 3 — Security & Threat Model

W3 deferred the backend, so there is still no endpoint, no account and no COPPA
surface. One input boundary, now carrying more structure:

| Threat | Likelihood | Impact | Mitigated? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Layout file with a layer index of 7, or -1, or a string | Med | Med | **No** - the parse boundary must validate the enum, and T13's caps are unbuilt |
| Layout file with a zero, negative or absent step scalar | Med | High | **No** - would make every clearance verdict wrong rather than erroring |
| Deep or huge file (prototype pollution, nesting, 100MB) | Med | Med | **No** - T13 specified caps; still unbuilt |
| Pathological layout freezing the tab | Low-Med | Med | **No** - T14 deferred, and the measured 4.6s at 641 pieces makes this concrete rather than theoretical |
| Supply chain | Low | Med | Partly - still zero new runtime dependencies |
| Secrets, PII | - | - | N/A |

The layer model widens the parse boundary: two new field families (the per-placement
index and the layout stackup) both feed verdicts directly, so an invalid value
produces a wrong answer rather than a crash. That is the dangerous direction.

**Decision gate:** no new choice. Validation is inside K5's approved prove-note.

## Section 4 — Data Flow & Interaction Edge Cases

```
  APPEND A PIECE (X1b)                      LAYER FOLLOWS THE RAMP (see finding)
  palette click
      |
      v
  append to the active component
      |
      +--> is the piece a ramp?  yes --> layer = prev layer +/- 1, direction from the placement
      |                           no --> layer = prev layer
      v
  layer within {0,1,2}?  no --> REJECT the append, say why
      |
      v
  solve the active component (synchronous, in render)
      |
      v
  whole-layout feasibility: same-layer overlap, cross-layer clearance
      |
      v
  Z1 aggregation: worst component, NAMED; open components NOT APPLICABLE
```

| Interaction | Edge case | Handled? | How |
|---|---|---|---|
| Append a ramp at L2 | would reach L3 | **No** | must reject, with a reason |
| Append a ramp at L0 going down | would reach L-1 | **No** | must reject, with a reason |
| Start a new component | where does it start, and on which layer? | **No** | new action, undecided default |
| Two components' ends coincide | merge, or reject? | **No** | X1a prove-note, unresolved |
| Remove last from a multi-component layout | which component shrinks? | **No** | needs an active-component concept |
| Close a component | ends must meet AND layers must balance | **No** | exact integer check, new |
| Overlap reported | which two pieces, on which layer? | **No** | X4 scope says FAIL, not where |
| Open component | reads NOT APPLICABLE, not PASS | **No** | Z1 approved it; unbuilt |

`[P1] (confidence: 8/10)` **The layer should be DERIVED at append time, not picked
by the user.** In an append-only editor the layer of each piece follows from the
previous piece plus whether this piece is a ramp. That removes a layer picker from
the UI entirely, and it removes the index-disagrees-with-height invalid state by
construction. A component that is entirely elevated still needs a declared base
layer, so "start a new component" carries a base layer defaulting to 0. This is
required mechanics of X2 and X1b together, so it is a task rather than a question.

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` **Append-only plus multi-component needs an "active
component" concept**, otherwise "remove last" is ambiguous once there are two.
Required mechanics of X1a plus X1b.

**Decision gate:** no new choice. Every row is mechanics of approved scope, except
the shared-endpoint disposition which is already unresolved.

## Section 5 — Code Quality Review

`[P1] (confidence: 9/10)` **X1a's component iteration touches exactly the files
K0c repairs.** `fk.ts` (walker), `residual.ts` (sensitivities), `result.ts`
(verdict assembly) are all in both work sets. Doing them in parallel invites
conflicts; doing components first would mean repairing code that is about to
change shape. **Sequencing: K0c first, then X1a on the repaired surface.**

`[P2] (confidence: 8/10)` **Z1's "name the component" needs component identity
that survives an edit.** Numbering by array position means appending to component
0 can renumber component 1 in the user's mind. A stable id per component, assigned
at creation, costs nothing now and is painful to retrofit.

`[P2] (confidence: 10/10)` Carried unchanged from the eng review and still true:
`VerdictLine.tsx:69,81` duplicate `evaluate.ts:19,21`, and `:58`'s `closesExactly`
branch bypasses the predicate. Z1 makes this worse, since the aggregation rule
would become a fourth place that knows the thresholds.

**Decision gate:** no new choice. Sequencing and identity are mechanics.

X1a's component iteration must leave `8xE`, `8xE1`, the stadium and the 72mm

**No LLM or prompt changes**, so no eval scope.

**Decision gate:** no new choice. The narrowphase remains unresolved and is

## Section 7 — Performance Review

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` **Partitioning by layer index is both a correctness
requirement and the cheapest broadphase available.** Overlap only needs testing
between pieces on the SAME layer, because cross-layer pairs are legal whenever
global clearance holds, and with a uniform step clearance is a single global
predicate. So the overlap test partitions by the integer index first and compares
only within each partition. Three layers evenly loaded cuts the pair count to
about a third. This falls out of X2's integer index for free and is strictly
better than a spatial grid for this data, which supersedes R15's framing without
contradicting its cost reasoning.

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` k components means k independent solves, so the measured
per-solve cost multiplies by k. At the measured 4.6s for 641 pieces in one
component, three 200-piece components are cheaper than one 600-piece component,
because the cost is superlinear (measured ~n^2.9). Multi-component is therefore a
performance *benefit* at equal total pieces.

Unchanged and still unmeasured: the allocation profile, and the scale harness
(H1) remains the gate on all of it. No N+1, no database, no cache, no pools.

**Decision gate:** no new choice.

## Section 10 — Long-Term Trajectory Review

| Change | Reversibility (1 one-way, 5 easy) | Why |
|---|---|---|
| X2 uniform step scalar | **5** | One number; becomes a per-layer table with no placement change |
| X2 layer index on placements | **3** | A data contract, but K5 has not shipped so no files exist yet |
| X1a multi-component | **3** | Changes the shape of every walk, but is additive to the solver |
| X1b append-only | **5** | A UI ceiling, logged with its trigger; adding drag does not undo it |
| X4 overlap | **4** | Additive check on an existing axis |
| Z1 worst-named aggregation | **4** | Presentation rule over per-component results |

**Maintainability of the remaining scope is good, with one caveat.** The upgrade
paths are all designed: the scalar step becomes a per-layer table; append-only
becomes drag with its logged trigger; components become switches when the coupled
solve is proven. The caveat is that **three of today's decisions (R15, R16, R17)
remain suspended**, and R16 blocks K5, which is accepted v1 scope. v1 cannot
finish without reopening at least one suspended decision.

Debt introduced: none new. Debt retired: the "no rollback target" item.

**Decision gate:** no new choice.

## Section 11 — Design & UX Review

UI scope detected: the layer display, the new-component action, the Z1 verdict
rule and the overlap feedback.

```
  USER FLOW — v1 (append-only, multi-component, layered)

  [ empty ]
      |
      v
  click palette piece --> appends to the ACTIVE component
      |                     layer follows: ramp = +/-1, else unchanged
      v
  [ canvas: components drawn, layers distinguished ]
      |
      +--> "new component" --> active component switches, base layer 0
      +--> "remove last"   --> shrinks the ACTIVE component only
      |
      v
  VERDICT (Z1): worst component, NAMED
      joint axis:  PASS / TIGHT / FAIL / UNKNOWN / NOT APPLICABLE (open)
      feasibility: same-layer short | cross-layer clearance
```

`[P1] (confidence: 9/10)` **Layer cannot be encoded in colour without colliding
with the verdict channel.** The design review's Pass 5 carry-list caps the palette
at four verdict colours, and the shipped canvas already uses colour plus glyph
plus a dotted ring for calibration. Adding three layer colours would overload the
one channel that currently carries correctness. PCB tools get away with
colour-per-layer because they have no verdict to show. Available channels that do
not collide: a vertical offset with a drop shadow (the PCB-style stacked look),
line weight, or drawing non-active layers at reduced opacity. This routes to
`/design-consultation` with the constraint stated, rather than being decided here.

`[P2] (confidence: 8/10)` **Interaction state coverage for the new surfaces is
empty**: no loading, empty, error or partial state is specified for the layer
display, the new-component action or the overlap report. The existing canvas
empty state and the four-channel joint glyphs remain the one part of this UI that
is genuinely well specified.

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` **AI slop risk is low and dropping.** The layer model
came from a specific domain analogy with a stated cap, which is the opposite of a
generic pattern. The provisional token palette remains the one slop risk, and
`/design-consultation` still owns it.

Responsive intention: unchanged, and the explicit phone refusal under 620px still
holds. Accessibility: the four-channel glyph work remains the project's best
accessibility asset; the new layer channel must meet the same bar, which is
exactly why it cannot be colour-only.

**Consider running /plan-design-review** for a deep design pass on the layer
display before it is implemented: this is the first genuinely new visual concept
since the original design review, and it interacts with the verdict channel.

**Decision gate:** no new choice. The layer encoding routes to
`/design-consultation`, which is already task V34.

## Outside Voice (CEO re-review, 2026-10-07)

**Provider: native Claude subagent (`Plan` type), completed.** Codex preflight
returned `CODEX_MODE: not_installed`, so **outside coverage is unavailable** for
all three of today's reviews. A native fallback is a finding source, never
outside coverage; no cross-model agreement is claimed. 12 numbered findings plus
4 smaller ones, every load-bearing claim verified by me against source.

### Remaining disagreement

None on substance. One narrowing on finding 9: I had already withdrawn the
unconditional half of that claim before the report arrived, and the reviewer's
additional point about realistic ~90/9/1 layer distribution is accepted.

### Corrections now required as mechanics, not questions

1. A ramp is a TRANSITION spanning two layers; for clearance it must be tested
   against both adjacent layers, so the global-predicate shortcut does not apply
   to ramp pieces.
2. K0b enters the v1 scope table explicitly. It was always meant to be first and
   its omission was an error in my table, not a decision.
3. Z1 is amended: if ANY component is open, the headline cannot read "This
   works". It reports what is unfinished. Open components stop being invisible.
4. Z1 needs a stated total order over PASS / TIGHT / FAIL / UNKNOWN / NOT
   APPLICABLE, with UNKNOWN ranking worse than FAIL, since an uncheckable
   component must never render green.
5. `PartBase.heightMm` is retired; height is derived from the placement's layer.
6. The 8L+8R fixture requires a plank-WIDTH footprint test. A centreline test
   does not catch a tangency.
7. X1b's cost is restated: not zero once X1a-style state exists.

Question:
D8 — Y1: X1a is unbuildable as scoped. Reverse it, or pay for component origins?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; the outside voice showed multi-component and append-only are incompatible, and I verified it in the source.
ELI10: I approved letting a layout hold several separate tracks so you could have a loop running under a bridge. The problem is that nothing in the code says WHERE a track starts: every layout begins at the origin, and there is no field for anything else. So a second track would start in the same place as the first, overlap it, and be correctly reported as broken, with no way for you to move it, because we also decided the editor cannot move pieces. Separately, my own note under the layer decision already said your bridge works as one loop that climbs over itself, which does not need several tracks at all. So the thing I bought with that decision was something you already had.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Reverse it and a layout is one connected track, so two genuinely separate loops wait for v2; keep it and you must add positionable origins, which is repositioning, which reopens the append-only decision and pulls drag-and-drop back in.
Recommendation: A because your bridge example works as a single self-crossing chain, which my own layer analysis established, so reversing costs nothing you asked for and removes an unbuildable combination.
Completeness: A=8/10, B=10/10, C=3/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A removes an incompatibility at no cost to your stated example; B is complete and reopens the editor decision; C keeps the feature and breaks the use case it exists for.
Header: Multi-component
A) Reverse D4: one connected chain for v1 (recommended)
A layout is a single connected run again, optionally closed. The bridge case is a self-crossing chain that ramps up, passes over its own lower section and ramps down, which X2's analysis already established works. Multiple components move to TODOS.md with the origin requirement recorded: any future component origin must be a lattice point in Z[zeta16] with a heading that is a multiple of 22.5 degrees, or exactness dies. Effort S, and it REMOVES work: no active-component state, no per-component closed flag, base layer, origin, switcher or stable ids. ✅ eliminates a combination that cannot be built, where every two-component layout fails on creation with no user action available to fix it. ✅ costs nothing against your stated bridge example, which works as one self-crossing chain, and removes six pieces of app state that X1b was mis-costed as not needing. ❌ two genuinely separate loops, for instance a figure-eight beside an unrelated circle, cannot be expressed in v1 at all.
B) Keep D4 and add lattice-snapped component origins
Add an origin to each component, constrained to lattice points with 22.5-degree headings, plus a way for the user to set it. Effort L (human ~2 weeks / CC ~2 hours), risk high. Completeness 10/10. ✅ keeps genuinely separate tracks, which is what a large layout actually looks like. ✅ forces the snap-epsilon decision that the plan has been deferring, which has to happen eventually. ❌ positioning a component IS repositioning, so it reopens D5's append-only decision and drags undo, the overlap guard and keyboard parity back into v1 with it.
C) Keep D4 with a tool-chosen auto-offset
New components are placed automatically at an offset the tool picks. Effort S (human ~2 days / CC ~20 min). Completeness 3/10. ✅ smallest change that stops two-component layouts failing on creation. ✅ no new user-facing concept and no reopening of the editor decision. ❌ the user cannot put the loop UNDER the bridge, which is the entire reason D4 was approved, so the feature exists without its use case.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Reverse D4: one connected chain for v1 - D8 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: a layout is a single connected run, optionally closed, as it is today. Multiple components are deferred to TODOS.md, carrying the requirement that any future component origin must be a lattice point in Z[zeta16] with a heading that is a multiple of 22.5 degrees or exactness dies. The bridge case is served by a self-crossing chain that ramps up, passes over its own lower section and ramps down.
History: D4 (2026-10-07) approved multiple independent components, justified as the only way to express a separate track under a bridge. Reversed at Y1 because the justification was contradicted by X2's own text in the same review, and because the source has no component origin: `fk.ts:58-60` and `pose.ts:61` hardcode it and `Layout` has no field for it, so a second component would default to the origin, overlap the first and be failed by X4 with no user action available under append-only.

### Consequence: Z1 is superseded, and the shipped behaviour was already right

With one component there is nothing to aggregate, so **Z1's worst-component-named
rule is moot** and is withdrawn. The outside voice's finding 7 was correct on the
deeper point: the shipped code already handles the open case properly.
`result.ts:60` returns `OPEN_CHAIN` and `VerdictLine.tsx:48` renders grey with
"Not a loop yet. Close it to check whether it works." That is accurate, it is not
green, and it needed no fifth verdict state. Z1 invented one for a case four
states already covered, and in doing so introduced a false-green of its own.

What survives from Z1's subject matter:
- **V21 (eng review) still stands**: an open chain should also report what the
  feasibility axis can already tell it (overlap, grade, clearance), instead of a
  blanket grey that hides those. That is an addition to the existing grey state,
  not a replacement for it.
- **No fifth verdict state is added.** `VERDICT_COLOUR` and the four-channel
  glyph set at `TrackCanvas.tsx:24-28` stay as the design review built them,
  which Section 11 flagged as the project's best accessibility asset.

Net effect of Y1 on scope: it REMOVES work. Gone are the active-component
concept, the per-component closed flag, base layer, origin and switcher, stable
component ids, the Z1 aggregation rule and the fifth verdict state.

Question:
D9 — Y2: Two layers or three?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; you instructed a cap of three, and the outside voice argued for two.
ELI10: You asked for three levels: the floor, one up, two up. The reviewer pointed out that your own example only uses two, and that two levels collapse to a single yes-or-no flag per piece instead of a number, which removes a handful of rules: no cap to enforce, nothing to reject when someone tries to go higher, and no need for the special case where getting from the floor to the top takes two ramps. Against that, once the layer number exists at all, which it must for the exact arithmetic, the extra cost of allowing a third level is fairly small, and two stacked bridges is a real thing people build.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Cut to two and a layout with a bridge over a bridge is unrepresentable, and widening later touches the data format; keep three and you carry a cap, two rejection paths and a two-ramp special case for a level your stated example never uses.
Recommendation: B because you asked for three deliberately, and the marginal cost of the third level over the second is small once the integer index exists, which it must for rise-equals-fall to stay exact.
Completeness: A=6/10, B=9/10
Pros / cons:
Net: Two is genuinely simpler but gives up stacked bridges; three costs a cap and two rejection paths on top of an index you need anyway.
Header: Layer cap
B) Keep three layers as instructed (recommended)
L0, L1, L2 with the integer index, as decided at D3. Costs cap enforcement at append, a reject-with-reason path for L3 and L-1, the adjacent-vias-only rule so L0 to L2 needs two ramps, and 3-way partitioning for the overlap test. Effort M (human ~1 week / CC ~1 hour), risk low. ✅ a bridge over a bridge is a real layout and stays expressible, and the cap you chose is already a large simplification over arbitrary height. ✅ the integer index is required regardless for rise-equals-fall to be exact arithmetic, so the third level adds rules rather than a new concept. ❌ carries a cap, two rejection paths and a two-ramp special case for a level your own stated example never uses.
A) Cut to two layers, one boolean per piece
Replace the index with a single `elevated` flag. Rise-equals-fall becomes "count of up-ramps equals count of down-ramps", still exact. Clearance becomes one global predicate evaluated once. Effort S (human ~3 days / CC ~30 min), risk low. Completeness 6/10: covers your stated example and nothing above it. ✅ removes the cap, both rejection paths, the two-ramp rule and 3-way partitioning outright, and a boolean cannot hold an out-of-range value. ✅ matches what your own bridge example actually uses, so nothing you described is lost. ❌ a bridge over a bridge is unrepresentable, and widening to three later changes the per-placement field, which touches the saved file format once K5 has shipped.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Cut to two layers, one boolean per piece - D9 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: each placement carries `elevated: boolean`. Rise equals fall becomes "the count of up-ramps equals the count of down-ramps" around a closed chain, still exact integer arithmetic. Clearance becomes ONE global predicate evaluated once: step minus plank thickness against vehicle height. The overlap test partitions two ways. Removed outright: cap enforcement at append, the reject-with-reason paths for L3 and L-1, the adjacent-vias-only two-ramp rule, and 3-way partitioning.
History: the user instructed a cap of three layers (L0/L1/L2) on 2026-10-07 and D3 approved a discrete index to match. Reopened at Y2 after the outside voice showed the user's own bridge example uses only two levels and that two collapse to a single boolean. The user chose two, superseding their own earlier cap and the index half of D3.

### Accepted shortcut, recorded

This option is Completeness 6/10 and a durable scope cut, so it is logged as
decision `f6bc20a3-29a8-4643-84d0-9ab8d6e63999` with its ceiling and trigger.

**Upgrade trigger:** the first layout that needs a bridge over a bridge. At that
point the boolean widens back to an integer index. **Note the ordering hazard:**
widening touches the per-placement field, which is part of the saved file format
once K5 ships, so if stacked bridges are ever likely, widen before any file
exists. When the layer field is next touched in code it carries the marker
`gstack-shortcut(dec-f6bc20a3-29a8-4643-84d0-9ab8d6e63999): two layers only, elevated boolean; upgrade
when a layout needs a bridge over a bridge`.

**What survives unchanged from X2 and the Outside Voice corrections:**
- A ramp is a TRANSITION that spans both levels, so for clearance it must be
  tested against both. The global-predicate shortcut does not apply to ramp
  pieces themselves. This correction is independent of the cap.
- The ramp part (#33332) is absent from `BRIO_PARTS` and must be added by K4,
  along with plank thickness. The 64mm rise currently exists only in a docblock.
- `PartBase.heightMm` is retired; elevation lives on the placement.
- The ramp's grade is 29.63% against an estimated 30% limit, a 0.37pp margin.
- K0b's measurement decides whether the clearance predicate passes at all.

Question:
D10 — Y3: Elevated track needs physical support. Model it, or say you do not?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; the outside voice found supports absent from the plan, the schema and K4.
ELI10: Raised BRIO track does not float. It sits on risers, stacked track or piers, and those take up space on the floor below. Your own example is a loop crossing over a track that continues underneath, and in reality the thing holding the bridge up often lands exactly on that lower track. The tool currently says such a layout is fine, because it has no idea supports exist. The choice is to model supports properly, to say plainly that the tool does not check them, or to leave it silent.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Model it and you add a whole class of parts and placement rules to v1; say nothing and the tool blesses bridges that cannot physically stand, in the one scenario you described.
Recommendation: B because this project's standing rule is that a verdict never claims more than it checked, and an honest "supports are not verified" costs one line while a support model costs a new part class and placement rules.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=2/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A is correct and expensive; B keeps the tool honest about what it did not check; C is the false PASS this product cannot afford.
B) State it, do not check it (recommended)
The verdict explicitly reports that elevated runs are not checked for physical support, so a layer verdict never implies the bridge can stand. One line of copy plus one line in the verdict composition, flagged whenever any placement is above L0. Supports go to TODOS.md as a v2 candidate with the pier-footprint requirement recorded. Effort S (human ~half day / CC ~15 min), risk low. Completeness 7/10: honest about a real gap rather than closing it. ✅ upholds the project's standing invariant that a verdict never claims more than it checked, which is the same discipline behind grey-is-not-green and the dotted calibration ring. ✅ costs almost nothing and keeps the layer feature shippable while naming its limit, so a user is not misled into trusting a span that cannot stand. ❌ the tool still cannot tell you that your pier lands on the lower track, which is a real failure in the exact scenario you described.
A) Model supports and their footprints
Add support and riser parts to the catalogue, require elevated runs to be supported, and let pier footprints participate in the same-layer overlap test so a pier landing on lower track is a short. Effort L (human ~2 weeks / CC ~2 hours), risk medium. Completeness 10/10. ✅ catches the real failure in your own bridge example, where the thing holding the bridge up lands on the track underneath. ✅ makes the layer feature physically trustworthy rather than geometrically trustworthy, which is the difference between a drawing and a build plan. ❌ a new part class with its own placement rules, on top of a v1 whose solver repair has not landed, and the plan has no support dimensions to work from.
C) Leave the gap silent
Change nothing; a floating or badly supported span simply passes. Effort none. Completeness 2/10. ✅ no work and no new copy on screen. ✅ keeps v1's surface area at its smallest. ❌ the tool prints a confident verdict on a layout that cannot physically stand, which is the precise failure mode this product exists to prevent and the one it cannot survive.
Header: Supports

State: approved
Actual answer: B) State it, do not check it - D10 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: whenever any placement is elevated, the verdict reports that physical support is NOT verified, so a layer verdict never implies the bridge can stand. One line of copy plus one line in the verdict composition. Supports and risers go to TODOS.md as a v2 candidate, carrying the requirement that pier footprints participate in the same-level overlap test so a pier landing on lower track is a short.
History: none. Raised by the Outside Voice (finding 5) and verified by absence from the plan, the schema and K4's part list.

### Accepted shortcut, recorded

Completeness 7/10 and a durable scope call, so it is logged as decision
`2f1af71e-4277-4cab-ae07-bd5f205d0b7f`. **Upgrade trigger:** the first report of a bridge that passed
but could not be supported, or the arrival of support and riser parts in the
catalogue. The cut corner carries
`gstack-shortcut(dec-2f1af71e-4277-4cab-ae07-bd5f205d0b7f): elevated support stated, not checked;
upgrade when pier footprints join the overlap test`.

Why this is the right shape rather than a dodge: the project already has a
standing invariant that a verdict never claims more than it checked. It is why
a failed solve renders grey instead of green, and why an unmeasured tolerance
wears a dotted ring. Saying "supports are not verified" is that same rule applied
to a newly discovered gap, rather than a silent false PASS.

### Lake Score detail

Two questions offered a coverage-scored 10/10 option and neither was taken. Y1's
10/10 (add lattice-snapped component origins) was rejected because positioning a
component is repositioning, which would reopen the append-only decision and pull
drag-and-drop back into v1. Y3's 10/10 (model supports and pier footprints) was
rejected because it is a new part class with no measured dimensions, on a v1
whose solver repair has not landed. Both are reasoned rejections of completeness
in favour of a smaller correct thing, which is what SCOPE REDUCTION mode is for.
Recorded rather than scored as a failure.

### Unresolved Decisions

1. **R15 overlap cost** - suspended by R18, and its AABB choice is now known to be a broadphase only.
2. **R16 layout fingerprint** - suspended by R18. **Blocks K5, which is accepted v1 scope**, so v1 cannot finish without reopening it.
3. **R17 solve trigger** - suspended by R18; its "warm-started and component-scoped" has no implementation path.
4. **The overlap narrowphase primitive** - never decided. AABB cannot answer X4's own acceptance criterion.
5. **The snap / vertex-identity epsilon** - never decided, and multi-component now depends on it too (origins must be lattice points).
6. **A shared vertex inside one chain** - the figure-eight is two cycles in one chain (E-V+C = 16-15+1 = 2), modelled as one. No switch and no second component needed to reach it.
7. **E1, the ascending-track datum** - if 216mm is the plank rather than the run, the run is irrational and the exact lattice does not survive ramps. K0b settles it.

### What this review actually did

It started as a scope reduction and became a correction of itself. Ten decisions
were asked and answered. **Two of them reversed decisions made earlier in the
same review**: Y1 reversed X1a after the source showed no component origin
exists, and Y2 cut X2's three-layer index to a two-layer boolean. A third, Z1,
was withdrawn entirely once Y1 made it moot.

The honest headline: **net scope went UP for most of this review and only came
down at the end.** The Outside Voice called the SCOPE REDUCTION label
miscalibrated and was right at the time it said so. After Y1, Y2 and Z1's
withdrawal, the balance is restored: v1 lost multi-component, the third layer,
the fifth verdict state, the component aggregation rule and six pieces of app
state, and gained two layers as a boolean, overlap with width footprints, and an
honest disclosure about supports.

Five of my own errors were caught and corrected: X2's text contradicted D4's
justification; the 8L+8R fixture description was geometrically wrong; the
layer-partition claim was a category error; "derived layer removes a picker" was
false; and K0b was missing from the v1 scope table.

## Scope Challenge

### A. Assessment

**Search check** (Aside unavailable; host WebSearch used, read-only). One new
algorithmic pattern, the overlap narrowphase:

- **[Layer 1]** Arc intersection is classic computational geometry; pairwise
  tests reduce to degree-2 orientation tests.
  Source: users.cs.duke.edu/~pankaj/publications/papers/arc-intersect.pdf
- **[Layer 2]** The literature's O(n^4/3) results concern COUNTING all
  intersections among n arcs, which is not this problem: here each pair is
  tested after a cheap reject.
- **[EUREKA, logged]** The dominant case needs no geometry. An arc centre is
  `pos + r * zeta^(h +/- 4)` with integer `r`, so **every arc centre is an exact
  lattice point in Z[zeta16]**, and every arc angle is a multiple of 22.5
  degrees. Two same-radius arcs therefore lie on the SAME circle iff their
  centres are equal as exact integers, and then overlap iff their angular
  intervals overlap: both exact integer tests. **Verified by execution:** all 8
  arcs of `8xE` and all 16 of `16xE` share centre (0, 182); `8L then 8R` has two
  distinct centres, (0, 182) and (0, -182). Only genuinely different circles need
  float geometry.

## Decision ledger

### R19: What primitive actually decides overlap
Finding: Scope Challenge C, [P1], confidence 10/10, X4's acceptance criterion with R15's suspended cost model - reviewer: plan-eng-review Scope Challenge A (search check and EUREKA).
Plan baseline: R15 (D2, suspended by R18) chose "AABB all-pairs with an early reject", and the cost reasoning was sound. The narrowphase was never decided and sits on the UNRESOLVED list as "never decided; AABB cannot answer X4's own acceptance criterion".
Runtime evidence: MEASURED. AABB inflation for real parts: 1.46x at best orientation for a 45-degree r182 curve, 3.11x at 22.5 degrees, 3.80x at 45 degrees, 3.79x for a 216mm straight at 45 degrees; BRIO headings are multiples of 22.5 degrees so the inflated cases are the common ones. Arc centres verified as lattice points: all 8 arcs of `8xE` and all 16 of `16xE` share centre (0, 182) to within 8.5e-14, which is float noise from `walkFloat`; the exact `Cyc` poses would give zero error. `8L then 8R` has two distinct centres, (0, 182) and (0, -182), separation 364 = the sum of the radii, so those circles are TANGENT.
Comparison grid:

```text
Commitment                      | Source/approval       | Current            | A (two-tier, exact first)      | B (float narrowphase)        | C (AABB only)
Overlap detection exists        | X4 (D6) approved      | approved, unbuilt  | unchanged                      | unchanged                    | unchanged
Broadphase reject               | R15 (suspended)       | AABB all-pairs     | AABB, unchanged                | AABB, unchanged              | AABB IS the test
Same-circle case                | pending               | undecided          | EXACT integer centre equality  | float, same as any other     | missed or false-positive
Angular interval overlap        | pending               | undecided          | EXACT, 22.5-degree multiples   | float angles                 | n/a
Different-circle case           | pending               | undecided          | float annulus-band distance    | float, same code path        | false positive
16xE / 24xE caught             | X4 criterion          | currently PASS     | YES, exactly, no epsilon       | yes, within float tolerance  | yes, by accident
8L+8R tangency caught          | X4 criterion          | currently PASS     | yes, float tier                | yes                          | yes, by accident
False FAIL risk                 | D9 option A's con     | n/a                | low: exact tier has none       | one tolerance to tune        | HIGH: 3.8x inflation
New dependency                  | none so far           | none               | none                           | none                         | none
Epsilon count                   | project concern       | n/a                | ONE, float tier only           | one, everywhere              | one, everywhere
Same-level only (two layers)    | Y2 approved           | n/a                | unchanged                      | unchanged                    | unchanged
Other pending remedies          | Sections 1-4          | pending            | pending                        | pending                      | pending
```

Question D2:
D2 — R19: What primitive decides whether two pieces overlap?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; X4 kept overlap in v1 and its deciding primitive was never chosen.
ELI10: The overlap check needs something that actually answers "do these two pieces of track occupy the same floor". A bounding box is only a quick reject: for a curve at a typical BRIO angle the box is nearly four times the real footprint, so a box-only test would refuse layouts that are perfectly fine. The classic answer is floating-point arc geometry. But there is a better one here: because every arc's centre lands exactly on this engine's integer lattice and every arc angle is a multiple of 22.5 degrees, two curves on the same circle can be detected with pure integer comparisons, no tolerance at all. That is the common failure, pieces retracing a circle they already covered. Only genuinely different circles need the floating-point test.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Choose box-only and the tool refuses buildable layouts, which is worse than the bug it was meant to fix; choose all-float and the one part of this engine that could be exact gets a tolerance, in a project whose verdicts already flip on small numbers.
Recommendation: A because it makes the dominant case exact and confines floating point to one clearly bounded tier, which is the same discipline that makes the closure test trustworthy.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=8/10, C=3/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A is exact where it can be and float only where it must be; B is uniform and tolerant everywhere; C is fast and wrong.
Header: Overlap primitive
A) Two tiers: exact integer first, float only for different circles (recommended)
AABB stays the reject filter. Then: if two arcs share a radius AND their centres are equal as exact lattice values, they lie on one circle, and overlap is an exact comparison of angular intervals in units of 22.5 degrees, with no tolerance. Everything else, including straights and arcs on different circles, falls to a float annulus-band distance test with one stated tolerance. Effort M (human ~1 week / CC ~1 hour), risk low. ✅ makes the dominant real failure, pieces retracing a circle, exactly decidable with integers, which is the same property that makes this engine's closure test trustworthy. ✅ confines floating point to one bounded tier with a single stated tolerance, instead of spreading an epsilon across every comparison in a project whose verdicts already flip on small spreads. ❌ two code paths for one question, so a bug can hide in the boundary between them, and the exact tier needs the exact `Cyc` centres rather than the float ones `walkFloat` currently produces.
B) One float arc-and-segment narrowphase for every pair
Classic computational geometry: circle-circle intersection plus angular-interval checks, segment-segment, and segment-arc, all in floating point with a tolerance. Effort M (human ~1 week / CC ~1 hour), risk medium. Completeness 8/10: correct everywhere, tolerant everywhere. ✅ one code path for every pair, so there is no boundary between tiers to get wrong. ✅ a standard, well-documented technique with known pitfalls, which is the Layer 1 answer. ❌ gives a tolerance to the one case that did not need one, and this project has already had two verdicts decided by an epsilon nobody chose deliberately.
C) Keep AABB as the whole test
Treat the bounding-box overlap as the decision. Effort S, near-zero work. Completeness 3/10. ✅ smallest possible implementation and the fastest to run. ✅ never misses a real overlap, since the box always contains the piece. ❌ measured 3.8x area inflation at 45 degrees and 3.11x at 22.5 degrees, and BRIO headings are multiples of 22.5, so it would refuse ordinary layouts such as two parallel diagonal sidings 60mm apart.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Two tiers: exact integer first, float for different circles - D2 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: AABB remains the reject filter. Tier 1, EXACT: two arcs sharing a radius whose centres are equal as exact lattice values lie on one circle, and overlap is an exact comparison of angular intervals in units of 22.5 degrees, with no tolerance. Tier 2, FLOAT: everything else, including straights and arcs on different circles, uses an annulus-band distance test with ONE stated tolerance. Supersedes the "AABB all-pairs" clause of R15 only on the decision primitive; R15's cost reasoning (once per edit over static geometry, no spatial grid) is retained and a grid is still earned by measurement.
History: R15 (D2 of the first eng review pass) chose AABB all-pairs over a spatial grid and was suspended by R18. The narrowphase was never decided and sat on the UNRESOLVED list. Resolved here after measuring AABB inflation (1.46x at best orientation, 3.80x at 45 degrees, and BRIO headings are multiples of 22.5 degrees) and verifying that arc centres are exact lattice points.

## Section 1 — Architecture review (v1, post Y1/Y2)

`[P1] (confidence: 9/10)` Y2's two-layer model - **ramp direction needs no field.**
A chain starts on the floor and every ramp toggles level, so at L0 a ramp can
only ascend and at L1 only descend. `elevated` is derivable by walking the chain.
**This retires task J5** (explicit per-placement ramp direction) and removes the
index-disagrees-with-height invalid state by construction. **Disposition:
factual correction plus a retirement, no question.**

`[P1] (confidence: 9/10)` Y2 plus C1 - **`rise = fall` collapses to a parity
check.** A closed chain returns to the floor iff it contains an EVEN number of
ramps. Not a sum, not a count comparison. Exact integer, one line. This is the
simplest form the invariant has taken across four reviews. **Disposition:
required mechanics, task.**

`[P1] (confidence: 10/10)` K5 is accepted v1 scope and **blocked on R16, which
R18 suspended and nothing has reopened.** v1 cannot finish. See R20.

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` R19's accepted scope - **`feasible.ts` consumes two
walkers.** Tier 1 needs `pose.ts`'s exact `Cyc` positions; tier 2 and clearance
need metric distances from `fk.ts`. The prove-note must state which tier reads
which, or float centres will be fed into the exact comparison and exactness is
silently lost. **Disposition: prove-note, task.**

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` Y2 - **the single point of failure is concentrated and
unchanged:** the uniform step, the plank thickness and the vehicle height, with
an 8mm margin, as one global predicate deciding every bridge in every layout.
K0b is the only mitigation. **Disposition: open risk with owner.**

`[P2] (confidence: 8/10)` carried correction - **a ramp is excluded from the
cross-level shortcut**, because it spans both levels and must be tested against
both. **Disposition: required mechanics, task.**

Scaling: one chain, so k-component multiplication is gone. Overlap is all-pairs
within a level, which with two levels and a realistic ~90/10 split is close to
all-pairs on the floor. Security: unchanged, one file-parse boundary. Rollback:
`git revert` works.

### R20: Reopen R16 - what a saved layout records
Finding: Section 1, [P1], confidence 10/10, K5's accepted scope against R16's suspended state - reviewer: plan-eng-review Section 1.
Plan baseline: R16 (D3, first eng pass) approved recording `partDbVersion`, `adapterVersion`, `solverVersion`, the slop convention and the feasible-set shape, dropping `planegcsVersion`. R18 then SUSPENDED it. The second outside voice found the enumeration was already incomplete at birth, missing the overlap tolerance and the snap epsilon.
Runtime evidence: no document type exists; K5 is unbuilt and `engine/src/doc.ts` is one of the four new modules. The count of verdict-affecting semantics has grown during today's reviews: part data, slop convention, feasible-set shape (W11 chose the coupled sum), the R19 float-tier overlap tolerance, the uniform layer step, plank thickness, vehicle height, and W10's restated solver guarantee. That is eight and still moving.
