
Question D3:
D3 — R20: Reopen R16. What does a saved layout record so its verdict cannot be silently reinterpreted?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; K5 is accepted v1 scope and cannot be built while R16 is suspended.
ELI10: When you save a layout and open it next month, the tool must know whether the answer it gave then still means the same thing. The earlier decision listed five version numbers to stamp into the file. The problem is that the list keeps growing: today's reviews added an overlap tolerance, a layer step height, a plank thickness and a vehicle height, all of which change verdicts, and none of which were on the list. Every addition is a change to the saved file format. The alternative is to stamp one fingerprint computed over the whole table of verdict-affecting constants, plus a human-readable version, so the list can grow without the format changing.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Enumerate and the format needs a migration every time a constant is added, and today alone added four; hash and the file carries one opaque value, so telling the user WHICH thing changed needs the constant table stored alongside it.
Recommendation: A because the enumeration was already incomplete the day it was approved and grew by four constants in one day, and a hash over a table lets the set grow without touching the file format.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=3/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A survives a growing constant set; B is explicit and needs a migration per addition; C tracks almost nothing and reopens the silent-reinterpretation hole.
Header: Layout fingerprint
A) One semantics hash over the constant table, plus a readable version (recommended)
Store a single `engineSemanticsHash` computed over the frozen table of every verdict-affecting constant, plus a human-readable `engineVersion`, plus the constant table itself so the diff can name what changed. A test asserts the hash changes whenever any constant in the table changes. On open, the verdict is recomputed and the diff names the changed constant. Effort M (human ~3 days / CC ~30 min), risk low. ✅ the constant set grew by four items in one day and will grow again; a hash over a table absorbs that with no file-format change and no migration. ✅ the stored table means the diff can still say exactly which constant moved, so nothing is lost against the enumerated version. ❌ the hash is only as good as the table's completeness, so a new verdict-affecting constant that nobody adds to the table is invisible, and the test can only check the constants it knows about.
B) R16's five enumerated fields, extended to eight
Keep the named-field approach and add the four constants today's reviews introduced: the overlap tolerance, the layer step, the plank thickness and the vehicle height. Effort S (human ~1 day / CC ~20 min), risk medium. Completeness 7/10: explicit for today's set and fragile against tomorrow's. ✅ every tracked value is visible in the file and in the type, with no indirection. ✅ no hashing, so a human reading a saved file can see exactly what it was verified against. ❌ it was already incomplete the day it was approved and grew by four in one day, and each future addition is a saved-file-format change requiring a migration.
C) Minimal pair: partDbVersion plus solverVersion
Stamp only the part-data version and a solver version, and rely on bumping the solver version whenever semantics change. Effort S (human ~2h / CC ~10 min), risk high. Completeness 3/10. ✅ smallest format and the least to maintain. ✅ no table, no hash, nothing to keep complete. ❌ reopens the exact hole R16 was created to close: a convention change that nobody remembers to bump reopens an old file with a silently different verdict.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) One semantics hash over the constant table, plus a readable version - D3 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: the layout document stores one `engineSemanticsHash` computed over the frozen table of every verdict-affecting constant, a human-readable `engineVersion`, and the constant table itself so the diff can name what changed. A test asserts the hash changes whenever any constant in the table changes. On open the verdict is RECOMPUTED, never trusted, and the diff names the changed constant. **SUPERSEDES R16's enumerated five fields and UNSUSPENDS the decision, which unblocks K5.** The constant table must include, at minimum: part data, the slop convention, the feasible-set shape (W11's coupled sum), R19's float-tier overlap tolerance, Y2's uniform layer step, the plank thickness, the vehicle height, and W10's solver guarantee.
History: R16 (D3, first eng pass, 2026-10-07) approved five enumerated fields. R18 suspended it the same day. The second outside voice then showed the enumeration was already missing the overlap tolerance and the snap epsilon. Reopened here because K5 is accepted v1 scope and cannot be built while this is unresolved.

Two things worth naming. **Y3's row is the only one rescued by disclosure
rather than a check**, and that is deliberate. And **the blank-page row is
rescued by accident**: X1b's append-only decision keeps the solve inside
`useMemo` during render, where a React error boundary can catch it. Move the
solve to an event handler later and that protection silently disappears.

capabilities and becomes a task.

`[P1] (confidence: 10/10)` **R21 is the largest code-quality improvement
available to this project, and it is a deletion.** `lp.ts` (136 lines) and
`refine.ts` (141) go outright; `residual.ts` and `result.ts` lose their slop
portions. Every measured defect in the repository goes with them.

`[P1] (confidence: 10/10)` `engine/src/solver/result.ts:17-25` - **the failure
taxonomy drops from eight reasons to three.** `INFEASIBLE`, `UNBOUNDED`,
`CYCLED`, `TIMEDOUT` and `DEGENERATE` are all LP statuses and become unreachable
under R21, leaving `OPEN_CHAIN`, `HEADING_MISMATCH` and `NUMERIC`. That also
retires the observability gap about eight reasons not being distinguishable on
screen: three are easy to distinguish. **Disposition: consequence of R21, task.**

`[P1] (confidence: 10/10)` `app/src/VerdictLine.tsx:58,69,81` - **the threshold
duplication becomes MOOT and the `closesExactly` bypass does not.** Lines 69 and
81 test `worstRatio`, which R21 deletes, so that duplication disappears on its
own. Line 58's `if (result.closesExactly)` still bypasses the predicate, and
under R21 it becomes the PRIMARY path rather than a special case, so it needs to
be the deliberate structure rather than an accident. **Disposition: task.**

`[P2] (confidence: 10/10)` `app/src/App.tsx:70` - **two exact walks per failed
edit.** On a failed solve the `evals` memo calls `computeResidual` a second time.
With R21 removing the solver's own walk this is a larger share of the remaining
cost. **Disposition: task.**

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` **three write-only fields, soon four.**
`gaugeWidthMm` and `heightMm` are written in data and read by nothing, and K4's
planned `footprintOutline` would be a third if R19's annulus primitive never
reads it. R19 reads plank width, not an outline, so the outline should either be
dropped from K4 or given a reader. **Disposition: task.**

`[P2] (confidence: 10/10)` `engine/src/parts/schema.ts:5` and
`engine/data/brio.ts:11` - a comment claiming connector geometry the schema
lacks, and a docblock line used as the source for a load-bearing constant.
**Disposition: task, carried from three prior reviews and still unfixed.**

over joint budgets with one caller; extracting anything would be premature. No
duplication with two verified first-party sites remains once the `worstRatio`
thresholds go.

**Cyclomatic complexity:** `VerdictLine` currently branches five times and R21
reduces that, because the TIGHT and FAIL ratio branches disappear. `result.ts`
likewise simplifies with five fewer failure reasons. This is the first review in
which the complexity trend is downward.

carried defect.

it with one fixture and one rotation), the arc-centre proof, and the shipped
OPEN_CHAIN grey state, which this review's predecessor confirmed was already
correct and which V21 extends rather than replaces.

**No LLM or prompt changes.** No eval suites apply.

**Decision gate:** no new choice.

## Section 4 — Performance review

`[P2] (confidence: 9/10)` **Overlap is all-pairs within a level, and the realistic
split is roughly 90/10 floor to elevated**, so the two-level partition is close to
all-pairs on the floor. The prior claim that layer partitioning beats a spatial
grid was already withdrawn as a category error; with two layers the constant is
at most 2 and in practice near 1.1. Scale: ~125k pair tests at 500 pieces,
~12.5M at 5,000, **once per edit**. R19's tier 1 is an integer comparison and is
far cheaper than tier 2, and most same-circle pairs resolve there.

`[P2] (confidence: 10/10)` **The solver numbers are measured and unchanged:**
101ms at 161 pieces, 2.6s at 241, 470ms at 301, hard CYCLED at 321 and 341,
988ms at 401, 4.6s at 641, with 8 passes saturated throughout and scaling about
n^2.9. These are the dominant cost by a wide margin, and H1's harness is the gate.

Unchanged: no N+1, no database, no cache layer, no connection pools. The
`mulZetaPow` allocation finding from the previous pass stays **withdrawn** as
mis-aimed; if allocation ever matters the offenders are `add`, `sub` and `scale`,
which are in the exact walk.

**Decision gate:** no new choice.

### Corrected coverage numbers

Recounted from the printed tree rather than carried by subtraction:
**10/42 paths tested, 32 gaps, three-star count 4.** The previously stated
11/40, 9/27, 2/13, 29 gaps and three-star 6 were edited by subtraction across
three passes and are withdrawn. The 62-to-40 narrative is also not pure removal:
`feasible.ts`, `doc.ts`, `ErrorBoundary.tsx` and `scale.test.ts` add 12 new paths.

### Corrections now required as mechanics, not questions

1. **R19 interval convention: half-open mod 16.** Without it the 8xE flagship
   fixture FAILS, because its eight arcs abut. Adjacency exclusion must name the
   seam pair (placements 0 and n-1 in a closed chain) explicitly.
2. **R19's band half-width is a MEASUREMENT (plank width / 2), not a tolerance.**
   Two constants for R20's table, not one. `gaugeWidthMm` is gauge, not plank width.
3. **R19's prove-note must pin the CONFIGURATION**, nominal or solved, not just
   the walker. Open chains have no solved configuration, so overlap on open
   chains uses nominal and the "Closed loop" toggle must not flip it.
4. **R20: the engine must IMPORT its constants from the frozen table**, enforced
   by a check for stray literals. The table must include `TIGHT_THRESHOLD`,
   `FAIL_THRESHOLD`, `MAX_PASSES`, `CONVERGED_MM` and both overlap constants.
5. **`elevated` is DERIVED, not stored. J4's storage is retired**, and J6's text
   is rewritten to the parity form.
6. **K0b needs a contingency branch**: what happens to the layer feature if the
   vehicle measures 48mm, the plank 16mm, or 216mm proves to be the plank.
7. **A DOM test project plus a dev dependency** is required for the three app
   gaps; the no-new-dependency claim covered runtime only.
8. **`scale.test.ts` moves out of the default glob.**
9. **"`fk.ts` needs no changes" is restated as contingent** on the ramp being a
   `StraightPart` whose `lengthMm` is its horizontal run, which E1 decides.
10. **A base-level question exists** that I raised myself: a fully elevated loop
    with zero ramps satisfies parity but needs a declared base level, which
    `Layout` does not have. Either chains are declared to start on the floor, or
    the field is added.

### Remaining disagreement

None on substance. One narrowing on finding 7: the exactness CLAIM is confirmed
by integer re-verification; only the evidence I originally offered was float.

### R21: Does Stage 2, the minimax LP, belong in v1 at all?
Finding: Outside Voice finding 1, [P1], confidence 10/10 - reviewer: plan-eng-review Outside Voice. Never offered as a cut across four review rounds.
Plan baseline: the engine is two stages. Stage 1 is the exact closure residual in Z[zeta16]. Stage 2 is the Chebyshev minimax LP over bounded per-joint slop, approved at D19A and repaired by K0c (tasks H1-H5).
Runtime evidence: MEASURED. Every defect found in this repository lives in Stage 2: hard `CYCLED` on buildable 321- and 341-piece layouts, 4.6s at 641, the 8-pass budget saturated from 21 pieces, the verdict flipping FAIL/TIGHT on the cut point at 42 pieces, the zero-lever seam joint, `max(axial, angular)` double-spending, ungated `remainingMm` read one pass early, and `suspectJoints` declared and never populated. That is `lp.ts` plus `refine.ts` plus the slop half of `residual.ts` and `result.ts`: roughly 490 of about 1,100 engine lines and five of the twelve critical gaps. Stage 1, by contrast, has produced zero defects across four reviews and is covered by 17 passing tests.
Its product contribution: converting "does not close exactly" into a TIGHT-versus-FAIL split, computed from `ESTIMATED_SLOP = {axialMm: 0.8, angularDeg: 1.5}` which the plan itself labels fabricated, sliced at `TIGHT_THRESHOLD = 0.8`. A threshold on a guess.
Comparison grid:

```text
Commitment                      | Source/approval      | Current               | A (repair as planned)      | B (replace with the bound)     | C (repair AND add the bound)
Stage 1 exact closure           | D19A approved        | works, 17 tests       | unchanged                  | unchanged                      | unchanged
Stage 2 minimax LP              | D19A approved        | measurably broken     | REPAIRED (K0c, H1-H5)      | DELETED                        | repaired
Necessary-condition bound       | pending              | absent                | absent                     | ADDED: gap <= sum of axialMax  | added, and it is the verdict
TIGHT vs FAIL split             | C3, shipped          | from a guessed budget | kept, from a repaired LP   | replaced by a hedged middle    | kept as unverified diagnostics
Per-joint attribution           | T11, never built     | fiction               | real after H2 and H4       | LOST                           | kept, labelled unverified
K0c, H1-H5, scale.test.ts       | R18 approved         | accepted              | kept                       | DELETED                        | kept
R15, R17                        | suspended            | suspended             | must be reopened           | become MOOT                    | must be reopened
Critical gaps closed by this    | 12 total             | n/a                   | 5 repaired                 | 5 DELETED outright             | 5 repaired
Verdict honesty on guessed input| project invariant    | precise-looking        | precise-looking            | HEDGED, matches the evidence   | hedged, with diagnostics
K0b still decides the budgets   | R18                  | yes                    | yes                        | yes, but only for the bound    | yes
Other v1 scope                  | approved             | unchanged             | unchanged                  | unchanged                      | unchanged
```

Question D4:
D4 — R21: Does the minimax LP belong in v1, or does an honest bound replace it?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; every measured defect in the repository lives in Stage 2, and four review rounds never put it on a menu.
ELI10: The engine has two halves. The first works out exactly whether a loop closes, using integer arithmetic; it has never produced a bug and has seventeen tests. The second tries to spread the leftover gap fairly across every joint's wiggle room, to tell you "tight but fine" rather than just "not exact". Every single measured problem in this project is in that second half: it gives up on a 321-piece layout a child could build, freezes for four and a half seconds at 641, and tells you a 42-piece layout either fails or is merely tight depending on which piece you listed first. And the numbers it divides up are, by the plan's own words, made up. The alternative is to drop it and report the plain arithmetic instead: "this needs 3mm absorbed across 14 joints", which is honest about exactly the same uncertainty without pretending to a precise answer.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Keep it and you spend one to two weeks of formulation work making a precise-looking verdict out of invented inputs, carrying five critical gaps; drop it and you lose the ability to say WHICH joint is the problem, which was a stated differentiator.
Recommendation: B because the half you would be repairing produces a confident number from a budget the plan itself calls fabricated, and the bound tells the same truth without the machinery or the five critical gaps.
Completeness: A=8/10, B=7/10, C=10/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A buys precision from guessed inputs; B buys honesty and deletes half the engine's defects; C buys both and pays for the repair anyway.
Header: Stage 2
B) Replace the LP with the necessary-condition bound (recommended)
Keep Stage 1 exactly as it is. Delete `lp.ts`, `refine.ts` and the slop half of `residual.ts` and `result.ts`. The verdict becomes: exact closure is PASS; a gap exceeding the sum of the joints' axial give is a definite FAIL; anything between is reported as "needs 3mm absorbed across 14 joints, seat them firmly", with no ratio and no per-joint claim. This deletes K0c, H1-H5, `scale.test.ts`, and makes R15 and R17 moot. Effort: NEGATIVE, it removes roughly 350 to 400 lines and five critical gaps. ✅ removes every measured defect in the repository in one move rather than repairing them one at a time over one to two weeks of formulation work. ✅ the hedged middle band is a more honest rendering of a guessed budget than a ratio sliced at 0.8, and it matches the project's own rule that a verdict never claims more than it checked. ❌ loses per-joint attribution permanently, so the tool can no longer say which joint is the problem, and that was a stated differentiator even though it was never actually built.
A) Repair Stage 2 as planned
Keep the LP and execute K0c: remove the privileged cut joint and prove gauge independence across every cut point, fix the cycling, resolve the pass budget, correct `remainingMm`, harden the tests. Effort L (human ~1-2 weeks / CC ~1 day), risk high. Completeness 8/10. ✅ preserves the fair-distribution idea and, after H2 and H4, would make per-joint attribution real for the first time. ✅ keeps the one genuinely novel claim in the project, that bounded slop can be allocated and reported per joint. ❌ spends the single largest block of remaining work making a precise-looking verdict out of inputs the plan labels fabricated, and carries five critical gaps until it lands.
C) Repair Stage 2 AND add the bound, with the bound as the verdict
Do the full repair, then report the bound as the user-facing verdict while keeping the LP's per-joint ratios as diagnostics labelled unverified. Effort L plus the bound (human ~2 weeks / CC ~1 day). Completeness 10/10. ✅ the only option that both tells the honest headline and retains per-joint attribution for when K0b makes the budgets real. ✅ nothing is deleted, so a later decision can promote the ratios to the headline once measured. ❌ pays the entire repair cost to produce diagnostics that are labelled unverified, which is the most expensive way to reach the same headline as B.

State: approved
Actual answer: B) Replace the LP with the necessary-condition bound - D4 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: Stage 1's exact closure residual is unchanged. `lp.ts`, `refine.ts` and the slop half of `residual.ts` and `result.ts` are DELETED, roughly 350 to 400 lines: `lp.ts` (136) and `refine.ts` (141) go outright, and `residual.ts` (90) and `result.ts` (119) lose only their slop portions. The verdict becomes: exact closure is PASS; a gap exceeding the sum of the joints' axial give is a definite FAIL; anything between reports "needs N mm absorbed across M joints, seat them firmly", with no ratio and no per-joint claim. DELETED with it: K0c, tasks H1 through H5, and `scale.test.ts`. MOOT as a result: R15 (overlap cost model, which was about the LP's sibling), R17 (solve trigger), W10 (the restated minimax guarantee) and W11 (the coupled joint budget). K0b is STILL required, because the bound sums per-joint axial give and clearance needs the same measurements.
**Accepted ceiling 7/10, logged as decision e2b5d55f-11a2-40c3-b8f4-6e7be653b1f7.** Upgrade trigger: once K0b produces measured per-joint budgets with a validated spread, per-joint allocation becomes worth reinstating, and the LP returns with the privileged-cut-joint fix as its first requirement. The cut corner carries `gstack-shortcut(dec-e2b5d55f-11a2-40c3-b8f4-6e7be653b1f7): no per-joint attribution, bound only; upgrade when measured budgets justify allocation`.
History: D19A approved the two-stage engine and R18 scheduled K0c to repair Stage 2. Four review rounds cut features the user touches and never put Stage 2 itself on a menu. Reopened at R21 after the Outside Voice observed that every measured defect lives there, and that its product contribution is a precise-looking split over fabricated inputs.

### What survives, and what this deletes

**Survives unchanged:** Stage 1's exact residual in Z[zeta16] with its 17 tests;
the open-chain gap report (it is Stage 1 arithmetic); K0b measurement, now needed
for the bound's axial sum as well as for clearance; K4's catalogue; K5 save/load
with R20's semantics hash; Y2's two layers; X4/R19's overlap, which is geometry
rather than LP; Y3's support disclosure; the error boundary.

**Deleted:** `lp.ts`, `refine.ts`, the slop half of `residual.ts` and
`result.ts`, K0c, tasks H1-H5, `scale.test.ts`.

**Moot, no longer requiring resolution:** R15, R17, W10, W11. That removes four
of the seven items on the unresolved list in one decision.

**Changed shape:** `evaluate.ts`'s ratio-based verdicts. `TIGHT_THRESHOLD` and
`FAIL_THRESHOLD` existed to slice an LP ratio; with the bound there is no ratio,
so the verdict words come from the bound itself. Task H7 becomes "VerdictLine
consumes the bound's verdict" rather than "import the thresholds".



### R22: Does K4 ship parts a single chain cannot express?
Finding: Outside Voice finding 5, [P1], confidence 10/10, `app/src/App.tsx:107` with the chain data model - reviewer: plan-eng-review Outside Voice.
Plan baseline: K4 (task H8) is "12 common parts with plug/nest, polarity, plank width and footprint outline; switches carry their centreline shift". The crossing part is recorded as arriving with K4, and R19 carries the prove-note "a placed crossing piece permits same-layer overlap at its own footprint, once K4 lands".
Runtime evidence: `Layout` is an ordered end-to-end list of placements. A switch has three ends and a crossing has four, so their extra arms cannot be expressed and the engine would model them as plain curves. **Verified:** `App.tsx:107` maps over every entry in `BRIO_PARTS` with no filter, so any part added to the data file immediately gets a palette button. The crossing prove-note is unsatisfiable: expressing the second traversal of one physical crossing needs piece identity across two placements, which is the shared-vertex problem that remains unresolved.
Comparison grid:

```text
Commitment                     | Source/approval     | Current              | A (two-ended parts only)       | B (ship all 12 as planned)
Straights, curves, ramp        | K4                  | 6 parts, no ramp     | all shipped                    | all shipped
Switches (3 ends)              | K4 / H8             | planned              | EXCLUDED from v1               | shipped, inexpressible
Crossing (4 ends)              | K4, R19 prove-note  | planned              | EXCLUDED from v1               | shipped, inexpressible
Palette gating                 | none                | every part shows     | the data file IS the gate      | needs a new filter in App.tsx
Figure-eight expressible       | shared-vertex, open | reachable            | stated as NOT expressible      | reachable and unhandled
Overlap FAIL on a figure-eight | X4 / R19            | would be a false FAIL| becomes CORRECT: refuse it     | stays a known false FAIL
Crossing-permits-overlap note  | R19 carried note    | unsatisfiable        | WITHDRAWN as not applicable    | stays unsatisfiable
UNRESOLVED #6 (shared vertex)  | open                | open                 | CLOSED by refusal              | stays open
"Extensive database" ask       | original brief      | 6 parts              | ~9 two-ended parts             | 12 including 3 unusable        |
```

Question D5:
D5 — R22: Should the catalogue ship parts a single chain cannot represent?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on main; K4 plans switches and a crossing, and `App.tsx:107` puts every part in the palette with no filter.
ELI10: A switch has three ends and a crossing has four. The layout model is one ordered run of track, end to end, so there is nowhere for the third and fourth arms to go. Worse, the palette is built by looping over every part in the data file, so adding a switch immediately gives the user a button for a piece the engine cannot model. The alternative is to ship only pieces with two ends for v1 and say plainly that branching layouts are not expressible yet, which also turns the self-crossing figure-eight from a known false refusal into a correct one.
Stakes if we pick wrong: Ship them and the user can place a switch that silently becomes a plain curve, producing a confident verdict about a layout that is not what they built; exclude them and the catalogue is smaller than the brief's "extensive" ask.
Recommendation: A because a part the engine cannot model is worse than a part that is absent, and excluding them closes the shared-vertex question by refusal instead of leaving it open under a check that will fire on it.
Completeness: A=9/10, B=4/10
Pros / cons:
Net: A makes the catalogue honest about the model's limits; B ships three buttons that silently mislead.
A) Two-ended parts only for v1 (recommended)
K4 ships straights, curves and the ramp: every part with exactly two ends, around nine pieces. Switches and the crossing are excluded and recorded in TODOS.md with the chain model as the blocker. The data file remains the palette gate, so no filter is needed in `App.tsx`. The plan states plainly that branching and self-crossing layouts are not expressible in v1, which makes the overlap refusal of a figure-eight correct rather than a false FAIL, and closes the shared-vertex unresolved item by refusal. R19's crossing-permits-overlap prove-note is withdrawn as not applicable. Effort S, and it REMOVES work. ✅ a part the engine cannot model is worse than one that is absent, because the user places it, gets a confident verdict, and the verdict is about a different layout than the one they built. ✅ closes an unresolved item by refusal and converts a known false FAIL into a correct refusal, with no new code. ❌ the catalogue is smaller than the brief's "extensive database" ask, and switches are what real large layouts are made of.
B) Ship all 12 parts as planned
Keep switches and the crossing in K4, and add a palette filter so unusable parts are hidden, or accept that they appear. Effort S plus a filter (human ~1 day / CC ~20 min). Completeness 4/10. ✅ the catalogue matches the brief's "extensive" language and needs no later data migration when branching arrives. ✅ the part data is correct even if the engine cannot yet use it, so the transcription work is not repeated. ❌ either three palette buttons produce verdicts about layouts the user did not build, or a filter exists whose only job is to hide data the plan deliberately added.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Two-ended parts only for v1 - D5 answer, 2026-10-07.
Accepted scope: K4 ships only parts with exactly two ends: straights, curves and the ramp, around nine pieces. Switches and the crossing are EXCLUDED from v1 and recorded in TODOS.md with the single-chain model named as the blocker. The data file remains the palette gate, so no filter is added to `App.tsx`. The plan states plainly that branching and self-crossing layouts are not expressible in v1. CONSEQUENCES: the overlap refusal of a figure-eight becomes CORRECT rather than a known false FAIL; the shared-vertex unresolved item is CLOSED by refusal; and R19's carried prove-note "a placed crossing piece permits same-layer overlap at its own footprint" is WITHDRAWN as not applicable.
History: K4 (task H8) planned 12 parts including switches with their centreline shift, and the crossing was recorded as arriving with it. Reopened at R22 after the Outside Voice verified that `Layout` is an ordered end-to-end list with nowhere to put a third or fourth arm, and that `App.tsx:107` maps every entry in `BRIO_PARTS` with no filter, so any added part immediately gets a palette button.

## Final planning decisions

### TODOS.md updates

One new proposal, and it is covered by R22's own answer, which records switches
and the crossing in TODOS.md as part of its accepted scope. Recorded without
re-asking.

- **What:** switch and crossing parts in the catalogue.
- **Why:** switches are what real large layouts are made of, and the crossing is
  what makes a legal same-level figure-eight possible.
- **Pros:** the part dimensions are already researched; only the model blocks them.
- **Cons:** a switch has three ends and a crossing four, and `Layout` is an
  ordered end-to-end list, so neither is expressible. `App.tsx:107` would give
  each one a palette button that silently behaves as a plain curve.
- **Context:** excluded at R22 (2026-10-07). The blocker is the single-chain
  model, not the data. Returns with the multi-end topology work already recorded
  in TODOS.md under switches and the coupled multi-cycle solve.

**Parallel lanes:** `Lane A: K0b -> K4 -> Y2 -> R19 -> K5` (the spine) /
`Lane B: open-chain gap (independent)` / `Lane C: DOM test project (independent)`.
**Execution order:** launch A, B and C together; B and C merge whenever ready; A
is strictly sequential.
**Conflict flags:** the bound and the open-chain gap both touch
`result.ts`; sequence them or expect a merge.

### What this review changed

It began as a check on the post-Y1/Y2 architecture and ended by **deleting half
the engine**. R21 removes `lp.ts`, `refine.ts` and the slop portions of two more
files, roughly 350 to 400 lines, because every measured defect in the repository
lives there and its only product contribution was a precise-looking split over a
budget the plan itself calls fabricated. That single decision:

- deletes K0c and tasks H1 through H5, the longest pole in the previous sequence;
- makes **R15, R17, W10 and W11 moot**, four of the seven items that were unresolved this morning;
- drops the failure taxonomy from **8 reasons to 3**;
- removes **5 of the 12 critical gaps** outright rather than repairing them.

R22 then excluded switches and the crossing, which closed the shared-vertex
question **by refusal** and turned the figure-eight overlap refusal from a known
false FAIL into a correct one. R20 unblocked K5. Between them, the unresolved
list went from seven to two.

### Five of my own errors, caught and corrected

1. I cited `render.test.ts` as evidence for R19's exact-centre premise. **It tests
   the float renderer's sweep flag.** My own verification probe also used
   `walkFloat`, so I verified an exactness claim in floating point. Re-verified in
   integer arithmetic: 8xE and 16xE each give ONE exact `Cyc` centre.
2. R19's accepted scope omitted the interval convention. **With closed intervals
   the 8xE flagship fixture FAILS.** Half-open mod 16 is required.
3. "`fk.ts` needs no changes" was stated unconditionally. It is **contingent** on
   the ramp being a `StraightPart`, which E1 decides.
4. "No new dependency" checked runtime only. **A dev dependency is required** for
   the DOM tests, and `vitest.config.ts` cannot collect anything under `app/`.
5. I mislabelled this skill's Section 2 as the CEO skill's Error & Rescue Map and
   **skipped Code quality entirely**, then repaired it with three unscoped string
   edits that landed in the wrong review sections. All four errors are fixed and
   the repair is recorded.

### Retrospective check against the first design review

The first design review ran against a seven-surface product. The CEO re-review
then cut v1 to a single-chain append-only validator, and the eng review deleted
the minimax LP. **Nobody re-rated the design work against either change.** The
result, reconstructed here:

**Eight of the twelve G-tasks target surfaces no longer in v1** - G1 and G2
(build mode), G3 (library), G4 and G5 (publish and moderation), and two of the
three arcs inside G6. Four survive, three of those already shipped. Two broke
outright:

1. **G10 lost its data source.** D11's justification was *"`evaluateJoint`
   already produces every fact needed - the glyph layer and the text layer are
   two renderings of one dataset."* R21 deleted that dataset. G10 is in neither
   the accepted v1 scope table nor M1-M16, and cannot be built as written.
   Replaced by decision 20A below, retargeted from per-joint verdicts to
   per-piece facts.
2. **G8 is a no-op in shipped code.** `app/src/app.css:31-36` transitions
   `.joint`; verified that no element in `app/src/*.tsx` carries
   `className="joint"`. The product's single authored motion moment currently
   animates nothing, and `README.md:126` still claims it does. Repaired by
   decision 12A.

So the first review's two highest scores - Pass 2 at 10/10 and Pass 6 at 9/10 -
both rested on per-joint data that has since been deleted. Both are re-rated
from scratch below.

### Pass 1 - Information Architecture: 4/10 -> 9/10

The plan states canvas -> verdict -> palette and `app.css:4-8` implements it as a
`1fr auto auto` grid. It was a 4 because the hierarchy is stated and then
contradicted three ways, and because the headline feature of the release had no
visual specification at all.

**The elevation gap, stated precisely.** The only text addressing it in 6,652
lines is line 5094: three pattern names routed to `/design-consultation`. That
routes to task V34, which is **P2 and scheduled after the code**, and whose tool
reported `No OpenAI API key found` again today. **None of M1-M16 touches
`TrackCanvas.tsx` for elevation.** The headline feature had no specification, no
task, and an owner that cannot execute.

Four decisions: **1A** real plank width, **2A** deepened track edge, **3A** the
elevation encoding, **4A** the verdict-line composition rule.

```
  AT A CROSSING                        AWAY FROM A CROSSING

  ════════════╗                          ░░░░░░░░░░░░░  <- cast shadow, offset
              ║   <- elevated run,     ══════════════   <- elevated run
  ───────  ───╫───  ───────               (shadow says "this one is up")
         ^    ║
         |    ║                        ON A RAMP
    lower run cut by a casing
    stroke in paper colour             ═╫╫╫╫┤┤┤┤├├├►   hatch closes up toward
    upper run continuous                               the high end, one arrow
```

Re-rated 9/10. Not a 10 because what the first click means on an empty chain was
still open at this point; settled at 22B in Pass 7.

### Pass 2 - Interaction State Coverage: 2/10 -> 9/10

Section 11 diagnosed this itself at line 5099 - *"no loading, empty, error or
partial state is specified for the layer display, the new-component action or
the overlap report"* - and then graded it P2.

```
 NEW SURFACE          | EMPTY            | ERROR              | SUCCESS        | PARTIAL
 ---------------------|------------------|--------------------|----------------|------------------
 Elevated display     | 3A casing/shadow | 7A span highlight  | 3A             | implied (see below)
 Overlap failure      | n/a              | 7A own FAIL state  | n/a            | 23A span extent
 Support disclosure   | n/a              | n/a                | 8A narrowed    | 8A Not-checked list
 Open-chain gap (M9)  | shipped hint     | n/a                | 11A progress   | 11A
 Ramp in palette      | 22B base level   | 6A derived label   | 6A             | n/a
 Crash screen (M10)   | n/a              | 9A chain as text   | n/a            | n/a
 Mid-build chain      | shipped, good    | 5A no early verdict| 5A auto-promote| 11A
```

Seven decisions: **5A** default open and auto-promote on proximity, **6A** the
derived ramp label, **7A** overlap as its own FAIL state with the pair marked,
**8A** the narrowed EXACT claim, **9A** the crash screen, **10A** the unmatched
ramp, **11A** the open-chain progress line.

Re-rated 9/10. Not a 10 because one partial state remains implied rather than
specified: a chain whose last piece is a ramp, dangling mid-climb, is covered by
3A's hatch and 10A's copy between them but was never specified on its own.

### Pass 3 - User Journey & Emotional Arc: 4/10 -> 9/10

The plan's three failure arcs from D6 are the best design work in the document,
and *"8mm short. That's normal with 45-degree curves - here's how to close it."*
is its best sentence. **All three are written for a product that does not
exist**: they assume drag-and-drop ("releases the last piece", "the held breath",
"layout settles once"), cut at D5 of the CEO re-review, plus build mode, library
and moderation, all cut. Arc 2 needs the 11A calibration diff, also cut.

Storyboard for the product actually being built. Transcription of accepted
requirements plus this review's decisions, recorded without a separate question:

```
 STEP | USER DOES                      | USER FEELS            | PLAN SPECIFIES?
 -----|--------------------------------|-----------------------|---------------------------------
 1    | opens app, empty canvas        | mild dread: this      | YES - active empty state names
      |                                | usually ends badly    | the circle (TrackCanvas.tsx:51)
 2    | clicks a large curve           | nothing happens at    | YES now - 12A retargets the
      |                                | the click target      | settle to the arriving piece
 3    | keeps clicking, ends drift     | orientation: how far  | YES now - 11A quiet progress line
 4    | the two ends come close        | the held breath       | YES now - 5A promotes to the
      |                                |                       | closure verdict at that moment
 5    | reads the verdict              | relief or deflation   | YES - 4A one sentence, grey
      |                                |                       | never reads as green
 6    | clicks the ramp to try a       | curiosity, slight     | YES now - 6A tile says
      | bridge                         | uncertainty           | "Ramp up, +64mm"
 7    | closes the loop, one ramp      | confusion: why won't  | YES now - 10A names the geometry
      | only                           | this close            | and the one piece that fixes it
 8    | adds the ramp down             | earned satisfaction   | YES now - 8A "Closes exactly."
      |                                |                       | plus what was not checked
 9    | two runs cross on one level    | surprise, I thought   | YES now - 7A plus 23A highlight
      |                                | that was fine         | the conflicting span
 10   | builds it on the floor         | does it match the     | PARTIAL - no build mode in v1;
      |                                | picture               | plank now draws true width (1A)
```

Time horizons: **5 seconds** is the empty state and the canvas. **5 minutes** is
click, progress line, closure verdict. **5 years** is whether the bridge is the
reason you open it again.

One decision: **12A**, retarget the one authored motion moment from the deleted
joint glyphs onto the arriving segment, delete the dead `.joint` rule, and
correct the stale README claim.

Re-rated 9/10. Not a 10 because step 10 has no surface in v1 and arc 2's
calibration tone rule is retained with nowhere yet to fire.

### Pass 4 - AI Slop Risk: 5/10 -> 9/10

Classifier: **OPERATE** (App UI). No marketing surface in v1, so landing-page
rules do not apply. The printed build sheet would be READ, but build mode is out
of scope.

**Hard rejections.** #1 clear, full-bleed SVG stage rather than a card grid. #3,
#4, #6 not applicable. #7 clear - `app.css:4-8` is a three-row grid and D7's
card rule is honoured exactly: only `.piece` has a box. **#5 was live** - the
same sentence rendered twice about 40px apart, `VerdictLine.tsx:36-39` and
`App.tsx:129-133` - **and 4A resolved it.** #2's image half does not apply; its
brand half does and is blocked on D20's counsel question, not on design.

**The typography finding lands harder than a generic miss.** D9's direction is
toy warmth crossed with instrument precision, and the instrument half has exactly
three carriers: a technical mono on measurements, tabular figures, and hairline
measurement rules. The mono never loads. The hairlines measure 1.40:1. So the
only differentiating axis of the accepted direction is, in the shipped pixels,
almost entirely absent - leaving cream ground plus system sans plus a wood
accent, which is guessable from the category alone. D9 anticipated exactly this
risk and named it; what was missing was the measurement showing it had happened.

Three decisions: **13A** self-host IBM Plex Sans and Mono, **14A** visually
hidden headings plus landmarks, **15A** one job per region.

Re-rated 9/10. Not a 10 because litmus 1 stays NO, blocked externally.

### Pass 5 - Design System Alignment: 3/10 -> 7/10

Still no DESIGN.md. The first review rated this 2 -> 4/10 and routed the gap to
`/design-consultation` at D10, which became task V34, P2, after the code. The
blocker was reproduced today.

It scored 3 rather than 4 because something got worse: this review minted real
token values pass by pass - `--track-edge` #8a7349, a new `--track-shadow`
#847f76, a self-hosted type stack, a fifth verdict register (10A), an overlap
highlight (23A), a neutral open-chain mark (11A) - plus six new components. That
is design-system work being decided in a review that routed design-system work
elsewhere.

**New components introduced, checked against the existing vocabulary:** the
"Not checked" list (4A), the derived ramp tile (6A), the casing/shadow/hatch
triplet (3A), the crash screen (9A), the conflicting-span highlight (23A), the
quiet progress line (11A), the base-level control (22B). All but one reuse
existing primitives - text rows, SVG paths, the untouched card rule. The single
new primitive is the **hatch pattern**, which needs a definition in the token
file rather than an inline SVG `<pattern>` per use.

One decision: **16A**, write DESIGN.md from the decided values, marking which are
measured and which remain provisional, and keep `/design-consultation` for the
full researched system later.

Re-rated 7/10. **This is the overall score and the reason the plan is not
design-complete.** A file assembled from review decisions is not a researched
system: no type scale exploration, no considered palette, no depth spec beyond
the one data-bearing shadow.

### Pass 6 - Responsive & Accessibility: 3/10 -> 9/10

The first review scored this 5 -> 9/10 and the 9 rested on D11/G10, which R21
invalidated. Measured failures are in the evidence block above.

Five decisions: **17A** darken the two failing verdict colours to `--tight`
#875e08 (4.78:1) and `--unknown` #6b675f (4.66:1), **18A** fix the two real
paragraphs to 16px and name a separate floor for data labels inside controls,
**19A** raise both undersized controls to 44px under `pointer: coarse`, **20A**
a polite live region plus a keyboard-navigable chain listing, **21A** render the
phone refusal as real DOM.

A constraint worth recording from 17A: after the change all four verdict colours
sit in a luminance band of 0.106 to 0.137, so in greyscale they are near
identical and the four glyph shapes carry the entire print signal. **No verdict
may ever be colour-only.** That was already true before the change; the fix does
not degrade print legibility, it confirms the glyphs are load-bearing.
`--unknown` deliberately lands on the same tone as `--ink-soft`, which is correct:
UNKNOWN is the absence of a verdict colour.

Re-rated 9/10. Not a 10 because only one of D12's responsive pairings is still in
scope - editor desktop plus tablet, with build mode, library and buy list all cut
- and because 20A will rot untested unless M10's DOM test project lands first.

### Pass 7 - Unresolved Design Decisions (unscored register)

```
 DECISION NEEDED                        | DISPOSITION
 ---------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------
 Base level on an empty chain           | RESOLVED at 22B - the field and a control are
                                        | added; a zero-ramp elevated loop becomes
                                        | expressible. Closes a standing unresolved item.
 The overlap marker's visual form       | RESOLVED at 23A - highlight the conflicting span
 Product name                           | BLOCKED on D20's counsel question, not on
                                        | design. Holds litmus 1 at NO.
 Hierarchy for inventory, buy list,     | MOOT for v1 - all four surfaces cut by the CEO
 diagnostics, onboarding                | re-review. D3 -> B's deferral no longer applies.
 Build-mode back-step (G2)              | MOOT for v1 - build mode is out of scope.
 The full researched design system       | ROUTED, with 16A as the interim file. V34 stays
                                        | P2 and still cannot run.
```

**22B went against this reviewer's recommendation** (22C, reserve the field
without a control) and the user chose the control. Consequences recorded rather
than re-litigated: `Layout` gains a base-level field, so M11 resolves as "add
it" rather than "refuse it"; the ramp tile's empty-chain label follows the base
level; the Y2 parity check becomes relative to that base; a zero-ramp elevated
loop becomes expressible, which closes the eng review's standing base-level
question; the support disclosure from 8A fires for a base-1 zero-ramp layout
because every placement is elevated; the clearance predicate must handle a layout
with no floor run at all; and the control defaults to floor so the common path is
unchanged. It lives with the chain-edit controls that 15A moved next to the
layout, not in the palette.

### NOT in scope (design)

- **A researched design system** - 16A writes DESIGN.md from decided values only. The full system, with type scale exploration and a considered palette, stays routed to `/design-consultation` at V34.
- **Visual mockups** - unavailable, not declined. `$D variants` returned exit 2 with `No OpenAI API key found`. Second consecutive review with zero mockups.
- **A visible product name or wordmark** - 14B not taken. Blocked on D20's counsel question over the BRIO trademark, so litmus 1 stays NO by external dependency.
- **Hierarchy for inventory, buy list, diagnostics and onboarding** - no longer a deferral, now moot: all four surfaces were cut from v1 by the CEO re-review.
- **Build mode, library browse, publish and moderation design** - G1-G5 cut with their surfaces; filed as deferred-to-v2 at 26A.
- **Per-joint glyphs and the four-channel joint encoding** - gone with the solver that produced per-joint ratios. 7A and 23A bring a marker back only for overlap, which is a claim the engine actually makes.
- **Designing the editor for a phone** - still refused per D12. 21A improves the refusal's delivery, not its substance.
- **Reduced-opacity, line-weight and positional-offset elevation encodings** - all three measured and rejected at 3A: opacity has no contrast to spend (1.26:1 at 50%), weight became the plank channel at 1A, and an offset would move a piece off its true plan position.

### Unresolved design decisions

- **Product name** - blocked on D20's counsel question over the BRIO trademark, not on design. Holds litmus 1 (brand unmistakable in first screen) at NO until it returns. 14A deliberately works around it with a hidden h1 that says what the tool does rather than what it is called.
- **The researched design system** - routed, not unresolved: 16A writes the interim file from decided values, and V34 keeps the full consultation. It stays blocked by the missing OpenAI API key, which is now the second consecutive review to record that.
- **One partial state** - a chain whose last piece is a ramp, dangling mid-climb, is covered by 3A's hatch and 10A's copy between them but was never specified in its own right. Named rather than silently defaulted.

```
  | Pass 5  (Design Sys) | 3/10 → 7/10   interim DESIGN.md, not a system|

  | Pass 7  (Decisions)  | 2 resolved, 0 deferred, 1 blocked, 4 moot   |

  | Decisions deferred   | 0                                           |
  | Overall design score | 2/10 → 7/10  (lowest rated pass = Pass 5)   |
  +====================================================================+
```

**Not design-complete.** The overall score is the lowest rated pass, and Pass 5
is at 7/10 because a DESIGN.md assembled from review decisions is not a
researched design system. Every other rated pass is at 9/10. One decision went
against the reviewer's recommendation (22B over 22C) and its consequences are
recorded above rather than argued.

**What this review changed.** It found that the headline feature of the release -
two-level track - had **no visual specification, no implementation task, and an
owner that cannot run**, and that two of the three encodings the plan had routed
were already arithmetically impossible. It replaced them with occlusion, which
costs no colour and survives greyscale print. It then measured the shipped UI and
found the declared dominant element at 1.62:1, two of four verdict colours below
the text floor, the plank drawn 3.3 to 4 times too thin against an overlap check
that decides on plank width, the declared typefaces never loaded, zero headings,
zero motion, and a default that produces seven consecutive rejections on the path
to the app's own flagship fixture.

**Three of this reviewer's own corrections.** The independent voice claimed the
overlap failure "will render as UNKNOWN" at `VerdictLine.tsx:53`; that is a
prediction about unwritten code, so it was downgraded from defect to
specification gap. Its claim that the plank is drawn 3.3x too thin was verified
by execution rather than accepted, and the measurement also exposed something it
had not said: the width varies with layout extent, so the same piece draws at two
sizes. And the 44px touch floor was initially going to be applied at a width
breakpoint until checking showed the plan scopes it to touch surfaces and that
`pointer: coarse` is the precise query.

## Eng review, third pass (2026-10-08)

Target, fixed at the Scope gate: this plan file,
`~/.gstack/projects/radudiaconescu/ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md`.
Invoked immediately after the second design review to check the architectural
consequences of its decisions 22B, 23A and 20A, and to sequence the 22 new
N-tasks against the 16 existing M-tasks. Report file: this file.

### Review preparation

**Retrospective.** Three commits. `residual.ts`, `result.ts`, `evaluate.ts`,
`TrackCanvas.tsx`, `VerdictLine.tsx` and `App.tsx` each have exactly two, because
every one of them was rewritten one commit ago by the R21 deletion, and every one
is touched again by this work. Churn is concentrated in the files that were just
rewritten. The `git log --grep=revert` hit is a body-text match on the initial
commit, not a revert. Named future paths (`engine/src/feasible.ts`,
`engine/src/doc.ts`, `app/src/ErrorBoundary.tsx`, `DESIGN.md`,
`app/public/fonts/`) are `not available`: no history exists and none is claimed.

**Search check.** Aside is not installed (`NEEDS_ASIDE: Darwin`), so this ran on
host WebSearch. One genuinely new architectural pattern was found in the selected
work: 20A's accessible alternative to an interactive SVG canvas. Current practice
is settled and the plan's approach matches it: keep `role="img"` with a real
label on the SVG and add a **visually-hidden data table** as the alternative
representation, Tab between widgets and arrow keys within. Labelled **[Layer 1]**,
tried and true, not invention. Two pitfalls apply directly: never put
`aria-hidden` or `role="presentation"` on a focusable element, which creates a
phantom control, and WCAG 1.4.11 non-text contrast is named explicitly, which
independently confirms the design review's measurement of the track at 1.62:1.
Sources: a11y-collective.com/blog/svg-accessibility, uxpin.com keyboard patterns
for complex widgets, disabilityworld.org accessible data-visualisation tooling
2026, web-accessibility-checker.com ARIA label mistakes.

The other three changes are not new patterns: an interval return from a geometric
overlap test is ordinary computational geometry, a base-level field is a scalar on
an existing interface, and a live region is one ARIA attribute.

**TODOS cross-reference.** `brio-TODOS.md:429` carries **"Open and unassigned:
the overlap narrowphase primitive - Not deferred, not decided."** It records
measured AABB inflation of 1.46x (best orientation) to 3.80x (45 degrees) on the
real parts, notes that BRIO headings are multiples of 22.5 degrees so the inflated
cases are the common ones, states that a check whose job is to refuse layouts
"would refuse buildable ones", and ends "Needs a decision before X4 is
implemented." Decision 23A now requires that undecided primitive to return an
angular interval. The TODO already predicts the likely answer, "arc-against-arc
reduces to a centre-distance test against the radii plus an angular-interval
check", which is exactly the interval 23A wants, so the two converge rather than
conflict. This is carried into Section 1 as a finding.

**Complexity check.** Union of the 16 M-tasks and 22 N-tasks, excluding M2 and M3
which shipped in `826008e`:

## Decision ledger (eng review, third pass)

### SC1: which primitive decides overlap, now that 23A requires a conflicting interval

Finding: SC1, `[P1] (confidence: 10/10)`, `brio-TODOS.md:429`, raised by this
reviewer from the TODOS cross-reference.

Plan baseline: **undecided.** R15 chose AABB all-pairs over a spatial grid, and
that cost reasoning was accepted. The TODO that records it immediately states the
limit: "AABB is a reject filter, not a decision primitive," with measured
footprint inflation of 1.46x in the best orientation, 3.11x at 22.5 degrees,
3.80x at 45 degrees for the E curve, and 3.79x for a D straight at 45 degrees. It
closes "Needs a decision before X4 is implemented." No decision has been made.
M6's accepted scope names an exact tier with half-open intervals mod 16 plus a
float annulus band, which describes the *tiers* but never names the primitive
that decides a pair.

Runtime evidence: `engine/src/feasible.ts` does not exist, so there is no
behaviour to observe. What is verified in the shipped tree: `pose.ts`'s exact
`walk` produces an exact `Cyc` centre for every arc (confirmed in integer
arithmetic, 8xE and 16xE each give one identical centre), and
`engine/data/brio.ts` carries `gaugeWidthMm: 40` for all six parts. Verified by
execution during the second design review: 16 large curves traverse the same
circle twice and still report `EXACT`, and 8 left curves followed by 8 right
curves produce two circles of radius 182 centred at (0,182) and (0,-182) whose
centre separation is exactly 364, the sum of the radii, so they are tangent and
no centreline test can catch them.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current | A: exact arc/segment | B: polygon SAT | C: AABB decides |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Decision primitive | undecided | exact arc-arc, arc-seg, seg-seg | SAT on oriented quads, arcs subdivided | AABB extents |
| Exactness of the arc-arc case | none | exact, integer where available | approximate, depends on subdivision | not applicable |
| Conflicting interval for 23A | not produced | produced directly | derived from the overlap polygon | not produced |
| False refusals on 22.5deg headings | 1.46x-3.80x inflation | none from the primitive | bounded by subdivision error | 1.46x-3.80x retained |
| Catches a tangency (8L+8R) | no | yes, with plank-width footprints | yes | yes, and also false ones |
| Broadphase | AABB all-pairs, approved R15 | AABB all-pairs, unchanged, demoted to filter | AABB all-pairs, unchanged, demoted to filter | AABB all-pairs, unchanged |
| Half-open intervals mod 16 | approved R19/D2 | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |
| New module | `feasible.ts`, approved D3 | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |

Checks: the broadphase row cites R15 and the interval convention cites R19/D2;
neither is re-approved here, and both are identical across all three columns.
The new-module row cites D3. Only the decision primitive and what follows from
it vary.

Question D4:

D4 — SC1: which primitive actually decides that two pieces overlap?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; Scope Challenge C, the one
architectural choice this review exists to make.

ELI10: The tool is about to start refusing layouts because two pieces of track
occupy the same floor. Something has to decide that, and the only thing chosen so
far is a bounding box, which the plan's own notes measured as up to 3.8 times
larger than the real piece. A box that big would refuse layouts that actually
build. On top of that, the design review now needs the check to report exactly
which stretch of track conflicts, which a bounding box cannot say.

Stakes if we pick wrong: the headline refusal of the release fires on buildable
layouts, and the product's credibility depends on never doing that.

Recommendation: A, because arc-against-arc reduces to a centre-distance test plus
an angular-interval intersection, which is exact, cheap, and hands back the
interval 23A needs as a by-product rather than as extra work.

Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=2/10

Pros / cons:
A) Exact arc and segment narrowphase (recommended)
  ✅ Arc-against-arc is a centre-distance test against the radii plus an angular-interval intersection, which is exact and is precisely what the open TODO predicted as the likely answer.
  ✅ Returns the conflicting interval directly, so 23A's canvas highlight costs nothing extra, and the half-open mod 16 convention already approved at R19 is exactly the comparison it performs.
  ✅ Catches the tangency case that defeats a centreline test, once plank-width footprints are used: 8 left plus 8 right curves give centre separation 364 against radii summing to 364.
  ❌ Three distinct pair cases to implement and test (arc-arc, arc-segment, segment-segment), and the segment cases need their own closed forms rather than reusing the arc one.
B) Polygon SAT on oriented quads
  ✅ One uniform algorithm for every part shape, so adding a new part never adds a new pair case, and switches and crossings would need no new geometry later.
  ✅ Produces an overlap region rather than an interval, which is strictly more information and would support an area threshold if tolerance ever needs one.
  ❌ Approximate: arcs must be subdivided, so the refusal depends on subdivision count, which is exactly the class of evidence this project rejects everywhere else.
  ❌ The interval 23A needs has to be recovered from the polygon rather than read off, adding work to the one consumer that exists.
C) Keep AABB as the decider
  ✅ Already chosen at R15 with accepted cost reasoning, and needs no new geometry at all.
  ✅ Fastest possible check and trivially correct as a conservative over-approximation.
  ❌ Measured 1.46x to 3.80x footprint inflation, and BRIO's 22.5-degree headings make the inflated orientations the common ones, so it would refuse buildable layouts.
  ❌ Cannot produce the conflicting interval, so 23A is unimplementable under this option.

Net: A is exact and hands back the interval for free, B is general and approximate, C is cheap and refuses layouts that build.

Header: Overlap primitive
Options:
A) Exact arc and segment narrowphase (recommended)
Arc-against-arc is a centre-distance test against the radii plus an angular-interval intersection, which is exact and is precisely what the open TODO predicted. Returns the conflicting interval directly, so 23A's canvas highlight costs nothing extra, and R19's half-open mod 16 convention is exactly the comparison it performs. Catches the tangency that defeats a centreline test once plank-width footprints are used. Cost: three distinct pair cases to implement and test, with their own closed forms.
B) Polygon SAT on oriented quads
One uniform algorithm for every part shape, so a new part never adds a pair case and switches would need no new geometry. Produces an overlap region rather than an interval, which would support an area threshold later. Cost: approximate, because arcs must be subdivided, so the refusal depends on subdivision count, which is the class of evidence this project rejects elsewhere; and 23A's interval must be recovered from the polygon.
C) Keep AABB as the decider
Already chosen at R15 with accepted cost reasoning and needs no new geometry. Fastest possible check and conservative. Cost: measured 1.46x to 3.80x footprint inflation, and BRIO's 22.5-degree headings make the inflated orientations the common ones, so it refuses buildable layouts; and it cannot produce the interval, so 23A becomes unimplementable.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Exact arc and segment narrowphase, answer D4, 2026-10-08
Accepted scope: `engine/src/feasible.ts` implements an exact narrowphase with
three pair cases. Arc-against-arc: compare centre distance against the radii,
then intersect angular intervals using R19's half-open convention mod 16.
Arc-against-segment and segment-against-segment: closed forms. The primitive
returns the conflicting interval, which 23A's canvas highlight consumes directly.
AABB all-pairs is retained from R15 and demoted to a reject filter that never
decides. Footprints are plank-width, so the 8L+8R tangency is caught. This closes
`brio-TODOS.md:429` "Open and unassigned: the overlap narrowphase primitive".
Not approved here and still pending: any tolerance or area threshold on the
overlap region, and the float annulus band's half-width, which M6 and K5's
frozen constants table own.
History: none

### SC3: self-hosting IBM Plex is a distribution change the plan has not covered

Finding: SC3, `[P2] (confidence: 9/10)`, `app/src/tokens.css:39-40` with task N15,
raised by this reviewer at Scope Challenge A's distribution check.

Plan baseline: decision 13A, answer D15 of the second design review, approved
"self-host the two faces" so that the declared `'IBM Plex Sans'` and
`'IBM Plex Mono'` actually load. The distribution details were never named.

Runtime evidence: verified, no `@font-face` and no font link exists anywhere in
`app/src` or `app/index.html`, so the effective faces today are `system-ui` and
`ui-monospace`.

**Disposition: necessary implementation of 13A, no new question.** Three items
are mechanics of the already-approved behaviour rather than separable choices,
so they are recorded here and carried into N15 without a further approval:

1. **Licence vendoring is a legal necessity, not a choice.** IBM Plex ships under
   the SIL Open Font License 1.1, which requires the licence to travel with the
   font files. `app/public/fonts/OFL.txt` ships beside the woff2 files.
2. **`font-display: optional`**, derived from an approved requirement rather than
   chosen here. Pass 5's carry-list states "Tabular figures are mandatory. This
   product renders columns of measurements everywhere... Proportional figures
   make those columns jitter as values change." A swap period produces exactly
   that jitter on first load, in the columns the requirement protects, so the one
   `font-display` value consistent with the approved requirement is the one that
   never swaps after its block period.
3. **Latin subset only.** The product's entire corpus is millimetre figures,
   part codes and English UI copy. No approval is needed to not ship glyphs the
   product cannot render.

Nothing user-visible is decided here that 13A did not already decide. If a future
requirement needs non-Latin glyphs or a visible swap, that is a new choice.

Checks: the D4 row and the 23A row cite their answers and are identical in both

Question D5:

D5 — SC2: does the prior-art read move ahead of the overlap implementation?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; Scope Challenge C, sequencing only.

ELI10: There is an existing open-source wooden-track tool nobody here has ever
opened, and the task to go read it has been sitting at low priority through five
reviews. The overlap geometry we just chose at D4 is the hardest remaining piece
of work. Reading someone else's solution first might save real time on it, or it
might be three hours that teaches us nothing.

Stakes if we pick wrong: either three hours spent on a repository that turns out
to be irrelevant, or the hardest geometry in the project written from scratch
while a working reference sat unopened for a sixth review.

the implementation, and three hours against the longest pole in the release is
cheap insurance.

Completeness: Note: options differ in kind, not coverage — no completeness score.
Both keep M15 at P2 and neither reopens D4.

Pros / cons:
A) Read it before the overlap work (recommended)
  ✅ D4 already fixed the primitive, so the read cannot destabilise the design; it can only inform three pair-case implementations that nobody here has written before.
  ✅ Three hours against the lowest-compression task in the release, and it finally closes an item five reviews have flagged and none has actioned.
  ❌ Three hours that may teach nothing, since the plan has never recorded what the repository actually contains or whether it handles arcs at all.
B) Keep M15 at P2, after the geometry
  ✅ Keeps the critical path focused on work that is already specified and decided, with no detour into an unknown repository.
  ✅ Reading it afterwards still has value as a cross-check on the implementation that was written.
  ❌ The task's own text says "before building the geometry", so leaving it last keeps the plan internally contradictory for a sixth review.
  ❌ Any reusable approach it contains arrives too late to save the work it would have saved.

Net: A spends three hours before the hardest task to possibly shorten it; B keeps the path clear and reads the reference afterwards, if at all.
