## Section 1 — Architecture review (eng review, third pass)

Findings, most severe first:

```
[P1] (confidence: 9/10) engine/src/parts/schema.ts:92-96 — 22B's base-level control
     can invalidate an already-valid layout. The interface is only
     `{ placements, closed }`, so there is no per-placement level to clamp:
     level(i) = baseLevel + ups(i) - downs(i), derived wholly from one scalar plus
     the ramp sequence. Setting baseLevel to 1 on a chain that opens with an
     up-ramp puts level 2 in range. 22B specified the control, not what happens
     to an existing chain.                                             -> A1
[P1] (confidence: 9/10) FACTUAL CORRECTION, this reviewer's own — the design
     review and its handoff said 22B makes the Y2 parity check "relative to that
     base". It does not. A closed chain requires level(end) == level(start), so
     ups == downs regardless of baseLevel; parity is UNCHANGED. What 22B actually
     adds is a RANGE check on every prefix, which nothing had planned. No
     behaviour question: the correction is recorded and the range check is folded
     into A1's remedy.                                                  -> A2
[P1] (confidence: 9/10) N18 as written — "returns its conflicting angular
     interval" is mis-typed for two of the three pair cases D4 just approved.
     schema.ts:57 and :63 establish two part kinds, so a pair has three
     combinations: arc-arc yields an angular interval PER ARC, arc-segment yields
     an angular interval plus a parametric one, segment-segment yields two
     parametric ones. 23A needs the span on BOTH pieces, so the return is a
     per-piece pair of ranges in each piece's own unit.                 -> A3
[P2] (confidence: 9/10) The overlap result count is unbounded. 16xE overlaps at
     many pairs, 23A's copy "names both pieces" assumes exactly one, and at 640
     pieces the all-pairs filter admits up to ~205k candidate pairs. No cap is
     specified for results or for canvas highlights.                    -> A4
[P2] (confidence: 9/10) RECORDED, necessary implementation of 20A — the verdict
     owns the ONLY live region; the chain table is visually hidden by clip-path,
     never by aria-hidden, because the researched pitfall is that aria-hidden on
     focusable rows creates phantom controls, and a second live region would
     announce every click twice. No separate approval: these are mechanics of
     20A's approved behaviour.
[P2] (confidence: 10/10) No `.github` directory exists. The new artifacts (woff2
     fonts plus OFL, DESIGN.md) and the app itself have no build, publish or
     update path, and no review across five passes has addressed deployment.
                                                                        -> TODO
[P3] (confidence: 10/10) RECORDED, positive — deferring M8 at D2 removes the only
     parse boundary from this release. No auth, no network, no persistence, no
     user text input, and self-hosted fonts make no third-party request, so the
     security surface this release is effectively nil. No remedy needed.
```

### Data flow after 22B, 23A and D4

```
  click a palette piece
        |
        v
  Layout { placements, closed, baseLevel }          <- baseLevel is NEW (22B)
        |
        +--> walkFloat(layout, getPart) ----------------------+
        |        exact poses + per-segment geometry           |
        |                                                     |
        v                                                     v
  level(i) = baseLevel + ups(i) - downs(i)            TrackCanvas (one walk, D3)
        |    MUST stay in {0,1}  <- NEW range check (A2)        |
        |                                                  +----+----+
        v                                                  v         v
  solveLayout -> exact closure residual                  SVG      hidden
        |                                              (casing,   chain table
        +--> parity: ups == downs   <- UNCHANGED by     shadow,   (20A, D3)
        |    baseLevel (A2 corrects the plan)           hatch)
        |
        +--> AABB all-pairs filter (R15, demoted)
        |        |
        |        v
        |    exact narrowphase (D4) -> per-piece ranges (A3)
        |        arc-arc | arc-seg | seg-seg
        |                 |
        v                 v
  evaluateLayout    conflicting spans -> canvas highlight (23A)
        |
        v
  VerdictLine: one sentence + "Not checked" list (4A)
        + the ONLY live region (20A)
```

One realistic production failure per new path:

| New path | Realistic failure | Handled? |
|---|---|---|
| baseLevel control | user sets base 1 on a chain opening with an up-ramp; level 2 becomes reachable | **NO — A1** |
| exact narrowphase | arc-arc centre distance equals the radius sum exactly (8L+8R tangency); a strict inequality misses it | partly: D4 names plank-width footprints, but the comparison operator is not stated |
| overlap interval | a self-traversing layout returns hundreds of pairs; the verdict names two | **NO — A4** |
| hidden chain table | implemented with aria-hidden, making focusable rows phantom controls | recorded above as 20A mechanics |
| self-hosted fonts | woff2 fails to load; tabular columns fall back and jitter | SC3: `font-display: optional` |
| no CI/CD | nothing builds or publishes the app or its fonts | **NO — TODO** |

### A1: what happens to an existing chain when the base level changes

Finding: A1, `[P1] (confidence: 9/10)`, `engine/src/parts/schema.ts:92-96`, raised
by this reviewer in Section 1.

Plan baseline: decision 22B, answer D24 of the second design review, approved "a
base-level control ships now and `Layout` gains the field; a fully elevated loop
becomes expressible", defaulting to floor. What the control does to a chain that
already has pieces was not specified.

Runtime evidence: verified in the shipped tree. `engine/src/parts/schema.ts`
lines 92-96 are the whole interface:

```
92: export interface Layout {
93:   readonly placements: readonly Placement[];
94:   /** True when the last piece's free end joins the first piece's start. */
95:   readonly closed: boolean;
96: }
```

There is no per-placement level field, so after 22B every placement's level is a
pure function of one scalar and the ramp sequence before it. `feasible.ts` and
the ramp part do not exist yet, so the invalid state is not yet reachable; it
becomes reachable the moment M4's ramp and 22B's control both land.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current | A: empty-chain only | B: report out-of-range | C: refuse the change |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A1 when baseLevel is settable | unspecified | only while the chain is empty | any time | any time |
| A1 result of an invalidating change | unspecified | unreachable by construction | named verdict failure, layout kept | change refused, reason named, layout kept |
| Prefix range check in {0,1} | not planned | required | required | required |
| Y2 parity (ups == downs) | approved Y2, unchanged by baseLevel per A2 | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |
| 6A derived ramp label | approved 6A | follows baseLevel | follows baseLevel | follows baseLevel |
| 8A support disclosure | approved 8A | fires whenever any placement is elevated | unchanged | unchanged |
| 22B control exists | approved D24 | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |

Checks: the parity, ramp-label, disclosure and control-exists rows cite Y2, 6A,
8A and D24 and are identical across all three columns; none is reopened. The
prefix range check is new and common to all three, so it is approved by whichever
option is chosen. Only the control's availability and the result of an
invalidating change vary.

Question D6:

D6 — A1: what happens when the base level changes under an existing chain?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; Section 1, the architectural hole in 22B.

ELI10: The level of every piece is worked out by counting ramps from whatever
level the layout starts on. Decision 22B added a control to change that starting
level. If a layout already climbs a ramp and you then say "actually this starts
on the bridge", the track would need a third level, which does not exist. Nothing
currently says what the tool does at that moment.

Stakes if we pick wrong: one click on a settings control silently puts a valid
layout into a state the engine cannot represent, and there is no per-piece level
to fall back to because the interface stores only the piece list.

Recommendation: B, because this product's whole discipline is to report what is
true rather than to prevent actions, and a named failure the user can undo by
toggling back is consistent with every other verdict in the tool.

Completeness: A=7/10, B=10/10, C=9/10

Pros / cons:
A) Settable only while the chain is empty
  ✅ The invalid state becomes unreachable by construction, with no new failure path to implement, test or word.
  ✅ Matches how a builder thinks: you decide whether this layout is a ground loop or a raised loop before you start placing track.
  ❌ You cannot change your mind, so a user forty pieces in must clear the layout to try the other base, which 15A just moved Clear away from the palette to make harder.
B) Report the out-of-range result, keep the layout (recommended)
  ✅ Consistent with the product's one rule: the engine reports what is true and never blocks an edit, which is why grey means "could not check" rather than "not allowed".
  ✅ The user can always undo it by toggling back, and the verdict names the specific piece whose level left the range, so the fix is obvious.
  ❌ Introduces a reachable state where the layout is briefly unrepresentable, so every consumer of level(i) must handle out-of-range rather than assuming {0,1}.
C) Refuse the change, name the reason
  ✅ The layout can never hold an out-of-range level, so every consumer keeps the clean {0,1} assumption.
  ✅ The user is told exactly why the control did nothing, so it is not a silent no-op.
  ❌ A refused control action is an interaction this product has never had, and it contradicts the report-rather-than-prevent stance the verdict line is built on.

Net: A removes the problem by removing the freedom, B keeps the freedom and reports the consequence, C keeps the freedom and blocks the consequence.

Header: Base-level change
Options:
A) Only while the chain is empty
The invalid state becomes unreachable by construction, with no new failure path to implement, test or word, and it matches how a builder thinks: decide ground loop or raised loop before placing track. Cost: you cannot change your mind, so a user forty pieces in must clear the layout to try the other base, which 15A just moved Clear away from the palette to make harder.
B) Report it, keep the layout (recommended)
Consistent with the product's one rule, that the engine reports what is true and never blocks an edit, which is why grey means "could not check" rather than "not allowed". The user undoes it by toggling back, and the verdict names the piece whose level left the range. Cost: a reachable state where the layout is briefly unrepresentable, so every consumer of level(i) must handle out-of-range rather than assuming {0,1}.
C) Refuse the change, name the reason
The layout can never hold an out-of-range level, so every consumer keeps the clean {0,1} assumption, and the user is told exactly why the control did nothing rather than it being a silent no-op. Cost: a refused control action is an interaction this product has never had, and it contradicts the report-rather-than-prevent stance the verdict line is built on.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Only while the chain is empty, answer D6, 2026-10-08
Accepted scope: `baseLevel` is settable only while `placements` is empty, so no
control action can invalidate an existing chain and every consumer of level(i)
keeps the clean {0,1} assumption. Two consequences follow and are recorded here
rather than asked again, because both are mechanics of this answer:

1. **The control moves into the empty state**, beside the three presets that 15A
   relocated there. A control that is only settable before the first piece does
   not belong in the chain-edit row; the empty state is where it is both usable
   and discoverable. This refines 22B's placement without changing it.
2. **The prefix range check is still required**, and is approved under this
   answer. It is not made unnecessary by fixing `baseLevel` at creation: two
   consecutive up-ramps push level to 2 from base 0 regardless. 6A's derived ramp
   label makes that unreachable through the palette, since it only ever offers
   the legal direction, but the check is the engine-side guarantee behind that UI
   affordance and must exist independently of it.

Chosen over this reviewer's recommendation of B (report the out-of-range result
and keep the layout). Accepted cost, stated at the question: a user who is forty
pieces in cannot change the base level without clearing the layout, and 15A
deliberately moved Clear away from the palette. UPGRADE TRIGGER: the first time a
user asks to re-base an existing layout, at which point B's reachable
out-of-range state and its named verdict failure become the smaller cost.

### A3: what shape does the narrowphase return, now that two kinds can pair three ways

Finding: A3, `[P1] (confidence: 9/10)`, task N18 as written, against
`engine/src/parts/schema.ts:57` and `:63`, raised by this reviewer in Section 1.

Plan baseline: N18's accepted scope, from decision 23A (answer D25 of the second
design review), reads "The overlap check returns its conflicting interval, and
the canvas highlights that span on both pieces in `--fail`". The phrase "its
conflicting angular interval" appears in the task description.

Runtime evidence: verified in the shipped tree. There are exactly two part kinds,
so an unordered pair has three combinations:

```
56: export interface StraightPart extends PartBase {
57:   readonly kind: 'straight';
62: export interface CurvePart extends PartBase {
63:   readonly kind: 'curve';
```

An angle is meaningless on a straight, so "angular interval" cannot describe the
arc-segment or segment-segment result. 23A requires the span on **both** pieces,
so a single interval cannot describe any case: arc-arc alone needs one angular
interval per arc, because the same physical region sits at different angles about
two different centres. `engine/src/feasible.ts` does not exist, so there is no
behaviour to observe.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current | A: kind-tagged exact | B: arcs exact, pieces whole | C: normalised 0..1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A3 return shape | one "angular interval" (mis-typed) | `{i, j, spanI, spanJ}`, each span tagged arc or seg | arc-arc: two angular intervals; any pair with a straight: whole piece | `{i, j, spanI, spanJ}`, each a 0..1 fraction |
| Arc span unit | unspecified | integer 1/16 turns, R19's own unit | integer 1/16 turns | float fraction of arc |
| Straight span unit | not addressed | millimetres along the piece | not produced | float fraction of length |
| Exactness of the arc-arc span | unspecified | exact, integer | exact, integer | float, derived |
| 23A both-piece highlight | approved 23A | satisfied for all three cases | satisfied only for arc-arc | satisfied for all three cases |
| D4 three pair cases | approved D4 | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |
| R19 half-open mod 16 | approved R19/D2 | unchanged, and is literally this comparison | unchanged | unchanged |

Checks: the 23A, D4 and R19 rows cite their answers; D4 and R19 are identical
across all columns and are not reopened. Only the return shape and its units vary.

Question D7:

D7 — A3: what shape does the overlap check hand back?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; Section 1, correcting N18's contract.

ELI10: The overlap check has to tell the drawing which stretch of each piece
conflicts. A curve's stretch is naturally an angle, a straight's stretch is a
distance along it, and a pair can be curve-curve, curve-straight or
straight-straight. The task as written promises one angle, which cannot describe
two of those three cases or either piece in the third.

Stakes if we pick wrong: either the highlight overstates the conflict by lighting
up whole pieces, or the exact integer comparison that makes this check
trustworthy gets converted to floating point on its way to the screen.

Recommendation: A, because the arc case's natural unit is already R19's integer
sixteenth-turn, and a fraction can always be derived from it at draw time while
exactness can never be recovered from a fraction.

Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10, C=8/10

Pros / cons:
A) Kind-tagged spans in exact units (recommended)
  ✅ The arc span stays an integer count of sixteenth-turns, which is literally the half-open mod 16 comparison R19 already approved, so the exact tier stays exact end to end.
  ✅ Covers all three pair combinations with one return shape, and the canvas derives a 0..1 fraction for drawing without the engine ever holding a float.
  ❌ Two span units to handle, so the renderer needs a small branch on span kind rather than one uniform mapping.
B) Exact for arc-arc, whole piece when a straight is involved
  ✅ Smallest implementation: only the common case needs interval arithmetic, and arc-arc is the case that matters on a layout made mostly of curves.
  ✅ No second unit to define, so the return shape stays a single angular interval pair.
  ❌ Highlighting a whole piece when only part of it conflicts is exactly the overclaim 23A rejected when it chose a span over whole-piece highlighting.
  ❌ A straight crossing a straight is the simplest geometry in the system and would get the least precise feedback.
C) Normalised 0..1 fraction of each piece
  ✅ One unit for every kind, so the renderer maps spans onto arcs and straights with identical code and no branch.
  ✅ Trivial to consume and to serialise later, and unit-free across future part kinds.
  ❌ Converts the arc case's exact integer sixteenth-turns into a float at the engine boundary, which is the one conversion this project has removed everywhere else.
  ❌ Loses the ability to assert an exact span in a test, since the assertion becomes a tolerance comparison.

Net: A keeps the exact unit and pays one renderer branch, B pays in overclaimed highlights, C pays in exactness for uniformity.

Header: Overlap return
Options:
A) Kind-tagged spans, exact units (recommended)
The arc span stays an integer count of sixteenth-turns, literally the half-open mod 16 comparison R19 already approved, so the exact tier stays exact end to end. One return shape covers all three pair combinations, and the canvas derives a 0..1 fraction at draw time without the engine holding a float. Cost: two span units, so the renderer branches on span kind rather than using one uniform mapping.
B) Exact arc-arc, whole piece otherwise
Smallest implementation: only the common arc-arc case needs interval arithmetic, and no second unit has to be defined, so the return stays a single angular interval pair. Cost: highlighting a whole piece when only part conflicts is the overclaim 23A rejected when it chose a span, and straight-against-straight, the simplest geometry in the system, gets the least precise feedback.
C) Normalised 0..1 fraction
One unit for every kind, so the renderer maps spans onto arcs and straights with identical code and no branch, and it is trivial to consume or serialise across future part kinds. Cost: converts the arc case's exact integer sixteenth-turns to a float at the engine boundary, the one conversion this project has removed everywhere else, and an exact span can no longer be asserted in a test.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Kind-tagged spans, exact units, answer D7, 2026-10-08
Accepted scope: the narrowphase returns `{ i, j, spanI, spanJ }` per conflicting
pair, where each span is tagged by the kind of the piece it describes: an arc
span is a half-open integer interval of sixteenth-turns mod 16, which is R19's
own unit and comparison, and a straight span is a half-open millimetre interval
along the piece. All three pair cases from D4 return this one shape. The engine
holds no float in the exact tier; `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx` derives a 0..1
fraction from the span at draw time for 23A's highlight, and the renderer
branches on span kind. N18's task text is corrected from "its conflicting
angular interval", which was mis-typed for two of the three cases.

### A4: how many conflicting pairs does the overlap check report and draw

Finding: A4, `[P2] (confidence: 9/10)`, task N18 with decision 23A, raised by this
reviewer in Section 1.

Plan baseline: 23A (answer D25) approved highlighting the conflicting span on both
pieces, and N7's copy from 7A "names both pieces", singular pair. No cap on the
number of reported pairs or drawn highlights appears anywhere in M6, N7 or N18.

Runtime evidence: computed from the shipped geometry, not estimated. The overlap
result is bounded above by the all-pairs count:

```
  n=16   pairs=120          n=321  pairs=51,360
  n=24   pairs=276          n=640  pairs=204,480
  n=42   pairs=861
```

Two of these are real, not hypothetical. **16 large curves traverse the same
circle twice and report `EXACT` today**, verified by execution in the second
design review; once overlap ships, every one of those 120 pairs conflicts.
**640 large curves is 40 stacked circles and is reachable by holding the E
palette button**: 204,480 conflicting pairs, and at two highlight paths per pair
that is 408,960 SVG nodes in `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx`. The engine itself is not
the problem; it reached 1.4ms at 641 pieces after R21. The renderer is.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current | A: no cap | B: cap and disclose | C: first pair only |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A4 pairs reported | unspecified | all of them | first 20, count disclosed | 1 |
| A4 highlights drawn | unspecified | all of them | at most 20 pairs | 1 pair |
| Worst observed node count | unspecified | 408,960 at n=640 | at most 40 | 2 |
| Truncation visible to the user | unspecified | nothing to truncate | stated in the verdict | not stated |
| 23A span highlight | approved 23A | unchanged | unchanged, applied to the capped set | unchanged, applied to one pair |
| 7A/N7 copy naming both pieces | approved 7A | needs a plural form | needs a plural form plus a remainder count | matches as written |
| D4 primitive and D7 return shape | approved D4, D7 | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |

Checks: the 23A, 7A, D4 and D7 rows cite their answers; D4 and D7 are identical
across all columns and are not reopened. Only the reported count, the drawn
count and the disclosure vary.

Question D8:

D8 — A4: how many overlapping pairs get reported and drawn?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; Section 1, a measured renderer limit.

ELI10: When two pieces of track conflict the drawing will highlight both. A
layout can conflict in many places at once, and one that is easy to make by
accident conflicts in two hundred thousand places: hold the large-curve button
and you stack forty circles on top of each other. Drawing a highlight for each
would put roughly four hundred thousand shapes on the page and stop the browser.

Stakes if we pick wrong: the feature that refuses impossible layouts is itself
the thing that freezes the tab, triggered by holding one button.

Recommendation: B, because a bound the user is told about keeps the page alive
without hiding anything, which is the same discipline as every hedged verdict in
this product.

Completeness: A=6/10, B=10/10, C=5/10

Pros / cons:
A) Report and draw every conflicting pair
  ✅ No information is ever withheld, and the verdict count is exactly the number of real conflicts in the layout.
  ✅ Nothing extra to implement, word or test, and for every realistic failing layout the count is small.
  ❌ Measured: 204,480 pairs and 408,960 SVG nodes at 640 curves, reachable by holding one palette button, which stops the page.
  ❌ The failure arrives exactly when the user is already in trouble, so the tool dies at the moment it was supposed to help.
B) Cap the reported pairs and say so (recommended)
  ✅ Bounds the renderer at roughly 40 nodes regardless of layout size, so the page survives any input including the stacked-circle case.
  ✅ The verdict states the full count and that only the first N are shown, so nothing is hidden, which matches the project's rule of never claiming more than it checked.
  ❌ The user must fix conflicts in batches on a pathological layout, and the cap is one more constant that belongs in the frozen table K5 will own.
C) Stop at the first conflicting pair
  ✅ Cheapest in every dimension and matches 7A's existing copy, which already names exactly two pieces.
  ✅ Guarantees the smallest possible render cost and the simplest possible verdict sentence.
  ❌ A layout with fifty conflicts becomes fifty edit-and-recheck cycles, with no sense of how much is wrong.
  ❌ The engine computes the other conflicts anyway once the filter has run, so the information is discarded rather than saved.

Net: A is complete and can freeze the page, B is bounded and honest about the bound, C is cheapest and hides how much is wrong.

Header: Overlap result cap
Options:
A) Report and draw every pair
No information is ever withheld, the verdict count equals the real number of conflicts, and nothing extra needs implementing, wording or testing; for every realistic failing layout the count is small. Cost: measured 204,480 pairs and 408,960 SVG nodes at 640 curves, reachable by holding one palette button, which stops the page at exactly the moment the user is already in trouble.
B) Cap the reported pairs and say so (recommended)
Bounds the renderer at roughly 40 nodes regardless of layout size, so the page survives any input including the stacked-circle case, and the verdict states the full count plus that only the first N are shown, so nothing is hidden. Cost: conflicts get fixed in batches on a pathological layout, and the cap is one more constant belonging in the frozen table K5 will own.
C) Stop at the first conflicting pair
Cheapest in every dimension, matches 7A's existing copy which already names exactly two pieces, and guarantees the smallest render cost and simplest verdict sentence. Cost: a layout with fifty conflicts becomes fifty edit-and-recheck cycles with no sense of how much is wrong, and the other conflicts are computed by the filter anyway and then discarded.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Report and draw every pair, answer D8, 2026-10-08
Accepted scope: the narrowphase reports every conflicting pair and the canvas
highlights every one. No cap, no truncation, and therefore no truncation
disclosure to word. N7's copy from 7A needs a plural form, since it currently
names exactly two pieces.

**Implementation mechanic, not a modification of this answer.** Every highlight
span shares one stroke and one colour, so all of them batch into a single
`<path>` element whose `d` carries many subpaths, rather than two elements per
pair. That takes the node count at the measured worst case from 408,960 to 2
while drawing exactly what this answer asked for. It is the mechanics of the
chosen behaviour and needs no separate approval.

**Residual risk, accepted knowingly with the number in view.** Batching bounds
the node count but not the path data, which stays O(pairs): 640 large curves
still produce roughly 204,480 subpaths in one attribute, a multi-megabyte string.
Chosen over this reviewer's recommendation of B (cap and disclose). Accepted
ceiling: Completeness 6/10 on the pathological input, which is reachable by
holding one palette button.

**UPGRADE TRIGGER:** the first report of the page becoming unresponsive on a
large self-overlapping layout, at which point B's cap plus its truncation
disclosure lands, and the cap constant joins K5's frozen table. At
implementation time, mark the uncapped loop in `engine/src/feasible.ts` and the
batched path in `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx` with
`gstack-shortcut(dec-cae368ab): no cap on reported overlap pairs, upgrade when a
large self-overlapping layout is reported unresponsive`. Accepted-shortcut
decision logged as `cae368ab-2b67-42a2-a3ec-546c67265b31`.

### C2: should a new failure reason be a compile error or fall back to generic copy

Finding: C2, `[P1] (confidence: 10/10)`, `app/src/VerdictLine.tsx:27` and `:58`,
raised by this reviewer in Section 2.

Plan baseline: no decision covers the typing. 7A (answer D9) approved that overlap
must not render as the grey "could not check" state, and 10A (D12) and A1 (D6) add
two more new states, but none of them says how the copy table is typed.

Runtime evidence: verified in the shipped tree.

```
app/src/VerdictLine.tsx
27: const FAILURE_COPY: Record<string, string> = {
28:   OPEN_CHAIN: 'Not a loop yet. Close it to check whether it works.',
29:   HEADING_MISMATCH: 'The track does not come back pointing the right way.',
30:   NUMERIC: 'Could not check this layout.',
58:           {FAILURE_COPY[result.reason] ?? 'Could not check this layout.'}{' '}

engine/src/solver/result.ts
41: export type SolveFailure = 'OPEN_CHAIN' | 'HEADING_MISMATCH' | 'NUMERIC';
```

The index type is `string`, not `SolveFailure`, so a fourth union member needs no
corresponding entry and the `??` fallback supplies "Could not check this layout."
Contrast `VerdictLine.tsx:67`, where `switch (e.verdict)` over `LayoutVerdict` has
no `default`, so a fifth verdict is a compile error. The two patterns sit in one
file and behave oppositely.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current | A: exhaustive type | B: keep the fallback |
|---|---|---|---|
| C2 index type | `Record<string, string>` | `Record<SolveFailure, string>` | unchanged |
| C2 missing copy for a new reason | compiles, prints generic text | compile error | compiles, prints generic text |
| C2 `??` fallback at :58 | present and reachable | removable, unreachable once typed | present and reachable |
| 7A overlap must not read as grey | approved 7A | enforced by the compiler | enforced only by review attention |
| New states this review adds | 3 (overlap, unmatched ramp, out-of-range) | each must carry copy to compile | each may ship without copy |
| Verdict switch at :67 | exhaustive, no default | unchanged | unchanged |

Checks: the 7A row and the verdict-switch row cite their sources and are
identical in both columns; neither is reopened. Only the index type and what
happens when copy is missing vary.

Question D9:

D9 — C2: should a failure reason without copy fail the build or print generic text?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; Section 2, one type annotation.

ELI10: There is a lookup table that turns a failure reason into a sentence for the
user. It is typed so loosely that adding a new kind of failure does not require
adding its sentence, and when the sentence is missing the screen says "Could not
check this layout." This release adds three new kinds of failure, and for two of
them that sentence would be a lie: the tool checked perfectly well and found a
definite problem.

Stakes if we pick wrong: a proven "you cannot build this" prints as "could not
check", which is the one inversion four reviews were spent preventing, and
nothing in the build would catch it.

Recommendation: A, because the same file already proves the pattern works: the
verdict switch is exhaustive and a new verdict there is a compile error.

Completeness: A=10/10, B=3/10

Pros / cons:
A) Type the table by the union (recommended)
  ✅ `Record<SolveFailure, string>` makes a reason without copy a compile error, so the three new states this review adds cannot ship wordless.
  ✅ Matches the exhaustive `switch` already in the same file at line 67, so one file stops holding two opposite safety patterns.
  ✅ The `??` fallback at line 58 becomes unreachable and can be deleted, removing the branch that would have hidden the problem.
  ❌ Every future failure reason must be worded before the build passes, so a quick engine experiment cannot compile until its user-facing copy exists.
B) Keep the loose type and the fallback
  ✅ The app never fails to render, whatever the engine returns, which is a real property for a tool whose verdict line is its whole output.
  ✅ Lets an engine change land and compile before its copy is written, keeping the two sides decoupled.
  ❌ Measured: three new failure states are planned and each would print "Could not check this layout." with no build error, which directly contradicts 7A.
  ❌ Makes review attention the only guard, in the exact place where the product's core invariant lives.

Net: A spends a compile error to protect the core invariant; B keeps rendering guaranteed and leaves the invariant to vigilance.

Header: Failure copy typing
Options:
A) Type the table by the union (recommended)
`Record<SolveFailure, string>` makes a reason without copy a compile error, so the three new states this review adds cannot ship wordless, and it matches the exhaustive switch already in the same file at line 67 so one file stops holding two opposite safety patterns. The `??` fallback at line 58 becomes unreachable and is deleted. Cost: every future failure reason must be worded before the build passes, so a quick engine experiment cannot compile until its copy exists.
B) Keep the loose type and the fallback
The app never fails to render whatever the engine returns, a real property for a tool whose verdict line is its whole output, and an engine change can land and compile before its copy is written. Cost: three new failure states are planned and each would print "Could not check this layout." with no build error, contradicting 7A, which leaves review attention as the only guard in the exact place the product's core invariant lives.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Type the table by the union, answer D9, 2026-10-08

`app/src/VerdictLine.tsx:27`, and the now-unreachable `?? 'Could not check this
layout.'` fallback at `:58` is deleted. A `SolveFailure` member without copy
becomes a compile error, so the new states this review and the design review add
cannot ship wordless. This aligns the file with its own exhaustive
`switch (e.verdict)` at `:67`. Covered by the regression contract in Section 3.

### C1: where is level(i) computed, given three consumers need it

Finding: C1, `[P1] (confidence: 9/10)`, raised by this reviewer in Section 2 under
the shared-code rubric.

Plan baseline: M5 places the `elevated` derivation in
`engine/src/parts/schema.ts` and `engine/src/solver/residual.ts`. N17, from 22B,
adds `baseLevel` and names `schema.ts`, `residual.ts` and `app/src/App.tsx`. No
decision says there is one definition.

Runtime evidence, with the rubric's caller proof. Level is a pure function of the
placement list and one scalar: `level(i) = baseLevel + ups(i) - downs(i)`. It has
no geometric content at all, which matters because the repo has two walkers and
neither owns it:

- **Existing, authored:** `app/src/App.tsx:50-56`, the `layout` useMemo, is where
  6A's derived ramp label must read the current end level.
- **Existing, authored:** `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx:38`,
  `walkFloat(layout, getPart)`, is where 3A's casing and cast shadow must know
  each segment's level.
- **Existing, authored:** `engine/src/solver/residual.ts`, the exact walk, is
  where Y2's parity and A1's prefix range check must run.

Three verified first-party locations, two of them already calling a walker. The
proposed consumers are labelled as plan assumptions: 6A's label, 3A's two
rendering passes, Y2's parity and A1's range check. Accounting: one shared
function is roughly 15 lines; three independent copies are roughly 12 lines each,
so the saving is small in lines (about 20 removed against 15 added) and the real
gain is that three consumers cannot disagree about which level a piece is on.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current | A: one pure helper | B: both walkers return it | C: compute at each site |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| C1 definitions of level(i) | unspecified, implied 3 | 1 | 1, surfaced twice | 3 |
| C1 home | unspecified | `schema.ts` beside `Layout` | `fk.ts` and `pose.ts` return values | App, TrackCanvas, residual |
| Divergence risk | three consumers may disagree | none, one definition | none, one definition | real: 3 copies of the same rule |
| Couples level to geometry | n/a | no, it is placement arithmetic | yes, it rides on a walker | no |
| D3 one-walk-per-render | approved D3 | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |
| 22B baseLevel field | approved D24, settable while empty per D6 | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |
| A1 prefix range check | approved D6 | one place to enforce it | one place to enforce it | three places to enforce it |

Checks: the D3 and 22B rows cite their answers and are identical across all three
columns; neither is reopened. The A1 row cites D6 and is not reopened, only
relocated. Only the number of definitions and their home vary.

Question D10:

D10 — C1: one definition of a piece's level, or one per consumer?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; Section 2, shared-code rubric.

ELI10: Which level a piece sits on is worked out by counting ramps before it.
Three different parts of the code need that answer: the palette, to label the ramp
button; the drawing, to know which pieces to draw as raised; and the solver, to
check the loop comes back level. If each works it out separately, they can
disagree, and the one that disagrees silently is the drawing.

Stakes if we pick wrong: the picture shows a piece on the ground while the verdict
treats it as elevated, and nothing fails or warns.

Recommendation: A, because the level of a piece is arithmetic on the piece list
with no geometry in it, so it belongs beside the Layout type rather than riding on
either of the two walkers.

Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=3/10

Pros / cons:
A) One pure helper beside Layout (recommended)
  ✅ One definition, three call sites, so the palette, the drawing and the solver cannot disagree about which level a piece is on.
  ✅ Keeps placement arithmetic out of both walkers, which matters because the repo has two of them, an exact one and a float one, and level has no geometric content.
  ✅ A1's prefix range check gets exactly one place to live, so the {0,1} guarantee is enforced once rather than asserted three times.
  ❌ Each consumer calls it separately, so the O(n) integer pass runs up to three times per render; cheap, but it is genuine duplicated work.
B) Both walkers return levels alongside poses
  ✅ Any caller that already walks gets levels with no extra call, which suits TrackCanvas and residual since both already walk.
  ✅ Still one definition if both walkers call the same helper internally, so divergence is avoided.
  ❌ Couples placement arithmetic to geometry, so the float walker and the exact walker both grow a field that has nothing to do with either's job.
  ❌ App.tsx needs the end level for 6A's ramp label without wanting a walk at all, so it would walk purely to read one integer.
C) Compute it at each of the three sites
  ✅ No new shared surface, and each site computes exactly the slice it needs with no unused results.
  ✅ Smallest possible diff per task, so M5, N6, N3 and N17 each stay self-contained.
  ❌ Three copies of one rule, which is how the drawing and the verdict come to disagree about a piece's level with nothing failing.
  ❌ A1's range check would be written three times or, more likely, once and missed in the other two.

Net: A pays a few redundant integer passes for a single source of truth; B gets it free where a walk already happens and couples arithmetic to geometry; C is the smallest diff and the only one that can silently disagree.

Header: Level derivation
Options:
A) One pure helper beside Layout (recommended)
One definition and three call sites, so the palette, the drawing and the solver cannot disagree about a piece's level; placement arithmetic stays out of both walkers, which matters because the repo has an exact one and a float one and level has no geometric content; and A1's prefix range check gets exactly one home. Cost: each consumer calls it separately, so the O(n) integer pass runs up to three times per render, cheap but genuinely duplicated work.
B) Both walkers return levels
Any caller that already walks gets levels with no extra call, which suits TrackCanvas and residual since both already walk, and divergence is still avoided if both walkers call one helper internally. Cost: couples placement arithmetic to geometry so both walkers grow a field unrelated to their job, and App.tsx would have to walk purely to read one integer for 6A's ramp label.
C) Compute it at each of the three sites
No new shared surface, each site computes exactly the slice it needs, and M5, N6, N3 and N17 each stay self-contained with the smallest possible diff. Cost: three copies of one rule, which is how the drawing and the verdict come to disagree about a piece's level with nothing failing, and A1's range check gets written three times or once and missed twice.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) One pure helper beside Layout, answer D10, 2026-10-08
Accepted scope: one exported pure function in `engine/src/parts/schema.ts`,
beside the `Layout` type it reads, returning the per-placement level array from
`baseLevel` and the ramp sequence. It is the single place A1's `{0,1}` prefix
range check is enforced. Three call sites: `app/src/App.tsx` for 6A's derived
ramp label, `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx` for 3A's casing and cast shadow, and
`engine/src/solver/residual.ts` for Y2's parity and the range check. Neither
walker gains a levels field, so placement arithmetic stays out of the exact and
float walkers. M5's and N17's file lists are corrected to name one definition
rather than implying three. Accepted cost: the O(n) integer pass runs up to three
times per render.

### T1: the regression contract for the overlap check

Finding: T1, `[P1] (confidence: 10/10)`, `engine/test/analytic.test.ts:60` and
`:199`, raised under the REGRESSION RULE.

Plan baseline: R19 (D2) approved "the exact same-circle tier with half-open
intervals mod 16". D4 chose the exact narrowphase. No regression contract names
which existing behaviour must survive.

Runtime evidence: verified by execution. `8 x E` closes exactly and reports
`EXACT` today (`analytic.test.ts:60-66`). It is also the project's flagship
fixture and it appears in the UI as the "Circle" preset. The recorded learning
`half-open-intervals-on-a-tiled-circle` (confidence 10/10) states the failure
mode precisely: eight 45-degree curves tile a circle as sixteen 22.5-degree units
covering [0,2), [2,4) and so on, so consecutive arcs share an endpoint, and with
closed intervals and no tolerance **the eight-curve circle reports an overlap with
itself and FAILS**. Separately, `16 x E` and `8L + 8R` both report `EXACT` today
and must both start failing.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current | A: full contract | B: the circle only |
|---|---|---|---|
| T1 8xE must not overlap | untested, at risk | asserted, no tolerance | asserted, no tolerance |
| T1 16xE must overlap | reports EXACT, a known false pass | asserted | not asserted |
| T1 8L+8R tangency must overlap | reports EXACT, a known false pass | asserted, plank-width footprints | not asserted |
| T1 adjacency exclusion | unspecified | asserted, the seam pair is named | not asserted |
| T1 72mm EXCEEDS, order independence | tested at :125 and :199 | re-run unchanged | re-run unchanged |
| Intentional changes named | no | yes: 16xE and 8L+8R stop passing | partially |
| R19 half-open mod 16 | approved D2 | unchanged | unchanged |

Checks: the R19 row cites D2 and is identical in both columns. The existing-test
row is common work, not a new approval. Only which new assertions are required
varies.

Question D11:

D11 — T1: what must the overlap check be proved not to break?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; Section 3, the mandatory regression contract.

ELI10: Adding the overlap check puts the project's flagship example at risk. Eight
large curves form a perfect circle, and each curve touches the next end to end. If
the check treats touching as overlapping, the circle starts failing, and that is
the one layout everything else is measured against. Two other layouts have the
opposite problem: they pass today and should not.

Stakes if we pick wrong: the headline fixture silently flips to FAIL, or the two
known false passes stay passing and the whole feature achieves nothing.

Recommendation: A, because two of the three layouts at issue are already known
false passes, so asserting only the circle would ship the feature without proving
it does the job it exists for.

Completeness: A=10/10, B=5/10

Pros / cons:
A) The full contract: four assertions (recommended)
  ✅ Preserves 8xE explicitly, which the recorded half-open-interval learning shows fails under closed intervals with no tolerance.
  ✅ Turns the two known false passes into proof: 16xE and 8L+8R both report EXACT today and must both start failing, the tangency only catchable with plank-width footprints.
  ✅ Names the intentional changes, so a reviewer can tell a fixed false pass from a new regression.
  ❌ Four new fixtures to write and maintain, and the tangency case needs the plank-width footprint path working before it can be asserted at all.
B) Assert the circle only
  ✅ Covers the single highest-risk regression, which is the flagship fixture flipping to FAIL, with one new assertion.
  ✅ Cheapest possible contract, and the two false passes are already recorded as known limitations in the README.
  ❌ The feature exists to catch self-overlap, so shipping it without asserting that 16xE now fails proves nothing about whether it works.
  ❌ Leaves adjacency exclusion unspecified, which is the mechanism that decides whether the circle passes in the first place.

Net: A proves both that nothing broke and that the feature works; B proves only that nothing broke.

Header: Overlap regression
Options:
A) Full contract, four assertions (recommended)
Preserves 8xE explicitly, which the recorded half-open-interval learning shows fails under closed intervals with no tolerance; turns the two known false passes into proof, since 16xE and 8L+8R both report EXACT today and must both start failing, with the tangency only catchable using plank-width footprints; and names the intentional changes so a fixed false pass is distinguishable from a new regression. Cost: four new fixtures to write and maintain, and the tangency case needs the plank-width footprint path working before it can be asserted.
B) Assert the circle only
Covers the single highest-risk regression, the flagship fixture flipping to FAIL, with one new assertion, and the two false passes are already recorded as known limitations in the README. Cost: the feature exists to catch self-overlap, so shipping without asserting that 16xE now fails proves nothing about whether it works, and adjacency exclusion stays unspecified even though it is the mechanism that decides whether the circle passes.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Full contract, four assertions, answer D11 reopened and
re-answered, 2026-10-08
Accepted scope: four assertions in `engine/test/analytic.test.ts`, no tolerance
on any of them.

1. **`8 x E` must report NO overlap.** The live regression. The recorded
   `half-open-intervals-on-a-tiled-circle` learning identifies the mechanism:
   eight 45-degree curves tile a circle as sixteen 22.5-degree units covering
   [0,2), [2,4) and so on, so consecutive arcs share an endpoint, and closed
   intervals with no tolerance make the flagship fixture FAIL.
2. **`16 x E` MUST overlap.** It reports `EXACT` today, verified by execution,
   and traverses the same circle twice. This is the assertion that proves the
   feature does its job rather than merely leaving the circle alone.
3. **`8L + 8R` MUST overlap.** Two circles of radius 182 centred at (0,182) and
   (0,-182), centre separation exactly 364 against radii summing to 364, so they
   are tangent and the centrelines meet in a set of measure zero. Only catchable
   with plank-width footprints, which makes this assertion the proof that
   footprints are actually being used.
4. **Adjacency exclusion names the seam pair.** The mechanism assertion 1
   depends on, so it is asserted directly rather than inferred from the circle
   passing.

**Behaviour to preserve:** `8 x E` reports no overlap and still closes exactly;
`analytic.test.ts:60` closure and `:199` order-independence re-run unchanged as
common work.
**Intentional changes, named so a fixed false pass is distinguishable from a new
regression:** `16 x E` and `8L + 8R` both report `EXACT` today and must start
failing.

Accepted cost, stated at the question: four fixtures to write and maintain, and
assertion 3 cannot be written until the plank-width footprint path works, so it
sequences after that part of M6 rather than alongside it.

History: first answered B) Assert the circle only, 2026-10-08, which accepted a
Completeness 5/10 ceiling and left assertions 2, 3 and 4 unasserted, with the
consequence recorded that the overlap check could ship without catching
self-overlap while the suite stayed green. The user asked to see the brief again
and reopened the choice, selecting A. Reason for reopening: the user's own
changed decision, taken before any work depended on B. Accepted-shortcut
decision `f9b6f39b-4ed8-4d00-a524-5af11984d0bd`, logged under B, is superseded by

### T2: how are the two end-to-end flows covered

Finding: T2, `[P2] (confidence: 9/10)`, the two `[→E2E]` rows in the coverage
diagram, raised by this reviewer in Section 3.

Plan baseline: M10 is accepted scope and already includes "a DOM test project",
because `vitest.config.ts` includes only `engine/**` and sets no environment. No
decision covers browser-level end-to-end testing, and no E2E framework exists in
the repository.

Runtime evidence: verified. `package.json` devDependencies are
`@types/react`, `@types/react-dom`, `@vitejs/plugin-react`, `typescript`, `vite`
and `vitest`. No playwright, no cypress, no testing-library. `vitest.config.ts`
sets `include: ['engine/**/*.test.ts']` and `globals: true` with no
`environment`, so no DOM exists in the current suite. There is no `.github`
directory, so nothing would run a browser suite automatically.

The two flows at issue: two runs crossing on the same level produce a FAIL plus a
span highlight; a bridge crossing over a lower run produces no failure. Both are
deterministic functions of the layout: the same click sequence always yields the
same verdict text and the same path data.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current | A: DOM level | B: add a browser framework | C: defer both |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| T2 two E2E flows | uncovered | covered in the M10 DOM project | covered in a real browser | uncovered |

| Needs CI to be useful | n/a | no, runs with `bun run test` | yes, and no `.github` exists | n/a |
| Proves greyscale print and real paint | no | no | yes | no |
| M10 DOM project | approved M10 | unchanged, this rides on it | unchanged | unchanged |
| T1 four overlap assertions | approved D11 | unchanged, engine level | unchanged | unchanged |

columns; neither is reopened. Only how the two flows are covered varies.

Question D12:

D12 — T2: do the two crossing flows need a real browser?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; Section 3, the last test decision.

ELI10: Two journeys are worth testing end to end: crossing two pieces of track at
the same level, which should be refused and highlighted, and running a bridge over
a lower track, which should be fine. Both can be checked either by rendering the
app in a fake browser inside the existing test runner, or by driving a real
browser, which needs a new tool and something to run it on.

Stakes if we pick wrong: either the two most important journeys in the release go
untested, or the project takes on a browser-automation dependency it has no
continuous-integration setup to run.

Recommendation: A, because both flows are deterministic functions of the layout
and the DOM project M10 already approved can assert the verdict text and the
highlight path data without a browser.

Completeness: A=9/10, B=10/10, C=4/10

Pros / cons:
A) Cover both in the M10 DOM project (recommended)
  ✅ Rides on a dependency M10 already approved, so it adds coverage without adding a second test stack to maintain.
  ✅ Both flows are deterministic given a layout, so mounting the app, clicking the palette and asserting the verdict text plus the highlight path data proves the behaviour exactly.
  ❌ A simulated DOM does not paint, so it cannot prove the greyscale print legibility the build sheet needs or that the casing visually cuts the lower run.
B) Add a browser framework for the two flows
  ✅ Proves what only a real browser can: actual paint, real stacking of the casing over the lower run, and print rendering.
  ✅ Gives the project a path to visual regression testing later, which a canvas-centred tool will eventually want.
  ❌ A second test stack plus a browser download, in a repository with no `.github` directory, so nothing would run it except by hand.
  ❌ Browser tests on a canvas are the slowest and flakiest category available, for two flows whose logic is deterministic.
C) Defer both flows
  ✅ Keeps the release focused on engine assertions, where T1's four overlap cases already carry the highest-value coverage.
  ✅ No new dependency of any kind beyond M10's DOM environment.
  ❌ The two journeys that define the feature, crossing on one level and bridging over, would ship with no test at either level.
  ❌ The span highlight from 23A would have nothing asserting it renders at all.

Net: A proves the logic without a browser, B additionally proves the paint and pays for a second stack, C ships the headline journeys untested.

Header: E2E coverage

A) Cover both in the M10 DOM project (recommended)
Rides on a dependency M10 already approved, so coverage grows without a second test stack to maintain, and both flows are deterministic given a layout, so mounting the app, clicking the palette and asserting the verdict text plus highlight path data proves the behaviour exactly. Cost: a simulated DOM does not paint, so it cannot prove the greyscale print legibility the build sheet needs or that the casing visually cuts the lower run.
B) Add a browser framework for the two flows
Proves what only a real browser can, meaning actual paint, real stacking of the casing over the lower run, and print rendering, and it opens a path to visual regression testing that a canvas-centred tool will eventually want. Cost: a second test stack plus a browser download in a repository with no .github directory, so nothing runs it except by hand, and browser tests on a canvas are the slowest and flakiest category available for two flows whose logic is deterministic.
C) Defer both flows
Keeps the release focused on engine assertions where T1's four overlap cases already carry the highest-value coverage, and adds no dependency beyond M10's DOM environment. Cost: the two journeys that define the feature, crossing on one level and bridging over, ship with no test at either level, and 23A's span highlight has nothing asserting it renders at all.

Accepted limitation: a simulated DOM does not paint, so neither the greyscale
print legibility the build sheet needs nor the visual fact that 3A's casing cuts
the lower run is proved by any test. Both remain manual checks.
History: none

## Outside Voice (eng review, third pass, 2026-10-08)

Codex preflight: `CODEX_MODE: not_installed`, as on all five previous reviews of
this plan. **No external provider has ever run on this project.** A native Claude
`Plan` subagent completed inside the bounded five-minute wait and produced 15
findings (3 Critical, 5 High, 5 Medium, 3 Low). A native result is a finding
source and never outside coverage, so no cross-model agreement is claimed.

Four load-bearing claims were verified against source before acceptance, and all
four are confirmed:

| Claim | Verification |
|---|---|
| `cyclotomic.ts` has no general multiply, conjugation, norm or order comparison | **CONFIRMED.** Exports are exactly `Cyc, ZERO, fromInt, add, sub, scale, isZero, equals, mulZetaPow, zetaPow, toXY, magnitude`. No ring multiply and no sign test exist. |
| Plank width is not the gauge, and is recorded nowhere | **CONFIRMED verbatim.** `CALIBRATION.md:76` "Plank width (NOT the 40mm gauge - the wood's width)"; `:83-84` "the angular lever needs plank width, which is not the gauge and is currently not recorded anywhere"; `schema.ts:44` "Track gauge in millimetres. 40 is the de facto standard; Thomas is 42." |
| Q5 is a second open gate whose contingency says stop | **CONFIRMED verbatim.** "Clearance margin is zero or negative: **Single-level bridges are impossible.** The two-layer model cannot express a legal bridge, so layers are deferred entirely... **Stop before building the layer work; this is a design change, not a tuning change.**" The margin is 8mm from three unmeasured figures. |
| `heightMm` records a decision against an integer level index | **CONFIRMED verbatim.** `schema.ts:48-52` "Deliberately not an integer level index: BRIO's ascending track happens to rise 64mm, but books, bridge supports, other brands and printed risers do not, and a level index would be a reversibility-1 mistake." |

### Three errors by this reviewer, corrected

1. **Decision 1A is wrong in its value.** The second design review reported the
   plank as 40mm and the drawn stroke as 10-12mm. The 40mm is the **gauge**, the
   distance between the rail grooves. The plank is the wood, it is wider, and
   `CALIBRATION.md` states it is recorded nowhere. The finding's direction stands:
   a stroke width derived from a viewport fraction varies with layout extent and
   is a real bug, measured at 10.0mm for one piece and 12.0mm for eight. The
   number and the field do not stand.
2. **D8's mitigation reasoning was wrong.** Batching does take the node count from
   408,960 to 2, but a hang is not a throw, so 9A's rescue never fires, and the
   cumulative cost of clicking to 640 pieces is O(n^3) because `useMemo` is keyed
   on a fresh `layout` object per click. Per-edit cost was computed and presented
   as the worst case.
3. **The D8 repro was false.** "Reachable by holding one palette button" is wrong;
   a React `onClick` does not auto-repeat. The risk is real but needs 640 clicks
   or a preset, and that matters because the repro was the stated justification
   for overriding the cap.

### Release split (answer D13)

**Answer D13 option A: split the release.** The ramp and layer work stops until
the caliper afternoon, honouring `CALIBRATION.md`'s own contingency row. Recorded
as a scope change under Recovery routing, which returns this review to the
affected stages.

**Release 1, unblocked, needs no measurement:** the overlap check on the existing
single-level model, closing both verified false passes; persistence; and every
app-side decision with no layer dependency. Decisions carried forward unchanged:
2A, 4A, 7A, 9A, 11A, 12A, 13A, 14A, 15A (minus the base-level control), 16A,
17A, 18A, 19A, 20A (minus the level column), 21A, 23A, D3 (simplified), D9, D11,
D12 (minus the bridge flow).

**Release 2, blocked on K0b:** every elevation decision. 3A, 6A, 8A, 10A, 22B,
D6, D10, and the ramp half of M4, M5, M13 and N17. H2's contradiction is carried
here with them: `schema.ts:48-52` records a deliberate decision against an
integer level index, and the two-layer model is exactly that index, so **that
recorded decision must be explicitly retired or the model changed before Release
2 is built**. Five reviews never noticed it.

**Why the unblocked half is genuinely unblocked**, despite the footprint width
being unrecorded: both documented false passes are insensitive to the width.
`16 x E` places two runs on the *same* centreline, and `8L + 8R` is tangent, so
*any* positive footprint catches both. The width only adjudicates near-misses,
which this release therefore must not claim to decide. Recorded consequence: the
overlap verdict discloses the provisional footprint width it used, and says it
cannot rule on margins near that width. A new provisional `plankWidthMm` field
replaces 1A's mistaken use of `gaugeWidthMm`, carrying the same `estimated`
status as every other unmeasured number in the part data.

### Findings recorded without a separate question

- **H3, a correction to D9's justification.** Every `!result.ok` renders grey with
  the `?` mark today, so overlap (7A, definite FAIL), the unmatched ramp (10A, own
  register) and the open-chain progress line (11A, neutral mark) **cannot be
  `SolveFailure` members at all**. They belong on `LayoutVerdict` or a parallel
  field. D9's retyping of `FAILURE_COPY` to `Record<SolveFailure, string>` stands
  and is still correct, but its stated motivation named the wrong union. The
  exhaustive `switch` at `VerdictLine.tsx:67` is what actually protects the three
  new states, and it already does.
- **H4, necessary implementation.** The footprint is **flat-ended** (a rectangle
  for a straight, an annular sector for an arc), never a capsule. Inflating a
  centreline with round caps makes every pair of consecutive pieces overlap by
  about half the width, and D7's span types only make sense for the flat-ended
  reading.
- **M3, rides on D11.** Adjacency exclusion is currently correct only because no
  part in the catalogue turns 180 degrees or more. One invariant assertion on
  `arcSteps` makes that explicit instead of accidental.
- **M4, naming the dependency honestly.** D12's "no E2E framework" is accurate but
  incomplete: the DOM project needs `jsdom` or `happy-dom` plus
  `@testing-library/react`, three or four new devDependencies. The engine keeps
  its zero runtime dependencies; this is dev-only.
- **M5, already recorded elsewhere.** The BRIO trademark and the woodenrailway
  provenance question are both live P1 pre-launch items in `brio-TODOS.md` with
  their own sections. Not new, and not re-litigated here.
- **L2, routed to a TODO** in Final planning decisions below, with the no-CI
  finding from Section 1.

### SC1 reopened: the overlap primitive, after the outside voice

Finding: outside voice C2, `[Critical]`, verified by this reviewer.

Plan baseline: SC1 was approved at D4 as an exact arc-and-segment narrowphase
covering all three pair cases. Reopened on concrete contradictory evidence, which
is the only warrant the procedure allows.

Runtime evidence, verified:

1. **The arithmetic D4 assumed does not exist.** `engine/src/geom/cyclotomic.ts`
   exports exactly `Cyc, ZERO, fromInt, add, sub, scale, isZero, equals,
   mulZetaPow, zetaPow, toXY, magnitude`. There is no general ring multiply, no
   conjugation, no norm and no order comparison. An exact centre-distance test
   needs |delta|^2 in the real subfield as `a + b*sqrt2` and a sign routine in
   Z[sqrt2]; segment-against-segment needs cross and dot products; arc-against-
   segment needs division. None of that is present.
2. **The threshold is unmeasured.** `CALIBRATION.md:76` and `:83-84` state that
   plank width is not the gauge and is recorded nowhere. Exact arithmetic against
   a guessed threshold is the R21 mistake in a new file.
3. **The same-circle case, however, is already exactly decidable.** Verified by
   execution in integer arithmetic, computing each arc centre as
   `pos + r * zeta^(h +/- 4)`: `8 x E` gives **1** distinct exact centre,
   `16 x E` gives **1**, and `8L + 8R` gives **2**. So `equals()` on exact
   centres, which already exists, plus integer interval arithmetic on
   sixteenth-turns, is enough to decide the same-circle case with no new ring
   arithmetic at all.

That third point is R19's original accepted design, which D4 overreached past:
"the exact same-circle tier with half-open intervals mod 16; float annulus band".
The exact tier was only ever meant for the same-circle case.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current (D4) | A: R19's two tiers | B: build the ring arithmetic | C: sampled centreline only |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Same-circle case | exact, via unbuilt arithmetic | exact, via existing `equals` + integer intervals | exact | approximate, ~5mm samples |
| Tangency and near-miss cases | exact, via unbuilt arithmetic | float band, tolerance disclosed | exact | approximate, tolerance disclosed |
| New arithmetic needed | ring multiply, conjugation, norm, sign test in Z[sqrt2] | **none** | all of it | none |
| Catches 16xE (verified false pass) | yes | yes, exactly | yes | yes |
| Catches 8L+8R tangency (verified false pass) | yes | yes, via footprint width | yes | yes |
| Honesty about the guessed threshold | exact result from a guessed input | tolerance stated in the verdict | exact result from a guessed input | tolerance stated in the verdict |
| D7 span return shape | approved D7 | unchanged for arcs; float spans in the band tier | unchanged | spans become sample indices |
| D11 four assertions | approved D11 | all four still assertable | all four | the adjacency case stays exact |
| R19 half-open mod 16 | approved R19/D2 | **this is literally R19** | unchanged | convention becomes moot |

Checks: the D7 and D11 rows cite their answers; D11's four assertions survive in
every column. R19 is cited, not reopened. Only where exactness is applied and
what new arithmetic is required vary.

Question D14:

D14 — SC1 reopened: where does exactness actually belong in the overlap check?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; reopened after the outside voice.

ELI10: I previously recommended making the whole overlap check exact. Checking the
code shows the exact arithmetic it needs was never written: there is no way to
multiply two of these numbers, take a norm, or compare them. Building that is a
small mathematics library. And the thing it would compare against is a width
nobody has measured, so the precision would be decorative. But one case is
already free: two arcs on the same circle have identical exact centres, which the
code can already test, and that is exactly the case that catches the sixteen-curve
false pass.

Stakes if we pick wrong: either weeks of exact-geometry work whose precision is
spent on a guessed number, or an approximate check that quietly claims more than
it can support.

Recommendation: A, because it is the design R19 already approved, it needs no new
arithmetic, it catches both verified false passes, and it puts exactness only
where exactness is free.

Completeness: A=10/10, B=5/10, C=8/10

Pros / cons:
A) R19's two tiers: exact same-circle, float band elsewhere (recommended)
  ✅ Needs no new arithmetic: `equals()` on exact centres already exists, and verified by execution 8xE and 16xE each give one distinct exact centre while 8L+8R gives two.
  ✅ Catches both verified false passes: 16xE exactly, through 32 sixteenths covering a 16-unit circle, and the 8L+8R tangency in the float tier where the footprint width does the work.
  ✅ Restores the design R19 already approved and keeps the half-open mod 16 convention as the thing it was written for.
  ❌ Two tiers with different guarantees, so the verdict has to say which tier decided it, and near-miss cases carry a disclosed tolerance rather than a proof.
B) Build the exact ring arithmetic for all three pair cases
  ✅ One uniform guarantee: every overlap answer is exact, with no tolerance to disclose and no tier to explain.
  ✅ The arithmetic would be reusable for any future exact geometry, including switches and crossings.
  ❌ Requires ring multiply, conjugation, norm and a sign test in Z[sqrt2], none of which exist, in a 1,350-line project, as the cost of one check.
  ❌ Spends that precision comparing against a plank width the calibration sheet says is recorded nowhere, which is the R21 mistake repeated.
C) Sampled centreline for everything, tolerance disclosed
  ✅ Simplest to write and reason about, roughly sixty lines over the existing float walk, and it catches both verified false passes.
  ✅ One uniform disclosed tolerance, honest about being approximate, with no tier distinction to explain.
  ❌ Throws away an exactness that is already free, in the one case where this project's whole claim is exactness.
  ❌ Makes the sixteen-curve answer depend on sample spacing, so the flagship false-pass fix would rest on a tuning constant.

Net: A puts exactness where it costs nothing and discloses tolerance everywhere else; B buys uniform exactness at the price of a maths library spent on a guess; C is uniformly approximate and gives up free exactness.

Header: Overlap exactness
Options:
A) R19's two tiers (recommended)
Needs no new arithmetic, since `equals()` on exact centres already exists and, verified by execution, 8xE and 16xE each give one distinct exact centre while 8L+8R gives two. Catches both verified false passes: 16xE exactly via 32 sixteenths covering a 16-unit circle, and the 8L+8R tangency in the float tier where footprint width does the work. Restores the design R19 approved. Cost: two tiers with different guarantees, so the verdict says which tier decided and near-miss cases carry a disclosed tolerance rather than a proof.
B) Build the exact ring arithmetic
One uniform guarantee with no tolerance to disclose and no tier to explain, and the arithmetic would be reusable for future exact geometry including switches. Cost: requires ring multiply, conjugation, norm and a sign test in Z[sqrt2], none of which exist, in a 1,350-line project, as the price of one check, and it spends that precision against a plank width the calibration sheet says is recorded nowhere.
C) Sampled centreline only
Simplest to write and reason about, roughly sixty lines over the existing float walk, catches both verified false passes, and carries one uniform disclosed tolerance with no tier distinction. Cost: throws away exactness that is already free in the one case where this project's whole claim is exactness, and makes the sixteen-curve answer depend on sample spacing, so the flagship fix rests on a tuning constant.

State: approved
Actual answer: B) Build the exact ring arithmetic, answer D14, 2026-10-08
Accepted scope: `engine/src/geom/cyclotomic.ts` grows the arithmetic the exact
narrowphase needs, and `engine/src/feasible.ts` holds the three pair cases. All
three are exact, so D7's kind-tagged exact span units now have arithmetic behind
them rather than being aspirational.

**Mechanics that make this materially smaller than it first appears, recorded
rather than asked, because they implement the chosen behaviour without altering
it: no division is required anywhere.** Every comparison cross-multiplies into
squared form and stays inside the ring:

```
  arc vs arc        |dC|^2        against (r1 +/- r2)^2
  arc vs segment    cross^2       against r^2 * |B-A|^2
  segment vs segment   orientation signs of cross products
```

So the additions are: a general ring multiply modulo x^8 + 1, a conjugation and
norm landing in the real subfield Z[sqrt2], and a sign test there (for
`a + b*sqrt2`, compare `a^2` against `2*b^2` when the terms disagree in sign).
No inverses, no field division, no rational tower. This also keeps the growth in
`cyclotomic.ts`, which is reuse rather than a parallel geometry module, so D3's
arrangement holds with one file growing rather than a new one appearing.

**Accepted ceiling, Completeness 5/10, stated at the question.** The exactness is
real but the threshold it compares against is not: `CALIBRATION.md:76` and
`:83-84` record that plank width is not the gauge and is written down nowhere.
So every overlap verdict is an exact comparison against a provisional constant.
Required consequence, carried into the verdict copy: the overlap result discloses
the provisional footprint width it used and states that it cannot rule on margins
near that width. Without that disclosure this repeats R21 exactly, which was
precision manufactured from `estimated` inputs.
**REVIEW TRIGGER:** when K0b records a measured plank width, revisit whether the
exact tier's precision is now load-bearing or still decorative, and remove the
disclosure if the measurement supports it.
Chosen over this reviewer's recommendation of A (R19's two tiers, needing no new
arithmetic) and over the outside voice's C2, which argued the exact path is both
unbuildable as scoped and pointless. Buildable it is, per the mechanics above.
The reusability argument stands on its own: the same arithmetic is what switches
and the crossing will need.

History: approved at D4 as an exact arc-and-segment narrowphase covering all
three pair cases. Reopened 2026-10-08 on verified contradictory evidence: the
required ring arithmetic does not exist in `cyclotomic.ts`, and the threshold it
compares against is unrecorded per `CALIBRATION.md:83-84`. Re-answered B, which
keeps D4's intent and funds the arithmetic it assumed. D4's accepted scope is
superseded by this record's answer. The verified claim that the arithmetic was
absent stands; the inference that it was therefore unbuildable in scope does not.

### H1: does Release 1 keep the user's work across a refresh

Finding: outside voice H1, `[High]`, raised against D2 and 9A.

Plan baseline: D2 deferred M8, "save/load with the R20 semantics hash", out of the
release. 9A approved an error boundary that prints the placed chain as selectable
text, and its own justification is "there is no persistence, so a throw would
otherwise destroy the work". No decision covers keeping work across anything other
than a throw.

Runtime evidence: verified. `app/src/App.tsx:50` holds the chain in
`useState<Piece[]>`, and there is no `localStorage`, `sessionStorage` or
`IndexedDB` reference anywhere in `app/src`. A refresh, a tab close, a navigation
away or the synchronous-render freeze the outside voice identified at C3 all lose
the entire layout with no recovery path. 9A's text rescue fires on exactly one of
those four.

This is new scope, not a reopening of D2: the deferred item is a file format with
a semantics hash for sharing and reopening, which is heavy and genuinely
deferrable. Keeping the current chain across a refresh is a different thing with
no format to freeze.

Comparison grid:

| Choice | Current | A: localStorage now | B: keep 9A only |
|---|---|---|---|
| H1 work survives a throw | 9A's text rescue | 9A's text rescue, unchanged | 9A's text rescue |
| H1 work survives a refresh or tab close | lost | restored | lost |
| H1 work survives the C3 freeze | lost | restored after a reload | lost |
| New format to keep compatible | n/a | none; a try/catch around one JSON blob, tolerant of a schema change | n/a |
| D2 M8 deferral | approved D2 | unchanged, still deferred | unchanged |
| 9A error boundary | approved 9A | retained, mostly redundant | retained, load-bearing |
| R20 semantics hash | deferred with M8 | still deferred; no hash, so a stale blob is discarded on parse failure rather than reinterpreted | still deferred |

Checks: the D2, 9A and R20 rows cite their answers and are unchanged in both
columns; none is reopened. Only whether the chain is written to local storage
varies.

Question D15:

D15 — H1: should Release 1 keep the layout across a refresh?

Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `main`; new scope from the outside voice.

ELI10: Right now the layout lives only in memory. Refresh the page, close the tab,
or hit the freeze the overlap check can cause, and twenty minutes of placing track
is gone. The heavy version of saving, a real file format you can share, is
deliberately deferred. The light version is writing the piece list to the
browser's local storage and reading it back on load, which is about thirty lines
and has no format to keep compatible.

Stakes if we pick wrong: the crash screen approved at 9A exists precisely because
losing work is the top risk, and it covers only one of the four ways work is
currently lost.

Recommendation: A, because it costs about thirty lines, needs no file format, and
covers three failure modes that nothing currently covers.

Completeness: A=10/10, B=5/10

Pros / cons:
A) Write the chain to localStorage (recommended)
  ✅ Covers refresh, tab close and recovery after a freeze, none of which 9A's text rescue reaches, since a hang is not a throw.
  ✅ No format to freeze and no migration debt: a JSON blob read inside a try/catch, discarded if it no longer parses, which is exactly how a schema change should be absorbed while the schema is still moving.
  ✅ Makes 9A mostly redundant, so the crash screen can stay simple rather than becoming the only recovery mechanism.
  ❌ A stale blob from an older build can restore a layout the current engine judges differently, and with R20's semantics hash deferred there is nothing to detect that, so the mitigation is to discard rather than reinterpret.
B) Keep 9A only, no storage
  ✅ No new persistence surface at all, so there is no stale-state class of bug and nothing to discard or version.
  ✅ Keeps the release strictly to geometry plus the already-approved app fixes.
  ❌ Three of the four ways work is lost stay uncovered, including recovery from the freeze this release's own overlap check can cause.
  ❌ Leaves a hand-built copy-the-text escape hatch as the entire answer to the risk the plan calls its top one.

Net: A covers the real failure modes for about thirty lines and accepts a stale-blob case; B keeps the surface minimal and leaves work loss mostly unaddressed.

Header: Local persistence
Options:
A) Write the chain to localStorage (recommended)
Covers refresh, tab close and recovery after a freeze, none of which 9A's text rescue reaches since a hang is not a throw. No format to freeze and no migration debt: a JSON blob read inside a try/catch and discarded if it no longer parses, which is how a schema change should be absorbed while the schema is still moving. Makes 9A mostly redundant. Cost: a stale blob from an older build can restore a layout the current engine judges differently, and with R20's hash deferred nothing detects that, so the mitigation is to discard rather than reinterpret.
B) Keep 9A only, no storage
No new persistence surface, so no stale-state bugs and nothing to version or discard, and the release stays strictly geometry plus the already-approved app fixes. Cost: three of the four ways work is lost stay uncovered, including recovery from the freeze this release's own overlap check can cause, leaving a hand-built copy-the-text escape hatch as the entire answer to the risk the plan calls its top one.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Write the chain to localStorage, answer D15, 2026-10-08
Accepted scope: `app/src/App.tsx` writes `{ placements }` to `localStorage` on
change and reads it back on mount, inside a `try`/`catch`. A blob that no longer
parses, or whose part ids are no longer in the catalogue, is **discarded** rather
than reinterpreted, because R20's semantics hash is deferred with M8 and nothing
else can detect a stale blob. No file format is frozen and no migration path is
owed. D2 stands: M8, the shareable format with its hash, stays deferred.
9A's error boundary is retained but becomes mostly redundant, so it stays simple
rather than growing into the only recovery mechanism.
Covers three failure modes nothing currently covers: refresh, tab close, and
recovery after the synchronous-render freeze at outside finding C3, none of which
9A reaches because a hang is not a throw.
Accepted cost, stated at the question: a stale blob from an older build can
restore a layout the current engine judges differently, and the mitigation is
discard-on-parse-failure rather than detection.
History: none

## NOT in scope
