## What already exists

Reuse, unchanged: `cyclotomic.ts` (**grows** at D14 with ring multiply, norm into
Z[sqrt2] and a sign test, rather than a parallel geometry module being created),
`pose.ts`'s exact `walk` (second caller arrives with the overlap tier),
`fk.ts`'s `walkFloat` (now feeding the hidden chain table too, per D3),
`residual.ts:48-56`'s open-chain gap (finally reachable once N11 moves the
`OPEN_CHAIN` return below `computeResidual`), `evaluate.ts`, and
`render.test.ts`, which tests the float renderer's sweep flag and not exact
centres.

Retired rather than reused: nothing this release. `gaugeWidthMm` is **not**
reused as the footprint width, contrary to decision 1A; a new provisional
`plankWidthMm` carries `estimated` status instead, because `CALIBRATION.md:76`
records that the plank is not the gauge and is written down nowhere.

Still unexamined after six reviews: `mattsouth/woodentrack`, now resequenced
ahead of the overlap work at D5.

## Worktree parallelization strategy

| Step | Modules touched | Depends on |
|------|----------------|------------|
| M15 prior-art read | none | — |
| localStorage | `app/src/` | — |
| D14 ring arithmetic | `engine/src/geom/` | M15 |
| Overlap narrowphase | `engine/src/feasible.ts` (new) | D14 ring arithmetic |
| Overlap rendering | `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx` | narrowphase |
| Verdict composition | `app/src/VerdictLine.tsx`, `engine/src/joints/` | narrowphase |
| Tokens, type, targets, fonts | `app/src/tokens.css`, `app/src/app.css`, `app/index.html` | — |
| DOM test project | `vitest.config.ts`, `package.json` | — |
| a11y surfaces | `app/src/` | DOM test project |
| K0b measurement | `CALIBRATION.md` | — |

**Parallel lanes:** `Lane A: M15 -> ring arithmetic -> narrowphase -> overlap
rendering + verdict composition` (the spine) / `Lane B: localStorage, tokens,
type, touch targets, fonts (independent)` / `Lane C: DOM test project -> a11y
surfaces` / `Lane D: K0b measurement, which gates Release 2 entirely and is the
only lane that cannot be done by an agent`.
**Execution order:** launch A, B, C and D together. B merges whenever ready. C
must land before any a11y work. A is strictly sequential. D's result reopens
Release 2.
**Conflict flags:** `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx` is touched by the overlap
rendering, the settle and the chain table, all in Lane A and C; sequence them or
expect a merge. `app/src/VerdictLine.tsx` is touched by four decisions.

## Unresolved decisions that may bite you later

- **M1, decision 5A's auto-promote threshold.** "Within one piece-length" is undefined: longest in the catalogue, last placed, 54mm or 216mm? And it creates a discontinuity nobody decided, where 215mm apart shows a quiet progress line and 217mm shows a red X, so a 2mm move flips between "making progress" and "won't fit". It also puts a float comparison upstream of the float-free closure test, deciding *which* test runs. Raised by the outside voice, not asked, not defaulted.
- **H2, the `heightMm` contradiction.** `engine/src/parts/schema.ts:48-52` records a deliberate decision against an integer level index, calling it "a reversibility-1 mistake". The two-layer model is exactly that index. The recorded decision must be explicitly retired or the model changed before Release 2 is built. Deferred with the layer work, so not asked here, but it is unresolved rather than closed.

Blocked rather than unresolved, because a measurement settles them and no decision can: **Q4**, whether the ramp's 216mm is the run or the plank, and **Q5**, the clearance margin, which with guessed numbers is 8mm from three unmeasured figures and whose contingency row says single-level bridges become impossible if it is zero or negative.

### What this review changed

It began as a check on three design decisions and ended by **splitting the
release**. The outside voice found that `CALIBRATION.md` — written by this
project two days earlier — contains a contingency row saying in writing that if
the bridge clearance margin measures zero or negative, "**Single-level bridges
are impossible... Stop before building the layer work; this is a design change,
not a tuning change.**" The margin is 8mm composed of three unmeasured figures.
**Five reviews made 31 decisions about the layer feature without anyone reading
that row.** D13 split the release: everything elevation waits for the caliper,
and the half that needs no measurement — the two verified false passes, plus
persistence and the app fixes — ships first.

Three further outside findings changed accepted work rather than confirming it:

- **D4 was reopened and refunded.** The exact narrowphase assumed ring arithmetic
  that `cyclotomic.ts` does not have: no multiply, no conjugation, no norm, no
  sign test. D14 funds it, and the review found that no division is needed, since
  every comparison cross-multiplies into squared form.
- **1A is wrong in its value.** The plank is not the gauge. `CALIBRATION.md:76`
  says so verbatim and adds that plank width "is currently not recorded
  anywhere". A provisional `plankWidthMm` replaces the mistaken `gaugeWidthMm`.
- **H3 corrected D9's reasoning while leaving its fix standing.** Overlap, the
  unmatched ramp and the open-chain line cannot be `SolveFailure` members,
  because every `!ok` result renders grey; they belong on `LayoutVerdict`.

### Four errors by this reviewer, caught and corrected

1. **I conflated gauge with plank width** across the second design review and into
   this one, and recommended drawing the track at 40mm on that basis.
2. **I presented D8's per-edit cost as its worst case.** Clicking to 640 pieces is
   O(n^3), not O(n^2), because `useMemo` is keyed on a fresh `layout` per click.
3. **I claimed 640 pieces was "reachable by holding one palette button."** A React
   `onClick` does not auto-repeat. The risk is real; that repro was not, and it
   was the stated justification for overriding the cap.
4. **I let the shell's working directory stay inside the gstack install** after the
   auto-upgrade, so `gstack-slug` resolved `garrytan-gstack` and the design-doc
   check returned gstack's own plan. Caught before anything was logged to the
   wrong project namespace, and logged as a durable learning.

and put tolerances and the calibration document in the backlog.

and selected by the user at D1. Review depth: **strategy-only** — the live
questions were sequencing and feasibility, not endpoint contracts. No design doc
exists; `/office-hours` was offered earlier in the session and declined, so it
was not re-offered.

### What the outside voice found, and what it cost me

Codex: `not_installed`, sixth consecutive review. **No external provider has ever
run on this project.** The native subagent's bounded five-minute wait **expired**
and returned a raw in-progress transcript; the completed report arrived after the
stop was issued. **Outside coverage is therefore recorded `unavailable` and no
late result is credited as coverage.** Its claims were treated as leads and the
decisive ones verified independently against source and by execution. Four were
checked, all four confirmed:

| Claim | Verification |
|---|---|
| A coincident-connector check dominates the same-circle tier | **CONFIRMED by execution.** One `equals()` loop, adjacency and closing seam skipped: `16xE` FLAGGED at 9 pairs, `8L+8R` FLAGGED at 0~8 and 8~16, `8xE` clean, stadium clean. Catches both false passes including the tangency, with no plank width |
| The schema claims connector geometry it does not have | **CONFIRMED.** `schema.ts:4-5` says it "carries connector geometry per part, because retrofitting those fields would migrate every part record"; grep finds no connector, polarity, plug, nest, socket or peg field anywhere. Plan line 3189 recorded `two male ends can be joined \| unjoinable layout PASSes \| none`; F1 at line 2829 CONFIRMED it |
| `CALIBRATION.md` asks nothing about switch geometry | **CONFIRMED.** Zero matches for switch, turnout or diverging in the entire sheet |
| A cycle basis is not unique, so a per-cycle gap number is representation-dependent | **ACCEPTED by reasoning.** Closure itself is basis-independent because the residual is linear over the cycle space, but the individual cycles a spanning tree produces are not, so the reported gap and the highlighted loop can change when an unrelated piece is added |

**Three errors of mine, corrected:**

1. **D3's premise was wrong.** I said the 8L+8R tangency needed the footprint
   tier. It does not: the tangency places three joints at one exact position, so
   position equality finds it. I conflated "a centreline-intersection test cannot
   see a single-point touch" with "no exact test can find it." Second time this
   session I have mis-reasoned about that fixture. Reversed at D10.
2. **D8 created a reachable critical gap.** Append-only could not violate
   connector polarity because orientation stays consistent all the way round.
   Letting the user weld any two free ends makes a peg-to-peg join reachable,
   with exact closure and an `EXACT` verdict on a layout that cannot be
   assembled. Fixed at D11.
3. **D4's claim that "a graph expresses both" is false for the crossing.** Its
   two routes share no connector node, so a pure connector graph never states
   their rigid relative placement. The graph decision stands; the claim needed
   correcting, and a piece layer or a zero-length rigid edge is now owed work.

| Sign-in, accounts, backend, published library | D2 | Inventory is local data and localStorage already works; the backend would buy only cross-device sync and a public library, both blocked on open provenance and trademark questions |
| Tolerances and the calibration afternoon | user instruction | Backlogged with its own TODOS entry naming everything that follows |
| Elevation, layers, ramps, clearance | follows the calibration deferral | Q5's contingency says single-level bridges may be impossible; the margin is 8mm of unmeasured numbers |
| The footprint overlap tier and plank width | D10 | Replaced by a check that needs no measurement; what stays uncovered is near-miss adjudication and visible non-joint crossings |
| Exact ring arithmetic in `Z[sqrt2]` | D3 then D10 | Funded at D14 of the eng review, then made unnecessary twice over |
| An intermediate crossing-only step | D4 | A graph expresses both, so building piece identity in the chain model first is throwaway |
| Full drag-and-drop with undo | D5 | Free-end clicking is the smallest interaction that makes a branch buildable |
| Bendy track, flexible track, turntables | D6 | Arbitrary curvature has no lattice representation; approximating one would make exact closure conditional for every layout containing it |
| Unconditional exactness | D9 | Traded for switch support, with a stated per-switch modelled error |

### What already exists

Reuse unchanged: `cyclotomic.ts` (D10 removed the need to extend it at all — `equals` is sufficient), `pose.ts`'s exact walk (needs a reverse-traversal companion, owed), `fk.ts`, `evaluate.ts`, `localStorage` persistence from `68800f8` (its discard-on-anything-unexpected design makes the graph format change migration-free by accident), the self-hosted fonts and corrected tokens from `a933206`.

Rebuilt rather than reused: `Layout` and both walkers' return shapes. Retired: `closed: boolean`, append-only placement, 5A's proximity auto-promote.

Already-settled design the graph work inherits: the TODO's note that one spanning forest serves both the cycle basis and the pose walker, because parent pointers give poses downward and fundamental cycles upward to the LCA. Do not write two traversals.

work**, because C2 and C3 are confirmed gaps in shipped code and C1 is the
measurement D9 turned into a dependency.

Effort assumptions: exact geometry ~5x, data transcription ~30x, app features
~30x, tests ~50x, research ~3x. C1 does not compress.

### Unresolved decisions that may bite you later

- **Set manifests versus count-per-part inventory.** D2 accepted "declare which sets you own," which needs a second body of transcribed BRIO data (set contents) and creates the ManifestDrift gap where a manifest naming an absent part silently corrupts counts. The outside voice argues a count-per-part input is what "which pieces do I own" actually needs, deletes the drift class entirely, and avoids transcribing more third-party data while provenance is an open legal question. Not asked, not defaulted; it changes C12's shape.
- **`PartBase.heightMm` and the integer level index.** `engine/src/parts/schema.ts:48-52` records a deliberate decision against an integer level index, calling it "a reversibility-1 mistake." The two-layer model is exactly that index. Previously filed as mattering only for deferred elevation work, but the field is live in every part record and D6 multiplies those records, so the cost of leaving it open now scales with the catalogue.

## Completion Summary (CEO re-review, 2026-10-08)

- Mode: **SCOPE REDUCTION**, selected by the user at D1
- Review depth: strategy-only
- Scope proposals: **5 cuts proposed, 5 accepted, 0 deferred**
- Sections 1-10 evaluated; Section 11 run (UI scope detected)
- Error & Rescue Registry: 14 capability rows
- Failure modes: **6 critical gaps**, all six in the graph work, five found by the outside voice after the sections had passed
- Decisions: **11 answered** (D1-D11), 0 pending; D3 superseded by D10, 5A and M1 superseded by D8
- Unresolved: **2**
- Outside voice: codex `not_installed` for the sixth consecutive review; the native subagent's bounded wait **expired** and its completed report arrived late, so **outside coverage is `unavailable`** and no late result is credited as coverage. Four of its claims were verified independently; all four confirmed
- Tasks: **14** (C1-C14)
- TODOS.md: 1 entry written (the calibration backlog, per direct instruction)
- Lake Score: **7/9** answers picking a 10/10 option

### What this review changed

It took four asks and cut one outright. **Sign-in is gone**: inventory is local
data and the one-hour-old localStorage commit already carries it, so a month of
accounts, a database and a children's privacy posture bought nothing the ask
needed. It then found that **the switches blocker recorded in TODOS no longer
existed** — R21 deleted K0c, the gauge sweep now passes exactly at every cut
point by construction, and the "coupled minimax over a cycle basis" the TODO
described was LP-era work with nothing left to couple. What remains is graph
topology, not numerical method.

Then the outside voice took the review apart. It showed that **D3's premise was
false** and a simpler check catches both known false passes including the
tangency, that **D8 had made a recorded critical gap reachable**, that
**`CALIBRATION.md` asks nothing about switch geometry** while the whole direction
assumes the answer, and that **a cycle basis is not unique**, so D7's reported
gap can drift when an unrelated piece is added. Five of the six critical gaps in
the registry came from that pass, after eleven sections had already run.

The net: the direction survives, reduced and resequenced, with three
confirmed-gap fixes in front of it and exactness now conditional on switches by
explicit choice.


## Design re-review: switches, welds, inventory (2026-10-08)

Invoked as `/plan-design-review` on the plan file, immediately after the CEO
re-review cut the scope to a graph model, an expanded catalogue, and local
inventory. The CEO review was strategy-depth by its own declaration and said so:
it did not design any of the three new surfaces. This pass designs them.

Scope gate: **B, the plan file**, chosen by the user. D1: **all seven passes**.
D2: **run the native design voice** (it timed out; see coverage below).

What is new since the first design review's 23 decisions: free ends are
clickable, welds are user-declared, a layout is a graph rather than a chain, the
catalogue grows from 6 parts to every lattice-expressible part plus switches and
a crossing, exactness is conditional on switches, and inventory exists. Every one
of those changes something a user looks at.

### Scores, before and after

```
  PASS                              BEFORE   AFTER   WHAT MOVED IT
  ----------------------------------|-------|-------|---------------------------
  1 First impression (visceral)      3/10    10/10   D3, D4, D5, D6, D10
  2 Interaction & affordances        2/10     9/10   D7, D8
  3 User journey (reflective)        7/10     7/10   storyboard recorded; step 9
                                                     is a dead end, UNRESOLVED
  4 Information hierarchy            8/10     8/10   no findings
  5 Visual craft                     4/10     4/10   no NEW findings; the open
                                                     items are N-tasks already
  6 Responsive & accessibility       3/10     9/10   D9
  7 Unresolved register               —       —      D10; register below
```

Pass 1 reached 10 only at D10, which was asked under Pass 7: the empty state had
accumulated three competing invitations across four decisions, and no single pass
owned it. Pass 5 stays at 4 because the first design review's visual tasks
(N-series) are still unimplemented, not because this pass found new problems.
Pass 3 stays at 7 for one reason, stated plainly in the register.

### Decision ledger

| ID / owner | Contract and evidence | Current | Proposed | Status | Approval and scope |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D3 verdict / app | the verdict now carries three unrelated things: can it close, how much do we trust it, what do I do next | one line | three registers | **approved** | A: verdict, trust, action |
| D4 render / app | `TrackCanvas.tsx:46` scales stroke by `min(w,h)*0.022`, so a piece's drawn width changes with layout extent; plank width is recorded nowhere (`CALIBRATION.md:76`) | viewport fraction | provisional `plankWidthMm`, marked estimated | **approved** | B |
| D5 render / app | a switch and a crossing are not centrelines; drawing them as strokes cannot show which route is which | per-segment stroke | footprint as one body per piece | **approved** | A |
| D6 inventory / app | the CEO review left set-manifests-vs-counts explicitly unresolved and said it changes C12's shape | undecided | sets as a fast path, part counts as the truth | **approved** | C. RESOLVES the CEO review's open item |
| D7 weld / app | D8 of the CEO review made welds user-declared, with no way to undo one | declare only | welds clickable, clicking breaks | **approved** | A |
| D8 inventory / app | a shortfall is information about the real world, not an error in the layout | undecided | advice, never a gate | **approved** | A. Palette greyed, never disabled |
| D9 a11y / app | every new interaction is spatial; E12 specified the listing as read-only | read-only announcement | the listing becomes operable | **approved** | A. Needs E11's DOM project to verify |
| D10 empty state / app | three invitations now compete in the one place a first-time user looks | hint + 3 presets + inventory | preset first, inventory after the first layout | **approved** | C. Raises Pass 1 to 10 |

### Pass 3 storyboard, as the plan now stands

```
  STEP                                   WHAT THE USER SEES           STATE
  ---------------------------------------|----------------------------|--------
  1  lands on an empty canvas             one preset, prominent        D10
  2  clicks it                            exact circle, EXACT verdict  shipped
  3  wants a branch, looks for a way       free ends are marked         D5, C10
  4  clicks a free end                     it activates, visibly        C10
  5  clicks a compatible second end        weld declared, redrawn       C2, C10
  6  changes their mind about the weld      clicks it, it breaks         D7
  7  adds a switch from the palette         two routes, drawn as bodies  D5, C11
  8  reads the verdict                      EXACT except switches, with  D3, C13
                                            the modelled error stated
  9  hits a near miss: "8mm short"          ...nothing. No next step.    <-GAP
  10 declares inventory, sees a shortfall   advice, palette stays usable D6, D8
  11 reloads                                the layout is still there    shipped
```

Step 9 is the only break in the arc and it is the same break the first design
review found. Nudge-to-fit and ranked suggestions are both deferred, so the
journey's emotional low point is also the point where the product stops talking.
That is a product decision, not an oversight, and it is why Pass 3 is 7 and not
higher. Not asked, because adding a suggester to a plan this heavily cut is new
scope the user has twice chosen not to fund.

### What the three registers are, concretely (D3)

The verdict line was one sentence. It now has to carry three answers that move
independently, and collapsing them is how a user ends up trusting a number that
was never trustworthy:

```
  REGISTER   ANSWERS                         WORST CASE IF MERGED
  -----------|-------------------------------|-------------------------------
  verdict    can this close?                  EXACT reads as "will fit"
  trust      how much of that is measured?    estimated give reads as measured
  action     what do I do about it?           a FAIL with no next step
```

Today's line merges all three and the provisional note sits in a separate
paragraph at the bottom of the palette (`App.tsx:188-192`), which is the one
place nobody looks after the first session. With switches the trust register
stops being a disclaimer and becomes load-bearing: D9 of the CEO review traded
unconditional exactness for switch support, so "EXACT" now means "exact except
for N switches, each contributing a stated error."

### Error & Rescue Registry (design surfaces)

```
  SURFACE                  | WHAT THE USER DOES WRONG         | RESCUED? | WHAT THEY SEE
  -------------------------|----------------------------------|----------|----------------
  free-end click           | clicks a non-end                 | Y        | nothing activates
  free-end click           | clicks an incompatible end        | Y, C2    | end not offered
  weld                     | welds the wrong pair             | Y, D7    | click it to break
  weld                     | breaks a weld, splits the graph  | Y        | two components, both drawn
  switch                   | expects exactness                | Y, D3    | trust register states the error
  inventory                | declares nothing                 | Y, D8    | unconstrained, said explicitly
  inventory                | set manifest is wrong            | Y, D6    | counts are the truth, editable
  near miss                | wants it fixed                   | N <-GAP  | the gap, and no next step
  graph listing            | keyboard only                    | Y, D9    | full parity with the canvas
  empty state              | does not know where to start     | Y, D10   | one preset, prominent
```

### Diagram: the three new surfaces

```
  EMPTY STATE                                                      [D10]
    one preset, prominent
         |
         v
  CANVAS  (footprints, not strokes)                            [D5, D4]
    free ends marked -> click -> click -> weld            [C10, C2]
    welds clickable -> break                                      [D7]
         |
         +----> VERDICT, three registers                          [D3]
         |        verdict | trust | action
         |
         +----> GRAPH LISTING, operable                           [D9]
                  every piece, end and weld; Enter does
                  what a click does. One surface, both jobs.
         |
         v
  INVENTORY, offered after the first layout                  [D10, D6]
    sets as a fast path -> part counts as the truth
    shortfall is ADVICE: palette greyed, never disabled           [D8]
```

### What the outside voice cost me this time

Codex: `not_installed`, **seventh consecutive review**. No external provider has
ever run on this project. The native design subagent was dispatched per D2; its
bounded wait expired at 300s having produced only "I'll read the five files", and
it was killed with no usable output. **Outside coverage: `unavailable`.**

Visual mockups: **0 of 0 saved**, exit 2, `No OpenAI API key found`. Third
consecutive review with none. Every judgment in this pass about what a switch or
a footprint looks like is reasoning from source, not from a rendered image, and
D4 and D5 both deserve a picture before they are implemented.

That is two missing credentials, not one problem: `npm install -g @openai/codex`
for the outside voice, and an `OPENAI_API_KEY` for mockups.

### Correction

The first design review's 1A claimed plank width equals `gaugeWidthMm: 40`. It
does not: 40mm is the rail gauge, and the plank is wider and recorded nowhere.
That was caught two reviews ago and is restated here because **D4 depends on it**
— a provisional `plankWidthMm` is a new field with an estimated value, not a
rename of an existing one, and marking it estimated is the whole point of
choosing B over a hardcoded number.

### NOT in scope (design re-review)

| Item | Answer | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Nudge-to-fit, ranked fix suggestions | not asked | New scope on a plan the user cut twice. It is why Pass 3 is 7/10 and it is named in the register, not hidden |
| Drag-and-drop, undo for welds beyond break | D7 | Clicking a weld to break it is the smallest reversal that makes declaring one safe |
| A real design system | carried from the first review | `tokens.css` is still PROVISIONAL by its own header. The N-series owns this |
| Measured plank width | D4 | Follows the calibration deferral. D4 ships an estimate and labels it |
| Hiding unavailable palette pieces | D8 | Greying teaches the catalogue; hiding teaches nothing and reads as a bug |
| A separate inventory screen in the first session | D10 | Offered after the first layout, where it has something to be useful about |
| Elevation, layers, ramps | follows the calibration deferral | Unchanged from the CEO review |

Reuse unchanged: the `.num` tabular-figure class and the two self-hosted faces
from `a933206`; the four corrected verdict colours and `--track-edge` at 4.10:1;
the `@media (pointer: coarse)` 44px block in `app.css`; the 12px data-label

listing (D9 makes it operable).

Carried forward unimplemented: the first design review's N-series. Pass 5 is

## Completion Summary (design re-review, 2026-10-08)

### What this review changed

The CEO review said out loud that it was strategy-depth and had designed none of
the three new surfaces. It had not. **The verdict line was still one sentence**
carrying three answers that now move independently, with the trust disclaimer
parked at the bottom of the palette where nobody reads it; D3 split it. **The
canvas still drew centrelines**, which cannot show which route of a switch is
which, and still scaled stroke width by viewport fraction; D4 and D5 fixed both,
and D4 is the first field in the schema deliberately labelled estimated. **Welds
were declarable but not breakable**, which made a one-click action a one-way
door; D7 made them clickable.

Two things it found that were not about the new surfaces. D6 answered the CEO
review's own open question by inverting it: store **part counts** as the truth
and treat a set declaration as a fast path, which deletes the manifest-drift gap
rather than mitigating it. And D9 caught that every new interaction is spatial,
so E12's read-only chain listing had to become an operable graph listing or the
entire feature set would have had no keyboard path at all.

D10 closed the one thing no single pass owned: four decisions had each added
something to the empty state, and it had quietly accumulated three competing
calls to action.

What did not move: the near miss still ends the journey with a number and no next
step, and the first review's visual tasks are still unbuilt.

| **C4** | Coincident-connector refusal, new verdict `IMPOSSIBLE` | `engine/src/feasible.ts` (new), `result.ts`, `evaluate.ts`, `VerdictLine.tsx` |

### Two false passes closed, and the numbers

C4's check is one `equals()` loop over connector positions with adjacency and
the closing seam excluded. Run against the fixtures:

```
  FIXTURE    CLOSES EXACTLY?   COINCIDENT PAIRS   VERDICT BEFORE   VERDICT NOW
  -----------|----------------|------------------|----------------|-------------
  8xE         yes              0                  EXACT            EXACT
  16xE        yes              9  (0~8 ... 8~16)  EXACT  <-WRONG   IMPOSSIBLE
  8L+8R       yes              2  (0~8, 8~16)     EXACT  <-WRONG   IMPOSSIBLE
  stadium     yes              0                  EXACT            EXACT
  near miss   no               0                  EXCEEDS          EXCEEDS
```

`IMPOSSIBLE` is checked BEFORE closure and reported on the `ok: true` branch, not
as a solve failure. Both matter. Checking closure first would report EXACT for a
double circle, which is the bug. Reporting it as `ok: false` would route it
through `unknownLayout` and render GREY, saying "could not check" about a layout
the engine checked and definitively refused.

The verdict also states its own limit: only exact connector coincidences are
found, so a plank crossing a plank mid-span is still invisible. That needs
measured plank width and stays out of scope.

### Mutation-checked, not just green

Each fix was reverted in place to confirm the new tests actually bite:

```
  MUTATION                                  RESULT
  ------------------------------------------|---------------------------
  joint budget back to one side              2 of 3 C3 tests FAIL
  polarity check skips the closing seam      1 test FAIL
  clash check drops the seam exclusion       4 tests FAIL
  (all three reverted; 50 pass)
```

The third C3 test passes under its mutation and that is honest rather than
missing: with the loose piece last, the one-sided read happens to agree, so that
test pins the wrap-around target instead of the pair combination.

### Three corrections to my own work in this lane

1. **`max`, not `min`, for the pair budget.** The instinct is that a joint gives
   as much as its tighter end allows. But `totalGiveMm` backs a NECESSARY
   condition, and "gap exceeds give, therefore definitely will not fit" is only
   sound if the give figure is an UPPER bound on the give really available.
   Understating it would make the one definite verdict this engine issues a
   false one. Every part currently shares one budget, so this changes no output
   today; it stops being cosmetic the moment the catalogue differs.
2. **The greyed palette label failed the text contrast floor.** `opacity: 0.75`
   on `--ink-soft` over `--paper` measures **3.14:1**: above WCAG 1.4.11's 3:1
   non-text floor, below the **4.5:1** that 14px text owes. 0.55 would have been
   2.21:1 and failed both. Opacity removed; the label sits at full `--ink-soft`
   at 5.21:1 and the signal moved to border pattern.
3. **A dashed `--rule` border is not a signal.** `--rule` measures **1.40:1**
   against `--paper`, so dashing it changes a pattern nobody can see. The unowned
   border is `--ink-soft` dashed, at 5.21:1.

### What was deliberately NOT built, and why

- **Real BRIO set manifests.** D6 made counts the stored truth and a set
  declaration a one-way fast path, so no manifest is ever re-read. The fast-path
  MECHANISM is built and tested; its entries are described in terms of this
  catalogue and labelled in the UI as **not official BRIO set contents**.
  Transcribing real set contents is the same open provenance question already
  flagged over the part data, and inventing plausible-looking contents would be
  worse than offering none.
- **The remaining U-tasks.** U1 (three registers), U3 (footprint bodies), U5
  (breakable welds), U7 (operable listing) and U8 (empty state) all need C6 or
  C11. U7 additionally needs E11.
- **A DOM test project.** `vitest.config.ts` now includes `app/**/*.test.ts` for
  PURE LOGIC only, with no environment set. Widening an include pattern is not
  E11 and is not a substitute for it: anything needing a `document` still waits.

### What the eng review inherits

`Layout` is still `{ placements, closed }`. `schema.ts` now carries polarity and
plank width, so two of the fields the graph work needs already exist and are
tested. `feasible.ts` is new and is the natural home for the switch-heel and
crossing refusals. `residual.ts`'s joint loop now reads both sides of a joint,
which is the shape a graph needs; `pose.ts` is still forward-only, so C5's
reverse-traversal primitive is still owed.


## Eng review, fourth pass: the graph architecture (2026-10-08)

**Target (fixed):** this plan file,
`~/.gstack/projects/radudiaconescu/ceo-plans/2026-10-06-brio-track-designer.md`,
scoped by the user to the graph architecture described by tasks C6, C8 and C9.

the design-doc check all carried their existing state (see the ledger).

### Scope Challenge

**A. Assessment.**

What already solves each sub-problem: `pose.ts walk()` propagates poses but only
forward along a chain, so C5's reverse primitive is still owed. `computeResidual`
now reads both sides of a joint as of C3, which is already the shape a graph
needs. `feasible.ts` exists but is chain-indexed. Nothing exists for spanning
forests or cycle bases.

Reuse ladder: a spanning forest over a degree-<=4 graph of a few hundred nodes is
about thirty lines of breadth-first search. Pulling in `graphlib` or `ngraph` for
that fails rung 4 of the ladder. **[Layer 1]**, write it; no new dependency.

Search check, and it ruled an approach out rather than in: **a minimum cycle basis
is not unique.** The only canonical object over a graph's cycle space is the union
of all minimum cycle bases, the "relevant cycles" of Vismara 1997, reachable by
Horton at O(m^3 n) or De Pina at O(m^3 + m n^2 log n). That is far heavier than
this problem and it means C9's first option, "pick a canonical basis", cannot be
delivered by picking a better basis. **[Layer 1]** knowledge, applied as a
negative result. Sources: lirmm.fr/~vismara/papers/vismara97.pdf and
people.mpi-inf.mpg.de/~mehlhorn/ftp/CycleBasisImpl.pdf.

TODOS cross-reference: `brio-TODOS.md:358` still describes switches as needing
"per-(joint, cycle) sensitivities and a coupled minimax solve over a full cycle
basis", which is LP-era work decision R21 deleted; `:429` still carries the
overlap narrowphase primitive, superseded by D10. Both need correcting rather
than implementing, and both are TODO decisions below.

Complexity check: **8 files, 2 new modules.** The gate tripped, so B ran.

**B. Complexity selectors (post-answer scope record).**

```

                  engine/src/parts/schema.ts; spanning forest, cycle basis,
                  per-cycle residual and per-cycle bound inside
                  engine/src/solver/residual.ts. 8 files touched, 0 new.
accepted scope:   C6 as written, including the piece layer and the crossing's
                  rigid same-piece constraint. C8's spanning forest, fundamental
                  cycle basis, per-cycle residual, per-cycle bound and one gauge
                  anchor per connected component, with C8's own "one traversal
                  serving both" retained as a design requirement of the accepted
                  arrangement, not waived by it. C9's basis handling in scope;
                  its approach is pending R3.
pending remedies: R1, R2, R3, R4
```

Scope Challenge result: **scope accepted as-is.** No cut was accepted and the
smaller arrangement was declined, so MODE is FULL_REVIEW.


### Scope Challenge findings

### Decision ledger

#### R3: what gap number the verdict reports for a multi-cycle layout
Finding: 1, P1, confidence 10/10, plan line 9389 and `engine/src/solver/result.ts`, reviewer: eng review pass 4 (Scope Challenge search check)
Plan baseline: C9 as written, "make the reported gap basis-independent, or state in the verdict that it is not". No approach approved. D7 of the CEO review separately approved "worst loop reported and highlighted" and stays approved.
Runtime evidence: executed. Four valid bases of one rank-2 layout give worst-reported gaps of 10.592, 10.633, 10.633 and 10.674mm. In basis {c1, c1+c2} the 0.108mm loop reports as 10.592mm. Cycle-space enumeration measured at 4,095 combinations in 2ms, 65,535 in 15ms, 1,048,575 in 471ms, 4,194,303 in 2.4s.
Comparison grid:

| Row | Current | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Reported gap, multi-cycle | undefined; chain yields one residual | worst SIMPLE cycle, enumerated over the cycle space. Basis-independent | worst cycle of one arbitrary spanning-tree basis. Representation-dependent | unchanged, pending |
| Basis-independent? | n/a | yes, by construction | no, disclosed in the verdict | unresolved |
| Loop highlighted (D7, approved) | approved, unimplemented | the genuinely worst loop | a basis cycle, which may be the wrong loop | approved, unimplemented |
| Solve cost, 16 switches | n/a | 15ms measured | negligible | none |
| High-rank behaviour | n/a | above 20 switches, report "too many loops to rank" rather than silently falling back to a basis cycle | n/a | unresolved |
| R1 feasibility vertex lift | pending | pending | pending | pending |
| R2 per-component anchor | pending | pending | pending | pending |
| R4 per-cycle give | pending | pending | pending | pending |

Question D3:
D3 — Which gap number does the verdict report for a layout with switches?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `lane-b-tokens-persistence`; C9 of the graph architecture, with the crossing and piece layer kept in scope at D1.
ELI10: Once a layout has a switch it has more than one loop, and the solver has to pick which loops to measure. It does that by building a spanning tree and reading off one loop per leftover connection. The problem is that the tree is arbitrary: a different tree gives a different set of loops over the same track. I ran this on a real two-loop layout built from catalogue parts, and across four valid choices the reported worst gap moved between 10.592mm and 10.674mm. Worse, under one of those choices a loop that is genuinely 0.108mm from perfect gets reported as 10.592mm out. The user changed nothing; the solver just decomposed their track differently.
Stakes if we pick wrong: D7 already promises to highlight the worst loop on the canvas, so an arbitrary basis can point the user at the wrong loop and show them a number that moves when they add an unrelated piece elsewhere, which is exactly the representation-dependence decision R21 deleted the old solver for.
Recommendation: A because enumerating the cycle space is basis-independent by construction and measured at 15ms for 16 switches, so the correct answer is also the affordable one.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10, C=4/10
Pros / cons: see each option.
Net: A pays milliseconds for a number that does not move; B pays nothing and ships a number that moves, with a disclaimer.
Header: Gap reporting
Options:
A) Worst simple cycle, enumerated (recommended)
Enumerate the cycle space, keep the combinations whose support is a connected even subgraph, and report the worst of those. Basis-independent by construction, because the set of simple cycles is a property of the graph and not of a traversal. Above 20 switches the verdict says "too many loops to rank" rather than silently reporting a basis cycle.
B) Worst basis cycle, with the dependence disclosed
Build one spanning-tree fundamental basis, report its worst cycle, and state in the verdict that the number depends on how the solver decomposed the layout.
C) Investigate before choosing
Bound a spike that enumerates simple cycles on the existing fixtures and measures the real rank reached by plausible layouts, then choose. Approves no implementation; R3 stays pending.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Worst simple cycle, enumerated. Answer reference: D3, this pass, matching the reviewer's recommendation.
Accepted scope: the reported gap for a multi-cycle layout is the worst SIMPLE cycle, found by enumerating the cycle space and keeping the combinations whose support is a connected even subgraph. Basis-independent by construction. Above 20 switches the verdict reports "too many loops to rank" rather than silently falling back to a basis cycle. D7's highlighted loop is this same worst simple cycle. C9's verify condition stands as written: adding an unrelated piece must not change the reported gap for an untouched loop.
History: first asked this pass; no earlier value.


### 1. Architecture review

**A1. [P1] (confidence: 10/10) `engine/src/feasible.ts:81-82` and `:128-131` — both
feasibility predicates are chain-indexed, so a declared weld reads as an
impossible overlap.** The motivating lines:

```
  81      if (b - a === 1) continue;
  82      if (closed && a === 0 && (b === n || b === n - 1)) continue;
 128    const last = layout.closed ? n - 1 : n - 2;
 131    const outgoingId = layout.placements[(j + 1) % n]!.partId;
```

Every one of those is a statement about position in an ordered list. In the graph
model a weld the user declares makes two connector slots intentionally coincident:
they ARE one vertex. Run `findCoincidentConnectors` unchanged against a graph and
every weld is reported as two pieces occupying one place, which is the exact
opposite of the truth. `findPolarityViolations` has the same defect from the other
side: `(j + 1) % n` is a chain wrap, and a switch heel is a degree-3 vertex where
"the next placement" has no meaning.

The remedy is a simplification, not an addition: check **vertices**, not slots.
Two joined connectors are one vertex with one position, so the rule becomes "no
two DISTINCT vertices may share a position" and needs **no adjacency rule and no
seam rule at all** — both exclusions exist only to undo the chain model's
double-counting. Polarity likewise becomes a property of each weld rather than of
each chain joint. Recorded as necessary implementation of the already-approved
C4 (CEO D10) and C6 (CEO D4) contracts, carried forward without a new question.
The alternative mechanism, keeping slot-keyed checks and adding an exclusion list
of welded pairs, is rejected: it preserves the exclusions the graph model makes
unnecessary and grows a second source of truth about which slots are joined.

**A2. [P1] (confidence: 9/10) `engine/src/geom/pose.ts:33` and `:61-62`, with
`engine/src/geom/fk.ts:58-60` — there is exactly one hardcoded origin, so a
second component has nowhere to be.** The motivating lines:

```
 pose.ts:33   export const ORIGIN: Pose = { pos: ZERO, heading: 0 };
 pose.ts:61     const poses: Pose[] = [ORIGIN];
 pose.ts:62     let cur = ORIGIN;
 fk.ts:58-60    let x = 0;  let y = 0;  let h = 0;
```

C8 asks for "one gauge anchor per connected component" and the plan does not say
where an anchor comes from. Breaking a weld (D7 of the design review) splits the
graph, and the new component defaults to the same origin, landing on top of the
first. Remedy R2, pending.

A constraint the plan also does not record: a component anchor must be a lattice
point in Z[zeta16] with a heading that is a multiple of 22.5 degrees, or exact
closure dies for that component. A prior learning filed this as making a snap
epsilon a prerequisite. It does not, and that matters for R2: an anchor CAPTURED
from the pose the component already had is automatically a lattice point, because
that pose was produced by the exact integer walk. Captured, it is exact for free;
re-derived or user-entered, it needs an epsilon and exactness dies.

**A3. [P1] (confidence: 9/10) `engine/src/solver/result.ts:95` with
`app/src/App.tsx:121` — a sequencing hazard between C4's lift and C8.** Today
`const give = totalGiveMm(res.joints)` sums give over every joint in the layout
and compares one gap against that one pool. In the chain model this is safe for a
reason nobody wrote down: the only way a chain reaches cycle rank above 1 is for
two connectors to coincide, and C4 now refuses exactly that. Verified by
execution: the 8L+8R figure-eight has V=15, E=16, CC=1 and so cycle rank 2,
counted by integer equality on Z[zeta16], and C4 refuses it.

That safety disappears the moment welds become declarable, because a weld is a
legitimate shared vertex and rank 2 becomes the normal case. Until C8's per-cycle
bound lands, the bound would credit cycle B's give against a gap in cycle A.
Measured on an exhaustive sweep: at 10 pieces per cycle there are **9 distinct
gaps** in the band where a per-cycle bound refuses and a pooled bound says "might
fit", the smallest being 10.633mm against an own-cycle give of 8.0mm and a pooled
16.0mm. Direction of harm is the safe one, since overstating give only weakens a
necessary condition, so this is a missed refusal rather than a false definite
claim. Remedy R4, pending.

**A3 CORRECTION, added 2026-10-09 while settling the crossing question.** The
finding's conclusion stands; one piece of its evidence was wrong and is retracted
here rather than quietly edited above.

I wrote that the 8L+8R figure-eight "has V=15, E=16, CC=1 and so cycle rank 2,
counted by integer equality". That number came from merging coincident connector
POSITIONS into one vertex, and coincident is not joined. As a closed chain the
fixture is 16 pieces with 16 joints, so its **cycle rank is 1**, the same as the
plain circle. The layout is unbuildable and C4 correctly refuses it, but its
topology was never rank 2, and "a chain reaches rank above 1 only via two
coincident connectors" is false.

What survives, and why the gate still holds: rank >= 2 is genuinely reachable,
just not by that fixture and not by one switch. Verified on the piece-joint
graph, where nodes are pieces and edges are joints:

```
  LAYOUT                           CORRECT RANK
  --------------------------------|-------------
  8xE closed circle                1
  8L+8R closed chain               1   <- not 2, as I had it
  one switch, legs joined          1   <- heel dangling
  TWO switches, three paths        2   <- theta graph
  figure-eight via one CROSSING    2   <- one body, four attachment points
```

So pooling give across cycles is still unsound and C10 must still land after C8
(R4, answer D5 unchanged). The reachability threshold is two switches or one
crossing, not one weld on a chain. The decision-log entry for this pass repeated
the same wrong clause and has been superseded.

**A4. [P2] (confidence: 8/10) plan line 9377 (C6) — the switch heel needs three
distinct treatments and C6 names one.** C6 says the heel is a "switch-heel
interior point which donates no give". That is the first treatment. It also must
be excluded from polarity checks, because an interior point of a piece is not a
mating joint and has no peg or socket, and it must be INCLUDED in the
coincident-vertex check, because two pieces still cannot occupy it. One node kind,
three different behaviours, one of them written down.

**A5. [P3] (confidence: 9/10) `app/src/App.tsx:52` — `const STORAGE_KEY =
'brio.chain.v0'`.** When `Layout` becomes a graph the persisted shape changes.
`loadPieces` rejects a non-array and starts empty, so nothing corrupts, and the
plan calls this "migration-free by accident". Accident is the right word. Bump the
key with the model change so the discard is a decision rather than a side effect
of an `Array.isArray` guard.

**Covered, stated so it is not mistaken for a gap.** R3's rank ceiling prevents a
new hang: `App.tsx:121` runs `solveLayout` synchronously inside render, and
measured cycle-space enumeration reaches 2.4s at rank 22. A hang is not a throw,
so the error boundary cannot catch it. R3's accepted scope caps at rank 20 BEFORE
enumeration starts, which is a bound on input rather than a timeout, so the freeze
is unreachable by construction rather than recovered from.

**Distribution architecture.** No new artifacts, no new publish path; the Vite
build is unchanged. The absent CI remains the known gap already filed as a TODO
by the third eng review.

#### R2: where a connected component's pose anchor comes from
Finding: A2, P1, confidence 9/10, `engine/src/geom/pose.ts:33` and `:61-62`, `engine/src/geom/fk.ts:58-60`, reviewer: eng review pass 4 (Architecture)
Plan baseline: C8 as written, "One gauge anchor per connected component". Source of the anchor unspecified. D7 of the design review approved breakable welds, which is what creates a second component.
Runtime evidence: executed and read. `pose.ts` walks from the single exported `ORIGIN` constant and `fk.ts` from literal zeros; `Layout` carries no origin field. A second component therefore renders at the first component's origin. Poses produced by the existing walk are exact elements of Z[zeta16] with integer headings, so a captured anchor satisfies the lattice constraint with no rounding.
Comparison grid:

| Row | Current | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Anchor source | single hardcoded ORIGIN | captured from the pose the component already had at the moment of the split | user places the component after the split | splitting is refused; a weld cannot be broken if it would disconnect |
| Exact closure preserved | n/a, one component | yes, captured pose is already a lattice point with an integer heading | only with a snap epsilon, which reintroduces a float threshold into topology | yes, trivially |
| What the user sees on break | second component drawn on top of the first | both components stay exactly where they were | the broken-off part follows the pointer until placed | the break is rejected, with a reason |
| New stored field | none | per-component anchor pose | per-component anchor pose | none |
| Interacts with D8's no-float-threshold rule | n/a | holds, no threshold | VIOLATES it, needs an epsilon | holds |
| R4 per-cycle give | pending | pending | pending | pending |

Question D4:
D4 — When a weld breaks and the track splits in two, where does the second half go?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `lane-b-tokens-persistence`; C8's "one gauge anchor per connected component", which the plan leaves unspecified.
ELI10: The engine has exactly one starting point, a constant called ORIGIN, and it walks the whole layout from there. That is fine while a layout is one connected run of track. The design review then approved breaking a weld by clicking it, which can cut the track into two separate pieces, and the second piece has no starting point of its own, so it would be drawn on top of the first. Something has to say where it sits. The cheap and exact answer is to remember where it already was.
Stakes if we pick wrong: anything that computes a new position instead of remembering the old one needs rounding, and a rounded origin breaks exact closure for that whole component, which is the one guarantee this product is built on.
Recommendation: A because the pose each piece already has was produced by the exact integer walk, so capturing it is exact for free, while any route that re-derives a position needs a tolerance and loses exactness.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10, C=5/10
Pros / cons: see each option.
Net: A remembers, B recomputes and pays an epsilon for it, C sidesteps the question by forbidding the split.
Header: Component anchor
Options:
A) Capture the anchor from the pre-split pose (recommended)
At the moment a weld breaks, store the pose the new component's root already had. It came from the exact walk, so it is a lattice point in Z[zeta16] with an integer heading and needs no rounding. Both halves stay exactly where they were on screen.
B) The user places the split-off component
After the break, the detached part follows the pointer and the user drops it. Its origin is then whatever the drop produced, which needs snapping to the lattice.
C) Refuse a break that would disconnect the graph
Welds stay clickable, but breaking one that would split the layout is rejected with a reason. No component anchor is ever needed.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Capture the anchor from the pre-split pose. Answer reference: D4, this pass, matching the reviewer's recommendation.
Accepted scope: `Layout` grows a per-component anchor pose. When a weld breaks and the graph disconnects, the new component's anchor is the pose its root ALREADY held, captured at the moment of the split and never re-derived. That pose came from the exact integer walk, so it is a lattice point in Z[zeta16] with an integer heading and no snapping or epsilon is introduced anywhere; decision D8's rule that no float threshold decides topology is preserved. Both halves stay visually where they were. The persisted format grows the anchor field, so the storage key moves with it per finding A5.
History: first asked this pass; no earlier value.


#### R4: whether welds may ship before the per-cycle bound exists
Finding: A3, P1, confidence 9/10, `engine/src/solver/result.ts:95` with `app/src/App.tsx:121`, reviewer: eng review pass 4 (Architecture)
Plan baseline: the C-series is ordered C1 through C14 with no stated dependency between C10 (free-end clicking and declared welds, 4h human) and C8 (per-cycle residual and bound, 1 week human). Nothing in the plan says one must precede the other.
Runtime evidence: executed. `const give = totalGiveMm(res.joints)` at `result.ts:95` sums give over every joint in the layout and compares a single gap against that single pool. In the chain model this is sound only because cycle rank above 1 requires two coincident connectors, which C4 refuses; verified by counting V=15, E=16, CC=1 on the 8L+8R figure-eight using integer equality, giving rank 2, and confirming C4 reports it IMPOSSIBLE. An exhaustive sweep at 10 pieces per cycle found 9 distinct gaps in the band where a per-cycle bound refuses and a pooled bound passes, smallest 10.633mm against own-cycle give 8.0mm and pooled 16.0mm.
Comparison grid:

| Row | Current | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Can a weld be declared before C8 lands | n/a, welds do not exist | no; C10 depends on C8 | yes | yes |
| Bound used for a rank >= 2 layout | unreachable, C4 refuses rank >= 2 | per-cycle, correct from the first weld | pooled over all joints, optimistic, disclosed | none; verdict reports UNKNOWN with a reason |
| Optimistic number ever shown | never | never | yes, while the gap sits in the measured 9-value band | never |
| Temporary code written then deleted | none | none | the disclosure copy | the rank >= 2 UNKNOWN branch and its test |
| Rank 1 layouts, including closing a loop by welding | n/a | fully rated once C8 lands | fully rated | fully rated |
| Task ordering changed | no | yes, C10 after C8 | no | no |

Question D5:
D5 — May welds ship before the per-cycle bound, and what happens if they do?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `lane-b-tokens-persistence`; sequencing between C10 (declared welds, 4h) and C8 (per-cycle bound, 1 week).
ELI10: The verdict's bound adds up the wiggle room of every joint in the layout and compares the whole gap against that one total. That works while a layout is a single loop. The moment a user can weld two ends together they can make a layout with two loops, and then the wiggle room in loop B gets credited against a shortfall in loop A, which is wiggle room that physically cannot help. I measured it: at ten pieces per loop there are nine distinct gap values where the correct per-loop answer is a definite no and the pooled answer is "this might fit". Right now nobody can reach that state, because the overlap check refuses any chain that doubles back on itself, and that is the only thing holding it closed.
Stakes if we pick wrong: ship welds first and the product starts telling people a branched layout might fit when it definitely will not, which is the one kind of error this engine was rebuilt to stop making.
Recommendation: A because it costs nothing to implement, writes no code that later gets deleted, and C8 is on the critical path for switches regardless.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=4/10, C=8/10
Pros / cons: see each option.
Net: A reorders two tasks and the hole never exists; C ships the interaction sooner and pays for a temporary state; B ships a wrong number with a note next to it.
Header: Weld sequencing
Options:
A) Make C10 depend on C8 (recommended)
Record the dependency in the task list: declared welds land after the per-cycle residual and bound. No disclosure copy, no temporary verdict state, and the first weld a user ever declares is rated correctly.
B) Ship welds now with the pooled bound, disclosed
Let welds land first and add verdict copy saying the bound is optimistic for a layout with more than one loop.
C) Ship welds now, refuse to rate a branched layout
Welds land first. Any layout whose cycle rank exceeds 1 reports UNKNOWN with the reason that multi-loop bounds are not implemented yet. Single-loop layouts, including a loop closed by welding, are rated normally.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Make C10 depend on C8. Answer reference: D5, this pass, matching the reviewer's recommendation.
Accepted scope: C10 (free-end clicking and declared welds) gains a hard dependency on C8 (per-cycle residual and bound). No weld may be declarable until the per-cycle bound exists, so the pooled bound is never applied to a layout of cycle rank 2 or more and no optimistic figure is ever displayed. No disclosure copy and no temporary verdict state are written. The dependency is recorded in the Implementation Tasks dependency table below, and C4's vertex lift (finding A1) must not land ahead of C8 either, since it is what makes rank >= 2 expressible.
History: first asked this pass; no earlier value.


### 2. Code quality review

**B1. [P1] (confidence: 10/10) `engine/src/geom/pose.ts:60` and
`engine/src/geom/fk.ts:53` — three consumers will need the same traversal and the
plan has each of them build it.** Today there are two walkers over the same
structure, differing only in arithmetic:

```
 pose.ts:60   export function walk(layout, resolve): Pose[]          15 lines, exact Cyc
 fk.ts:53     export function walkFloat(layout, resolve, slop): FkResult   54 lines, float x/y/h
```

Both iterate `layout.placements` in order, which is trivially the same order
because a chain has only one. A graph has no canonical order, so order becomes a
choice, and the accepted arrangement puts a traversal in `pose.ts`, another in
`fk.ts`, and the cycle basis in `residual.ts`. That is three consumers of one
spanning forest: exact pose propagation, float render poses, and fundamental
cycle extraction. C8's own wording is "One traversal serving both", and the plan's
inherited note says "Do not write two traversals."

Shared-code rubric, applied: **callers proven** at the two authored source
locations above, plus `residual.ts`'s per-cycle work as the third, all first-party.
**Reuse first:** nothing existing covers it; `walk` and `walkFloat` are the prior
art and they are the duplication. **Helper kept small:** one function returning
parent pointers and a visit order; no arithmetic, no options. **Accounting, stated
honestly: this ADDS lines.** Roughly 35 added for the forest build and order,
roughly 20 of traversal logic removed from the two walkers, so implementation
savings are about **minus 15 lines**, plus shared-contract tests on top. The case
is reliability, not size: three consumers that must agree on a traversal order,
where disagreement shows up as the renderer drawing a layout the solver rated.
Remedy R5, pending.

**B2. [P1] (confidence: 10/10) `engine/src/solver/result.ts:47` —
`export type SolveFailure = 'OPEN_CHAIN' | 'HEADING_MISMATCH' | 'NUMERIC'`.**
`OPEN_CHAIN` is a chain concept and has no meaning in a graph: the condition is
not "the chain is open", it is "the graph has cycle rank 0, so there is no loop to
check". The name must become `NO_CYCLE` with the detail text rewritten. Recorded
as a correction, not a question. One piece of luck worth naming: `FAILURE_COPY`
in `app/src/VerdictLine.tsx:29` was retyped from `Record<string, string>` to
`Record<SolveFailure, string>` earlier today, so the rename is now a compile error
in the UI rather than a silent fall-through to generic copy.

**B3. [P2] (confidence: 9/10) plan line 9397 (C10) — a dangling weld is not
covered.** C10's verify condition reads "deleting the piece under the active end
does not leave clicks silently dead", which covers the transient selection. It
does not cover a DECLARED weld whose endpoint piece is later deleted. In a graph
that is an edge pointing at a vertex that no longer exists, and it is reachable by
the ordinary remove path. Needs its own stated behaviour: deleting a piece removes
every weld touching it, and if that disconnects the graph, R2's captured anchor
applies to the new component.

**B4. [P3] (confidence: 10/10) `app/src/App.tsx:13-18`** — the header comment
still states "A layout is ONE connected chain, optionally closed (Y1). Several
separate tracks need a per-component origin, which the engine has no field for"
and "Pieces append to the end and remove from the end (X1b)". Both become false
with this work, and the per-component origin sentence is specifically answered by
R2. Already owned by C14; noted so C14's scope is not read as docs-only polish.

#### R5: whether the spanning-forest traversal is extracted once or built per consumer
Finding: B1, P1, confidence 10/10, `engine/src/geom/pose.ts:60` and `engine/src/geom/fk.ts:53`, reviewer: eng review pass 4 (Code Quality)
Plan baseline: the accepted arrangement at D2 places a traversal in `pose.ts`, a traversal in `fk.ts` and the cycle basis in `residual.ts`. C8 says "One traversal serving both" and the plan's inherited note says "Do not write two traversals", but no task assigns the shared traversal a home. Extraction is not approved by the arrangement answer.
Runtime evidence: read. `walk` is 15 lines and iterates `layout.placements`; `walkFloat` is 54 lines and iterates the same list with float arithmetic and an optional slop array. A chain imposes one order so the two cannot currently disagree; a graph imposes none.

| Row | Current | A | B |
|---|---|---|---|
| Traversal implementations | 2, chain order, cannot disagree | 1 shared order, 3 arithmetic consumers | 3 independent graph traversals |
| Can consumers disagree on order | no, a chain has one order | no, one order by construction | yes |

| Blast radius of a shared bug | n/a | all three consumers at once | one consumer |
| Honors C8's "one traversal serving both" | n/a | yes | no |
| R1, R2, R3, R4 | R1 carried, R2/R3/R4 approved | unchanged | unchanged |

Question D6:
D6 — One shared traversal, or one per consumer?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `lane-b-tokens-persistence`; Code Quality on the graph work, with the file arrangement already fixed at D2.
ELI10: Walking a chain is trivial because there is only one way to walk it, so the exact engine and the renderer cannot disagree about the order. A graph has no natural order, so whoever walks it has to choose one, and the plan currently has three different places choosing independently: the exact pose walk, the float walk the canvas draws from, and the cycle extraction. If any two of them pick differently, the picture on screen and the verdict underneath it stop describing the same layout, and nothing would fail loudly.
Stakes if we pick wrong: a renderer and a solver that disagree about which loop is which produce a correct-looking drawing with the wrong verdict attached, which is the hardest class of bug to notice and the one the plan already warned about in its own notes.
Recommendation: A because three consumers have to agree and the only way to guarantee that is to give them one order to share, even though it adds about fifteen lines rather than removing any.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10
Pros / cons: see each option.
Net: A costs a few lines and a shared-contract test to make disagreement impossible; B saves the abstraction and leaves three places free to drift.
Header: Traversal
Options:
A) Extract the forest and its order once (recommended)
One function builds the spanning forest and returns parent pointers plus a visit order. The exact walk, the float walk and the cycle extraction all consume it and apply only their own arithmetic. Shared-contract tests cover the order, the parent pointers and a disconnected graph.
B) Each consumer builds its own traversal
`pose.ts`, `fk.ts` and `residual.ts` each walk the graph their own way, as the accepted arrangement literally describes. No new module, no shared contract.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Extract the forest and its order once. Answer reference: D6, this pass, matching the reviewer's recommendation.
Accepted scope: one function builds the spanning forest and returns parent pointers plus a deterministic visit order. Its three consumers are the exact pose walk in `pose.ts`, the float render walk in `fk.ts` and the fundamental cycle extraction in `residual.ts`; each applies only its own arithmetic and none chooses its own order. Shared-contract tests are required and cover the visit order, the parent pointers, and the forest over a disconnected graph, which is also what R2's per-component anchor depends on. Accepted knowing this adds roughly fifteen net implementation lines; it is taken for reliability, not size. The accepted D2 arrangement is unchanged: the forest lives with the cycle work in `engine/src/solver/residual.ts`.

#### Findings

**C1. [P1] (confidence: 10/10) `engine/test/render.test.ts:20` and
`app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx:30` — the only test touching the renderer cannot
detect a renderer regression, demonstrated by mutation.** The motivating lines:

```
 render.test.ts:19-20   /** The sweep flag the renderer uses. Must match TrackCanvas.segmentPath. */
                        const SWEEP_FOR_LEFT = 1;
 render.test.ts:43        const sweep = (s.sweep === 'left' ? SWEEP_FOR_LEFT : 1 - SWEEP_FOR_LEFT) as 0 | 1;
 TrackCanvas.tsx:30       const sweepFlag = s.sweep === 'left' ? 1 : 0;
```

`SWEEP_FOR_LEFT` exists only in the test. The renderer computes its flag inline,
and the two files' comments point at each other: the test says "Must match
TrackCanvas.segmentPath", the renderer says "Pinned by engine/test/render.test.ts".
Nothing enforces either claim. **Mutation run: inverting `TrackCanvas.tsx:30` to
`s.sweep === 'left' ? 0 : 1`, which the test file's own header says renders the
eight-curve circle "as a flower", leaves all 50 tests passing.** The test asserts
the SVG spec against its own copy of the constant, which is a circular pin. This
matters now rather than later because the graph work changes this renderer: U3
draws footprint bodies and U2 already moved stroke width to a per-part value.
Fix: export the flag from the renderer and have the test import it, so the
constant has one owner.

**C2. [P1] (confidence: 10/10) plan line 9381 (C7) — "all 23 existing fixtures
run unmodified" is not achievable, and the estimate assumes it is.** C7 proposes a
`chainToGraph(ids, closed)` adapter at 1h human / 10min CC. The adapter does solve
the fixture half: a piece list can be rebuilt as a graph. It cannot solve the
assertion half. Counted in the current suite: **13 assertion sites** bound to chain
identity (`:179` the failure-reason name, `:396`/`:410`/`:411` clash pair slot
indices, `:335`/`:349`/`:351`/`:362` and `:482`/`:487`/`:511`/`:513` joint indices
and ordered `between` tuples), plus **10 fixture sites** passing a `closed:` flag
that C6 retires, plus one test at `:200-207` that rotates a chain's cut point and
becomes **vacuous** in a graph, because the adapter would build the identical graph
from every rotation and the assertion would pass without testing anything. The
suite is also 50 tests now, not 23. Remedy R6, pending.

**C3. [P2] (confidence: 9/10) `engine/test/render.test.ts:54` and throughout —
the render suite is float-only and must not be cited as exactness evidence.** It
imports `walkFloat` and asserts `toBeCloseTo(..., 6)`. That is correct for its
actual job, which is the sweep flag, and a prior learning already recorded it
being mis-cited as proof of exact arc centres. Stated here so the graph work does
not inherit the same mistake: the exact claims belong in `analytic.test.ts` with
integer comparisons.

Prior learning applied: **evidence-class-must-match-claim-class** (confidence
10/10, from 2026-10-07).

#### R6: the regression contract for the graph rewrite
Finding: C2, P1, confidence 10/10, plan line 9381 and the 13 assertion sites listed above, reviewer: eng review pass 4 (Test review)
Plan baseline: C7 as written, a `chainToGraph(ids, closed)` adapter so "all 23 existing fixtures run unmodified against the new engine", 1h human / 10min CC. No regression contract is approved for the rewrite as a whole. The overlap-specific contract approved as T1 in the third eng review stays approved and is satisfied by the four cases now in the suite.
Runtime evidence: executed and read. 50 tests pass today. 13 assertion sites reference chain identity, 10 fixture sites pass `closed:`, and `:200-207` becomes vacuous under an adapter. Mutation run: inverting the renderer sweep flag leaves all 50 green, so the render suite contributes no regression protection to the renderer.
Comparison grid:

| Row | Current | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fixture reuse | n/a | adapter | adapter | adapter |
| 13 chain-coupled assertions | pass against the chain engine | migrated to graph identity | deleted | migrated to graph identity |
| Vacuous rotation test at :200-207 | meaningful on a chain | replaced by a graph-identity test | deleted | replaced by a graph-identity test |
| Differential check, old engine vs new | none | none | none | required: both engines run every rank-1 fixture and must agree exactly on gapMm, verdict and clash set |
| Chain engine lifetime | the only engine | deleted at cutover | deleted at cutover | kept until the differential suite is green, then deleted |
| Catches "graph engine disagrees with the proven engine" | n/a | only where an assertion happens to cover it | no | yes, on every rank-1 fixture |
| Renderer sweep pin (C1) | circular, proven useless | fixed: constant exported and imported | fixed | fixed |
| Effort | n/a | ~1 day / ~1h | ~3h / ~20min | ~2 days / ~1.5h |

Question D7:
D7 — How is the graph rewrite kept from silently changing a verdict?
Project/branch/task: brio-designer on `lane-b-tokens-persistence`; the regression contract for replacing the chain engine with a graph engine.
ELI10: There are 50 passing tests, and they are the only evidence the exact engine works. The rewrite replaces the structure every one of them walks. About a quarter of them do not just place pieces, they assert things like "the clash was between connector 0 and connector 8" or "the failure reason is OPEN_CHAIN", and those sentences stop meaning anything once connectors become graph vertices. One test rotates the chain's starting point and checks the answer does not change; after the rewrite it would build the identical graph every time and pass without checking anything. On top of that I proved by mutation that the renderer test cannot catch a renderer bug at all, because it compares the renderer against its own copy of the constant.
Stakes if we pick wrong: the graph engine quietly disagrees with the chain engine on a layout both can express, every test still passes because the assertions that would have caught it were deleted or became vacuous, and the product starts giving a different verdict for the same track with nothing to point at.
Recommendation: C because the chain engine is small, already proven, and can be run beside the new one on every single-loop fixture to prove they agree, which is far stronger evidence than any assertion migration and is the standard way to de-risk a rewrite with no CI.
Completeness: A=8/10, B=4/10, C=10/10
Pros / cons: see each option.
Net: C keeps the old engine alive briefly to prove the new one matches it; A migrates assertions and trusts them; B drops the assertions that are in the way.
Header: Regression contract
Options:
C) Differential: run both engines until they agree (recommended)
Keep the chain engine during the migration. Every existing fixture that is rank 1, which is all 50, runs through both engines and must agree exactly on gapMm, verdict and the clash set. The 13 chain-coupled assertions are migrated to graph identity and the vacuous rotation test is replaced by a graph-identity test. The renderer constant gets one owner. The chain engine is deleted once the differential suite is green.
A) Migrate the assertions, no differential check
Adapter for the fixtures, and all 13 chain-coupled assertion sites rewritten against graph identity. The vacuous rotation test is replaced. The renderer constant gets one owner. The chain engine is deleted at cutover.
B) Adapter only, drop what does not port
Adapter for the fixtures. The 13 chain-coupled assertions and the rotation test are deleted rather than migrated, on the grounds that they assert a model that no longer exists.

State: approved
Actual answer: C) Differential: run both engines until they agree. Answer reference: D7, this pass, matching the reviewer's recommendation. The question was first declined, then re-presented verbatim on request and answered C.
Accepted scope, stated as the regression rule requires:
  BEHAVIOUR TO PRESERVE: for every fixture of cycle rank 1, which is all 50 today, the graph engine must agree with the chain engine EXACTLY on gapMm, on the verdict, and on the clash set. The chain engine stays in the tree and is exercised by that differential suite until it is green, after which it is deleted. The graph model may not drop `placements` before then.
  INTENTIONAL CHANGES, named so they are not read as regressions: the failure reason `OPEN_CHAIN` becomes `NO_CYCLE` (finding B2); clash identity moves from chain connector slots to graph vertex ids, so the `0~8` and `8~16` style assertions are re-pointed; joint identity moves from chain joint index to weld id, re-pointing the `jointIndex` and ordered `between` assertions; the `closed` boolean is retired from all 10 fixture sites; the cut-point rotation test at `:200-207` is replaced by a graph-identity test, because an adapter would build the identical graph from every rotation and the old assertion would pass without testing anything.
  ACCEPTANCE ASSERTIONS: (1) the differential suite passes on all 50 rank-1 fixtures with exact equality, not tolerance; (2) the 13 chain-coupled assertion sites listed in finding C2 are migrated, not deleted; (3) the renderer sweep flag has ONE owner, exported from `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx` and imported by `engine/test/render.test.ts`, and inverting it fails the suite, which it does not today.
History: first asked this pass. The question was declined once at first presentation, re-presented verbatim at the user's request, then answered C. No option content changed between presentations.


### 4. Performance review

No database, so no N+1 and no query patterns. `useMemo` in `app/src/App.tsx:112`
and `:121` is already keyed on the layout, so nothing re-solves without a change.
Everything below is measured on the committed tree with JIT warmup and a median
of 15 runs, not estimated.

```
  pieces   full solve   clash pairs   scaling
  -------|------------|-------------|----------------------------
       8      0.01ms             0
      64      0.20ms           231
     160      0.61ms         1,539
     320      2.29ms         6,279
     640     11.02ms        25,359   2x pieces -> 4.04x pairs
```

**D1. [P2] (confidence: 10/10) `engine/src/feasible.ts:76-90` — the clash check is
quadratic, and its measured worst case supersedes the number the plan records.**
The loop is `for (let a = 0; a <= n; a++) for (let b = a + 1; b <= n; b++)`, so
pairs grow as n squared; confirmed at 4.04x pairs for 2x pieces. At 640 large
curves, 80 stacked circles, the solve takes **11.02ms and reports 25,359 pairs**.
Eleven milliseconds synchronously inside render is acceptable for a click, which
is the only trigger, and it is nowhere near a hang.

The finding is the number, not the cost. Decision A4 of the third eng review
accepted "report and draw EVERY conflicting pair, with no cap" and recorded a
measured worst case of **204,480 conflicting pairs and 408,960 SVG nodes**. That
figure was computed for the plank-width footprint check, which decision D10 of the
CEO review then replaced with the coincident-connector check. The real worst case
under the shipped check is **25,359 pairs**, about eight times smaller. The
accepted decision, its upgrade trigger and its logged rationale all cite a number
that no longer applies to the code that exists.

**D2. [P2] (confidence: 10/10) `engine/src/feasible.ts` — decision A4's required
shortcut markers were not written, and half of A4 is unimplemented.** A4 says in
terms: "At implementation time mark the uncapped loop in engine/src/feasible.ts
and the batched path in app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx with a gstack-shortcut comment
naming this decision." `feasible.ts` landed earlier today with no such marker.
Separately, A4 approved reporting AND DRAWING every pair;
`app/src/VerdictLine.tsx` reports the count and `app/src/TrackCanvas.tsx` draws
nothing, so the drawing half does not exist. Both are owed work rather than new
decisions, since A4 is already approved.

**D3. [P3] (confidence: 10/10) cumulative cost of building click by click is
0.33s to 640 pieces.** Recorded because the first design review raised an
O(n squared) concern about `useMemo` being keyed on a fresh layout per click. The
concern is real in form and immaterial in size: every intermediate solve summed
together is a third of a second across 640 clicks. Closed with a number.

**Measurement caveat worth keeping.** `solveLayout` returns at
`engine/src/solver/result.ts:85-93` on a heading mismatch, BEFORE `computeResidual`
runs. A piece count that is not a multiple of 8 therefore never reaches the
expensive path: 641 large curves is 1282 steps, 2 modulo 16, so it short-circuits
in 0.17ms and reports no clashes. A first pass at this measurement read 641 as if
it were the worst case and understated the cost. Any future timing of this engine
has to use closing piece counts.
